# Tide: Agent Guide

Rules and decisions for anyone, human or AI agent, working on Tide. Only agreed decisions go in this file. Open questions stay out until they're decided.

## Working agreement

- The project owner is the lead programmer and writes most of the code.
- Agents help with specific, scoped tasks. Do what was asked. Don't widen scope, scaffold systems, or refactor nearby code unless asked.
- Agents validate changes locally and merge directly into `dev`. Do not create pull requests or push feature branches unless the owner explicitly requests them.
- If it's unclear whether the owner wants code or discussion, ask.
- **Exception: Claude owns the transpiler.** The owner decides the language design (what it should do, its syntax and semantics). Claude implements and maintains the transpiler, including its tests and build integration, and proposes designs for the owner to approve.
- The contract between the two sides is the C the transpiler generates and the engine runtime functions that code calls. The owner writes the runtime. Any runtime function the generated code needs is agreed on before either side depends on it.

## What Tide is

A networking-first game engine:

- The simulation is deterministic and uses floats, not fixed point, on supported hardware.
- User code and the engine's built-in systems are written in a custom language that compiles to native code.
- An ECS runs systems on multiple threads automatically. Data dependencies come from the ECS and the language, so users never have to think about threads.
- Graphics work is kept to a minimum. Rendering is not the focus.

## Platforms

- The main targets are desktop and consoles.
- Mobile is a target too: Android first, then iOS.
- A phone that goes to the background, or closes, can no longer simulate, so it leaves the match: with host migration on (see Networking), another player's machine takes over the match it hosted. Nothing keeps a phone's match running in the background.
- Touch is input on every platform, not only phones: the web (on phones and desktops) and desktop touchscreens have it.
- The web (WebAssembly, rendering with WebGL) is actively supported and tested, not an afterthought. Changes must keep the web build working.
- Determinism holds on every platform, the web included, so web and desktop players can share one simulation.

## Rendering and platform

- Windows, input and rendering are the platform layer's own, with no engine under it:
  - `platform/src/platform.c` is the frame loop, the devices and the renderer. What it needs of the system is `platform/src/window.h`: a window, what draws in it, and what its input devices say. Each build has one: `platform/desktop/glfw.c` (GLFW, for the window, keyboard, mouse, gamepads through its mappings, and clipboard), `platform/web/canvas.c` (the page, through `tide.js`) or `platform/android/activity.c` (NativeActivity and EGL).
  - The renderer is written once, against `platform/src/gpu.h`: buffers, textures, pipelines, targets and passes, whichever API is under them (shared, agreed, rather than a renderer per backend). It's shaped by WebGPU, the strictest: a pipeline is made once with its shaders, the vertices it reads and how it blends and tests depth; its uniforms are one block of bytes; drawing happens in passes that start cleared or as they were left, so a clear inside a list is drawn (a triangle over the target, which the clip cuts, and which sets depth back). Clip space is OpenGL's, and pixels and rows count from the top left; each backend turns those into its own. Compute joins it when Tide code computes on the GPU.
  - Backends so far: OpenGL (`platform/src/gpu_gl.c`: OpenGL 3.3 on desktop, OpenGL ES 3 on Android, WebGL 2 on the web) and, on the web, WebGPU (`platform/web/gpu_web.c`, whose functions are the page's: `tide.js` has the WebGPU objects, as it has WebGL's). Vulkan is next. The window says which draws (`tide_window_gpu`), and `tide_platform_renderer` names it; the stats overlay shows it.
  - The web draws with WebGPU by default, and with WebGL 2 only where that isn't possible (agreed): the browser has none, gives no device, or only its software one, which is slower than WebGL on a GPU. The page asks for the device before the program starts (`tide.js`), as asking takes a while and the program can't wait for an answer. `Tide.backend`, or `backend=` in the page's address, is `webgpu` (the software one too, and no falling back) or `webgl`: for tests, and for a player whose browser gets one of them wrong. A device that goes away while the game runs (a driver that starts over) takes what was made on it: the page asks for another, and the renderer, which sees it's another (`tide_gpu.epoch`, `gpu_ready` in `platform/src/platform.c`), forgets what it had there and makes it again as lists draw, from the copies the lists and the font keep. Nothing draws meanwhile. `tide_platform_draw_webgpu` loses its device on purpose and checks its pixels again. A canvas that draws with WebGPU can't go to WebGL, so with no device to be had again it stays as it is, and the page says to reload.
  - `platform/src/gl.h` is OpenGL as its backend uses it: only the functions it calls, which the three share, with no system header. On the web they're the page's (`tide.js` has each by its C name), on Android libGLESv3's, and on desktop they're looked up in the window's context through GLFW (`platform/desktop/gl.c`). A function it starts calling is added there and in `tide.js`, and it uses no extensions, but for one, which adds no function: drawing into float images (see A target holds several color images, below).
  - The renderer's own shaders (shapes, text, clears, the built-in meshes) are in `platform/src/shaders.h`, each written by hand for every backend: GLSL (one source, with a version line per platform, attributes at fixed locations, uniforms in a std140 block) and WGSL. A change to one goes to the other, and the same tests check both draw the same pixels. A game's shaders are tidec's, from its `frag` and `vert` declarations (below).
  - A pipeline samples up to 8 textures (`pixels0`... in GLSL; in WGSL, texture i and its sampler at bindings 1 + 2i and 2 + 2i), each through a sampler of the backend's, nearest, blended or between its mipmaps too, and clamped, repeated or mirrored past its edges (`sample`'s `filter` and `wrap`), so one texture can be read several ways in a draw (OpenGL's are sampler objects, bound by unit). Its vertex attributes are floats or ints (`TIDE_GPU_INT`...), 16 at most, and its uniform block 4096 bytes at most (`TIDE_GPU_MAX_UNIFORMS`), of which a game's shader has 3984 of its own (`TIDE_SHADER_UNIFORM_BYTES`, which tidec checks at the draw) after the engine's 96 (its camera, the screen and the tint): 255 float4s in all, as Android's emulator links no shader whose block is 256, counting its members as the vertex shader's uniform vectors; on WebGPU a draw's uniforms take as many 256 byte slots of the uniform buffer as they need. A texture that's a target's depth is read as a number or compared, through samplers of its own, which take no wrap (see A target says what it holds, below). `pipeline_free` lets one go.
  - A backend sets all the state it draws with at each pass, so nothing depends on what drew before it: a pass on another target or, on the web, the build this one took over from (see Hot reloading).
  - Text is `stb_truetype`'s (`platform/third_party/stb_truetype.h`, version 1.26 from nothings/stb, public domain, kept static to `platform/src/font.c` so a game's C can bring a copy of its own), in Tuffy, in two weights, each a file: regular (`platform/font/Tuffy.ttf`) and bold (`platform/font/Tuffy_Bold.ttf`), both version 1.28, from the one archive at tulrich.com/fonts (`tuffy-20120614.zip`), under the one `LICENSE.txt` beside them, public domain too, so games carry no license terms for any of it; GLFW's zlib license asks nothing of binaries. The build puts each in the library as an array (`cmake/embed.cmake`), an object of its own. A text's size is its font size, as CSS's and Unity's: the height of a line of it, with its capitals in the line's middle (what the font draws past a line, accents above and tails below, goes past evenly). Text has to be sharp at the sizes it's read at, and the font has no hints (stb_truetype would read none), so it's fitted to the target's pixels (`platform/src/font.c`): drawn at the whole number of pixels nearest its size, never stretched (but past 128 pixels, where nobody sees it); each glyph's outline moved onto them before it's drawn, as a hinted font's is; and each glyph a whole number of pixels from the last. The fitting finds a glyph's stems in its outline (an edge is a run of it along an axis, a straight side or a curve where it turns back; two edges that are each other's nearest across ink are a stem): upright stems and flat ones (bars, the tops and bottoms of bowls) are a whole number of pixels thick between pixel edges, those about as thick as the font's usual stem all alike, so letters keep one weight; the heights letters share (the baseline, the x-height, the capitals', the descenders') are on the same pixel edges in every glyph, and what round letters go past them by is dropped until it's a pixel; and the rest of the outline moves in proportion between the edges that moved. Stems and bars then fill their pixels, where an outline drawn as it falls blends them between two. What's slanted or round still covers pixels in part, and colors blend as the display's values, not as light, so light text's parts count for more (the text shaders, `platform/src/shaders.h`: a pixel's share to a power under 1, 0.55 for white), or they'd fade beside the stems; dark text is as it is. `tide_platform_font` (`platform/tests/font.c`, no window or GPU, so every platform's tests run it) checks that glyphs of upright and flat strokes have no pixel covered in part at any size from 8 to 48, and that letters share their heights and their stems' thickness, in both weights. Glyphs are drawn the first time each is needed at a size and in a weight into an atlas of a byte a pixel that grows as it needs, and text goes to the GPU as two triangles a glyph. `tide_platform_measure_text` is the size that's drawn (`tide_font_size`): its widest line by the same whole-pixel advances, and its lines' height, a line as high as the size and each newline the whole pixels lines are apart (`tide_font_pitch`, which drawing uses too), at the window's scale or, for a render target, at its image's own (the `tide_target_size` it's given). It needs no window open. It's the `tide_measure_fn` (`tide/draw.h`) hosts give the GUI, and what Tide code measures with: `Draw.MeasureText` is `tide_draw_measure_text` (`engine/src/draw_coordinates.c`), which asks it at the size the frame's 2D camera draws text at and gives the answer back in the units the view draws in, so a view measures what it draws, to the pixel. A host with no measure function gets a guess (`tide_draw_guess_text`). `tide_platform_draw` (`text_measures_as_drawn` in `platform/tests/draw.c`) draws text in parts, each where the last measures to, and checks they're the pixels of the text drawn at once, in both weights.
  - A text's position is a place of its box (agreed): `Draw.Text(text, position, size, color[, anchor][, font])`, with the GUI's `Anchor` (`tide_anchor`, in `tide/draw.h`), its top left corner without one. The box is what `MeasureText` gives, upper is towards the top of the screen under a camera too, and each line is against the anchor's side. The command keeps the anchor (`tide_draw_command.words`), and the platform places each line (`line_start`, `add_text` in `platform/src/platform.c`), on whole pixels as ever: text at an anchor is, to the pixel, its lines drawn from the corners its box puts them at, which `text_anchors` in `platform/tests/draw.c` checks for the nine, on the screen and under a camera, with one line and several, in both weights. `Anchor.UpperLeft` goes through the arithmetic it always did.
  - Bold is the font's own bold weight, an argument of the draw (agreed: `Font.Bold`, as `Filter.Point` is an argument), and a game that never uses it doesn't carry its file (agreed). How:
    - C names a weight by its file: `TIDE_FONT_REGULAR` (NULL) or `TIDE_FONT_BOLD`, the array `tide_font_bold_ttf` (`tide/draw.h`). `tide_draw_text_at` takes one, and so does a renderer's `tide_measure_fn`. A text command only says it's bold (`words.bold`), and its list has the file (`tide_draw_list.bold`, set by the frame's first bold text and cleared by `tide_draw_reset`), which the platform draws with. So the file reaches the renderer as data, from the code that draws with it.
    - Nothing in the engine or the platform layer names `tide_font_bold_ttf`: no source file of either may. Its array is alone in its object (`tide_font_bold.c`, which the build makes) in the platform layer's library, which stays a static one, so a linker only takes that object out for a program whose own code names the array. Generated C does for a game that says `Font.Bold`, or makes a `Font` of a number (`program.names_bold`, in the `tide_font_file` helper that turns a `Font` into its file), and for no other.
    - That's a program's link: CMake's, and `tide build`'s on every target. `tide run`'s library of the game has none of the platform layer, as its host does, so for a game that names the file (`game_info.bold`) tide links the library with the platform layer's too, which gives it that one object (`link_objects` in `compiler/cli/build.c`). A program with no platform layer that such a game is built into (a test's host, a server) needs the object too: `tide_add_game` links a `HOST`'s program with `tide_font_bold`, a library of that one object (`cmake/Tide.cmake`), so `duel_tests` builds as it did; a host built some other way links the platform layer's library, or defines the array itself, as nothing draws with it there.
    - `platform/src/font.c` keeps what it measures of a font per weight (`weight`: the file, its lines, its advances, and the fitting's heights and usual stems), and a glyph's key has its weight's number, which is another whenever a weight's file is: a game's library that was built again has its own copy of it, at another address, and nothing of the old one is read again. Both weights have the same lines, so text of both stands on one baseline. A code point the bold weight has no glyph for (it has 887 code points of the regular's 1499) is the regular weight's, drawn and measured so.
    - Stems are a whole number of pixels thick in both, by each weight's own usual stem, so under 13 pixels both are one pixel thick (and at 20, both two): there, bold differs in its letters' shapes and widths alone. Nothing fattens it to tell them apart. What the agreed design left open (this, the regular weight's name, what a missing glyph is) is in `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, awaiting the owner.
    - The GUI, the stats overlay and the debug views are in the regular weight.
    - `tide_platform_font_files` (`platform/tests/font_files.mjs`, every platform) reads two games as the build left them, `tests/text.tide`, which says `Font.Bold`, and `tests/text_plain.tide`, which doesn't, and checks the bold's file is in the first and none of it in the second. `tide_platform_text` is also text to look at: `build/debug/bin/tide_platform_text --frames 2 --capture text.png --size 1180 900` draws both weights at the sizes text is read at, light on dark and dark on light, and a text at each anchor.
- Tide code runs on the GPU on the backends there are (agreed, ahead of Vulkan): `frag` and `vert` (see `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing), which tidec compiles to GLSL (OpenGL 3.3, OpenGL ES 3, WebGL 2) and WGSL (WebGPU). What the GPU computes is for what's seen, never the match: GPUs don't give the same bits everywhere.
- The agreed order from here, with OpenGL staying as a small fallback for machines without the main backends (browsers without WebGPU, older phones):
  1. Vulkan natively (MoltenVK on Apple until a Metal backend is worth writing), under the same `gpu.h` as WebGPU: explicit pipelines, buffers and compute. tidec writes SPIR-V for it then, beside GLSL and WGSL.
  2. The rest of render state (which side draws, masks), as draw settings like the ones there are (below), frags with other outputs than one color (drawing into a texture is there already: render targets), and Tide code that computes on the GPU. On the OpenGL fallback, compute runs on CPU threads instead, so games work everywhere.
  - How a draw blends was part of that second step, and the owner brought it forward (2026-10-05), with tints, transforms and the settings rule: "these are things we need as part of the foundation of our drawing apis". What a 3D mesh does with depth followed (2026-10-06), from Alley's shadows, which hid each other: "this seems like our Draw3D or shaders or whatever might need some tweaking".
  - More of that second step is agreed (2026-10-07; `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, What the GPU side gains, which has each rule and which names are picks), and built in part: a target's format and its depth read are (below: A target says what it holds), the rest isn't. It came of how the demos drew shadows: Sumo laid its casters flat on the floor and shaded the floor again under them, as nothing could read a target's depth (its shadows are a shadow map since: see The physics games). The owner asked for no shadows feature but for a complete set of raw pieces a game builds any technique from, and decided each:
    - A draw with no frag keeps the engine's default look, and never means "depth only": what a draw writes is said.
    - A target says what it holds when it's made (a target of depth alone is the new one), and a frag reads a target's depth, plainly or compared with a number. Built.
    - Cull is a setting of `Draw3D`'s whose default is that the back of a triangle doesn't draw ("its what its expected in game engines"), so every 3D mesh in the repo has its winding checked with it: "we should NEVER be afraid to make changes based on our existing work (well until we release v1)". **Built 2026-10-07**: see the draw settings, below (Which side of a 3D mesh's triangles draws).
    - How `uv` wraps is chosen where a texture is sampled, which is built (below: What a `uv` past a texture's edges reads). Stencil comes as a GPU has it (a number a pixel, a test, an operation), last. **Built 2026-10-07**: see the draw settings, below (Stencil is a setting too).
    - A frag's outputs are `out`, its color too (the owner's idea), and it can have several. A vert hands its frag a struct through an `out` parameter. Verts and frags stay declarations of their own, paired at the draw (the owner, of grouping them in a `shader` block, which stays an open idea: "lets keep them separate"). **Built 2026-10-07**, several outputs too: see What a frag and a vert write, and A target holds several color images, below.
    - No setting for whether a draw writes color.
- **The drawing surface is being reshaped (agreed 2026-10-07, from a review of the graphics side the owner asked for: "I want to tackle all your points and I agree with them").** The owner wants the foundation complete from a rendering standpoint and simple to approach, and found three things off: `Grid2<Color>` being a texture, the names, and where settings go. What's agreed, each a card, in stages; a card that lands updates this file with what it built, and the users' pages with it:
  1. **One `Draw`, which has every method a render target has, and nothing else.** `Draw2D` and `Draw3D` go: the 2D and 3D forms are overloads told apart by type (`Draw.Camera(float2 center, float size)` beside `Draw.Camera(float3 position, quaternion rotation, float fov)`; `Draw.Mesh` of a `List<Vertex>` beside one of a `List<Vertex3>` with a transform), and the window is the same object a target is (`target.Clip`, `target.Depth`, `target.Stencil`, `target.Mesh` are the API; `Draw.X` is the window's). Every setting belongs to the destination it's called on, blend and tint too, with one rule for depth and cull: only 3D meshes have depth or a back. The old spellings are errors with the rename as a quick fix, as the earlier renames were. **Built 2026-10-07**: see One `Draw`, under Drawing below, and `docs/spec.md`, Provisional, One `Draw`, for the picks.
  2. **One word for the window, and `Screen` for the window's measurements alone:** `RenderTarget.Window()` and `Window(factor)` for the target that follows the window, `Draw.Screen()` and `Draw.SetScreen()` for drawing in the screen's pixels (the card named them `Pixels`, which went back to `Screen` the same day, below), `Screen.width` and `Screen.height` as they are. Depth alone is `Format.Depth`, and the formats are named by what they store so the scheme scales. **Built 2026-10-07**: see One word for the window, under Drawing below, and `docs/spec.md`, Provisional, One word for the window, and formats by what they store, for the picks.
  3. **`Texture` is the one name a draw and a `Sample` take.** A `Grid2<Color>` converts to one implicitly where a draw or a `Sample` wants one, as it already does underneath, and so does a `Grid2<float>` (a texture of one channel: a heightmap, a lookup table). A `Texture` value carries its filter and wrap, so `art.sprite = tiles` draws as `tiles` declared: the `[Filter]` and `[Wrap]` attributes become the sampling a `Texture` is born with, and a draw's or a `Sample`'s arguments an override, which leaves two places to say it where there were three. No fourth CPU image type. **Built 2026-10-07**: see `Texture` is the one type, below, and `docs/spec.md`, Provisional, Textures, for the picks.
  4. **A per-draw parameter struct** for `Mesh` and `Blit`, bound as an `Instanced` draw's struct is, so a blur's direction isn't a singleton field written between two blits: `Draw.Blit(src, dst, Blur, BlurParams { direction = float2(1, 0) })`. **Built 2026-10-07**: see A draw's values, below (after What a frag and a vert write), and `docs/spec.md`, Provisional, A draw's values, for the picks.
  5. **The cameras' matrices are readable** in a view, so a shadow map's frag isn't handed a world-to-clip matrix the game built by hand, z-flip and all. **Built 2026-10-07**: see The cameras as matrices, under Drawing below, and `docs/spec.md`, Provisional, The cameras as matrices, for the picks.
  6. **Mipmaps**, the one capability gap that keeps the 3D side from being complete (shimmer and cache thrash on textured floors at a distance); the card designs how a mipmapped sample is written where code may sample in a branch. **Built 2026-10-07**: see Mipmaps are a filter, below (after `Texture` is the one type), and `docs/spec.md`, Provisional, Mipmaps, for the picks.
  7. **Alpha and color space are decided, not left by omission:** everything blends straight alpha in display space today, assets aren't premultiplied and nothing is sRGB-aware. The card shows the owner the fringe bilinear sampling of straight-alpha sprites makes and the two ways out (premultiply at pack time with a premultiplied default blend, or keep display-space blending and document it), and the owner picks. **Picked and built 2026-10-07** (premultiplied under the hood, display space): see Alpha is premultiplied under the hood, under Drawing below, and `docs/spec.md`, Provisional, Alpha and color space, for the picks.
  8. **MSAA on render targets**, a `samples` argument, as 3D mesh edges have no antialiasing (smooth shapes are analytic, so 2D needs none). **Built 2026-10-07**: see A target's samples, below (after A target holds several color images), and `docs/spec.md`, Provisional, A target's samples, for the picks.
  9. **Cubemaps**, for skyboxes and reflections, last. **Built 2026-10-07**: see Cubemaps, below (after A target holds several color images), and `docs/spec.md`, Provisional, Cubemaps, for the picks. With it every card of this list is built.
  - What stays as it is, by the same review: frags and verts bound by their parameters, instancing that falls out of a view running per entity, textures with nothing to create or free, settings as a block form and a `Set` form reset every frame, logical pixels on targets, the GPU kept out of the match, `gpu.h`'s shape, the analytic shapes and the fitted text, and the enums `Blend`, `Depth`, `Cull`, `Wrap`, `Filter`, `Pixel`, `Instance`.
- Rendering stays disconnected from the simulation, so it can be replaced later (for example for consoles):
  - The simulation never includes a platform header.
  - The view reads the world and draws it. It never writes simulation state.
  - The platform layer's only link to the simulation is filling `Devices`.
  - Game code never shares a source file with the system's headers or GLFW's: generated headers name types after the game's components (`Transform`), and those define many of the same names. Hosts include `tide/platform.h`, which includes neither, and `tide_platform` keeps them private.
- `tide/run.h` is the standard host: `tide_run(&(tide_run_desc){.title = "...", .argc = argc, .argv = argv})` opens a window and runs the game in a session (`tide/session.h`), sampling this machine's input from the devices once per tick it runs, and draws its views and their GUI. The devices keep what happened for the next sample (a key that went down and up between two ticks, how far the mouse moved), and a sample clears them (`tide_devices_consume`). Where no tick can sample, the host clears them itself at the end of each frame (`tide_run_frame`): in a game with no input, and while the session isn't connected (in no match, connecting, or finding a match that changed hands), which is the session's to say, never a count of frames. So a match started from a menu reads what's held as it starts, never what was pressed before it; in a match that runs, a frame with no tick clears nothing. `tide_platform_web_first_tick` (`platform/tests/web_first_tick.c` with `first_tick.tide`) checks both in a page, and `tide_platform_first_tick` (`first_tick.mjs`, label `gpu`) the first in a run with no window. When `Main` is the match's, it starts at once, and opens it with `--host [port]`, or joins another with `--join code` (a room's) or `--connect address`. This machine's player joins before the first tick. A game needs no other C. Its loop is in `tide/host.h`, which runs a game through a table of its functions (`tide_host_game`), so that `tide run` can swap builds (see Hot reloading).
- Seeing what a game draws needs no window (agreed: agents never open one on the owner's screen, and CI wants pictures too; the names are provisional). The standard host runs a game headless when its command line has `--capture <file.png>` (the run's last frame, as a PNG), `--frames <n>` (the run ends after them) or `--script <file>`, with `--size <width> <height>`, `--scale <s>` (as on a display scaled that much) and `--seed <n>` (the seed of its matches, below), so every game `tide_add_game`, `tide run` and `tide build` make has it: `build/debug/bin/duel --script play.txt --size 1280 720`. `tide run` passes them on and runs the game once: nothing is watched or reloaded, and tide's exit code is the game's. It's the way to look at a game: no scratch host, no hidden window of one's own.
  - A script (`platform/src/script.c`, `docs/tools/cli.md`) is a line an event, the frame it happens at (from 1, in order) and what: `120 key d down`, `200 key enter` (a tap), `210 mouse 640 360` (logical pixels from the top left), `211 button left`, `220 move 40 -8`, `230 scroll 0 -1`, `260 text K7QF2M`, `300 touch 1 down 100 200`, `330 pad buttonSouth down`, `400 capture fight.png` (beside the script) and `500 quit`. A line that's wrong is named, with what to write, before anything opens.
  - `tide_platform_open_headless` (`platform/src/platform.c`) is what the host opens. Its window is GLFW's, never shown, and only there for its graphics context: frames draw into a target of the platform's own (`gpu_state.frame`), the size asked for times the scale, through the path a window's frames take (not `offscreen`), so text is measured and fitted at that scale, and the stats and the profiler are in the picture. `tide_platform_frame_pixels` reads a frame whole, and `tide_platform_capture` writes it as a PNG (`platform/src/png.c`: ours, from the RFCs, deflate's fixed codes with matches, or stored blocks where that's shorter, so a frame of flat colors is a few kilobytes).
  - Nothing about a run is this machine's, or timed. The frame loop makes time up, a 60th of a second a frame (`step`), frames are counted, and the host's session takes no clock and no entropy (`tide_run_new_session` in `tide/host.h`, which every session the host makes comes from), so its matches start with seed 0 and a game that rolls plays the same every run. `--seed <n>`, from 0 to 2147483647, picks another seed, to see the game roll otherwise: it's the seed of every match the machine starts in that run (`tide_session_desc.seed`, which a session with no entropy starts its matches with). It's the standard host's on every platform and with a window too, where it stands in for the system's entropy, and `tide run` passes it on. The devices are made up too (`script.c` says what `window.h` would, and `tide_platform_poll` takes it the same way, so the GUI's hiding and views' claims go as in play): never the system's, as even a hidden window knows where the real cursor is. The cursor locks whenever the game asks, and the clipboard is left alone. So a run's match is the same on every machine, and its pictures on one machine byte for byte; GPUs differ in what they blend.
  - Exit codes: 0 at `--frames` or `quit`; 1 when a picture can't be written; 2, before anything ran, for a flag or a script's line that's wrong, or a run nothing ends. Desktop only: a web page is driven and screenshotted in headless Chrome, as the web tests do.
  - `tide_platform_png` (`platform/tests/png.mjs`, every platform, no GPU) reads what the writer wrote with Node's zlib; `tide_platform_capture` (`capture.mjs`, label `gpu`) runs `capture.tide` with a script of every kind of input and reads its pictures' pixels, the match's seed and what it rolled from it among what they show, then runs it again for the same bytes, and with `--seed` for the seed it asked for and other rolls; `tide_platform_capture_refused` checks what a run refuses, with no GPU.
- That nothing an agent runs shows on the owner's screen doesn't rest on the agent remembering: `TIDE_HIDDEN` in the environment, set to anything but nothing or 0, says nobody watches a run (`tide_hidden`, `tide/page.h`), and then no Tide program shows a window or a dialog. Desktop only.
  - Agents' shells have it. Claude Code sessions get it from `.claude/settings.json` (`env`), the one file of `.claude/` that's checked in, so every checkout and worktree has it. A Codex session exports it before anything else it runs: `export TIDE_HIDDEN=1`, or in PowerShell `$env:TIDE_HIDDEN = '1'`. It's never set where it would reach the owner's own runs: not the user's environment, not the editors' run configurations. `tide run`, CTest and the tests' scripts pass the environment on as it is.
  - Two rules stand beside it, as a program built before the variable doesn't know it: an agent runs only programs built in its own checkout (another checkout's `build/` can be older than the flags it's given, and opens a window), and never runs a game without `--capture`, `--frames` or `--script`.
  - A window that isn't `hidden` has only its closing to end its run, which nobody could do, so the program is refused: it ends with exit code 2 before it opens anything, having said what to give it instead (decided over running hidden for a counted number of frames, which would end with 0 as if something had been checked, and as a run with no window that nothing ends is refused already). `tide_platform_open` refuses any such window (`platform/src/platform.c`), the standard host names its flags first (`tide_run_open` in `tide/host.h`), and `tide run` refuses before it builds (`compiler/cli/main.c`); `tide run --web` serves its page and opens no browser. A window that's `hidden`, as every test's is, and a run with no window go as they do without the variable. Under the refusal, `tide_window_open` shows no window whatever asked (`platform/desktop/glfw.c`).
  - A program that stops shows no dialog either. On Windows a debug build's `abort()`, which every stop of the engine's and of generated code ends in, shows one that waits for a click, a failed `assert` shows one in a program with no console, and the system shows its own for a crash or a library that's missing. With the variable they're off before `main` (`hidden_start` in `engine/src/page.c`, a constructor, there because every program of the engine's links that file: programs with no window stop too, and none has to remember it), and tide turns the system's off for what it starts (`sys_hidden`).
  - `tide_platform_hidden` (`platform/tests/hidden.mjs`, with `hidden.c`, no GPU) checks what's refused, by a game and by a host of its own, that a stop ends at once, and that nothing changes without the variable; `tide_platform_hidden_runs` (label `gpu`) that a run with no window draws the same picture with it and without, and that a window asked to show doesn't. `tide_tests_hidden` and `tide_tests_watched` check a program with no window, and `tide_cli_hidden` checks `tide run`. Whoever runs the tests may have the variable, so a test that starts a program says which it's for: set, or taken out of the environment.
- Views are written in Tide (`view` declarations). Their `Draw` calls record commands into a renderer-agnostic draw list (`tide/draw.h`), which grows as it needs, with no limit but memory. The platform layer renders the list (`tide_platform_draw`), so another backend only means another of that function. Drawing a lot has to stay cheap: shapes (rects, circles, their outlines and lines, and the smooth shapes, below) go to the GPU as instances of one quad, each its shape in window pixels, so a run of them between text and clears is one draw call (`platform/src/platform.c`), and so is a run of text. `tide_platform_draw --bench` (`?bench` on the web) times frames of 65,536 shapes, of as many textured quads of one mesh, and of as many instances of a 3D cube. The stats show in the bottom right corner when the host asks (`tide_run_desc.stats`: `STATS`, `tide run --stats`) or the game's `stats` setting does (`tide_game.stats`), which the host reads every frame (`tide_run_stats` in `tide/host.h`), so a reload brings them or takes them away. They're a drawer (below): one line, which opens to the frame rate, what a frame's work takes on its thread (`cpu`: the frame function up to the drawer, so not the wait for the display), ping, bandwidth (sessions count the bytes of their datagrams over networks, not loopback: `tide_session_status.sent_bytes` and `received_bytes`), entity count, the threads ticks run on (`tide_platform_jobs`), the memory the program has (`tide_platform_memory`, `platform/src/memory.c`: the working set on Windows, what's in RAM on Linux and Android, the footprint on macOS, and on the web the WebAssembly memory's size), what draws and in how many draw calls (`tide_platform_draw_calls`: the steps of the last list that drew, the drawer's aside). It's for a glance, so a number that joins it has to earn its line: the drawer holds 15, including rendering CPU and GPU time. `vram` is one (the owner, 2026-10-07: "lets keep track of it ourselves"): what the renderer has on the GPU by its own count, `tide_platform_gpu_memory` (`platform/src/platform.c`: every buffer, texture, cube and target it asks the backend for goes through `made_*` and `free_*` there, which keep each one's bytes by the backend's id, a texture's smaller images a third more, and a lost device zeroes it all in `gpu_ready`). It's what was asked for, by the formats' bytes, so it's the same number on every backend, and never the window's own framebuffer, the pipelines or what the driver keeps; `check_gpu_memory` in `platform/tests/targets.c` pins a target's bytes and that a disposed one's go. The profiler shows the same two numbers in its frame column's last row, `Memory`, with no bar (`tide_profiler.memory`, `gpu_memory`, which the host sets once a frame, reading the program's memory once a second as the drawer does).
- The stats are a drawer (agreed 2026-10-05: always open, they covered two thirds of a game at the size the docs embed one, and a game can't ask where they are; what's picked is Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Settings). `tide/stats.h` (`engine/src/stats.c`, in the engine beside the GUI and the profiler, so its tests need no window) is the drawer, and `tide/host.h` drives it:
  - Closed, it's one line, with what's worth a glance: the frame rate, and the ping when another machine runs the match (`tide_stats_values.remote`: this machine's own is the loopback's). Open, the rest is listed above that line, which stays where it is, so a click in the same place closes it again. The list is cut from its end where a screen is too low for it, and a system's name is cut where a line is too wide, never what the system took. It shows outside a match too, with the lines that aren't a match's.
  - **The list reads as two columns, and nothing in it shifts but the digits** (2026-10-07; the owner found it hard to read, "things shift a lot", and `cpu` unclear as a total; the shape is Claude's pick, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Settings). Each line is a label on the left and its value on the right (`tide_stats_line.label` and `value`, which `tests/test_stats.c` checks by; `entities 2472`, where it was `2472 entities`), labels dimmer than values, in four groups with a little space between (`gap`): a frame's times, the match's, the machine's, and the systems. `cpu` is the whole of a frame's work, and `render` is indented under it (`indent`) as the part of it that's rendering; `gpu` stands beside them, as the two overlap. What belongs together is one line (the owner, the same day): the frame's draw calls are in the GPU line's label, `gpu (22 draw calls)`, and a line of their own, `draw calls`, only where the backend has no GPU timer; the input delay is in the ping's, `ping (delay 3 ticks)`, and a line of its own only in a remote match, whose ping is in the drawer's one line; up and down are one `network` line, `↑2.0  ↓0.5 KB/s`, whose arrows the drawer draws itself (`draw_arrow`, a shaft and a head of thick lines, as its chevron is: Tuffy has U+2191 and U+2193, and at 16 pixels they look like a 1); and the tick is gone (`tide_stats_values` has no `tick`), as it said nothing at a glance and changed every frame. A system keeps its row while it stays among the three slowest, and one that joins them takes the row of the one it replaces (`tide_stats_collect`), so the rows don't shuffle twice a second; the first three are slowest first. Open, the panel keeps the widest it has been (`tide_stats.held`), so its edge doesn't jump as a long system name comes and goes, and closing it, or `tide_stats_off`, lets that go; the screen still bounds it, and a label is cut where the values' column leaves it no room.
  - A click or a tap on it opens it and closes it (the left button or a finger that went down on it and is let go on it), and so does F8, beside the profiler's F7: the game reads the key too. `tide_run_drawer_input` asks right after `tide_platform_poll`, before anything reads the devices.
  - It starts closed, and `tide run --stats` starts it open (`tide_run_desc.stats_open`, which tide sets for `run` alone: `opts.stats_open` in `compiler/cli/main.c`), since whoever typed that wants to read it. A game's setting, `tide build --stats` and `STATS` are what players get: the one line.
  - Open or closed is the host's (`tide_run_drawer`), never a build's, so a hot reload keeps it: on desktop the host outlives the game's library, and on the web it goes with what a program leaves the next one (`saved.stats_open` in `platform/src/reload.c`).
  - **A press on it is the host's, as the pointer on a widget is the GUI's,** but by where the press began, not where the pointer is now: a button that goes down with the cursor on the drawer, and a finger that touches it, are the drawer's until they're let go, wherever they go meanwhile, and a press that began elsewhere stays the game's when it crosses the drawer (a brush that paints across the corner isn't cut). A mouse whose cursor is locked is on nothing. `tide_stats_hide` takes the drawer's presses out of a copy of the devices: the copy `tide_run_sample` samples the game's input from, after `tide_gui_hide`, and the copy the frame's GUI and views read (`tide_gui_begin` is given it while a press is on the drawer), so neither sees the press, from the frame it lands in. The drawer keeps a press once it's let go while the devices still say it was down (a button's `pressed`, `down` and `up` stay until a sample consumes them, and a finger's slot until then), or the next tick's sample would take it for a click; in a host that never consumes, it's the drawer's until the button goes down elsewhere. The GUI knows nothing of the drawer: a widget under it still shows the pointer on it, and games keep the corner free (the line's rect is about 84 by 40 logical pixels, and up to about 190 wide with a ping).
  - **Nothing is measured that isn't shown.** Closed, the frame rate is frames counted over made-up or real seconds the host has anyway, and the ping is the session's: no clock is read, ticks get no `tide_profile`, and the memory isn't asked for. Open, a frame's work is timed (`began`), the pool gets the profile (`tide_run_debug_views`), and the memory is read: the list's `cpu` line shows once a frame was timed. The numbers that are rates change once a second, and the first time after a quarter of one: the drawer shows from then (`tide_run_stats_known`), never a frame rate of 0.
  - Timings refresh every half second (2026-10-06, the owner asked for readable system times and GPU timing): the three slowest systems are ranked by their average measured run, with names and rows held between updates (above), rather than by the last tick. Their counters survive the F7 profiler clearing its own totals. The CPU total includes rendering; `render`, under `cpu`, shows preparing and submitting the draw list, and `gpu` shows GPU drawing with its render targets, without the drawer or display wait. Desktop uses core OpenGL elapsed queries after draw preparation; WebGPU sums render-pass timestamps when the optional timestamp-query feature is available. Both read results asynchronously and skip samples when their small queues are full; WebGL 2 and OpenGL ES 3 show no GPU line. Closing or reloading discards old samples. Timers run only while the drawer is open. CPU and GPU overlap, so their times aren't added. `platform/tests/gpu_timing.c` and `gpu_timing_web.mjs` cover delayed results, full queues and cleanup; `tide_platform_gpu_time` checks real devices, natively and on both web backends.
  - It's drawn into a list of the host's own (`tide_run_over`), over the frame (`tide_platform_draw_over`, as `tide_platform_draw_overlay` draws a host's text: neither counts in the frame's draw calls, nor moves its camera). So a run with no window has it in its pictures, and a script's click or `key f8` opens it as a player's would. Closed, it shows nothing this machine measured, so such a run still repeats byte for byte (a frame is a 60th of a second, so the line says 60); open, it shows this machine's clock, and pictures differ there.
  - Tests: `tests/test_stats.c` has the drawer with made-up numbers and devices, each frame in the host's order (closed and open, the key, a click, a tap, a tap shorter than a frame, a second finger, whose each press is, a game that samples nothing, small screens); `tide_platform_stats` (`platform/tests/stats.mjs` with `stats.tide`, label `gpu`) runs the standard host with no window and reads the drawer and what the game read off its pictures; `tide_platform_web_stats` (`platform/tests/web_stats.c`) does it in a page with the DOM's own events, and checks nothing is measured while it's closed, and that it's open as it was left when the setting goes and comes back.
  - Examples that ask for the stats keep their own things out of that corner by their own numbers: Duel (`examples/duel`, `StatsCorner` in `hud.tide`) has its Menu button in the top right corner on every layout, its touch ring above the line, and its row of keys as far left as the line needs.
- **Audio is planned, and its eight cards are built** (2026-10-07; the design is `docs/spec.md`, Audio, with what's agreed and what's Claude's picks, and `docs/game/audio.md` is the users' page; the Tide side is whole but for what's listed as not built, a window plays through the system's device on every platform, clips are WAV or Ogg Vorbis, and the microphone is a clip). Audio is presentation, never the match: it reads the world and writes nothing to it, and the simulation never includes an audio header. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **`tide/audio.h` is the frame's audio, as `tide/draw.h` is its drawing** (`engine/src/audio.c`): a `tide_audio_frame` records one-shots (`tide_audio_play`, `tide_audio_play_at`), voices named by an id (`tide_audio_voice`: clip, position, volume, pitch, loop, playing, bus, effect and its knobs, copied in), the listener, a falloff and each bus's volume and effect. `tide_audio_reset` empties it for a frame with every bus at 1 and nothing said, as `tide_draw_reset` does; every frame starts from the defaults. A `tide_clip` is interleaved float samples of any rate and 1 or 2 channels, which stay where they are while a voice plays them: the mixer reads them on its thread and copies nothing. A `tide_audio_effect` is code the audio thread runs once a block (`tide_audio_effect_fn(samples, state, knobs)`: 512 interleaved floats, the voice's own state of up to 4 KiB, a copy of the frame's knobs of up to 1 KiB), named by an id whose change starts the state over; on a voice with no clip it's a source. The host records nothing into it yet: `tide_run_audio` in `tide/host.h` is reset each frame and handed over after the draw (`tide_platform_audio`), for the Tide side to fill when it comes.
  - **The mixer is `platform/src/mixer.c`**, stereo at 48 kHz in blocks of 256 frames (`TIDE_AUDIO_RATE`, `TIDE_AUDIO_BLOCK`, `TIDE_AUDIO_SAMPLES`), in floats, with nothing of a clock's: `tide_mixer_submit` (the frame's thread) copies a frame into one of three buffers and publishes it with one atomic exchange, and the renderer takes the newest at its next block (`tide_mixer_take`, from `tide_mixer_render`), so neither thread waits for the other; one-shots go through a ring of 1,024 (`TIDE_MIXER_ONE_SHOTS`), so none is lost between two blocks, and past it they're dropped and counted (`tide_mixer_dropped`). The renderer allocates nothing and blocks on nothing: 256 voice slots (`TIDE_MIXER_VOICES`, a new one past that taking the quietest's), each with its own state and knobs bytes. `tide_mixer_render(out, frames)` renders any count by whole blocks, carrying the remainder, so any sizes give the same samples; `tide_mixer_flush` drops the remainder. What a block does: each voice's clip read at its own rate and pitch by linear interpolation (a double cursor), its effect, its gains ramped linearly over the block from the last block's (volume, and for a spatial voice equal-power pan by the listener's right and `near / distance` falloff scaled to 0 at `far`; defaults 1 and 50), into its bus; each bus's effect and ramped volume into the master; the master's out. A voice not named by a frame, or named with `playing` false, fades out over a block and goes; `playing` false then true, or another clip, starts the clip over; a named voice whose clip ended stays silent until then; a one-shot ends with its clip. A frame's `forget` stops everything at once and drops every effect, for a reload. Every float operation is a basic one (`__builtin_sqrtf` for the pan), so the mix is the same bytes on every platform: `tide_platform_audio` pins a hash of a scene of everything, the same natively and under Node.
  - **A headless run has audio with no device**: after each frame's function, the platform renders 800 frames (a 60th of a second) into a buffer that holds what a capture can still ask for and no more (`headless_audio_frame` in `platform/src/platform.c`: the whole run with `--capture-audio`, what played since the last `capture-audio` line while the script has another, `tide_script_captures_audio`, and otherwise a frame, so a long run that captures nothing stays flat; it grew by 12.5 MB a 30 seconds before 2026-10-07), `--capture-audio <file.wav>` (`tide_headless_desc.capture_audio`, `tide_run_configure`) writes the whole run as the run ends, and a script's `capture-audio <file.wav>` line (`EVENT_CAPTURE_AUDIO` in `platform/src/script.c`, `tide_platform_capture_audio`) writes what played since the last such line. The WAV writer is `platform/src/wav.c` (16-bit stereo PCM, samples clamped and rounded to the nearest, ties away from zero; `tide_wav_header` reads one back, for tests). A window with a device gets nothing from `tide_platform_audio` yet: the mixer is submitted frames and nothing renders them until the backends come (WASAPI, CoreAudio, ALSA at run time, AAudio, an `AudioWorklet`), each a device callback that calls `tide_mixer_render`.
  - **`Clip` and `Audio.Play` are the Tide side** (card 2). `Clip` is `TY_CLIP` (`tide_clip_ref`, 8 bytes: the name the pack's loader gives a declaration's sound, as a `Texture` of the pack's names its image; `tide_pack_clip_of`, `tide_pack_clip_find` in `engine/src/pack_load.c`, kinds `TIDE_PACK_CLIP` and `TIDE_PACK_CLIPS`, laid out after the region as textures are), an asset kind (`ASSET_CLIP`: `asset_files.c` decodes the file to check it, with the engine's own WAV reader, `tide/wav.h`, which tidec compiles too), and a resource of this machine's wherever a `Texture` is one (`holds_render_resource`, `element_ok` for an asset's glob only, the layout table in `codegen.c`). `tide_clip_of(ref)` (`engine/src/audio.c`) finds the file's bytes in the loaded pack and decodes them once, by what's in them, into a cache of the engine's own that `tide_clips_free` empties: so a voice never plays samples of a pack that's gone (a repack keeps the old file's beside the new), and a library's unload frees them after the host had the mixer forget (`tide_platform_audio_forget` before `old->unload()` in `platform/src/reload.c`: a reload stops every sound). `Audio` is a builtin owner (`builtins.c`: three `Play` signatures, plain, at a `float2`, at a `float3`, with volume and pitch defaults that codegen fills in as `1.0f`), its call `CALL_AUDIO`, checked by `check_audio_use` (views, local handlers, functions; the match, the input, tasks, methods and GPU code refused) and `check_audio_calls` (a function that plays is local code's to call, as `Frame.dt`'s is), and generated as `c_callee(tide_l->tide_audio, tide_clip_of(clip), ...)`: a function that plays takes the local state (`takes_local`), the local state has the frame's audio list when the game plays (`program.uses_audio`: `TIDE_HAS_AUDIO`, `tide_local.tide_audio`, `tide_local_audio`, which `tide_host_game.audio` is and the host calls before each frame). A game that plays nothing and has no `Clip` asset generates the C it did, byte for byte (old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without: 248 games the same, and the two that play, the e2e test and the capture game, only compile with the new one).
  - **`Sound` fields and the listener are card 3.** `Sound` is a built-in struct (`prog->sound` in `check.c`'s `add_builtins`, beside `Shape`; `named_only`, so it's in generated C, its blend helper and the layout only for a game that names it, which also sets `program.uses_audio`). The frame names every `Sound` of local state that plays: `gen_sounds` (`codegen.c`) writes `tide_local_sounds`, which walks local singletons, local archetypes' rows and the attached local parts of match components (the walk `gen_rebind_resources` makes, for `sound_fields`), structs in them too, and records each playing one as a `tide_voice` (`tide_sound_voice`: its clip through `tide_clip_of`) named by its field's path hashed and, for a row, the entity's bits (`tide_sound_entity`); `tide_frame` calls it last, after the local commands applied. `Audio.SetListener` (a `float3` and a quaternion, or a `float2`) and `Audio.SetFalloff` are `Audio`'s other signatures, through `CALL_AUDIO` like `Play` (whose default-filling in `gen_builtin_call` is by name, `Play` alone). Generated C for a game that names no `Sound` is byte for byte what it was (old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without: 252 games the same, and the `sound` e2e test only compiles with the new one).
  - **Buses and `audio` declarations are cards 4 and 5.** `[Bus]` marks the game's enum of buses (`prog->bus_enum`, found right after `collect_decls` so `Sound.bus` resolves as it, checked in `check_attributes`); `Audio.SetVolume` and `Audio.SetEffect` are checked by hand (`check_audio_volume`, `check_audio_bus_effect`: the bus as the enum, or a number with none) and generated with a `(uint32_t)` cast. An `audio` declaration is a `DECL_FUNCTION` with `is_audio` (`parse_audio`; `check_audio_signature` sorts its parameters into `audio_state` and `audio_knobs`; `check_audio_bodies` refuses what the audio thread can't do, after the call-site passes, with `makes_text` noted per function in `check_expr` and propagated through calls); a call of one is `CALL_EFFECT`, an `Effect` value (`check_effect_value`), generated as `tide_effect_<Name>(knobs)`. `gen_effects` (in `gen_sounds`) writes each declaration's wrapper `tide_audio_fn_<Name>` (the block, state and knobs cast, with `0u` for each `mut` parameter's `where`), the `tide_effects` table by id and `tide_effect_of`, which the voice walk and `tide_audio_set_bus_effect_value` resolve an `Effect` with; `TIDE_AUDIO_KNOBS_BYTES` is 256, what an `Effect` holds. **Three built-in structs are in generated C only for a game that names them** (`builtin_struct_unnamed`: `Sound`, `Samples`, `Effect`), and so are the arrays only their fields made (`array_unwritten`, with `order_arrays` numbering them after the game's own); naming `Sound` names `Effect`, and `program.quiet_names` keeps a built-in struct's own field resolution from counting as the game naming anything. Old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without: every game that plays no sound the same, byte for byte.
  - **The device is card 6.** `platform/src/audio_out.h` is what each build has of it: `tide_audio_out_open` (with the window, in `tide_platform_open`; a run with no window opens none) and `_close` (in `finish`), and `tide_audio_out_fill`, the one pull every backend fills its device from (`audio_out.c`: the mixer a block at a time, read out at the device's rate by linear interpolation between two of the mixer's frames, a mono device averaged, more channels given the two and zeros; one thread at a time, the device's). The backends: `platform/desktop/audio.c` (WASAPI on Windows, shared mode and event driven at the mix format, its COM GUIDs spelled out so the package's MinGW needs no libuuid, `ole32` linked for CoCreateInstance; CoreAudio on macOS, the default output unit given stereo floats at 48 kHz, `AudioToolbox` linked; ALSA on Linux, `libasound.so.2` through `dlopen` as OpenGL is, a thread of ours writing blocks), `platform/android/audio.c` (AAudio, `aaudio` linked) and `platform/web/audio.c` with `tide.js` (an AudioContext and an AudioWorklet the page makes from a blob, which plays the chunks the main thread posts; `tide_web_audio_render` renders what the page asks for, from its frame and a 20 ms timer, to keep about 90 ms queued by the context's clock; the context resumes at the first pointer, key or touch). A backend that renders on a thread of its own says so (`tide_mixer_rendered_elsewhere`), and `tide_mixer_forget` then waits for that thread to take the forget (`forget_asked`, `forget_done`), half a second at most. `tide build` links games with `ole32`, `AudioToolbox` and `aaudio` too (`compiler/cli/build.c`, and the MinGW package's library list).
  - **Ogg Vorbis is card 7** (`tide/vorbis.h`, `engine/src/vorbis.c`: the engine's own decoder from the Vorbis I specification and RFC 3533, which tidec compiles too for the build's check; floats, with a sine of its own, so a file is the same samples everywhere; floor type 0 and more than two channels refused). `tide_vorbis_open` reads every page (checksums checked) and copies the packets out, parses the headers and decodes nothing; a `tide_vorbis_reader` decodes from the start, as many frames as asked, and rewinds; `tide_vorbis_decode` is the whole file as `tide_wav_decode` gives one. The reader stops at the last page's granule. A clip's decoding moved to load: `tide_clips_prepare(jobs)` (called by `tide_run_assets_from` in `tide/host.h`, so at start and at each pack `tide run` writes) decodes every clip of the pack the cache hasn't got, each file once, on the pool's threads (`tide_pack_clips_each` lists them), a WAV whole and Vorbis whole up to `tide_clips_whole_seconds` (10); a longer Vorbis clip is kept opened, and its `tide_clip` says `stream` with no `samples`. The mixer streams such a clip (`read_stream` in `platform/src/mixer.c`): the voice's `reader` is made at its first block, two frames (`a`, `b`) go through the same interpolation a decoded clip's samples do, a loop rewinds at the end, and `clear_voice` closes the reader wherever a slot is emptied (a forget among them), before `tide_clips_free` closes the files. The asset check accepts an Ogg (`'<path>' isn't a WAV or an Ogg Vorbis file that decodes`, with a note of what's wrong with a file that starts as Ogg), and the editor completes `.ogg` beside `.wav` (`asset_site.format2`). The reference files are `tests/audio` (three ffmpeg encodes and its float decodes, as C arrays through `cmake/embed.cmake`, which writes a `_size` beside each array now, in the library `tide_audio_refs`).
  - **The microphone is card 8.** `Clip.Microphone` and `Clip.none` are members of `Clip` (`members[]` in `compiler/src/builtins.c`; the checker sets `program.uses_microphone` on the first, which `game_info.microphone` carries to `tide build --android`, whose manifest then names `RECORD_AUDIO`, `apk_desc.microphone`). `tide_clip_of` of the microphone's name (`TIDE_CLIP_MICROPHONE_NAME`, `UINT64_MAX`) is a clip that says `microphone`, mono at the mixer's rate; `tide_audio_voice` sets the frame's `microphone` for a playing voice of it, and `tide_audio_play` of it records nothing. `platform/src/audio_in.h` is the capture side: `tide_audio_in_open` and `_close`, which `tide_platform_audio` calls as frames start and stop saying `microphone` (tried again every 60 frames while it couldn't open: a permission still asked), and `tide_audio_in_push` (`audio_in.c`), the one path every backend pushes through, averaging channels and resampling linearly into the mixer's ring (`tide_mixer_capture`, 16384 frames). The renderer takes a block from the ring at each block's start (`take_capture`: primed at four blocks, silence and a new prime when dry, a skip to the newest a prime back when more than eight behind, emptied by a forget), and `read_clip` plays it for every microphone voice, pitch aside. The backends are under the outputs': WASAPI capture (`platform/desktop/audio.c`, `IAudioCaptureClient` on a thread of ours), CoreAudio's `AudioQueueNewInput`, ALSA's capture stream, AAudio's input (`platform/android/audio.c`, after `tide_android_microphone_permitted` in `activity.c`, which checks and asks through JNI), and the page's `getUserMedia` with a `tide-input` worklet (`tide.js`, `audio_capture_open`/`_close` imports, the `tide_web_audio_capture_buffer` and `tide_web_audio_captured` exports in `platform/web/audio.c`). A run with no window has no device: `--microphone <file>` (`tide_headless_desc.microphone`, read in `tide_platform_open_headless`) plays a WAV or Ogg Vorbis into the path, looped, a 60th of a second a frame.
  - **Not built:** the listener's block form and its camera default, bus block forms, `Samples.Frame`, more than one knobs or state struct, a `Clip` of bytes at runtime, the stats' line, a better resampler than linear, seeking a streamed clip, chained Ogg streams, choosing a capture device, stereo capture, voice chat.
  - **Tests:** `tide_platform_audio` (`platform/tests/audio.c`, no device, every platform: a one-shot as its clip is, pitch and a clip of another rate, a voice that loops, fades when unnamed, ends and starts over, a volume ramp, spatial pan and falloff with the per-frame defaults, effects on voices and buses with state and knobs and a source, one-shots from frames between blocks and the ring's limit, renders of 800 and of blocks the same bytes with a pinned hash, and the WAV writer); `tests/test_wav.c` (every depth and format the reader takes, what it refuses, what the writer writes); `compiler/tests/e2e/audio` (a `Clip` asset, a glob's list and map, a local handler playing the match's event once, a view, functions two deep with a kept `Clip`, a host with no audio list, and a pack loaded again with another file under the name), natively and under Node; `compiler/tests/e2e/sound` (a match component's local `Sound` on two entities, named by entity and the same voice frame after frame, a local singleton's and a struct's, one not playing, one stopped and started again as the same voice, a local entity spawned and named in one frame, the listener and falloff set and then not, and a host with no audio list), natively and under Node; `compiler/tests/e2e/buses` and `effects` (a bus enum, a Sound's bus and the volumes; an effect with state and knobs on a Sound, a source with none that calls a function, a bus effect, each called from the host as the mixer calls it); `tests/test_vorbis.c` (the three files against ffmpeg's decodes to 2e-4, what a file says it holds, reading in odd pieces and rewinding, the transform against its definition at three sizes, and what's refused: garbage, a WAV, cuts everywhere, a flipped byte by its checksum); `tide_platform_audio` has a streamed clip play as its decode does, loop at the granule, end as a one-shot and pitch as a decoded clip pitches; the e2e `audio` test an Ogg clip decoded whole, streamed past a threshold of 0, a WAV never streamed, and `tide_clips_prepare`; `compiler/tests/errors/asset_clip_bad_ogg` the checksum's note. For the microphone: `tide_platform_audio` (a ramp through the device's path at another rate and channels, the prime, running dry, two voices of it, far behind, a one-shot and a stopped voice, a forget), `tide_platform_audio_device` (the capture device opened and closed where there's one), the e2e `sound` test (the frame's flag and the voice's clip, `Clip.none`), `tide_platform_capture` (`--microphone ping.wav` heard from frame 2560, the same bytes again, silence without the file, a missing file refused) and `compiler/tests/errors/clip_member_unknown`. `tide_platform_audio` also pins the pull (the mixer's rate a frame late, half the rate, mono, six channels), and `tide_platform_audio_device` (every native platform) opens the machine's device, plays a voice of no volume through it on the device's thread, has the mixer forgotten there and closes it, passing where there's no device (CI's Linux runner, WSL); run on this machine's WASAPI. Not run: CoreAudio, AAudio, ALSA with a device; the web's worklets played in the continuation's headless Chrome fake-microphone check (see below); `compiler/tests/errors/audio_*`, `asset_clip_*`, `clip_list_field`, `sound_in_match`, `sound_in_list`, `bus_*`, `audio_volume_*` and `effect_*`, a file a diagnostic; `tide_platform_capture` (label `gpu`) runs a game with `--capture-audio` and a script's `capture-audio` lines and reads the WAVs back (silent, 800 frames a frame, the same bytes again, and a clip played at a key from the first whole block after its frame), and `tide_platform_capture_refused` the new line's and flag's refusals.
  - **The web microphone keeps each open request's identity** (`audioCaptureOpen` in `tide.js`): a permission or worklet answer that arrives after capture closed cannot attach to a new capture in the same output context, and queued messages from the old worklet are ignored. `tide_platform_web_audio` (`platform/tests/web_audio.mjs`, Node on every platform, no device or permission) checks both delayed answers, queued messages, sample copies and chunk splitting into the WebAssembly buffer, closing and reopening after refusal, and the embedded input processor. A scratch headless Chrome check with its fake microphone ran real `getUserMedia`, the input worklet and the WebAssembly capture bridge through the resampler and mixer: nonzero samples reached a muted voice's effect, output stayed silent, and closing capture ended the stream's tracks. Physical browser microphone hardware and permission prompts remain untested.
- Drawing is one `Draw` (built 2026-10-07, card 1 of the reshaping above; the picks are Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, One `Draw`): shapes, text, 2D meshes, the screen's pixels and clipping, 3D cameras, meshes, and cube, sphere, cylinder and plane primitives, with `Blit`, `window` and `list` beside them. **One `Draw`, and what code relies on:**
  - **The 2D and 3D forms are overloads of one owner, told apart by their arguments** (`add("Draw", ...)` in `compiler/src/builtins.c`, resolved first-fit by `resolve_builtin_call`, so each pair differs by type or count): `Camera(float2, float)`, `Camera(float3, quaternion, float)` and `Camera(float4x4, float4x4)`; `Transform` of a `float2` and the rest, of a `float3x3`, or of a `float4x4`; `WorldToScreen` and `ScreenToWorld` of a `float2` or a `float3`; `Mesh` and `Instanced` of a `List<Vertex>` or a `List<Vertex3>`, which `check_mesh_draw` sends to the one its vertices' `position` says (a 3D mesh takes a transform after its indices, a 2D mesh takes none; `[]` with a transform after its indices is a 3D mesh's). `SetCamera()` and `SetTransform()` with no arguments put both the 2D and the 3D one back (`tide_draw_unset_cameras`, `tide_draw_unset_transforms`), as a target's did.
  - **A render target has the same signature table** (`resolve_builtin_call(str_from("Draw"), e)` for a `TY_RENDER_TARGET` receiver, `e->targeted`; `target_draw_owner` is gone), but for `Blit`, which names its destination and is an error on a target, and the members `window` and `list`. The language server lists `Draw`'s members for a target and `Draw`'s signatures for its calls (`target_owner` is `Draw`).
  - **Every setting is the destination's own, blend and tint included** (`tide_draw_place.blend`, `tinted` and `tint`, where the list had them; `tide_draw_pop` puts them back into the place that saved them, `tide_draw_defaults` resets every destination's, and `tide_draw_append` leaves no destination's blend set). Every `Draw` call selects the window (`e->draw_dims` is 2 for all of them, where `Draw.Blend` and `Draw.Tint` used to select none), so the C generated for a game that only used those two calls differs there, and nowhere else: old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without. A command carries its blend as it did, so the renderer didn't change.
  - **Depth and cull around 2D draws change nothing, and are no error.** Nothing records what a 2D command does with either.
  - **`Draw2D` and `Draw3D` are errors that name the rename**, with the quick fix on the owner's name (`old_draw_owner` in `compiler/src/check.c` for calls, `check_member` for `Draw2D.list`; `builtin_old_draw_owner` keeps the two names reserved, so a game can't declare them). The settings' form errors are as they were.
  - **Tests:** `compiler/tests/e2e/draw_settings` (the overloads, a target's own blend and tint, the `Set` forms with no arguments), `tests/test_draw_settings.c` (`draw_settings_of_a_destination_are_its_own`), `compiler/tests/errors/draw2d_*_renamed`, `draw3d_*_renamed`, `draw_mesh2_transform`, `draw_custom_mesh2_transform`, `draw_custom_mesh3_no_transform`, `draw_target_mesh3_no_transform`, `draw_target_blit`, `draw_member_unknown`; `lsp_complete_builtin_owners`, `lsp_draw_depth`, `lsp_draw_cull` and `lsp_draw_stencil` for the editors.
  - Screen and world conversions (`engine/src/draw_coordinates.c`) go through the frame's current camera, in logical pixels from the top left with y down. Device positions count from the bottom left with y up, as Unity's Input System's do (decided), so views take them through `Screen.Flip(position)` (`tide_draw_screen_flip`: the same point counted from the window's other edge, its own inverse; the name is provisional), never `Screen.height - y` by hand. 3D depth is 0 at the near plane and 1 at the far limit, and picking rays start on the near plane with a normalized direction in the world. The primitives (`engine/src/primitives.c`) are cached unlit meshes, given their full sizes and an optional rotation; a cylinder's axis is its local y, and a plane spans its local xz. Their triangles' fronts are outside, and a plane's is its top (see Draw settings, Which side of a 3D mesh's triangles draws).
- **The cameras as matrices** (built 2026-10-07, card 5 of the reshaping above; that they're readable is agreed, and the shape, the names and each rule are Claude's picks, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, The cameras as matrices). What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **Tide:** `Draw.WorldToClip()`, `Draw.CameraTransform()` and `Draw.CameraProjection()` (each a `float4x4`, the 3D camera's) and `Draw.WorldToScreenMatrix()` (a `float3x3`, the 2D camera's), a target's as its methods. They're four signatures of `Draw`'s with no arguments (`compiler/src/builtins.c`), so a target has them, editors hover and complete them, and local code alone calls them, by the code the conversions go through; `gen_expr` passes the window's size after the list, as for the conversions. Functions and not a struct read as a member, as a target has `Draw`'s functions and none of its members.
  - **C** (`engine/src/draw_coordinates.c`, `tide/draw.h`): `tide_draw_world_to_clip`, `tide_draw_camera_transform`, `tide_draw_camera_projection` and `tide_draw_world_to_screen_matrix`, of the selected destination, each given the window's size as the conversions are. `camera3`, which the 3D conversions and rays go through, is `tide_draw_world_to_clip`'s arithmetic (`fitted`), and the 2D matrix is `camera2`'s.
  - **The fitting is one function, the renderer's too:** `tide_draw_camera_fit(matrix, width, height)` (`engine/src/draw.c`), which `camera_3d_to_clip` in `platform/src/platform.c` calls where it had the four multiplies. So what `WorldToClip()` gives is the renderer's matrix bit for bit when the two sizes are the same floats.
  - **A camera keeps its parts** (`tide_draw_camera_3d_data.transform` and `projection`, beside `matrix`): what `tide_draw_set_camera_matrices` was given, or for a camera set with a field of view its position and normalized rotation as a matrix and the square projection, which the readers fit (`perspective_camera`; `tide_draw_first_camera_3d` is each frame's first). `camera_3d` takes the whole struct. A camera that reaches a list as a command's matrix alone (`tide_draw_append` replaying another list's commands) has the identity for its transform and the matrix with z turned back for its projection (`camera_of_matrix`), which make the same matrix; one a place still has is copied whole.
  - **The parts given back set the same camera:** bit for bit for one set from matrices, and within rounding for one set with a field of view (its `matrix` is still worked out as it always was, from the rotation's conjugate, never from the parts, so no camera's bits moved and a list records what it did).
  - **A destination with no size** has zeros for all but the transform; a singular camera is given as it is.
  - **Generated C:** all that changed in codegen is the window's size after these four calls, by their names on a `CALL_DRAW`; no diff of every game's C was run for it.
  - **The shadow maps use it:** `docs/examples/shadows.tide`, Sumo's `Cast` (`examples/sumo/play.tide`) and pass 7 of `platform/tests/shaders.tide` say `shadow.map.WorldToClip()` after the target's `SetCamera`, where each multiplied the projection, a flip of z and the transform's inverse by hand. Sumo's pins are what they were.
  - **Tests:** `draw_cameras_are_read_as_matrices` (`tests/test_draw.c`: against `tide_draw_world_to_screen_3d` and `tide_draw_world_to_screen` for each kind of camera, the window's shape and a target's own, the parts setting the same camera, a transform left out, no size); `compiler/tests/e2e/draw_apis` reads all four in a view, the window's and a target's; pass 7 of `tide_platform_shaders` (`run_shadow_checks`) for the same pixels on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU; `lsp_camera_matrices` for the editors.
- **One word for the window, and formats named by what they store** (built 2026-10-07, card 2 of the reshaping above; the words are agreed, and the picks are Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, One word for the window, and formats by what they store). What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **Tide:** `RenderTarget.Window()` and `RenderTarget.Window(factor)`, with formats after them as before, where they were `RenderTarget.Screen`; `Draw.Screen() { ... }` and `Draw.SetScreen()`, a target's own as `target.Screen` and `target.SetScreen`, as they were before the card: the card renamed them `Pixels` and `SetPixels`, which went back the same day (2026-10-07, the owner having left the review's follow-ups to Claude): with the target named `Window`, `Screen` means one thing, the screen's pixel space, as `Screen.width` and `Screen.Flip` do, and `Pixels` sat beside a frag's `Pixel` as two names that read as one; `Draw.Pixels` and `Draw.SetPixels` are errors that name `Screen`, with the quick fix; `Format { Rgba8, Depth, Rgba16f }`, where it was `{ Color, DepthOnly, Float }`. `Screen.width`, `Screen.height`, `Screen.Flip` and `Draw.window` are as they were: `Screen` is the window's measurements alone.
  - **The format names are a scheme** (Claude's pick): channels, the bits of each, `f` for floats, so the formats to come are named already (`R8`, `Rg8`, `R16f`). There's no `Format.Color` alias for `Rgba8` (a pick: one name for one thing, and the first target anyone makes says no format).
  - **The enum's numbers didn't change** (`Rgba8` 0, `Depth` 1, `Rgba16f` 2), so nothing that carries them did: the bits of a target's id (`TIDE_RENDER_FORMAT`), `tide_render_target_recreate` across a hot reload, `tide_gpu_color`, `colorFormats` in `tide.js`. A game that names `Format` has other member names in its header, so another `tide_game` hash: a reload across this change migrates by name, and its targets are made again in the formats they had.
  - **In C the formats are `TIDE_TARGET_RGBA8`, `TIDE_TARGET_DEPTH` and `TIDE_TARGET_RGBA16F`** (`tide/draw.h`; they were `_COLOR`, `_DEPTH_ONLY` and `_FLOAT`). Function names stayed: `tide_render_target_window`, `tide_render_target_screen` (a part of the window), their `_as` and `_of`, `tide_draw_push_screen`, `tide_draw_set_screen`, `TIDE_DRAW_SCREEN`. `gpu.h` is as it was (`TIDE_GPU_TARGET_COLOR`, `TIDE_GPU_TARGET_DEPTH`, `TIDE_GPU_COLOR_BYTES` and `_FLOAT` are kinds of target and of image, not Tide's names).
  - **The old spellings are errors that name the new one, with the rename as a quick fix** (`compiler/src/check.c`): `RenderTarget.Screen` in `check_window_target`; `Screen()` with no arguments and `SetScreen` on `Draw` or a target in `old_setting`, which runs before the name is looked up; `Format.Color`, `Format.DepthOnly` and `Format.Float` in `check_enum_member` (`old_format_member`). Each is a `suggest_replacement` on the name's own token.
  - **Generated C:** old tidec over every game's sources as they were against new tidec over them as they are, with `--layout` and without: 237 games the same, byte for byte, and the 8 that name `Format` differ in the enum's members and their header's hash alone.
  - **Tests:** `compiler/tests/errors/render_target_old_screen`, `draw_old_screen`, `draw_old_set_screen`, `draw_old_target_set_screen`, `format_old_color`, `format_old_depth_only`, `format_old_float`, one a diagnostic (and `render_target_window_*`, which were `render_target_screen_*`); `lsp_window_pixels_and_format_names` for the quick fixes. Everything else that named them was respelled and runs as it did.
- Draw settings change how everything drawn after them comes out (agreed 2026-10-05; `docs/spec.md`, Draw settings, where the picks beyond what was agreed are Provisional). One family, two forms each, told apart by name: the plain name wraps a block and puts back what it found when the block ends (`Draw.Blend(Blend.Add) { ... }`), and the `Set` name stays for the views that follow, until it's set again or the frame ends (`Draw.SetBlend(Blend.Add);`). A form written as the other is a compile error that names the other, and so are the spellings from before there were two (`Draw.Camera(...);`, `Draw.Clip(rect);`, `Draw.Clip();`, `Draw.Screen();`), with the rename as a quick fix. A setting that joins the family gets both forms and the same rule, as stencil did.
  - The family: `Blend`, `Tint`, `Transform`, `Clip`, `Camera`, `Screen`, `Depth`, `Cull` and `Stencil`, each the destination's own: the window's as `Draw`'s, a target's as its methods (since one `Draw`, 2026-10-07; before it, blend and tint were the list's, the same wherever a draw went, and depth and cull were `Draw3D`'s by the rule that a setting is in the namespace of what it changes, the owner, 2026-10-06: "is this also a 2D thing? if not then we should stick it to the rendering namespace that makes the most sense"). Every frame starts from the defaults (`tide_draw_reset`), and the GUI, the profiler and the stats overlay never draw with a game's: `tide_draw_append` and `tide_draw_defaults` start from them, and the overlay's list is its own.
  - In `tide/draw.h`, a `Set` is `tide_draw_set_*` (and `tide_draw_unset_*` with no arguments), and a block `tide_draw_push_*`, which saves what it found on the list's stack and returns a mark, then `tide_draw_pop(list, mark)`, which puts back everything saved since: generated code pops the outermost block's mark where code leaves early (`closing` in `compiler/src/codegen.c`, with the GUI's containers).
  - A setting never comes between draws a renderer would draw at once. A command carries its blend (`tide_draw_command.blend`), a 3D mesh's its depth too (`depth`, below), a tint and a 2D transform are worked into shapes, text and 2D meshes as they're recorded (`plain_altered`, `alter_shape`, `alter_vertices` in `engine/src/draw.c`), a 3D transform into each instance's, and a 3D mesh's and a shader's tint are their run's. The list has the cameras and clips it was given (`tide_draw_list.cameras`, `clips`, a clip naming the clip it's inside and the camera it was made under), and each destination's `tide_draw_place` says which the commands to come go through and which the commands so far play: the commands that say so (`settle`) are recorded before the next one that draws, and only where the two differ. So a setting set to what it was, or set and put back around nothing, records nothing, and the same block around each entity's draw is still one run.
