  - Recording a plain shape is a few stores (`push` is part of each function that records one, and what's rare isn't, nor what a tint or a transform does to a shape or a mesh's corners): a change there is measured, as the renderer's path is (`bench/results/draw.jsonl`, `draw-settings-before` and `draw-settings`).
  - A pipeline is made with how it blends, and a 3D mesh's with what it does with depth and which side of a triangle it leaves out, so the engine's are made once for each way the first time a list draws that way (`engine_pipeline` in `platform/src/platform.c`: `engine_made`, for each kind of target a list draws into, by kind, blend, depth and cull, of which only the 3D meshes' kind has more than the first depth and cull), and a game shader's too (`pipeline_for`, by `tide_shader.id`, blend, depth and cull). What multiplies needs a color that's gone to white where it's clear: the shaders do that by a uniform (`fix.x`, a game shader's `tide_screen.w`), so no shader has a version of its own for a blend, and the plain shapes' pipeline has none of it: a run of plain shapes that multiplies goes through the smooth shapes' pipeline. A game shader's color is multiplied by `tide_tint` after its frag (`put_fragment_main` in `compiler/src/shader.c`).
  - Text a transform turns, stretches or mirrors can't be fitted to pixels: it's laid out as upright text of the size the transform's mean scale makes it, and each glyph goes through the transform (`add_text_turned`), so it measures as upright text does; its anchor's place goes through the transform and its box is laid out against it, as upright text's is (`add_text`). Moved and scaled as much each way, it's fitted text of that size.
  - `tide_platform_draw` checks each blend, tint and transform's pixels for every kind of thing that draws, clips in clips, and that a blend, a depth, a cull, a clip, a tint and a transform around each of 200 draws are still one draw call, on OpenGL, WebGL 2 and WebGPU; `tests/test_draw_settings.c` checks the list without a GPU, and `compiler/tests/e2e/draw_settings` what blocks, lists of them and early exits generate.
  - **What a 3D mesh does with depth is a setting, for 3D meshes alone** (built 2026-10-06 as `Draw3D`'s, `Draw`'s since one `Draw`; that depth is a draw setting, and that only 3D meshes have it, are agreed; the enum's name, its modes and what each means are Claude's picks, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Draw settings): `Draw.Depth(mode) { ... }` and `Draw.SetDepth(mode);`, with the built-in enum `Depth`: `ReadWrite` (each frame's first, and what 3D meshes always did), `Read` (hidden behind what's nearer and already drawn, writing none: what's see-through) and `None`. What's built, and what code relies on:
    - **It's a destination's own, as every setting is** (`tide_draw_place.depth`): the window's as `Draw`'s, and a target's as `target.Depth` and `target.SetDepth`, so `Draw.Depth` around a target's meshes doesn't reach them. Around 2D draws alone it changes nothing, and is no error.
    - **Only a 3D mesh's command carries it:** `tide_draw_command.depth` (`tide_depth`, the byte that was `unused`), set by `mesh_instances` and, for a 3D shader, `shaded_instances` (`engine/src/draw.c`). Every other command says 0, whatever is set: 2D commands have no depth, so the setting never comes between two of their runs, and `push`, which records a plain shape, is the code it was (nothing to measure against `bench/results/draw.jsonl`). 0 is `TIDE_DEPTH_READ_WRITE`, so a list filled by hand draws as it did.
    - `tide_draw_set_depth`, `tide_draw_unset_depth` and `tide_draw_push_depth` are its forms in C, for the destination that's selected (`TIDE_DRAW_SAVED_DEPTH`, which a pop puts back into its own destination's place), and `tide_draw_append` and `tide_draw_defaults` start every destination from the default.
    - `gpu.h` has `TIDE_GPU_DEPTH_READ` beside the three it had. OpenGL's is `glDepthMask` (in `gl.h` and `tide.js`), which `gl_use` sets with each pipeline that tests depth, and `gl_begin` sets back before a pass's clear, as a clear only reaches the depth that's written. WebGPU's is the pipeline's `depthWriteEnabled` and `depthCompare` (`depths` in `tide.js`, by `tide_gpu_depth`'s numbers: a change to the enum goes there).
    - A clear sets depth back and a blit leaves it, whatever is set: neither's command has a depth.
    - **Generated C has the enum only in a game that names it** (`decl.named`, set where a name is looked up, as for `Random`; `enum_generated` in `compiler/src/codegen.c`, which the header's enums, their text helpers and the layout's enums go by). Every other built-in enum is in every game's header; one more there would have changed every game's header, and with it `tide_game`'s hash. So a game that says nothing of depth generates the C it did, byte for byte: checked by running old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without.
    - The setting is a signature like the others (`add_setting` in `compiler/src/builtins.c`), so both forms, each one's misuse and its quick fix, the lists with commas and early exits are the family's code.
    - Decals that only read depth are still at the surface's own depth where they lie on it, so a game draws them a little off it (Sumo's `ON_FLOOR`): the docs say so.
    - Which side of a triangle draws didn't fall out of this, and is a setting of its own since (below).
    - Tests: `draw_depth_is_the_3d_meshes_own` (`tests/test_draw_settings.c`) for the list; `depth_settings` in `platform/tests/draw.c` for pixels, for ordinary 3D meshes and for ones through a shader (see-through meshes that only read depth blend with each other, stay behind an opaque mesh in front and hide nothing drawn after; one that does neither draws over what's near and hides nothing; blocks and Sets put back what they found; `Depth.ReadWrite` said outright is what a frame starts with, pixel for pixel); `check_render_depth_alpha` in `platform/tests/targets.c` for a target's own depth; `compiler/tests/e2e/draw_depth` for what tidec generates, a target's own beside the window's among it; `compiler/tests/errors/draw_depth_*` for each diagnostic; `lsp_draw_depth` for the editors.
  - **Which side of a 3D mesh's triangles draws is a setting too, for 3D meshes alone** (built 2026-10-07 as `Draw3D`'s, `Draw`'s since one `Draw`; that it's a setting with both forms, that each frame starts with the back of a triangle not drawn, that a target has its own and that nothing in 2D culls are the owner's; that the front is the side a triangle's corners go clockwise on, as Unity's, and the enum `Cull` with `Back`, `Front` and `None` are Claude's picks, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Draw settings, with the rules below): `Draw.Cull(side) { ... }` and `Draw.SetCull(side);`. What's built, and what code relies on:
    - **It's built as depth is, part for part.** A destination's own (`tide_draw_place.cull`, a `tide_cull`, 0 for `TIDE_CULL_BACK`, so a zeroed place is a frame's first; `target.Cull` and `target.SetCull` are the same signatures); only a 3D mesh's command carries it (`tide_draw_command.cull`, the byte `kind` gave up: `kind` is 8 bits now, and `push` writes the same four bytes it did), set by `mesh_run` and, for a 3D shader, `shaded_instances`, and 0 for every other command; `tide_draw_set_cull`, `tide_draw_unset_cull` and `tide_draw_push_cull` with `TIDE_DRAW_SAVED_CULL`; a signature like the others (`add_setting` in `compiler/src/builtins.c`), so both forms, each one's misuse, its quick fix and the lists with commas are the family's code; and the enum in generated C only for a game that names it (`enum_generated`). A game that says nothing of cull generates the C it did, byte for byte (old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without: 200 games), so what the default changes is what's drawn, never a game's C or its hash.
    - **The rule is the mesh's own, not the screen's:** the front is the side the corners go clockwise on as the mesh has them, which is the side `(b - a) x (c - a)` points to, and it stays the front through whatever mirrors it on the way to the screen. Three things can, and each says so where its parts are known, never by asking the finished matrix (a camera far from the origin would answer by its rounding):
      - An instance's own transform: `mirrors` in `engine/src/draw.c` is the sign of its 3 by 3 determinant, under 0. The instances that are mirrored are a run of their own, whose command says `TIDE_DRAW_MIRRORED` with its `tide_cull`: `mesh_instances` splits a list given at once, and `tide_draw_shaded_instances` a shader's by the transform that starts each instance. With `TIDE_CULL_NONE` nothing is asked and nothing is split.
      - `Draw.Transform`'s: `TIDE_DRAW_MOVED_MIRROR` in the place's `moved`, worked out where the transform is set, pushed or put back, so a mesh asks of its own transform alone and the two are an exclusive or. A 3D transform's bits in `moved` are `MOVED_3D`: what sets or saves the 2D one leaves both.
      - A camera: `tide_draw_camera_3d_data.mirrored`, which its `TIDE_DRAW_CAMERA_3D` command carries. `tide_draw_set_camera_3d` never is. `tide_draw_set_camera_matrices` is when its transform mirrors or its projection's determinant is above 0, and not both: a projection as OpenGL's has one under 0, as it turns the view's z around, so the identity as a projection is a mirror (it looks back along z). `check_render_depth_alpha` in `platform/tests/targets.c` draws through one, and its quads are wound for it.
      - `tide_draw_append` passes what a command said (`SIDES_KNOWN`), and asks no transform again.
    - **The platform resolves it per step** (`gpu_cull` in `platform/src/platform.c`): none, or the back or the front as the GPU will see them wound, which is the command's side, turned when its instances are mirrored and its camera isn't, or the other way about. Pipelines are by kind, blend, depth and that (`engine_pipelines.made`, `shader_pipeline.cull`, the ones into targets of depth alone among them), and nothing but a 3D mesh's has any but `TIDE_GPU_CULL_NONE`, which is 0, so a pipeline description that says nothing draws both sides.
    - **`gpu.h` says the front goes clockwise in its clip space, y up,** in the window and in a target alike, and each backend makes that so. OpenGL's `gl_use` sets `glCullFace` and `glFrontFace` with each pipeline (`glCullFace`, `glFrontFace` in `gl.h` and `tide.js`): `GL_CW` in the window, and `GL_CCW` in a target's pass, where its vertex wrapper turns y over (`compile`). WebGPU's pipelines are made with `frontFace: 'cw'` and `cullMode` by `tide_gpu_cull`'s numbers (`culls` in `tide.js`: a change to the enum goes there), with nothing to turn for a target.
    - **Every pixel test draws into a target** (`tide_platform_read_pixels`), so OpenGL's window, the one place its front face is `GL_CW`, is seen by none of them. It was checked by hand (2026-10-07): a scratch game with a square and a plane seen from the front and one each from the back, as a page in headless Chrome, `--screenshot`, with `backend=webgl` and `backend=webgpu`: the canvas showed the fronts and not the backs on both. WebGL's canvas is the code a desktop window's is.
    - **A transform that flattens has a determinant of 0, which isn't under it:** nothing is mirrored, and each triangle is drawn or not by how it's wound once flat. A closed mesh still covers its outline (the triangles that faced along the flattening are the ones left, which `cull_settings` pins with a cube), and a flat one may be gone, so the docs say to draw what's flattened with `Cull.None`.
    - **A game's vert can turn triangles round** (mirror its mesh, or turn it inside out), and nothing knows but the game: it draws with `Cull.Front`. `Sides` in `platform/tests/shaders.tide` pins that.
    - **The primitives were wound outward already,** their fronts outside and a plane's its top (`tests/test_primitives.c` had pinned it, by the same cross product): nothing in `engine/src/primitives.c` changed. A plane seen from below isn't drawn.
    - **What it costs:** recording an instance of a 3D mesh takes a determinant more, about 1.7 ns (65,536 instances of a cube: 0.76 ms to 0.87, `bench/results/draw.jsonl`, `cull-before` and `cull`; written four floats at a time it was no quicker). `tide_draw_instance` is `mesh_instances` for a count of one, not a call to `tide_draw_instances`: through the call it was 0.95. Plain shapes are recorded in the time they were.
    - **The audit** (2026-10-07): each 3D game run with no window, with both sides drawn and as it's drawn now, and the pictures compared. Wound the other way, and turned: the docs' `lighting` sphere and the cube of its `shadows` example (which came in with a merge, wound as OpenGL has its fronts, and drew its insides), Marbles' ball, its coin's near face and rim and two sides of its island's skirt, Sumo's floor plate (the rings under its balls were gone) and the renderer bench's cube. Drawn with both sides on purpose: Sumo's shadows, which were flattened then (they're a shadow map since: see The physics games), and its sparks, which glow and add. Marbles' shadow discs are thin cylinders that blended their far cap in too, so their ink went up to what one layer needs for the same look (0.3 to 0.51). `draw3d`, `physics-3d`, Sumo's boxes and balls and the docs' sample triangle were right. What's left that differs is a pixel here and there where a back used to show through a seam.
    - Tests: `draw_cull_is_the_3d_meshes_own` (`tests/test_draw_settings.c`) for the list, mirrored instances, transforms and cameras among it; `cull_settings` in `platform/tests/draw.c` for pixels on each backend, for ordinary 3D meshes and for ones through a shader (each side, mirrored by its own transform, through a transform that mirrors, under a camera placed by a matrix that mirrors and one whose projection is upside down, set and in blocks, flattened; mirrored instances as draws apart, and one draw with both sides; `Cull.Back` said outright is what a frame starts with, pixel for pixel); `check_render_cull` in `platform/tests/targets.c` for a target's own, either way, and `check_depth_cull` for a target of depth alone, which is drawn from the side its own cull says; `Sides` in `platform/tests/shaders.tide` for meshes through tidec's verts and frags; `compiler/tests/e2e/draw_cull` for what tidec generates; `compiler/tests/errors/draw_cull_*` for each diagnostic; `lsp_draw_cull` for the editors.
  - **Stencil is a setting too: `Draw`'s, and a destination's own** (built 2026-10-07; that it's there as it is on a GPU (a whole number from 0 to 255 for each pixel beside its depth, a test against a number of the draw's, and what becomes of the stored one where the draw draws), that it's a draw setting with both forms, and that it has none of a GPU's other stencil state (each side of a triangle apart, what a failed depth test does, bit masks) are the owner's; `Draw.Stencil(test, value[, operation])`, the enums `StencilTest` and `StencilOp` with their members, that it's `Draw`'s with a target's own as a method, what a clear does and what a frame starts with are Claude's picks, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Draw settings, Stencil, with the rules below): `Draw.Stencil(test, value) { ... }`, `Draw.Stencil(test, value, operation) { ... }`, `Draw.SetStencil(...)` with the same arguments, and `Draw.SetStencil()`. What's built, and what code relies on:
    - **Whose it is.** 2D and 3D draws both take stencil (a mask for sprites and panels is its commonest use). It's a destination's own, as the numbers it's about are: `Draw.Stencil` is the window's, and its call selects the window (`draw_dims` 2 in `compiler/src/check.c`, as every `Draw` call's does since one `Draw`), and a target's is `target.Stencil` and `target.SetStencil`, the same signatures.
    - **The test's order is the GPU's:** the draw's number on the left, the stored one on the right (`StencilTest.Less` draws where the value is less than the stored number), as OpenGL, WebGPU and Unity have it. `Increment` and `Decrement` stay at 255 and 0 (`GL_INCR`, `increment-clamp`).
    - **In C** (`tide/draw.h`): `tide_stencil_test` and `tide_stencil_op`, each with its default first (`TIDE_STENCIL_ALWAYS`, `TIDE_STENCIL_KEEP`), so a zeroed `tide_stencil` is a frame's first; `tide_stencil_tested(test, value)` and `tide_stencil_stored(test, value, operation)`, which make one of what Tide passes (any numbers: an enum's that's no member's is the first, a value is kept from 0 to 255) and are the signatures' `pack` functions, as a call can leave the operation out; `tide_draw_set_stencil`, `tide_draw_unset_stencil` and `tide_draw_push_stencil`, with `TIDE_DRAW_SAVED_STENCIL`. What nothing reads is 0 (`tide_stencil_stored`: the number of `Always`, unless it's what `Replace` stores, and all of `Never`, which draws nothing), so two that do the same are the same, and `Draw.Stencil(StencilTest.Always, 9)` is none at all.
    - **It reaches a renderer as a camera or a clip does, not in each command.** A command has no byte to spare for it, and `push` is the code it was (nothing to measure against `bench/results/draw.jsonl`). A place has `stencil` and `played_stencil` (a `tide_stencil` as one number, `tide_stencil_packed`), `refresh` counts a difference as unsettled, and `settle` records a `TIDE_DRAW_STENCIL` command before the next command that draws. So one that's set to what it was, or set and put back around nothing, records nothing; the same block around each entity's draw records it once, with their shapes still side by side; and it's the selected destination's alone. `tide_draw_append` carries the commands across, and what was set after the last draw, and `tide_draw_defaults` sets every destination's back, so the GUI's commands are told there's none after a game left one set.
    - **In the renderer** (`platform/src/platform.c`): a `TIDE_DRAW_STENCIL` command sets its destination's (`frame_target.stencil`, `draw_stencil`) and adds a `STEP_STENCIL`, which draws nothing and only ends the run before it, so `run_goes_on` and `add_shape` are the code they were. Every step takes its destination's stencil as it's added (`add_step`), but a clear and a blit, which have none. A step's pipeline is by its test and operation (`stencil_state`, the low byte), never by its number, which is the pass's: `use_pipeline` sets it where the pass has another (`tide_gpu.stencil`), and `begin_pass` starts at 0. So pipelines don't multiply by 256. The engine's pipelines with any stencil are a list of their own (`stencil_pipelines`: by kind, blend, depth, cull, test and operation, and what they draw into), looked through, as a game has a handful; with none, the arrays there were. A game shader's key has it (`shader_pipeline.state`).
    - **`gpu.h`:** a pipeline's `stencil_test` and `stencil_op` (`tide_gpu_stencil_test`, `tide_gpu_stencil_op`, with `tide_stencil_*`'s numbers, by `_Static_assert`s in `platform.c`), zeroed for none, and `stencil(value)`: the number the tests of the draws after it compare with, and what `Replace` stores, which is the pass's whatever pipeline is used, and 0 as a pass begins. A pass that starts cleared sets stencil to 0. OpenGL keeps the number with the test, so `gl_use` and `gl_stencil` both go through `set_stencil` (`glStencilFunc` against all 8 bits, and `glStencilOp(GL_KEEP, GL_KEEP, op)`, both in `gl.h` and `tide.js`), and `gl_begin` turns the test off and clears with `GL_STENCIL_BUFFER_BIT`. WebGPU's is the pipeline's `stencilFront` and `stencilBack`, the same (`stencilTests` and `stencilOps` in `tide.js`, by the enums' numbers: a change to either goes there), and the pass's `setStencilReference`.
    - **A clear sets stencil back to 0, and a blit leaves it,** whatever is set: the clears' pipeline stores with `TIDE_GPU_STENCIL_ZERO` and tests nothing, within the clip as for depth, and a blit's pipeline has no stencil (`pipeline_for`, for `blit`).
    - **The window has stencil on each platform:** GLFW is asked for 24 bits of depth and 8 of stencil (`platform/desktop/glfw.c`), the WebGL context is made with `stencil: true`, Android's EGL config asks for `EGL_STENCIL_SIZE` 8, and WebGPU's canvas already had `depth24plus-stencil8`. A window without it would pass every test, with no error: OpenGL does, where there's no stencil to test.
    - **What a draw touches is what stores,** whatever its color, so the engine's shaders draw exactly those pixels where a step stores: `fix.y`, the second of their `fix` uniform, is 1 for a step whose operation isn't `Keep` (`step_uniforms`) and 0 otherwise, where both shaders are the code they were. With it, text discards where no glyph covers a pixel (under 0.002, half the least a glyph's pixel is), where it otherwise draws its quads clear; and a smooth shape that's clear is drawn, where it's otherwise discarded, at the pixels it covers a 255th of or more (0.004: with its edge on the pixels' own, the pixel just past it is then out on every GPU, where "more than 0" could be a millionth on one). That's a line more in each of those two shaders, measured (`bench/results/draw.jsonl`, `stencil-before` and `stencil`: six runs of each, taken in turn on a machine other sessions were busy on): 65,536 round rects render in 2.81 ms before and 2.83 after by the least of them, rects with text between in 1.72 and 1.81 with middles of 2.10 and 2.09, and recording is as it was. The plain shapes' shader has no `fix`, already draws exactly what it touches (a circle discards past its edge), and is unchanged: a run of plain shapes under a stencil goes through the plain pipeline. On a scaled display a plain circle is a smooth shape (`add_circle`), which is why a clear smooth shape has to store. A mesh stores over all of its triangles, whatever its texture's alpha: a game's frag discards for a mask the shape of a picture.
    - **A target of depth alone has it,** and whatever stores is drawn there (`writes_depth` counts `stencil_stores`): the engine's shapes, text and 2D meshes then have pipelines into it too (`bare`, in `stencil_pipelines`), their color going nowhere.
    - **Generated C has the two enums only in a game that names them** (`named_only` in `check.c`, which `find_in` goes by, and `enum_generated`, with `Depth`, `Cull`, `Wrap` and `Format`). A game that says nothing of stencil generates the C it did, byte for byte (old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without: 238 games). `default` where one of those enums goes names it too (`check_default_value`): `Draw.SetDepth(default)` generated `((Depth)0)` in a game with no `Depth` in its C, which didn't compile.
    - **Not built:** reading a pixel's stencil, in a frag or back into Tide; the operations that wrap round.
    - Tests: `draw_stencil_is_a_destinations_own` (`tests/test_draw_settings.c`) for the list; `check_stencil` in `platform/tests/targets.c` at `gpu.h` (each test and each operation against both ends of what a pixel holds, kept across passes, a pass's number, a draw hidden behind what's nearer, a clear, a target of depth alone and, on the web, the page's own canvas, read as the browser shows it) and `check_render_stencil` through the renderer, at scales of 1 and 2 (shapes, smooth shapes, text against the same text's ink, meshes, each test and each operation, 200 draws in one call, a clear in a clip, a blit, 3D meshes with depth, shaders, a target's own kept from frame to frame, a target of depth alone); pass 9 of `platform/tests/shaders.tide` with `run_stencil_checks` in `shaders.c`, from Tide, in one frame on a display scaled to twice its pixels (one, as that test's frames are slow in a browser with no GPU: on CI's Linux runner it took 110 of its 120 seconds before this pass was there). All the same pixels on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU, where the device is then lost and found again. `compiler/tests/e2e/draw_stencil` for what tidec generates; `compiler/tests/errors/draw_stencil_*` for each diagnostic; `lsp_draw_stencil` for the editors. The docs' `stencil` example shows it (`docs/game/views.md`, Stencil).
    - **Not run by any test:** the desktop OpenGL window's own framebuffer, as every pixel test reads a target. Checked by hand (2026-10-07, on Windows): a `glReadPixels` of the hidden window, put into `check_stencil` for one run, after a draw that passes on its left half only, which was drawn there and not beside it. Android (its apps of these tests are started by hand), and OpenGL on a Mac, which nothing runs.
- Calls that take a block can be listed with commas before one block (agreed 2026-10-05; `docs/spec.md`, Calls that take a block): draw settings, the GUI's containers and a game's functions with an `Action`, each beside the others. The parser makes each call's block the call after it (`expr.stacked`), so it means what nesting them does, and the rest of the compiler sees nested calls. A call in the list that takes no block is an error at that call. The formatter puts a call a line (`stacks_calls` in `compiler/lsp/analysis.c`).
- Smooth shapes are primitives, drawn by the engine's own shaders: they take no `frag` or `vert` (agreed: not every draw function takes a shader). `Draw.RoundRect`, `Ellipse`, `Arc`, their outlines, the thick `Line`, `WireRect` and `WireCircle`, the turned `Rect`, and `Draw.Shape(shape)`, the general one the others but `Arc` are short for: a built-in `Shape` struct's box or ellipse, turned, with a fill from `color` at its top to `color2` at its bottom (`Color.none`, its default: one color), an outline inside its edge, and an edge that fades outwards (`soft` above 0) or inwards (under 0). See `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, and `docs/game/views.md`, Smooth shapes.
  - `Rect`, `WireRect` and the `Line` with no width stay as they were, filling whole pixels, so rects side by side leave no seam (`tiles_leave_no_gaps` in `platform/tests/draw.c`). Only the smooth shapes are drawn by distance.
  - Angles are in radians from +x towards +y in the units the call is in (counterclockwise under a camera, clockwise on the screen), and a fill's first color is at the shape's top as the screen has it, in both. Outlines are inside the edge. `Arc`'s ends are cut straight across, so a width of the radius is a slice of a disc.
  - The list keeps one as a `TIDE_DRAW_SHAPE` command: its center, size and fill in the command, like a rect's, and the rest in `tide_draw_list.shapes` (`tide_draw_shape_data`: its rotation as cos and sin, its corners' radius, its outline, how far it fades, its form). The functions of `tide/draw.h` (`tide_draw_round_rect`, `tide_draw_thick_line`...) fill those themselves; `tide_draw_shape` takes a `tide_shape`, which generated C makes of Tide's `Shape` in a helper (`tide_draw_shape_value`), as the two are different C types.
  - The platform has two pipelines of shapes (`platform/src/platform.c`, `platform/src/shaders.h`). A run of the plain kinds alone goes to the GPU as it always did, an instance of 24 bytes each. A run with a smooth shape in it goes through the pipeline that draws every kind, each shape with a `shape_extra` (32 bytes) in a buffer of its own, so it's still one draw call whatever kinds it mixes. Both pipelines draw the plain kinds from the same shader lines, so a rect is the same pixels in either. The plain pipeline's shaders are what they were before there were smooth shapes, but for their color going out times its alpha (see Alpha is premultiplied under the hood), and a plain shape is added to its run by the code that always added it (a mixed run's plain shapes get their extras when the run ends, `settle_extras`), so a frame of plain shapes costs what it did: a change to that path is measured against `bench/results/draw.jsonl` (`smooth-shapes`). A plain circle's edge is smooth over a logical pixel, which would be soft on a scaled display, so there the renderer draws circles as the smooth shapes they also are (`add_circle`).
  - A pixel is in a smooth shape by how far it is from the edge, in the shape's own frame: a box's distance is exact; an ellipse's is found (three steps towards the nearest point of the edge, then two of Newton's, which 32-bit floats need for flat ellipses), so its outline is as wide all the way round; part of a ring is a ring and the wedge it's cut to. Edges are smooth over one pixel of the target, which the shaders know from `scale` (the platform's `pixel_scale`), and colors blend with their alpha in them, so a fill to a clear color fades and doesn't darken.
  - `tide_platform_draw` checks every kind's pixels, edge by edge, on each backend, and that a run of every kind is one draw call. `tide_platform_draw --bench` has workloads of smooth shapes too, and `--dump <file.ppm>` writes the smooth shapes' scene as an image, to look at.
- Meshes are the general primitive (`Draw.Mesh`, `tide_draw_mesh`): triangles with a position, a uv and a color at each corner, indexed, which the list keeps (`vertices`, `indices`) and draws in order with its other commands. The platform expands each run of them with one texture, filter and wrap into triangles in window pixels, a draw call. A clip (`Draw.Clip`, `Draw.SetClip`) is a scissor, its rect in the units the commands are in, which clears honor and the GUI never draws through; the screen (`Draw.Screen`, `Draw.SetScreen`: `TIDE_DRAW_SCREEN`) is the GUI's pixels, for views. The shapes' instanced path stays as it is.
- 3D is meshes too, kept bare so that games and libraries scale it up: `Vertex3` (`tide_vertex3`: a position, a uv and a color), drawn by `Draw.Mesh(vertices, indices, transform)` (`tide_draw_mesh_3d`) through a camera of their own, `Draw.SetCamera(position, rotation, fieldOfView)` or any `transform` and `projection`. No lighting is built in: normals and what a pixel does with them are a vertex struct and a `frag` of the game's.
- Manual instancing is `Draw.Instanced`, and a target's `Instanced` (`(vertices, indices, instances[, texture[, filter]][, vert][, frag])`): one mesh for each list item, without entities. In 3D, a `List<float4x4>` supplies transforms, or an ordinary struct's optional `float4x4 transform` does (identity without it). A shader's read-only ordinary struct parameter receives the list item; other components still come from the view's entity and singletons are shared. In 2D a vert places each item, and the engine applies no instance transform. Only read fields are sent, beside a 3D transform; an empty list draws nothing, and each compatible run is one GPU draw. See `docs/game/shaders.md`, Instances from a list.
- A game's code runs on the GPU as `frag` (once for each pixel) and `vert` (once for each vertex), declarations like views, which a `Draw.Mesh`, or a target's `Mesh`, names after its other arguments, for 2D and 3D meshes of any vertex struct with a `position` (`docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing; `docs/game/shaders.md`). Their signature says what they read and the engine supplies it: the vertex first, a frag's pixel as `out Color`, components of the entity the calling view runs for, singletons, and the built-in `Pixel` and `Instance`. Manual instancing can also supply one read-only ordinary struct from its list, a draw one read-only `Texture`, its explicit source, and a draw one more read-only struct, its values, written after the vert and the frag (below). No individual plain value parameters, no return value (decided: ECS style).
  - **What a frag and a vert write is `out`** (agreed 2026-10-07, built the same day; `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, Code the GPU runs, with the picks under Provisional there): a frag's color is `out Color color` (the owner's idea, now that `out` is a keyword), and a vert hands its frag a struct of its own, `vert Place(mut Corner corner, out FromLamp lamp, Sun sun)` and `frag Look(Corner corner, FromLamp lamp, out Color color)`, which the GPU blends across the triangle, so what a vert works out needs no field in every vertex of the mesh. A vert's vertex stays `mut`. What's built, and what code relies on:
    - **The parser lets `out` through for these two alone** (`parse_query_params` in `compiler/src/parser.c`, by `d->kind == DECL_SHADER`): `param.is_out` with `mode` `PARAM_MUT`, as a function's. A system's, a view's and a handler's `out` is the error it was. `param.mode_word` is the `mut` or `out` token as written, which the checker hands `suggest_replacement` for the quick fixes (`mut` to `out`, and `out` to `mut` on a vert's vertex).
    - **`check_shader_decl`** (`compiler/src/check.c`) sorts each parameter: the vertex first; a frag's `out Color`s, four at the most (`decl.outputs`, each one's place in `param.output`; an `out` of another type is the "isn't there yet" error); a vert's `out` structs, as many as it likes, each type once, whose fields have to blend (`check_handed_struct`: floats, their vectors and colors; an int, bool, enum, matrix, quaternion or struct is an error there); and the rest as before. `mut Color color` is an error that names `out`, and so is `mut` on a vert's struct; each then goes on as the `out` it meant (`is_out` set, `out_at` left alone, so editors show the word as written), so nothing else is said of it.
    - **A frag's read-only struct is its vert's or its instances' (or, since, its draw's values: see A draw's values, below), and the draw says which** (`check_shader_binding`, `shader_writes`): by type, as a system finds a component. The draw's vert writes it, or the draw is `Instanced` over a list of it; neither is the error at the draw ("frag 'Look' reads a 'FromLamp', which vert 'Lift' doesn't write", or "...and this draw names no vert that writes one"), which took the place of the two "reads instance data" messages for frags (a vert's are as they were, and a vert still takes one instance struct, a frag any number of structs). A vert that writes the type the draw's instances are is an error there too. Where an `Instanced` draw's list is written as a literal, its element type is taken from the first struct the vert or the frag reads that the vert doesn't write.
    - **Definite assignment is what makes a frag write its color** (`compiler/src/assigned.c`, which `check_method_body` now runs for frags and verts too): the same pass as a function's `out` parameters, with messages of its own (`gpu`): "frag 'Shade' can end without writing its pixel's color, 'color'", "'color' is read before it's written", "vert 'Place' can end without writing 'lamp.glow'". `pixel.Discard()` ends a path with nothing owed, as `fail` does (`CALL_DISCARD` in `flow_stmt`). It only runs on a body with no other error, so a frag whose code is wrong some other way says that first, and what the GPU refuses (`plan_shaders`) is said after: an error test of a GPU refusal has a frag that writes its color.
    - **A struct is assigned a field at a time, in functions too** (a Provisional pick that changed: it was whole assignment only; the agreed vert writes `lamp.place = ...`). `assigned.c` keeps a slot a variable, or one for each field of a struct (`add_var`, `field_slot`, `var_assigned`): `pair.a = 1`, `F(out pair.a)` and reading `pair.a` go by the field's slot, reading `pair` whole needs them all, and what's left names the field ("'Make' can end without assigning 'pair.b'", "'pair' is read before its 'b' is assigned"). One level: a field of a struct in it, and a vector's or a color's component, is a part of that field.
    - **In the shaders** (`compiler/src/shader.c`): `shader.handed` is what a combination's vert writes (`shader_handed`: the struct, the vert's parameter, the frag's parameter of that type or NULL, and which fields the frag reads), made in `plan`. The vertex stage's `main` makes a variable for each (`tide_handed<n>`), calls the vert with them after its vertex, in the vert's order, and writes the fields the frag reads to varyings (`tide_h<n>_<field>`, after the vertex's own in `put_varyings`); the fragment stage's `main` calls the frag with its parameters that are values in their order: the vertex, each struct its vert wrote (made of those varyings, zeros for what it doesn't read), and the color. `is_bound` says no for such a struct (`handed_to`), so the frag's code reads it as the function parameter it then is, where an instance's struct is read from the instance's streams. While checking (`em->sh` is NULL) both go the bound way, which only notes what's read. Each field carried counts among the 15 values corner-to-pixel checked at the draw.
    - **A frag's function still takes its color `inout` (a pointer in WGSL), and `main` still starts it white**, though no path reads that any more: the text tidec writes for a game that was only respelled is, byte for byte, what it was, and so is its generated C (old tidec over every game's sources as they were against new tidec over them as they are, with `--layout` and without: 230 games the same, and the three whose tests gained code), so shaders' ids and the pipelines hot reloading keeps are unchanged. Don't make it GLSL's `out`, whose value is undefined until it's written, on a path a shader compiler can't see ends in a discard.
    - **Every frag in the repo is respelled** (206, by script: `mut Color` to `out Color` inside each `frag Name(...)`'s parameters, in `.tide` files, the docs' samples and the tests' strings). Five leaned on the white start, all tests' own: the two `Blank` frags, which were there to check it and now write `Color.white`, `compiler/tests/shaders/shared_names.tide`, which wrote its color a component at a time (the one pin that moved), and two error tests' frags. No game's did.
    - **Editors** (`compiler/lsp/analysis.c`): `out` is a keyword on a frag's and a vert's parameter (`OCC_OUT`, as a function's), with hovers for the color, for what a vert writes and for a frag's struct, which is described as its vert's when some vert of the game writes that type (`vert_writes`, `handed_param`: the draw isn't known at the declaration) and as an instance's otherwise; a frag's hover lists the fields it reads "of what its vert wrote for it". Completion offers `out` after the vertex, `Color` after a frag's `out` and the game's structs after a vert's, and the `frag` snippet says `out Color color`. The quick fixes: "Change to 'out'" on `mut Color color` and on a vert's `mut` struct, "Change to 'mut'" on `out` before a vert's vertex, "Write 'color = Color.white;'" where a path writes no color and "...first" where it's read first (`filler`: white for a frag's color, which is what it started as, and `default` for anything else), with no "take it as 'mut'" for a frag or a vert.
    - **Tests:** `compiler/tests/errors/` has a file a diagnostic (`frag_mut_color`, `frag_mut_out`, `frag_mut_value`, `frag_color_not_written`, `frag_color_not_written_at_return`, `frag_color_read_first`, `frag_struct_no_vert`, `frag_struct_vert_lacks`, `frag_struct_parameter`, `frag_reads_handed_written`, `out_on_frag`, `out_on_frag_singleton`, `out_on_vert_vertex`, `out_on_vert_component`, `vert_mut_struct`, `vert_out_plain`, `vert_out_int_field`, `vert_out_matrix_field`, `vert_out_twice`, `vert_out_not_written`, `vert_out_not_written_at_all`, `vert_out_read_first`, `vert_out_instances`, `gpu_limit_varyings_handed`, and `out_field_*` and `out_struct_read_before_field_assigned` for functions); `compiler/tests/shaders/handed` pins the GLSL and WGSL (two structs, one read in part and one whole through a function, a field made by a call's `out` argument, an instance's struct beside them, a vert drawn with no frag, 2D); `tidec_e2e_shaders` checks a draw sends nothing for such a struct and carries only the fields read; `tidec_e2e_out_params` fills a struct a field at a time; `tide_platform_shaders` (`platform/tests/shaders.tide`, `shaders.c`) draws through them and reads the pixels, blended a quarter and three quarters of the way across, a struct read whole, a path that discards, an instance's struct beside the vert's, a vert with no frag, and 2D, the same on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU; `lsp_shader_outputs` and the updated `lsp_shaders_*` the editors' side; the grammar's probe has both.
    - Not built: the open `shader Name { ... }` block. Several outputs from a frag are, since: see A target holds several color images, below.
  - **A draw's values: a struct after its vert and frag, the same for the whole draw** (card 4 of the reshaping, built 2026-10-07. That `Mesh` and `Blit` take a struct their frag reads, bound as an `Instanced` draw's is, is the owner's, with the example `Draw.Blit(src, dst, Blur, BlurParams { direction = float2(1, 0) })`; where it's written elsewhere, what it can be and each error are Claude's picks, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, A draw's values): `Draw.Mesh(..., vert, frag, values)`, `Draw.Instanced(..., vert, frag, values)`, a target's two, and `Draw.Blit(source, destination[, blend], frag, values)`. `docs/game/shaders.md` (Values for one draw) is the users' page. What's built, and what code relies on:
    - **The checker** (`compiler/src/check.c`): an argument after the names is the draw's values. `check_mesh_draw` peels it off the end before the names (the last argument, when it names no frag or vert and one is named before it; with none named it's an argument like the others again), and `check_blit` takes it where the argument before the last names the frag. `check_draw_values` checks it, an expression of a struct's type: `expr.values` and `expr.values_type`. It's checked before the mesh's other arguments, as an `Instanced` draw's list written as a literal takes its element's type from the first struct the vert or the frag reads that's neither the vert's to write nor the draw's values.
    - **Three sources, by type** (`check_shader_binding`): a frag's read-only struct is its draw's vert's (`shader_writes`), its instances' (`expr.instance_type`) or its values'; a vert's is one of the last two. A struct none supplies is an error at the name, whose note says all three; values of the type the vert writes, or of the instances' type, are errors too, as a frag couldn't tell them apart; and values that neither the vert nor the frag takes are an error at the values (`check_values_taken`, `shader_takes_struct`). A vert takes any number of read-only structs now: the declaration's "a vert takes one instance struct" is gone, and a second one is the draw's values or an error at the draw.
    - **The shader** (`compiler/src/shader.c`): a `shader` is also by its draws' `values_type` (`plan`), and `plan_reads` adds what the vert and the frag read of a parameter of that type as values that aren't `per_instance` and aren't `bound`, so they're laid out in the uniform block among what's read of singletons, in the order the stages' parameters come (the vert's first), and `put_bound` finds them by the struct and the field. Nothing else in the writer knows of them: the text is what a singleton's fields give. `compiler/tests/shaders/values` pins it.
    - **Generated C**: `tide_shade_<n>` takes the struct by value, `tide_values`, after what it takes for its textures and before the components and singletons, and copies the fields that are read into `tide_uniforms`; the call passes the expression there (`gen_expr`'s `CALL_DRAW` branch, which counts the arguments before the names for a blit's blend where it counted them all). `arg_want` has no entry for it.
    - **Runs are the draw list's as they were**: `shaded_instances` joins draws in a row with the same shader, mesh, textures and uniform bytes, so the same values twice are one run and other values another, and a field nothing reads keeps no two draws apart, as it isn't in the bytes. Nothing in `engine/` or `platform/` changed.
    - **A draw whose values come from a call that writes** (`Rolled(art)` with a `mut` argument) has its lists read before the call, into scratch copies, by the evaluation-order rule, and a scratch copy of a list is another mesh to the draw list than the list itself: that draw joins no run of the others. It was so for any argument that writes. A game keeps such a value in a local first.
    - **A game that gives no values generates the C it did, byte for byte** (old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without: 204 games the same; the four that use it, Duel, its tests, the shaders test and the new e2e test, only compile with the new one). Duel's blur is the worked example: `BlurStep { along }` at each of its four Blits, where `Lens.along` was written before each; its picture in a run with no window is the same bytes before and after.
    - **Editors** (`compiler/lsp/analysis.c`): a frag's or vert's struct parameter that some draw of the game gives as values (`draw_gives`, `values_param`: the draw isn't known at the declaration) says so on hover, and is listed with what's sent for each draw; the signature help of `Mesh` and `Instanced` ends in `[, values]` (`MESH_VALUES`), and `Blit`'s form too; and completion after a name offers the structs that frag or vert takes, as `Swell { }` to fill in (`complete_draw_values`, in a Blit too).
    - **Tests:** `compiler/tests/e2e/draw_values` (two blits of one frag with two values, a blend before the frag, meshes whose vert and frag read different fields, the same values twice as one run, a field nothing reads, a value from a local and from a call, instances beside values, a vert alone in 2D, and what's sent, word for word); `compiler/tests/shaders/values`; `compiler/tests/errors/draw_values_*.tide` and `vert_struct_not_supplied.tide`, a file a diagnostic (`instanced_multiple_data.tide` is the draw's error now); pass 12 of `platform/tests/shaders.tide` with `run_values_checks` in `shaders.c` (pixels, and the runs by shader), the same on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU, where the device is then lost and found; `lsp_draw_values`.
    - **Not built:** values for a draw with no vert and no frag, more than one struct a draw, a value that isn't a struct, and a texture in the values.
  - `compiler/src/shader.c` writes the shaders: for each combination a draw uses (a vertex struct, with a vert, a frag, both or neither), GLSL for both stages and WGSL, in `plan_shaders`, which the checker calls once code is checked. `tidec --shaders` (`tide shaders`) prints them, and `compiler/tests/shaders/` pins what it writes.
  - The code that writes a shader is the code that checks it: a first pass (`emit.checking`) goes through each frag and vert and the functions they reach, writes nothing, and reports what a GPU can't run (`refuse`), with which frag reaches a function's code. What's written afterwards always compiles, with no warning: nobody sees a shader compiler's error. So every name of the game's gets a prefix no keyword has (`name_of`); conversions are written out; a `mut` parameter is `inout` in GLSL and a pointer in WGSL, whose argument is a whole variable or goes through a copy; WGSL's swizzles are assigned a component at a time; code after a `return` is dropped; and constants a shader compiler would fold into an error (WGSL's are: a division by zero, a function's argument out of its range) go through `tide_f` and `tide_i`. A number with a minus before it is written as that number. A loop's own line has only numbers and variables (`st_for`): what it starts at, and what it goes to and steps by when those are constants, are worked out before the loop when they're anything more, as compilers read how far a loop goes off that line, and the Android emulator's loses a function that only that line calls when the loop samples a texture (it stopped a blur's `for (var x = -1; ...)` from compiling there). Textures are sampled at their own level (`textureLod`, `textureSampleLevel`), or between their smaller ones with derivatives the frag asked for as it started (`textureGrad`, `textureSampleGrad`: see Mipmaps are a filter, below), so sampling is allowed in any branch and in a vert.
  - Everything in `Math` is on the GPU unless it makes no sense there (decided), with the math types and what builds them (`quaternion.AxisAngle`, `float4x4.TRS` and the rest): a function `Math` gains gets its GPU side with it, or an error in `ex_math` that says why not and what to write instead. One is left out: `Math.Hash` of a `long`, as these GPUs have no 64-bit integers (`Math.Hash(int2(low, high))` of its halves is the same number). What a shader language has no function for, or has one that does otherwise than `tide/math.h`'s, is a function of the engine's own in the shader (`helper_text` and `need` in `compiler/src/shader.c`): math.h's formula, in its order, written once in the GLSL both languages can say, which `put_helper` turns into WGSL, so a change is one change.
    - `Math.Hash` is the CPU's number exactly: unsigned integers, which wrap the same in every language.
    - Floats are the CPU's formula with the GPU's rounding, which differs between GPUs; the docs say so, and that warning stays accurate.
    - **Noise is `Math.Perlin`, `Simplex` and `Cellular`** (2026-10-07; the owner asked for noise functions in `Math` that work on the GPU; the kinds, names and rules are Claude's picks, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Vector math): of a `float2` or a `float3` and an `int` seed a call can leave out (0 then: `noise_call` in `compiler/src/codegen.c` and `ex_math` in `shader.c` each write the 0, as `Play`'s defaults are filled by name). The CPU's is `engine/src/noise.c` (declared in `tide/math.h`: `tide_perlin_f2`, `tide_simplex_f3`, `tide_cellular_f2`...), basic operations and `Math.Hash` of each cell and the seed for its gradient or feature point, so a point is the same bits everywhere and noise can be in the match; the GPU's is the same lines in GLSL (`H_PERLIN2` and the others in `helper_texts`, spelled into WGSL by `put_helper`), so a frag gets the cells the match computed, and floats as it rounds. **A change to one side goes to the other, line for line.** `Perlin` is within -1 to 1 by its gradients, `Simplex` scaled to about that (99.2 in 2D, 32.5 in 3D, from a search for its greatest value), `Cellular` the two nearest distances. Tests: `math_noise_stays_in_its_range_and_is_smooth` (`tests/test_math.c`), `crossplatform_noise` (`tests/test_crossplatform.c`, a pinned hash the web shares), `compiler/tests/e2e/math` (bit for bit noise.c's, with 0 for a left-out seed), `compiler/tests/shaders/math` (the GLSL and WGSL), and the `Rest` probe of `tide_platform_shaders` (CPU against GPU on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU). The docs' `noise` example draws the three through a frag (`docs/language/math.md`, Noise).
    - What's forgiving on the CPU is forgiving there: `Normalize`'s zero and identity, and `Min`, `Max`, `Clamp` and `Saturate` with NaN, which they tell by its bits (`tide_nan`), since a shader compiler may reckon without NaN and fold `x != x` away.
    - Where the CPU gives a number, the GPU does, though the languages' own functions leave it open: `Pow` of a number under 0 to a whole power and of anything to the power 0, `Atan2(0, 0)`, `SmoothStep` with its edges the wrong way around, a matrix's `Inverse`.
    - `tide_platform_shaders` runs each on the CPU and on the GPU, in a frag and in a vert, and compares (`Probe` in `platform/tests/shaders.tide`): the same number for integers, and floats within a tolerance. `compiler/tests/shaders/math` pins the functions' text in both languages.
  - What the code reads is what's sent (`param.reads`): a component's fields with each instance and a singleton's once for the draw, as words of float4s and int4s (`shader_value`), laid out the same in both languages: the instance's are vertex attributes after the vertex's own (a 3D mesh's transform first), and the draw's follow the engine's own (`tide_camera`, `tide_screen`) in one uniform block. What a frag reads of the instance, the vertex stage passes on, flat. A `local` field of a match component or singleton is read like any other (`param.local_reads`): its draws are handed the local part too (`shader_reads_local`).
  - Generated C has a `tide_shader` (`tide/draw.h`) for each, its sources as arrays of chars (C promises string literals of 4095 only), and a `tide_shade_<n>` that fills an instance and the uniforms from the view's components and singletons and calls `tide_draw_mesh_shaded` (`tide/mesh.h`). A view is handed what its frags and verts read that it doesn't take itself, as parameters codegen adds (`bind_views`), so they're gathered and blended like its own.
  - The draw list keeps such a mesh as its list's bytes (`TIDE_MESH_BYTES`, `tide_draw_mesh_fill_bytes`), and a run of instances through a shader as one `TIDE_DRAW_SHADED` command (`tide_draw_run`: its instances' bytes, its uniforms, its textures): instances drawn in a row with the same shader, uniforms and textures are one, which the platform draws in one instanced call. A 2D shader's `tide_camera` is the 2D camera's or the screen's, so its meshes land where the other 2D commands do, in order with them.
  - The platform makes a pipeline for a shader the first time it's drawn and finds it again by `tide_shader.id`, a hash of its sources, so a hot reload keeps the pipelines of shaders that didn't change: after `tide_draw_forget` it frees the ones the next frame doesn't draw. A lost device takes them all, and they're made again as lists draw.
  - `tide_platform_shaders` (`platform/tests/shaders.tide`, `shaders.c`) draws through frags and verts written in Tide and checks their pixels, the same on OpenGL 3.3 (label `gpu`), WebGL 2 and WebGPU, where the device is then lost and found again. It's also what makes every backend compile what tidec writes: a test page fails on a shader's warning (`Tide.checkGL`). A check of a blended sample allows what a byte holds: Mesa's software OpenGL, which is what Linux runs these on under a virtual display (`xvfb-run`; CI runs none of them), blends in bytes, so halfway between 0 and 255 is 127 or 128 there, where a GPU that blends in floats gives a half (`Halfway` in `shaders.tide`: three checks of an explicit `Texture` read both ways expected a half to a thousandth, and failed on Linux until 2026-10-07).
  - The list keeps a camera as one matrix from the world to clip space (`TIDE_DRAW_CAMERA_3D`); the platform fits the perspective one to the screen's shape. 3D meshes are the only commands that have depth, which they read and write unless `Draw.Depth` says otherwise (see Draw settings): everything else draws over what's there, in order, through its own camera, and a clear clears depth.
  - The list keeps 3D meshes as it keeps textures, by whose they are and a version (`tide_draw_mesh_slot`): a Tide list's from the hashes its heap keeps of its pages, C's from `tide_mesh.version`, and lists made along the way by what's in them, wherever they are in the scratch area. A mesh keeps its place in `meshes` while frames draw it, found through a hash table, and another version takes another place, so a mesh is never changed under a command that drew it. The platform keeps its GPU copy in the same place (`mesh_for` in `platform/src/platform.c`): its corners, each once, and its triangles as indices into them, of 16 bits where they fit.
  - Instances of one mesh drawn in a row are one command (`TIDE_DRAW_MESH_3D`, its transforms in the list's `matrices`), which the platform draws in one instanced call. So a view that runs for each entity draws thousands of them at once, and static scenery costs a hash per page a frame.
- **What a `uv` past a texture's edges reads is said where the texture is sampled**, as the filter is (agreed 2026-10-07: "sure, i dont have opinions here, seems grounded"; built the same day; the enum's name, its members and each rule are Claude's picks, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, `Wrap`): the built-in enum `Wrap`, with `Clamp` (what a call without one has, and what every draw did), `Repeat` and `Mirror`, one for both axes, after the filter wherever the filter is an argument: `Draw.Mesh(vertices, indices, texture, Filter.Point, Wrap.Repeat)`, `Draw.Mesh`, `Draw.Instanced`, a target's `Mesh` and `Instanced`, and a frag's or vert's `source.Sample(uv, Filter.Point, Wrap.Repeat)`. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **It's a sampler's, never a texture's or a shader's.** A wrap is no part of a shader's text (`compiler/tests/shaders/wrap` pins that), of a pipeline or of a texture's GPU copy: it's how one of a draw's textures is read, beside its filter. So a shader's `id` and its pipeline are the same whatever its wraps, and one grid or one target's color is clamped in one draw and repeated in the next.
  - **`gpu.h`:** `sample(slot, texture, blended, wrap)`, with `tide_gpu_wrap` (`TIDE_GPU_WRAP_CLAMP`, `_REPEAT`, `_MIRROR`), whose numbers are `tide_wrap`'s (a `_Static_assert` in `platform/src/platform.c`, where `gpu_wrap` makes what isn't one clamp). OpenGL's is six sampler objects, by filter and wrap (`sampler_for` in `gpu_gl.c`), with `GL_REPEAT` and `GL_MIRRORED_REPEAT` in `gl.h`: no function it didn't call, and no extension, as textures of any size wrap in OpenGL 3.3, OpenGL ES 3 and WebGL 2. WebGPU's is six samplers in `tide.js` (`addressModeU` and `addressModeV`), at 2 times the wrap's number plus whether it blends, which a bind group's key has for each texture: a change to the enum goes there, as `depths`' does. A texture's own parameters stay clamped and nearest; the unit's sampler stands in for them.
  - **The draw list:** a mesh's command has its wrap beside its filter (`tide_draw_command.mesh.wrap` and `mesh3.wrap`: the 32 bits `filter` had are 16 each, so a command is the size it was), and a run of meshes goes on while texture, filter and wrap are the same, in the list (`tide_draw_triangles_with`, `mesh_instances`) and in the platform's steps (`draw_step.wrap`). A shader's run says how each of its textures is sampled in one word, `tide_draw_run.sampling` (it was `blended`): bit i for texture i read blended, and the two bits from `TIDE_SAMPLING_WRAP` (8) + 2i its wrap (`tide_sampling_wrapped`, `tide_sampling_blended`, `tide_sampling_wrap`), so a word with only its low bits set clamps every texture, and the word generated C passes for a game with no wrap is the text it was.
  - **C:** `tide_wrap` (`tide/draw.h`), with `tide_wrap_of`, which makes any number one, as a Tide enum's value can be any. The functions that take a `tide_texture` have a twin that takes a wrap after the filter, and clamp themselves (`tide_draw_mesh_wrapped`, `tide_draw_triangles_wrapped`, `tide_draw_mesh_3d_wrapped`); the ones in steps, with what `tide_draw_texture_slot` gave, take it (`tide_draw_triangles_with`, `tide_draw_instance`, `tide_draw_instances`). Twins and not another parameter for the first three, as tests and games' C call those everywhere.
  - **Generated C** has the enum in a game's header only when its code names it (`prog->wrap`, `decl.named` in `find_in`, `enum_generated` in `compiler/src/codegen.c`, as `Depth`). A draw's filter and wrap are no arguments of a C function any more: they're put on the texture it's given (`tide_texture_filtered`, `tide_texture_wrapped`), which every textured draw then reads its sampling off (see `Texture` is the one type, below, which changed this on 2026-10-07: `tide/mesh.h`'s `_wrapped` twins and a shader function's `tide_wrap` parameter are gone).
  - **tidec:** the three textured mesh signatures (2D, 3D, 3D instances; one each since a grid is a `Texture` there) have two parameters a call can leave out (`sampled` in `compiler/src/builtins.c`), which editors show as `Filter filter = the texture's, Wrap wrap = the texture's`. `check_mesh_draw` says a wrap that isn't after a filter before either path takes the call (the engine's own vertices and a shader's), and a second one; a draw through a frag takes both too, as its texture's for that draw. `check_grid_sample` takes `uv, filter, wrap`, by name too, each of the last two written as an enum's member: the GPU is told before any pixel runs. In a frag or a vert, each filter and wrap a texture is read with is a texture of its draw's 8 (`shader_sample.wrap`, `shader_texture.wrap`), and the same way twice is one.
  - **The name is the engine's**, as `Blend`'s and `Depth`'s are: a game's own type or function called `Wrap` outside a namespace is an error. The asteroids sample's helper was one, and is `Around`.
  - Blended, a pixel at a texture's edge is blended with what the wrap says is past it. A render target's image is the other way up on OpenGL, which changes nothing: each wrap reads the same turned over.
  - Not built: a wrap for each axis and a border's color. (`gpu.h`'s `sample` takes a filter where this says `blended`, and the samplers are nine, since mipmaps: below.)
  - Tests: `compiler/tests/e2e/wrap` (what each draw records: the engine's vertices in 2D and 3D, instances, a target's color, into a target, a wrap as a value and of a number, a shader with no frag whose draws say one or none, a frag's and a vert's samples by their run's word, and C's functions); `compiler/tests/errors/gpu_sample_wrap*.tide`, `draw_mesh_wrap*.tide`, `draw_mesh_two_wraps.tide`, `texture_shader_draw_wrap.tide` and `wrap_*.tide`, one a diagnostic; `compiler/tests/shaders/wrap`; `lsp_wrap`; `wraps` in `platform/tests/draw.c` (each wrap's pixels with the nearest pixel and blended, before 0 and past 1, one texture read two ways in one draw through a shader, 3D meshes and their runs) and pass 7 of `platform/tests/shaders.tide` with `run_wrap_checks` in `shaders.c` (from Tide: mesh draws, frags, a vert, a target's color, a list's instances), the same pixels on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU, where the device is then lost and found again. The docs' `wrap` example shows the three (`docs/game/views.md`, Past the edges).
- **A texture's declaration can say how it's sampled** (agreed 2026-10-07, of `[Filter(Filter.Point), Wrap(Wrap.Repeat)]` before a `Texture` asset and a `Grid2<Color>` asset: "also add those attributes for both"; built the same day; where else they go and where a declaration's say ends are Claude's picks, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, Declared sampling). It's a default for where a draw or a `Sample` that names the texture leaves its filter or its wrap out, each apart. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **Where:** before an asset that's a `Texture` or a `Grid2<Color>`, alone or a glob's (`check_sampling_attribute` in `compiler/src/check.c`: `decl.sample_filter` and `sample_wrap`, 1 + the member's number), and before a field that's a `Grid2<Color>`, a `Grid2<float>` or a `RenderTarget`, for its color images (`check_field_sampling`, whose value stays in the field's attribute; the fields weren't asked for). An asset's are read with its declaration (`check_asset_decl`), before any code is checked, as the shaders are planned from them; `check_attributes` only says what a declaration that's no asset can't take. A `Texture` field refuses them with an error of its own: it holds whichever texture it's given, which says how it's sampled itself.
  - **It's the sampling the `Texture` made there is born with, which the value carries** (2026-10-07; until then it was resolved as code was compiled, by what a draw's texture expression named, and a `Texture` in a field had none). `declared_sampling` in `compiler/src/shader.c` still reads what an expression's declaration says (an asset by its name, one of its list or map, the variable of a `foreach` over it, a grid's field, a `RenderTarget` field's `.color` and `.colors[i]`), and codegen puts it into the value where the value is made (`declared_sampling_bits`): an asset `Texture`'s global as the pack is loaded (`tide_texture_sampled` in `tide_assets_load`, and `tide_pack_sample_textures` for each of a glob's, which the loader laid out), a grid where it converts (`tide_texture_of_grid`'s third argument, in `gen_as`), and a target's color where it's read (`tide_texture_sampled` around `tide_render_target_color`).
  - **A draw** reads its texture's sampling off the value (`tide_texture_filter`, `tide_texture_wrap`, in `engine/src/mesh.c` and in a shader's generated function), and what the call says is put on the value first (`gen_draw_texture` in `codegen.c`).
  - **A `Sample`** of an asset by name or of a grid's field has its declaration's filter and wrap worked into its binding (`ex_sample`), so it's the binding it would be had the `Sample` said them: that much is still known as the game is compiled. **A `Texture` that's no asset's by name** (a frag's own `Texture` parameter, a `Texture` field) has no declaration there: what its `Sample` leaves unsaid is a binding of its own (`shader_sample.filter_said`, `wrap_said`, part of a binding's key), and the shader's `tide_shade_<n>` works that texture's own filter and wrap into the run's `sampling` for those bindings (`tide_sampling_filter_of`, `tide_sampling_wrap_of`, of `tide_texture` or of the field). So a shader's text and its id are the same whatever its textures say, and two draws of one frag with textures sampled differently are two runs. `shader.sourced`, `wrapped` and `source_grid` are gone.
  - **Editors:** both attributes are offered in the brackets before a declaration and before a field, with hovers, and the enum member each names is an occurrence (the checker fills the attribute's `qname`).
  - A game with neither attribute generates the C it did, byte for byte (checked with the Texture assets, above).
  - Tests: `compiler/tests/e2e/asset_textures` (each place a declaration is named, a call that says one or both, a value through a field, a shader with no frag, samples by name, a frag's source from three kinds of draw, blits), `compiler/tests/shaders/declared`, `compiler/tests/errors/sampling_*.tide` (one a diagnostic), pass 10 of `tide_platform_shaders` for the pixels on each backend, and `lsp_asset_textures_and_declared_sampling`.
- **`Texture` is the one type a draw's texture and a `Sample` take** (item 3 of the reshaping, agreed 2026-10-07 and built the same day; that grids of colors and of floats convert, that a `Texture` carries its filter and wrap, and that `[Filter]` and `[Wrap]` are what one is born with are the owner's; each rule below is Claude's pick, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, Textures). `docs/game/views.md` (Textures, A Texture is a value, One channel, Saying it once) and `docs/game/shaders.md` (Textures) are the users' pages. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **The conversion is assignability** (`type_assignable` in `compiler/src/types.c`, with `type_is_texture_grid`: a `Grid2<Color>` or a `Grid2<float>` to `TY_TEXTURE`), so it's everywhere a value goes where another type is wanted, with no list of places: a mesh's texture, a blit's source, a `Texture` field, local, parameter and `return`. `gen_as` (`compiler/src/codegen.c`) writes it, `tide_texture_of_grid(grid, &shape, sampling)`. The match needs no rule: nothing there has anywhere a `Texture` goes (the errors rendering resources always gave). A grid that's no texture where one goes is said by `check_mesh_draw`, `check_blit` and `check_grid_sample`.
  - **One textured signature for each of the three mesh draws** (`builtins.c`), whose `arg_want` is `Texture`; a shaded draw's texture argument wants one too (`check_mesh_draw`), and so does a blit's source. `plan` (`compiler/src/shader.c`) no longer tells a draw given a grid from one given a `Texture`: a game that drew one vertex struct both ways has a shader fewer (`compiler/tests/shaders/render_sources` lost its second `Copy`).
  - **A draw gives a texture to a frag that takes one** (a grid too, which used to be an error that said the frag samples the grids it takes itself), and **a draw through a frag takes a filter and a wrap** (which used to be an error): they're its texture's for that draw, which the frag's `Sample`s that say nothing then read. `compiler/tests/errors/texture_shader_draw_filter` and `_wrap` went with that.
  - **A grid's `Texture` is a name, in the 48 bytes a `tide_texture_ref` always was** (`tide/draw.h`, `tide/mesh.h`): no pixels, no target, `image.height` `TIDE_TEXTURE_GRID`, and in `image.version` the grid's handle (its low 32 bits), its cells (1 colors, 2 floats) and its chunks' shifts. `tide_texture_grid` reads it back, and `tide_draw_texture_source` then calls `tide_draw_grid_texture`, as a grid's own draw always did: the list's copy, versioned by chunk hashes, nothing to upload or free.
  - **Sampling is 4 bits of the reference** (`tide_texture_sampling`: bit 0 for the nearest pixel, the wrap from bit 2), the top of `image.version` (`TIDE_TEXTURE_SAMPLING_SHIFT`, 60), free there for a target's reference (whose `image` is otherwise zero), a pack image's (whose name is masked to `TIDE_TEXTURE_NAME`: `texture_name` in `engine/src/pack_load.c`, `pack_slot` in `draw.c`) and a grid's. A reference to C's own pixels keeps all 64 bits of C's version, so its sampling is in `target.size.scale`, as a whole number, which nothing reads where there's no target. So a zeroed reference, and one C makes with nothing said, are blended and clamped. A `tide_texture_ref` is compared or hashed nowhere; code that starts to masks the sampling.
  - **`tide/mesh.h` has one function for each textured draw** (`tide_draw_mesh_texture`, `tide_draw_mesh3_texture`, `tide_draw_mesh3_instances_texture`), which take the reference and nothing else. The grid ones and every `_wrapped` twin there are gone.
  - **A shader's function takes one `tide_texture_ref tide_texture`** where its draw gives a texture, and no filter, wrap or shape: `gen_shaders` writes each texture's source as before and the run's `sampling` as a constant for what's known as the game compiles, or'd with `tide_sampling_filter_of` and `tide_sampling_wrap_of` of the reference for what isn't (the draw's own texture in a shader with no frag: both; a `Sample` of a `Texture` parameter or field: what it leaves unsaid).
  - **Nothing ties a grid's `Texture` to the grid's lifetime**, and the heap's handles are offsets, so `tide_draw_grid_texture` trusts nothing a handle names: the directory, its records and each chunk are read through `span` (`engine/src/mesh.c`), which gives bytes only where they're all within one page of the heap (`tide_pack_span` for the pack's region), the shape's shifts are checked before a chunk's size is worked out (shifts of 15 made a chunk of 0 bytes and passed, which `compiler/tests/e2e/textures` found), a chunk's place is worked out 64 bits wide, and the directory has to be one a grid of that size could have (its chunk counts, and no more records than chunks). A side past `TIDE_GRID_TEXTURE_MOST` (16384) is no texture, which also keeps bytes that aren't a grid's from asking for gigabytes. A stale reference draws nothing or anything. It's part of what No Tide code can corrupt memory rests on (see Language): a read path that's added for a grid named by a `Texture` goes through `span`.
  - **A `Grid2<float>` is `TIDE_TEXELS_HALF` in the draw list** (`tide_draw_texture.format`, `tide_draw_texture_slot_as`: 2 bytes a pixel) and `TIDE_GPU_R16F` on the GPU (`platform/src/gpu.h`; `GL_R16F` with `GL_HALF_FLOAT` in `gpu_gl.c` and `gl.h`, whose WebGL upload takes a `Uint16Array`: `pixels` in `tide.js`; `r16float` in `tide.js`'s `gpu_texture`, by `tide_gpu_format`'s numbers). Picked over 32 bits because every backend blends a half between pixels with nothing asked, where a float of 32 needs `OES_texture_float_linear` on WebGL 2 and `float32-filterable` on WebGPU, whose bind group layouts would then depend on the texture a frag is given. So no device falls back. `tide_half_from_float` (`mesh.c`) rounds to the nearest, ties to even, with 65504 for what's past it and for an infinity, and 0 for NaN. A shader reads it as `(r, 0, 0, 1)`, which is what each backend gives a one-channel texture: no shader text depends on it. The platform's texture cache tells a texture by its format too (`gpu_texture.format` in `platform.c`).
  - **A hot reload keeps a `Texture` of the pack's or of a grid's**, and a target's with its sampling (`gen_resource_fields`): only one of C's own pixels, or of a target that isn't the game's, is zeroed. (It zeroed the pack's too before, which nobody had noticed.) After a migration to another layout a grid's `Texture` may name another place; nothing clears it.
  - **Generated C:** 20 of 246 games differ from what the compiler before wrote (old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without), all in their `.c` and none in a header, so no game's hash moved: textured draws convert their grid at the argument and call the one function, shader functions take one reference and work their sampling out, and reloads carry sampling. The 224 others are byte for byte what they were.
  - **Not built:** a way to change a `Texture`'s sampling in Tide outside a draw; a `Texture`'s size; grids of other cells and `Grid3` as textures; a `Grid2<float>` asset; clearing a grid's `Texture` when its grid goes or when a reload migrates; a live docs example of a float grid.
  - **Tests:** `compiler/tests/e2e/textures` (a grid as a texture drawn, kept, passed, returned, in 3D, instanced and into a target; sampling carried from a grid's field and a target's, and a draw's over it; frags whose runs carry it, one shader in three runs; a float grid's halves and the conversion's rounding; every offset of a heap, and past it, as a grid's handle), natively and under Node; `compiler/tests/e2e/asset_textures` (a kept asset draws as declared, in a mesh and in a frag); pass 12 of `platform/tests/shaders.tide` with `run_height_checks` in `shaders.c` (a float grid in a frag, with no frag, blended, numbers past 1 and under 0, a kept `Texture` with and without the draw's wrap) and pass 10's kept image, the same pixels on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU, where the device is then lost and found; `compiler/tests/errors/` for the messages that changed; `lsp_wrap`, `lsp_meshes`, `lsp_asset_textures_and_declared_sampling`. Not run: Android, and OpenGL on a Mac.
- **Mipmaps are a filter, `Filter.Trilinear`** (item 6 of the reshaping, built 2026-10-07; that the drawing side gets mipmaps and that the card designs how a mipmapped sample is written where code samples in a branch are the owner's; each rule is Claude's pick, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, Mipmaps, with the options that were weighed). `docs/game/views.md` (Drawn smaller) and `docs/game/shaders.md` (Textures) are the users' pages. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **The filter's numbers**: `tide_filter` has `TIDE_FILTER_TRILINEAR` (2) after point and bilinear, with `tide_filter_of`, which makes any number one (what's none is bilinear). A mesh command's `filter` is that number, as it was. A texture reference's sampling has bit 1 for it beside bit 0 for the nearest pixel (`tide_sampling_bits`, `tide_texture_filter`; `tide_texture_filtered` replaces both and `tide_texture_wrapped` keeps both), so a zeroed reference is still blended. A shader run's `sampling` has bit `TIDE_SAMPLING_MIPS` (24) + i for texture i, set with its blended bit i (`tide_sampling_filtered`, `tide_sampling_filter`), so the word generated C writes for a game with no Trilinear is the text it was, and code that only asks `tide_sampling_blended` reads it as blended.
  - **`gpu.h`:** `sample(slot, texture, filter, wrap)` takes a `tide_gpu_filter` (`_NEAREST`, `_LINEAR`, `_MIPMAPS`, with `tide_filter`'s numbers by a `_Static_assert` in `gpu_filter`, `platform/src/platform.c`) where it took a bool, and `mipmaps(texture)` makes a texture's smaller images from its pixels as they are, outside a pass, for an uploaded texture and for a target's color image alike. Nothing keeps them up to date but calling it again.
  - **OpenGL** (`gpu_gl.c`): nine sampler objects, by filter and wrap, the mipmapped ones `GL_LINEAR_MIPMAP_LINEAR` drawn smaller and `GL_LINEAR` drawn bigger; `glGenerateMipmap` (in `gl.h` and `tide.js`, the one function this added). A texture with only its own image read through a sampler that reads the smaller ones is incomplete, and reads as black, so `gl_sample` gives such a texture the blended sampler until `gl_mipmaps` has run for it (`gl_texture.mipped`). OpenGL makes the images by drawing, so of a float texture only where it draws into floats (`gl_float_targets`): elsewhere a `Grid2<float>` has none, and is read blended.
  - **WebGPU** (`tide.js`): nine samplers at 3 times the wrap's number plus the filter's (a change to either enum goes there), the six that aren't mipmapped with `lodMaxClamp: 0`, so they keep to a texture's own pixels whatever it has and however a shader asks, which is what keeps every draw that isn't Trilinear the pixels it was. A WebGPU texture has as many images as it was made with, so the first `gpu_mipmaps` of one makes another with room for them, copies its pixels over, lets the bind groups that had it go, and keeps `first` (its own image, which a pass on a target draws into: a view of every level is no attachment) beside `view` (all of it, which draws sample). The levels are drawn one from the other by a pipeline for each format (`mipPipeline`: one sample between four pixels, blended), in one submit.
  - **The renderer makes them only when a draw reads between them and they're stale** (`prepare_mipmaps`, `prepare_step_mipmaps`, in the step loop after `prepare_source_targets`, with the pass ended around it as for that): an uploaded texture's once for each upload (`gpu_texture.mipped`, cleared in `texture_for`), and a target's color image's when a pass has begun on the target since they were made (`gpu_target.drawn`, counted where the loop begins a pass on it and where its image is made, against `mipped[image]`). So a texture never read so has none, one that doesn't change makes them once, and a frame with no Trilinear draw pays a comparison a step. `bench/results/draw.jsonl` (`mipmaps-before`, `mipmaps`) has the bench either side: nothing moved.
  - **The engine's own mesh shaders didn't change**: they already let the GPU pick (`texture()`, `textureSample`), in `main`, before any branch.
  - **In a frag, tidec asks for a Sample's derivatives as the frag starts, and reads with them** (`ex_sample`, `liftable`, `known_filter` in `compiler/src/shader.c`): a GPU only says how fast a `uv` changes outside branches and loops, and Tide samples anywhere. For each Sample that may read between smaller images, the frag's function begins with `tide_uv<n>` (the uv worked out a first time), `tide_dx<n>` and `tide_dy<n>` (`dFdx`/`dFdy`, `dpdx`/`dpdy`), collected in `emit.grads` while its body is written and put between its head and its body (`need_function`), and the Sample is `textureGrad` or `textureSampleGrad`. The uv is written that first time with `emit.lifting` set, where a local is written as the value it was declared with (`ex_name`). `compiler/tests/shaders/mipmaps` pins both languages.
  - **Which Samples**: one whose filter is known to be Trilinear (said there, or its grid's or asset's declaration's), and one that leaves its filter to the Texture it's given (`!filter_said`) in a game that names `Filter.Trilinear` (`program.names_trilinear`). Every other is `textureLod(..., 0.0)` as before, and so is every Sample in a vert.
  - **What a uv can be made of is `liftable`'s to say**: the frag's parameters that aren't `mut` or `out`, constants, members and swizzles of those, operators, `?:`, struct literals, constructors, the engine's functions, a game's functions that take nothing `mut`, `out` or the `Pixel` (which could discard), a texture read with a filter known not to be Trilinear, and locals that aren't `mut`, a loop's, an `out` argument's or assigned again, whose own values are so. Anything else isn't.
