Where the filter is known, that's an error at the uv (`compiler/tests/errors/gpu_sample_trilinear_*.tide`); where it's the texture's to say, the Sample reads level 0. **What `liftable` allows is written twice in the shader, so it has to change nothing**: an expression kind that joins it is one with no side effect.
  - **`program.names_trilinear`** is set where a `Filter.Trilinear` member is resolved (`check_member`'s enum path, and an asset's `[Filter]` in `check_sampling_attribute`), never by `Filter(2)`. `codegen` hides the enum's third member in a game that doesn't name it (its count is cut to 2 as codegen starts, so tidels still offers it), and the shader writer goes by the same flag: such a game's generated C and shaders are what they were, byte for byte (old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without: 209 games, none differs). A shader's `filter` (`shader_sample.filter`, `shader_texture.filter`: 0, 1 or 2, where they had a bool) is a binding's key, as it was.
  - **Not built:** anisotropy (a sampler's setting on each backend; left until something wants it); a level or a bias a game picks; a Trilinear Sample at a uv a loop makes; mipmaps an asset brings, or made at build time; Duel's blur doesn't use it.
  - **Tests:** `mipmaps` in `platform/tests/draw.c` (a texture of stripes drawn an eighth and a quarter of its size reads as the mean of what each pixel covers, the same texture read bilinear beside it in the same frame doesn't, its own size is its own pixels, a 3D mesh's, and other pixels make them again), pass 14 of `platform/tests/shaders.tide` with `run_mipmap_checks` in `shaders.c` (from Tide: a declared grid sampled in a branch, a carried filter after a discard, one frag in runs read two ways, a Sample that says it, a uv a loop changes, meshes with no frag and with a vert alone, a grid of floats, and a target's color made again as it's drawn into), the same pixels on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU, where the device is then lost and found; `compiler/tests/e2e/mipmaps` (what each draw records, natively and under Node); `compiler/tests/shaders/mipmaps`; the two error tests; `lsp_trilinear`. Not run: Android, and OpenGL on a Mac.
- Nothing but a render target (below) holds a GPU texture: a mesh names pixels where the game keeps them, and every layer keeps a copy it can make again. An image of the game's pack (an `asset Texture`: see Assets, under Tech stack) is the one whose pixels the draw list doesn't copy: it draws them where the pack has them.
  - The draw list copies a texture's pixels when they aren't what it has (`tide_draw_texture_slot`: whose they are, and a version), and the platform uploads from that copy (`texture_for` in `platform/src/platform.c`). Each lets a texture go once a frame draws without it.
  - Tide's own pixels are grids, `Grid2<Color>` and `Grid2<float>` (`tide/mesh.h`), local or the match's, which are `Texture`s where one is wanted (see `Texture` is the one type, below). Their version is a hash of their chunks' page hashes, which a heap keeps until a page changes (`tide_heap_block_hash`), so an unchanged grid costs a hash per chunk, not its cells. Asking counts as a move of the heap, so a chunk cache that held the page looks it up again and says it changed.
  - C's are `tide_texture`: its own pixels, and a version it changes when they do.
  - So reloads carry nothing. On the web a new program uploads again. On desktop the host has the list forget its copies when it swaps the game's code (`tide_draw_forget`, whose epoch the platform checks), as the new build's C may name other pixels at the same address and version.
- Render targets are the exception: images the GPU alone holds, local to a machine. `RenderTarget(width, height)` makes one, `target.Resize(width, height)` changes its size and discards what's in it, `target.color` is its `Texture` and `target.depth` its `DepthTexture`, and `target.Dispose()` lets it go, explicitly. Copies share the image, and its color and depth stay from frame to frame until they're cleared. A target's own drawing methods (`target.Clear`, `target.Mesh` and the rest) draw into it; `Draw`'s own calls, the GUI and the stats overlay draw into `Draw.window`. `Draw.Blit(source, destination[, blend], frag)` runs a frag over the whole destination, replacing its color (or going over it as its own `blend` says) and keeping its depth whatever the camera, the clip and the other draw settings are, with the source as the frag's read-only `Texture` parameter: a target's color or a `Grid2<Color>`, never the destination's own, which is an error. Tide code can't read a target's pixels back, so a hot reload and a lost device make its image again, empty. `tide_platform_targets` (`platform/tests/targets.c`) checks targets on each backend, at `gpu.h`.
- A target's size is in logical pixels, like the window's, at the display's scale (agreed 2026-10-05; `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing): drawing into one is the same as drawing into the window. `RenderTarget(width, height, pixelsPerUnit)` has pixels per unit of its own (`RenderTarget(320, 180, 1)` is exactly those pixels on any display), and `RenderTarget.Window()` and `RenderTarget.Window(factor)` are always the window's size, or that part of it: made again, empty, when the window's size or the display's scale changes, and never resized by the game. Nothing reads the display's scale (no `Screen.scale`). What the design left open is picked and Provisional there: sizes are floats, an image has the nearest whole number of pixels, `Resize` on a target that follows the window is a diagnostic, and an image past the GPU's largest texture keeps its shape with fewer pixels.
  - A size is a `tide_target_size` (`tide/draw.h`): a width and a height in logical pixels, `scale` (the image's pixels in one, 0 for the display's) and `window` (above 0: the part of the window's size it always is). A `tide_render_target` is an id and one, a managed target's state has one, and the draw list records one with each destination and each target it samples (`tide_draw_target_data.size`, `tide_draw_texture.target`), so a list says nothing in pixels. The engine only knows logical sizes: `tide_target_size_logical` is what the conversions and `MeasureText` use, with the window's size for a target that follows it.
  - The platform works a target's pixels out as a list is drawn (`target_extent` in `platform/src/platform.c`), under the draw's root (`draw_root`): what the list's window commands go to, with a size in logical pixels and one in its own. The root's pixels over its logical size are the display's scale for that draw: the window's, a headless run's `--scale`, or 1 for `tide_platform_read_pixels`. A target that follows the window has the root's own pixels times its factor, so `RenderTarget.Window()` is the window's pixels exactly. A destination's logical size and pixels are then all the renderer goes by (`draw_width`, `draw_pixel_width`, `pixel_scale`), the window's or a target's alike: text fitting, circles drawn as smooth shapes where the scale isn't 1, clips, and a shader's `tide_screen`.
  - The GPU's copy of a target is made again when its image comes to another number of pixels, as when its version changes (`target_for`, and `prepare_source_target` for one that's only sampled): that's all that happens when the window is resized or moves to another display. `tide_gpu.target_limit` is the most pixels a side an image has (OpenGL's largest texture, WebGPU's `maxTextureDimension2D`).
  - Text is measured where it's drawn: `tide_measure_fn` takes the target's `tide_target_size`, or NULL for the window, and `tide_platform_measure_text` works its pixels out as drawing does.
  - `tide_platform_read_pixels_as(list, width, height, scale, ...)` draws a list as a window of that size would on a display of that scale, and reads its pixels: how tests see a scaled display and a window that changes size on any machine and every backend, with no window resized. `tide_platform_targets` draws a pattern one real pixel wide into a screen target at scales from 1 to 3 and expects it back pixel for pixel, checks that a scene drawn into a screen target is the window's pixels (text, circles and smooth shapes), that a target with pixels of its own keeps them and its contents on any display, and that the others are made again when the scale or the window's size changes; `tide_platform_shaders` does it from Tide (`RenderTarget.Window`, `RenderTarget(4, 4, 1)`, a frag's `pixel.position`).
  - Hot reload keeps a target's size, its scale and whether it follows the window: they're in the `tide_render_target` values the local state holds, which `tide_render_target_recreate` makes a web build's targets from.
- **A target says what it holds when it's made, and a frag reads a target's depth** (built 2026-10-07; what's agreed is in `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, and the picks made to build it under its Provisional, Target formats and depth: `Format`, its members, where it's written, `DepthTexture` and its two reads' names and rules). What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **Tide:** `RenderTarget(width, height[, pixelsPerUnit][, format])` and `RenderTarget.Window([factor][, format])`, with the built-in enum `Format { Rgba8, Depth }`; `target.depth`, a `DepthTexture`, beside `target.color`; and in a frag or a vert, `depth.Sample(uv)`, a float, and `depth.Compare(uv, value)`, where the depth is a field of a component or a singleton the code takes, as a grid is. `docs/game/views.md` (What a target holds) and `docs/game/shaders.md` (A target's depth, with a live shadow map: `docs/examples/shadows.tide`) are the users' pages.
  - **What a target holds is in its id, never its size:** a managed target's id is its state's address with `TIDE_RENDER_MANAGED`, and what it holds in the bits the address leaves free (`TIDE_RENDER_FORMAT`; `managed_id` in `engine/src/draw.c`). They were three, for one format, fourteen once targets held several color images, which is where their layout is said (below), and are sixteen since a target says its samples (below that). `tide_render_target_formats(id)` reads them (`tide_render_target_format(id)` is the first image's, or depth alone), and `tide_render_target_state_of` masks them off. It's there, and not a field of `tide_target_size`, because `tide_render_target` is 24 bytes in every game's local state that has one (`type_layout` in `compiler/src/codegen.c`), and a field more would have changed those games' generated headers. So everything that carries a target's id carries what it holds, with no field of its own: the draw list's destinations and textures, the platform's `target_for`, and the value another build is handed to make the target again (`tide_render_target_recreate`, which takes the format from the id it can't read as an address: a hot reload keeps it, natively and on the web). A borrowed target (an id of C's own, without the managed bit) holds color: C that wants depth alone makes a managed one (`tide_render_target_make_as` and the three beside it). A format that's none of the enum's is color.
  - **A target's depth is a texture of the draw list's, of a space of its own:** `TIDE_PIXELS_TARGET_DEPTH` beside `TIDE_PIXELS_TARGET` (`tide_draw_depth_source`, `tide_draw_target_depth_sized`; `tide_depth_ref` is Tide's `DepthTexture`, a target's reference and nothing else). A shader says how it reads each of its textures (`tide_shader.texture_reads`: a color, a depth, a depth compared), and the renderer only hands a texture to a read of its kind (`texture_to_read` in `platform/src/platform.c`): a depth that's read with none given, or with a color given, is the far one (`gpu_state.far`, a target of depth alone of one pixel, made the first time a list draws through a shader that reads a depth), and a color read that's given a depth is the white pixel.
  - **`gpu.h`:** `target(format, width, height)` with `TIDE_GPU_TARGET_COLOR` or `TIDE_GPU_TARGET_DEPTH` (two of the formats there are since: see A target holds several color images, below), `target_depth(target)` beside `target_texture(target, color)`, a pipeline's `reads` (a `tide_gpu_read` for each texture: `TIDE_GPU_READ_COLOR`, `_DEPTH`, `_COMPARE`) and `into`, the kind of target it draws into: a pipeline is for one kind. `sample` comes after `use`, as how a texture is sampled is the pipeline's to say where it's a depth.
  - **Every target's depth is a texture now, with 8 bits of stencil beside it** (which draws test and store since: see Stencil is a setting too, under the draw settings): OpenGL's is `GL_DEPTH24_STENCIL8`, attached at `GL_DEPTH_STENCIL_ATTACHMENT`, where it was a renderbuffer, so `gl.h` and `tide.js` have no renderbuffer functions any more; WebGPU's is `depth24plus-stencil8` for every target, the canvas's too (a pipeline names one depth format), sampled through a view of its depth alone. The window's own framebuffer on OpenGL has stencil too, asked for on each platform since the stencil card.
  - **A target of depth alone has no color attachment.** OpenGL 3.3 wants that said (`glDrawBuffers(GL_NONE)`, `glReadBuffer(GL_NONE)`, both in `gl.h` and `tide.js` now), and the same program draws into it. WebGPU's pass has no color attachment and its pipelines no color target (`fragment.targets: []`), which is what `into` is for: the fragment stage still runs, as it may discard, and what it returns goes nowhere. So the renderer has a second pipeline of what draws there, as it has one for each kind of target: the clears' (`clear_pipeline`), the 3D meshes' (`engine_pipeline` with `into` `TIDE_GPU_TARGET_DEPTH`, one for each side they leave out: a lamp's image is drawn from the side its target's `Cull` says), and a game shader's (`shader_pipeline.into`, part of its key, with its cull).
  - **Only what writes depth is drawn into one, or stores stencil** (`writes_depth`, `leaves_nothing` in `platform/src/platform.c`): a clear, a 3D mesh or a 3D shaded run under `TIDE_DEPTH_READ_WRITE`, and any step whose stencil operation isn't `Keep`. Every other step is passed by there, with nothing said: shapes, text, 2D meshes, blits, meshes that only read depth. A frag is never skipped where it could be (one that can't discard, into depth alone): a backend may, some day.
  - **How each backend reads a depth.** As a number: a `sampler2D` whose red is the depth (`uniform highp`, as a sampler's own precision is the lowest there is on OpenGL ES) through the nearest sampler, and `texture_depth_2d` with a `sampler` bound as non-filtering: a depth is only whole at its nearest pixel on OpenGL ES 3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU alike, which is why `Sample` takes no `Filter`. Compared: `sampler2DShadow` through a sampler object with `GL_COMPARE_REF_TO_TEXTURE` and `GL_LEQUAL`, linear; `texture_depth_2d` with a `sampler_comparison` (`less-equal`, linear) and `textureSampleCompareLevel`. tidec clamps what's compared to 0..1 itself (`ex_sample` in `compiler/src/shader.c`): past them, GPUs differ. Both are read at the one level, so in any branch and in a vert. WebGPU's bind group layouts are by how a pipeline reads each texture (`layoutFor` in `tide.js`), made the first time one reads so.
  - **Clip space, depth and rows are the same on each:** a depth is `(z / w + 1) / 2` of OpenGL's clip space on all three (WGSL's vertex stage already takes OpenGL's z to WebGPU's), and a target's depth is the same way up as its color, as OpenGL's target passes are flipped as they're drawn.
  - **A draw into a target that reads that target's depth is passed by,** as one that reads its color is (`samples_destination`, with the same line on stderr; `tide_draw_blit` refuses as it records), and so is a draw that samples the color of a target of depth alone (`unavailable_texture`).
  - **tidec:** `TY_DEPTH_TEXTURE` (`tide_depth_ref`, 24 bytes), which is a rendering resource wherever `Texture` is one (`holds_render_resource`, the reload's rebinding in `gen_resource_fields`); `check_depth_read` for the two reads; `shader_sample.read` and `shader_texture.read`, so a depth that's both sampled and compared is two of the shader's textures; `Format` is in generated C only in a game that names it, as `Depth` is (`enum_generated`), and a shader's `.texture_reads` only where a texture isn't a color. A game that uses none of it generates the C it did, byte for byte: old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without (237 games; only the three that use it differ: the shaders test, `render_depth` and the docs' `shadows`).
  - **Not built:** a `DepthTexture` as a draw's own source or a frag's own parameter (an error that says where it goes); skipping a frag that can't discard.
  - **Tests:** `tide_platform_targets` (`platform/tests/targets.c`): at `gpu.h`, a target of depth alone drawn into column by column and read back as a number, compared at pixels and between them, kept and tested across passes, a color target's depth, a clear; and through the renderer, what draws into depth alone and what doesn't, a frag that discards there, the same way up as color, a target's own depth refused, the far limit where none is given, a screen target of depth alone at three scales, and a forgotten list. `tide_platform_shaders` (pass 7 of `shaders.tide`, `run_shadow_checks`): a shadow map written in Tide, casters through a slanted lamp's `float4x4.Ortho` camera, one through a frag that discards, a lit frag that compares, and the stored depth read as a number, at three display scales. Both on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU, where the device is then lost and found. `draw_targets_say_what_they_hold_and_lend_their_depth` (`tests/test_draw.c`) for the list and the id; `compiler/tests/e2e/render_depth` for what tidec generates, a reload in the same memory and in other memory among it; `compiler/tests/shaders/depth` pins the GLSL and WGSL; `compiler/tests/errors/render_depth_*` and `render_target_format_*`, a file a diagnostic; `lsp_render_target_formats_and_depth`. Not run: Android (its apps of these tests are started by hand), and OpenGL on a Mac, which nothing runs.
- **A target holds several color images, a frag writes them all, and an image can be floats** (built 2026-10-07. The owner picked each of three, asked that day: a format for each color image after a target's size, four at the most; a frag's `out Color` parameters to them by their order, each image read as `target.colors[i]`, an output with no image in its place dropped and an image with no output left as it was; and float images wherever a GPU draws into them, with 4 bytes a pixel and a line that says so where one doesn't. The rest is Claude's picks, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, Several colors and float images). What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **Tide:** `RenderTarget(width, height[, pixelsPerUnit], format...)` and `RenderTarget.Window([factor], format...)`, a `Format` for each color image; `Format.Rgba16f`, the enum's third member, beside `Rgba8` and `Depth`; `target.colors[i]`, a `Texture`, with `target.color` the first; and a frag with up to four `out Color` parameters. `docs/game/views.md` (What a target holds, Several colors) and `docs/game/shaders.md` (Several outputs) are the users' pages.
  - **What a target holds is still its id's, in more bits.** The low 14: 3 for each of four color images in order, 1 + its `tide_target_format` and 0 past the last, so none at all is a target of depth alone (`TIDE_RENDER_FORMAT`); then 2 that only a texture's reference has, for which image it names (`TIDE_RENDER_IMAGE`). Three bits a format, where two would hold the two there are, for the formats to come. So a target's state was on a boundary of 16 KiB (`TIDE_RENDER_BITS + 1`), and is on one of 64 KiB since its samples took two bits more (A target's samples, below): `make_target` allocates that much more than the state and keeps what it allocated in `tide_render_target_state.memory`, which is what's freed, by the build that allocated it or by one that adopted the state. `tide_render_target` is the 24 bytes it was, and a game's header says nothing of any of it. A test that makes a state by hand makes it on that boundary (`foreign_state` in `tests/test_draw.c`).
  - **A target's own id and an image's are told apart where it matters:** `tide_render_target_whole(id)` is the target's, which a destination, the renderer's image of a target (`find_target`, `target_for`) and a comparison of two targets go by (a blit that samples its destination, `samples_destination`), and `tide_render_target_image(id)` which image a texture's reference names. The draw list's textures keep the id as the reference has it, so a target's images are textures apart (`TIDE_PIXELS_TARGET`, by id). Both leave a borrowed id alone (an id of C's own, without the managed bit, which holds one color image): its bits are C's.
  - **An image a target hasn't got is still named** (`tide_render_target_color_at`: any of the four; past them, or of the window or a borrowed target, it's `TIDE_RENDER_INVALID`), and the renderer passes by a draw that samples it, with a line (`unavailable_texture`), as it does for the color of a target of depth alone: the target says what it has, and tidec can't follow a `Texture` to a target.
  - **A hot reload keeps all of it with no new generated code.** `tide_local_rebind_resources` is the C it was: for a `Texture` field, `tide_render_target_color(tide_render_target_recreate(ref.target))`. So `tide_render_target_recreate` makes the target from the formats in the id it's handed and gives back an id with that id's image bits, and `tide_render_target_color` of a value whose id names an image is that image again. A `RenderTarget` value's id never has them.
  - **C:** `tide_render_target_make_of(width, height, formats, count)` and the three beside it; the four `_as` there were are one format, as ever, `TIDE_TARGET_RGBA16F` among them. Among several, a format that's no color's (`TIDE_TARGET_DEPTH`, which tidec refuses where it's written, or a number that's none) is `TIDE_TARGET_RGBA8`, with a line for the first. `tide_shader.outputs` is how many colors a shader's fragment stage writes (`tide_shader_outputs`: one for 0, so a shader made by hand says nothing).
  - **Generated C is what it was, byte for byte, for a game that doesn't name `Format`** (old and new tidec over every game's source list, 239 of them, with `--layout` and without). The ones that do (the shaders test, `render_depth`, the docs' `shadows`, and Sumo since its shadow map) have the enum's third member in their header, and with it another `tide_game` hash, and nothing else. One format calls what it called; several are an array in the call, `(const tide_target_format[]){...}, n` (`gen_target_format` in `compiler/src/codegen.c`, by `expr.target_formats`); `target.colors[i]` is `tide_render_target_color_at`; and a shader says `.outputs` only when its frag has more than one.
  - **tidec:** `target_formats` (`compiler/src/check.c`) counts a constructor's trailing formats, and refuses a fifth and `Format.Depth` written among several. `target.colors` is only an index's object (`checker.colors_indexed`, as `device_whole_ok` is for a device's `touches`; `expr.target_colors`): alone, counted or looped over, it's an error that says to read one. `check_writable` refuses an assignment to `target.color`, `target.colors[i]` and `target.depth`: before, the first and the last compiled, to C that didn't. `check_shader_decl` counts a frag's `out Color`s (`decl.outputs`, `param.output`), and `compiler/src/assigned.c` names an output after the first where a path doesn't write it ("...without writing 'glow', its second output"): the first keeps the message it had.
  - **`compiler/src/shader.c`:** a frag with one output is written as it was, to the byte, so no shader's id moved (`compiler/tests/shaders/` but for the new `outputs`). With several, GLSL has `layout(location = k) out vec4 tide_pixel<k>` for each and WGSL a `tide_colors` struct its `fragment` returns, each a `tide_color<k>` the frag's function is handed in its parameters' order (`output_name`, `shader_outputs`), and each goes through what the draw finishes a color with: its tint, and what a draw that multiplies needs.
  - **`gpu.h`:** `tide_gpu_target_format` is a number, 4 bits for each color image in order (`tide_gpu_color`: bytes or floats) and 0 past the last, so `TIDE_GPU_TARGET_DEPTH` is 0 and `TIDE_GPU_TARGET_COLOR` 1, which is the window's kind too. **A pipeline description has to say `into`:** 0 is depth alone now, where it was color. One used in a pass on another kind of target stops the program on OpenGL too, naming it (`gl_use`), as WebGPU refuses it: OpenGL would have drawn all the same, and only the web's tests would have said (the stencil tests' own descriptions did, as the two were merged). `outputs` is how many colors its fragment shader writes (one for 0); `target_texture(target, color)` takes which image; `float_targets()` says whether a float image is one; `target_read` gives a target's first image where that's bytes, and zeros otherwise.
  - **A pipeline's outputs go to a target's images in order, and the rest of either is nobody's.** OpenGL: `gl_use` sets the bound target's draw buffers to its first images, as many as the pipeline has outputs, and `GL_NONE` for the others (`draw_into` in `gpu_gl.c`, only for a target of several, and only when it changes): an image with no output would get anything at all, and WebGL refuses the draw. A pass that starts cleared names them all first, as a clear reaches the buffers that are named. WebGPU: a pipeline's `fragment.targets` are the target's formats, with `writeMask: 0` past its outputs (`colorFormats` in `tide.js`: a change to `tide_gpu_color` goes there); a shader with more outputs than its pipeline has targets is fine by it, which the tests draw. One blend for all of a draw's outputs: OpenGL ES 3 and WebGL 2 have no other.
  - **A float image is `GL_RGBA16F` and `rgba16float`:** four floats of 16 bits, which every backend that has them draws into, blends and reads blended between pixels. OpenGL 3.3, OpenGL ES 3.2 and WebGPU have them; an older OpenGL ES 3 and WebGL 2 where the extension is there, which is nearly everywhere (99.7% of WebGL 2 by web3dsurvey, 2026-10). It adds no function: `tide.js` asks WebGL for `EXT_color_buffer_float`, or `EXT_color_buffer_half_float`, as it makes the context, and `gl_float_targets` finds out by making a framebuffer of one, once. Without it a float image is made as bytes, `gl_target_make` says so once on stderr, and nothing else differs. `tide_gpu_gl_no_floats` has OpenGL make targets that way while it's set, for tests.
  - **The renderer's pipelines are for a kind of target, which is what it holds** (`frame_target.into`, from `gpu_target_format`): the engine's own in `engine_made`, a set for each kind a list draws into (`pipelines_into`; the window's kind first), the ones that test or store stencil in their list by it too (`stencil_pipeline.into`), and a game shader's by `shader_pipeline.into`, where each had a flag for depth alone. The engine's own draw one color, into a target's first image. A clear of a target of several goes through a shader of four outputs (`clear_all_glsl_fragment`, `clear_all_wgsl` in `platform/src/shaders.h`), the same color into each.
  - **Not built:** a float of 32 bits and images of one or two channels (WebGPU neither blends nor filters 32-bit floats without features a device may lack); an output of another type than `Color`; a blend for each output; a clear's color past 1, or one for each image (a clear's color is bytes on its way to the renderer); a game asking whether float images are floats; `colors.count`. All Open in `docs/spec.md`.
  - **Tests:** `draw_targets_hold_several_colors_and_lend_each` (`tests/test_draw.c`) for ids, references, the list and another build's; `check_colors` in `platform/tests/targets.c` at `gpu.h` (three outputs into three images, one into three, three into one, floats that add up, what's read back, and OpenGL as a device with no floats), and `check_render_colors` through the renderer (the engine's own draws, a clear, blits of two outputs and of one, a target's own second image refused, one it hasn't got, a forgotten list, and the fallback again); pass 11 of `platform/tests/shaders.tide` with `run_output_checks` in `shaders.c` (frags of three outputs, two and one written in Tide, one that discards, a shape of the engine's, each image read back by a frag, a float image added into and read blended), the same pixels on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU, where the device is then lost and found; `compiler/tests/e2e/render_colors` for what tidec generates, a reload in the same memory and in other memory among it; `compiler/tests/shaders/outputs` pins the GLSL and WGSL; `compiler/tests/errors/render_target_format*`, `render_target_colors_*`, `render_target_*_assigned` and `frag_*_output*`, a file a diagnostic; `lsp_render_target_colors_and_frag_outputs`. Not run: Android, whose apps of these tests are started by hand, and OpenGL on a Mac. The docs' `outputs` example shows it (`docs/game/shaders.md`, Several outputs): a scene filled into three images by one frag, and lit from them by a blit's.
- **A target's samples: multisampling, said where a target is made** (card 8 of the reshaping, built 2026-10-07. That render targets have MSAA with a `samples` argument is the owner's; that it's a named argument and the last, the counts, what a device with fewer does, that a multisampled depth is refused, and that the window has none are Claude's picks, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, A target's samples). `docs/game/views.md` (Smooth edges) is the users' page. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **Tide:** `RenderTarget(width, height[, pixelsPerUnit][, format...], samples: n)` and `RenderTarget.Window([factor][, format...], samples: n)`, with n 1, 2, 4 or 8. Its color images are read as any target's (`target.color`, `target.colors[i]`): what each pixel's samples come to when a pass on it ends. `target.depth` of one isn't read: the draw that reads it is passed by, with a line.
  - **The id has it, in two bits more** (`TIDE_RENDER_SAMPLES`, bits 14 and 15: the count as a power of two; `tide_render_target_samples(id)`, 1 for the window and a borrowed target). So `TIDE_RENDER_BITS` is 16 bits, a state is on a boundary of 64 KiB (`make_target_sampled` allocates that much more than the state, as `foreign_state` in `tests/test_draw.c` does), and `tide_render_target_state.holds` is those 16 bits but the image's two. The low bits, and not the bits above a pointer's 48: Android tags the top byte of heap pointers, and a 32-bit one has none to spare by rule. `tide_render_target_whole` keeps the samples (they're the target's, not an image's), so everything that goes by a target's id goes by them with no field of its own, and `tide_render_target_recreate` makes a target again with the samples its old id said: a hot reload keeps them with the generated code it had (`tide_local_rebind_resources` is the C it was). `tide_render_target` is still 24 bytes.
  - **C:** `tide_render_target_make_sampled(width, height, formats, count, samples)` and the three beside it (`_make_scaled_sampled`, `_screen_sampled`, `_window_sampled`); NULL and 0 for the formats is one `TIDE_TARGET_RGBA8` image. `samples_of` in `engine/src/draw.c` makes any number one of the four (the one under it, with a line), and gives a target of depth alone one, with a line.
  - **tidec** (`compiler/src/check.c`): `target_samples` finds the argument named `samples`, which has to be the call's last and a whole number, and sets `names_taken`; any other name on a target's arguments is an error there. A constant that's none of 1, 2, 4 and 8 is an error, and so is `Format.Depth` with a constant above 1 (`depth_alone_sampled`). `target_formats` counts the formats before that tail. The call is `expr.target_samples`, with `expr.target_formats` then however many formats it has, none and one too, as C's `_sampled` functions take them as several (`gen_target_format` in `codegen.c` writes `NULL, 0` for none). **A game that says no samples generates the C it did, byte for byte** (old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without: 209 games), and its header and hash with it.
  - **`gpu.h`: a target's samples are in its kind,** `tide_gpu_target_format`, from bit 16 (`tide_gpu_target_sampled`, `tide_gpu_target_samples`; none said is 1), and not a fourth argument of `target`: a pipeline is made for a sample count on WebGPU, and everything the renderer makes for a kind of target is keyed by that one number already (`engine_pipelines.into`, `stencil_pipeline.into`, `shader_pipeline.into`, a pipeline's `into`), so no key gained a field and none can be forgotten. `target_samples(format)` is what a device has for what a format asks: the most of 8, 4 and 2 that's no more than asked and that each of the target's images and its depth can have (OpenGL: `GL_MAX_SAMPLES` on desktop; on OpenGL ES 3 and WebGL 2, `glGetInternalformativ` for each of `GL_RGBA8`, `GL_DEPTH24_STENCIL8` and, for a float image, `GL_RGBA16F`, which may have none); WebGPU has 1 and 4, so 2 is 4 there. **The format given to `target` and a pipeline's `into` say what `target_samples` gave**: the renderer's `gpu_target_format` asks it, and says once when it's fewer than asked. A multisampled target's color textures have one color a pixel, what `end` resolves its samples into, and those are what `target_texture` and `target_read` give; `target_depth` is 0.
  - **OpenGL** (`gpu_gl.c`): the target's framebuffer is of multisampled renderbuffers, each color image's and a `GL_DEPTH24_STENCIL8` one (`gl_target.sampled`, `sampled_depth`), and each color image, a texture as ever, is in a framebuffer of its own (`resolved`), which `gl_end` blits the samples into, attachment by attachment (`glReadBuffer`, `glBlitFramebuffer` with `GL_COLOR_BUFFER_BIT`), with the scissor test off, as a blit goes by it. `gl.h` and `tide.js` gained `glBindRenderbuffer`, `glBlitFramebuffer`, `glDeleteRenderbuffers`, `glFramebufferRenderbuffer`, `glGenRenderbuffers` and `glRenderbufferStorageMultisample`, and for OpenGL ES 3 and WebGL 2 alone `glGetInternalformativ` (desktop has it from OpenGL 4.2, so it isn't among the functions a 3.3 context has to have). No extension. `gl_use`'s check that a pipeline is for the pass's kind of target is by the samples too.
  - **WebGPU** (`tide.js`): a multisampled texture for each color image and one for depth and stencil (`sampled`, `sampledDepth`, `sampleCount: 4`), kept from pass to pass (`storeOp: 'store'`), with each color image's own texture as the attachment's `resolveTarget`; a pipeline's `multisample.count` is its `into`'s (`samplesOf`: bit 16 on, 4 or 1; a change to the format's layout goes there).
  - **A depth read of a multisampled target is refused, not resolved** (`unavailable_texture` in `platform/src/platform.c`: "draw skipped: source render target is multisampled..."), by what the target asked for, whatever the device gave, so the same draws are passed by everywhere. OpenGL could resolve a depth by a blit; WebGPU can't without a shader of the engine's that reads each sample, a pass more for every such target every frame whether anything reads it.
  - **The window has none** (see the spec for why: three ways to ask on three platforms, each refusable, and none on a WebGPU canvas). A game draws into `RenderTarget.Window(samples: 4)` and blits.
  - **Memory:** `samples` times the color and depth, and the resolved color beside: 36 bytes a pixel at 4 samples of `Rgba8` where a plain target has 8.
  - **Not built:** a query of what a device has or a target got; a frag for each sample; alpha to coverage; a resolve before which light is tone mapped; completion that offers `samples:` in the editors.
  - **Tests:** `draw_targets_say_their_samples_and_keep_them_through_a_reload` (`tests/test_draw.c`: the id's bits, each way to make one, counts that are none, references, recreate, a foreign state); `check_samples` in `platform/tests/targets.c` at `gpu.h` (a triangle whose edge crosses an 8 by 8 target: pixels in between with samples and none without, the texture sampled is what's read back, a second pass over the samples the first left under a scissor, a clear, and three color images with floats, each resolved) and `check_render_samples` through the renderer (a turned 3D mesh in a target of the window's size, blitted to the window, with 4, 1 and 8 samples: edge pixels in between or not, a shader's blit and a shape of the engine's into it, kept a frame, its depth refused), the same on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU, where the device is then lost and found; `compiler/tests/e2e/render_samples` (each way to write it, a constant, a variable, a function, numbers that are none, depth alone, references, a reload in the same memory and in other memory), natively and under Node; `compiler/tests/errors/render_target_samples_*`, `render_target_window_samples_*` and `render_target_argument_named`, a file a diagnostic; `lsp_render_target_samples`. Not run: Android (OpenGL ES 3's path, whose `glGetInternalformativ` is the web's), and OpenGL on a Mac.
- **Alpha is premultiplied under the hood, and colors are the display's values** (card 7 of the reshaping; the owner picked both 2026-10-07 from a proposal with captures of the fringe: "go with your recommendations, we will polish if we find out it wasnt ideal after all"; built the same day; the rules are Claude's picks, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, Alpha and color space). `docs/game/views.md` (Alpha, under Blending, and Colors) and `docs/game/shaders.md` (Textures) are the users' pages. What it's for: a straight-alpha sprite blended between its pixels took in its clear pixels' color (a white disc over white had a gray rim, 194 of 255 halfway across its edge), and a target's alpha came out as `a * a + d * (1 - a)`, so half-clear draws into a clear target and then from it were washed out. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **Nothing a game writes changed:** a `Color` is a color as it is, with its alpha, everywhere (draws, tints, vertices, grids, PNGs, C's `tide_texture` pixels, a frag's outputs). The engine multiplies where bytes for the GPU are made and where a shader writes a pixel.
  - **`gpu.h`'s contract:** a fragment shader writes its color times its alpha, a texture's pixels are that way, and so is what a target holds. `TIDE_GPU_BLEND_ALPHA` is `ONE, ONE_MINUS_SRC_ALPHA` for color and alpha; `TIDE_GPU_BLEND_ADD` is `ONE, ONE` for color and `ONE_MINUS_DST_ALPHA, ONE` for alpha; multiply and replace are as they were (`gpu_gl.c`, with `GL_ONE_MINUS_DST_ALPHA` in `gl.h`; `blends` in `tide.js`). A shader written by hand for a test or a host multiplies before it writes, and for a pipeline that multiplies adds `1 - a` after.
  - **Add's alpha never passes 1,** on purpose: `ONE, ONE` grew a float image's alpha to 3 after three adds, and a `Sample` of it, which divides by alpha, gave a third of the light (pass 11 of `shaders.tide` caught it). Onto an opaque image it stays 1.
  - **Texture bytes:** an asset `Texture`'s are multiplied by tidec as the pack is built (`channel_byte` in `compiler/src/codegen.c`), a grid's as the draw list copies its cells (`channel` in `engine/src/mesh.c`, each channel kept from 0 to 1 first), and C's pixels as the list copies them (`texture_slot` in `engine/src/draw.c`, in whole numbers, rounded). A `Grid2<Color>` asset stays as its file has it in the pack, as code reads it, and goes through the grid's copy. A `Grid2<float>` and the font's atlas have no alpha. `append_texture` copies what's already multiplied.
  - **The engine's shaders** (`platform/src/shaders.h`): `whiten(c, by)` takes a color as it is and gives it times its alpha, plus `by * (1 - a)` for a pipeline that multiplies; `over(p, tint, by)` takes a texture's pixel, which is multiplied already, and the tint it's drawn with, which isn't (2D and 3D meshes). The plain shapes' shader multiplies in its last line, the smooth shapes' no longer divides its paint back, text keeps its power under 1 (that's about display space), and a clear's color is multiplied in its shader, so `Clear(Color(1, 0, 0, 0.25))` leaves (0.25, 0, 0, 0.25).
  - **A game's shaders** (`compiler/src/shader.c`): `put_fragment_main` finishes each output as its tint, then `rgb * a`, then `+ (1 - a)` where the draw multiplies. A color read from a texture goes through `tide_straight` (`H_STRAIGHT`, a helper like Math's: the pixel divided by its alpha, zeros where that's 0), in `ex_sample` for a `Sample` and in the main of a shader with no frag, so code only ever sees colors as they are. A depth read has none of it. Every game shader's text changed, so every shader's `id`, and the pins of `compiler/tests/shaders/` were made again (`tidec --shaders`, LF).
  - **`Blend.Replace` multiplies like the others,** which isn't what was proposed (as the frag wrote it): written raw, a `Sample` of the result would divide by an alpha that was never worked in, and a blit of colors into a target that's then drawn from would be wrong. So an image always holds colors times alpha, and a round trip through `Sample` is the color that was written, for any alpha but 0. Onto the window a replaced color with an alpha under 1 is darker by that much (it was the color as written, the window having no alpha).
  - **What's lost:** a pixel's color where its alpha is 0, and the finer steps of a nearly clear color in bytes. **Not built:** Nothing sRGB (a `Format`, a texture's mark, `Math` helpers): the docs' Colors section has the recipe for a game that works in light, which the float targets are enough for.
  - **`[Raw]` is a texture of numbers** (built 2026-10-07, after the owner asked for the follow-up; the attribute was approved with the proposal, and its name and rules are Claude's picks, Provisional in the spec beside the rest). `docs/game/shaders.md` (Textures that are numbers) is the users' page. What code relies on:
    - **Where:** before an asset that's one `Texture` or one `Grid2<Color>` (`decl.sample_raw`, `check_raw_attribute` in `compiler/src/check.c`, read with the asset's declaration as `[Filter]` is), and on a `Grid2<Color>` field (`check_field_raw`; the attribute stays on the field, `field_is_raw`). Refused, each with what to write: a glob's list or map, any other asset, a `Grid2<float>`, a `Texture` or `RenderTarget` field, an argument, twice.
    - **Nothing carries it at run time, on purpose:** a `Texture`'s four sampling bits are all taken (the filter's two since `Filter.Trilinear`, the wrap's two), and a frag handed a `Texture` would need a uniform or a second shader to know. So a raw texture is only ever the object of a `Sample` by its name, where `declared_raw` (`compiler/src/shader.c`) knows it as the game compiles, and every other use is an error. For a raw `Texture` asset, each place its name is used is noted (`checker.raw_uses`, in `use_asset`), `check_grid_sample` marks its own (`expr.raw_sampled`), and `check_raw_uses` says the rest once code is checked: that catches a local or a function argument too, with no list of places. For a raw grid, which code still reads cell by cell, it's where a `Texture` is wanted: `check_expr_want_any`, `check_blit` and `check_mesh_draw` call `refuse_raw_grid`. A new place a grid becomes a `Texture` without going through one of those has to call it too: `compiler/tests/errors/raw_*.tide` has a file for each way in there is.
    - **Bytes:** tidec packs a raw `Texture` asset's channels as the file has them (`pack_item`'s `raw`), under the file's hash with two bytes turned, so the same file declared both ways is two blocks and two versions in the draw list. A raw grid goes through `tide_draw_grid_texture_raw` (`engine/src/mesh.c`: `grid_pixels` with no alpha worked in, and another version than the same cells as colors), which generated `tide_shade_<n>` calls for a raw asset grid or field. A `Grid2<Color>` asset is in the pack as its file has it either way.
    - **The shader:** `ex_sample` leaves `tide_straight` off a raw texture's read, and nothing else differs. `compiler/tests/shaders/raw` pins it beside the same file as colors.
    - A game with no `[Raw]` generates the C it did (the changes are a suffix and a flag that are empty and false without one).
    - **Tests:** pass 16 of `platform/tests/shaders.tide` with `run_raw_checks` in `shaders.c` (a raw `Texture` asset's clear pixel keeps its file's numbers where the same file as colors has none, a raw `Grid2<Color>` asset, a raw grid field's cell with no alpha, and numbers blended between cells), the same on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU; `compiler/tests/shaders/raw`; `compiler/tests/errors/raw_*.tide`, a file a diagnostic; `lsp_asset_textures_and_declared_sampling` (hover, completion, the diagnostic and its note).
    - **Not built:** a raw texture as a `Texture` value, and C's `tide_texture` pixels as numbers.
  - **Generated C** differs in shader text alone (and a pack's texture bytes where an image has alpha under 1); no header, so no game's hash.
  - **Tests:** `meshes` in `platform/tests/draw.c` (C's pixels: white blended towards a clear magenta pixel over white is white, a half-clear pixel is half); pass 14 of `platform/tests/shaders.tide` with `run_alpha_checks` in `shaders.c` (a grid blended towards a clear cell, what a `Sample` between the two gives, and a half-clear rect drawn straight, through a clear target, and through a frag that samples the target: the same pixels); `settings` in `draw.c` (`blended`: a replaced color is its color times its alpha); `check_render_depth_alpha` in `targets.c` (what an image holds after a clear with alpha); `tests/test_draw.c` and `compiler/tests/e2e/mesh_test.c` (the list's copies), `compiler/tests/e2e/asset_textures` (the pack's bytes where the file's clear pixels have a color). The hand-written shaders of `draw.c` follow the contract.
- **A `Cubemap` is six images a frag reads by a direction: an asset's six files, or a cube a view draws into a face at a time** (item 9 of the reshaping, the last, built 2026-10-07. That the drawing side gets cube maps, for skyboxes and reflections, is the owner's; each rule is Claude's pick, Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, Cubemaps, with what was weighed: a type of its own over a `Texture` with a flag, the faces' file names, `Cubemap(size)` giving a `Cubemap`, a cube's size in pixels, a filter said at the Sample and none carried). `docs/game/shaders.md` (Skybox and reflections, with the live `skybox` example), `docs/game/assets.md` (Cubemaps) and `docs/game/views.md` (Six of them, as a cube) are the users' pages. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **Tide:** `asset Cubemap sky = "art/sky_??.png";`; `Cubemap(size[, format])`, a constructor as `RenderTarget(width, height)` is (it was `RenderTarget.Cube` for an afternoon: a static of one type that makes another read oddly, and `RenderTarget.Cube` is an error that names the constructor now); `cube.faces[i]`, a `RenderTarget`; `cube.Resize(size)` and `cube.Dispose()`; and in a frag or a vert, `cube.Sample(direction[, filter])`, where the cube is an asset by its name or a field of a component or a singleton the code takes, as a `DepthTexture` is.
  - **The faces are `+x`, `-x`, `+y`, `-y`, `+z`, `-z`, each as it's seen from the cube's middle with its rows from the top:** `+y` up for the four around, `+x` to the right of `+z` (the world is left-handed, as `perspective_camera` is), `-z` at the top of the one above and `+z` at the top of the one below. It's OpenGL's and WebGPU's own layout, so pixels go in as they are on both, and a face drawn into is the same way up as one uploaded: OpenGL's target passes are flipped as they're drawn (`compile` in `gpu_gl.c`), which puts a face's top in its first row, where a cube face's `t` starts. `check_cubes` (`platform/tests/targets.c`) reads an uploaded cube and a drawn one towards the same twelve directions, and pass 17 of `shaders.tide` draws a card through `quaternion.LookRotation` cameras into each face and reads it back by the direction it was at: a change to either backend's rows shows there.
  - **`gpu.h`:** `cube(color, size, faces)`, a texture that's only ever read as a cube (`TIDE_GPU_READ_CUBE`, a pipeline's `reads`: a `samplerCube`, and a `texture_cube<f32>` with a `sampler`), with `faces` six pointers to 4 bytes a pixel or NULL for one that's drawn into; and `cube_face(cube, face)`, a target whose one color image is that face, with depth and stencil of its own, whose `target_texture` is 0, whose `target_read` reads the face, and which is freed before its cube. `sample`'s wrap isn't read for a cube, `mipmaps` makes a cube's smaller images, and `texture_free` frees one. It counts as one of a pipeline's 8 textures.
  - **OpenGL** (`gpu_gl.c`) adds no function: `glTexImage2D`, `glFramebufferTexture2D` and `glGenerateMipmap` take a cube's targets (`GL_TEXTURE_CUBE_MAP`, `GL_TEXTURE_CUBE_MAP_POSITIVE_X` + face, in `gl.h`). `gl_texture.cube` says which way a name is bound, and `gl_texture_free` zeroes what's known of a name, as OpenGL hands names out again. Desktop OpenGL 3.3 has to be asked to read across a cube's edges (`GL_TEXTURE_CUBE_MAP_SEAMLESS`, enabled once, where the samplers are made); OpenGL ES 3 and WebGL 2 always do, and WebGL would refuse the enable, so it's desktop's alone. A cube asked for floats on a device with none is bytes, and its faces' targets are still of the float kind (`asked_floats`), as a target's float image is.
  - **WebGPU** (`tide.js`): a texture of six layers with room for every smaller image from the start (a cube's samplers that read none of them keep to its own pixels by `lodMaxClamp`), a `cube` view that draws sample, and a 2D view of each face's each level, which a pass on a face draws into and `gpu_mipmaps` halves through, a face at a time, with the pipeline textures' use. One with nothing in its smaller images yet is read as `LINEAR` when it's asked for `MIPMAPS` (`gpu_sample`, by `t.mipped`), which is what `gpu.h` says of a texture with none. A cube that's uploaded is `rgba8unorm`; one that's drawn into is the canvas's own format (`gpuFormat`), as a target's image is, since the pipelines into it are made for that. A pipeline's `reads` are 2 bits each on their way to the page, and `READ_CUBE` is 3, the last there's room for: a fifth way to read a texture widens them (`tide_web_gpu_pipeline` in `gpu_web.c`, `gpu_pipeline_begin`). `layoutFor` gives a cube `viewDimension: 'cube'`, and `bindGroup` its clamping sampler by its filter alone.
  - **A cube's and a face's ids** (`tide/draw.h`): a cube that's drawn into is a managed target's state with `cube` set, and its id has `TIDE_RENDER_CUBE` (bit 51); a face's is the cube's with which face in `TIDE_RENDER_FACE` (bits 48 to 50, 1 + its number). Those are above where an address ends on every platform Tide builds for, and `tide_cubemap_make_as` stops the program for a state whose address has them. So a face is a destination of its own in a draw list (its own `tide_draw_place`: camera, clip and settings), with the cube's state, version and size; `tide_render_target_state_of` masks the bits, `tide_render_target_face` and `tide_render_target_cube_of` read them, and `tide_render_target_cubic` says either. `tide_render_target_color` and `_depth` of a face name no image (`TIDE_RENDER_INVALID`), and `tide_render_target_resize` and `_dispose` of one say so and do nothing. `tide_render_target_recreate` keeps both sets of bits, so generated C's reload code for a `RenderTarget` field that holds a face is what it was.
  - **`tide_cubemap_ref`** (Tide's `Cubemap`, 32 bytes: a `tide_render_target`, then a name) is a cube that's drawn into (`target`) or the pack's (`named`, as a pack `Texture`'s version names its declaration); zeroed, it's no cube. `tide_draw_cube_source` gives the list's texture: `TIDE_PIXELS_TARGET_CUBE` (the cube's id, size and version, no pixels) or `TIDE_PIXELS_PACK_CUBE`, told by what's in its six faces, whose `pixels` is the pack's six pointers (a `const uint8_t *const *`, cast: nothing here frees it, and `append_texture` hands it on), noted again each frame as a pack image's are. A shader's `texture_reads` says `TIDE_SHADER_READ_CUBE`.
  - **The renderer** (`platform/src/platform.c`): `gpu_cube` is a drawn cube's GPU texture, by the cube's id, and each face is a `gpu_target` by the face's id, made with the cube (`cube_for`, which clears all six, so a face nothing drew into is black on OpenGL too) and freed before it. `target_for` of a face goes through `cube_for`; the cube's own id is no destination. A pack's cube goes through `texture_for`, which makes it again whenever its version isn't what it has (`gpu->cube` has no write). `texture_to_read` gives a cube only to a read of that kind, and `gpu_state.dark`, a cube of one black pixel a face, where none was given. `of_destination` is what `samples_destination` asks: a face's draws don't read its cube. `prepare_source_target` makes a cube nothing drew into, `prepare_mipmaps` makes a drawn cube's smaller images when a pass began on any of its faces since (`gpu_cube.drawn`, counted where the loop begins a pass on a face), and a lost device and a hot reload forget cubes with targets.
  - **The pack:** `TIDE_PACK_CUBEMAP` (8) is a block of six handles to texture blocks (`tide_pack_texture`, shared by their files' SHA-256 with any `Texture` of the same file), in the faces' order; tidec writes it (`write_pack`, with `pack_item` for each face). The loader notes each cube whose six faces are whole, square and one size (`note_cubes`, after `lay_textures`, as the pointers it keeps are into the region where it stays), under a name made of the declaration's as a texture's is, with a version that's a hash of its faces' versions. `tide_pack_cubemap_of` gives the value and `tide_pack_cubemap_find` the faces. The file format's version didn't change: a pack with no cubemap is the bytes it was.
  - **tidec:** `TY_CUBEMAP` (`tide_cubemap_ref`), a rendering resource wherever `DepthTexture` is one (`holds_render_resource`, `resource_type`, the reload's rebinding in `gen_resource_fields`: `tide_render_target_adopt`, or `tide_cubemap_recreate`). `check_cube_target` (from `check_window_target`, as `Cubemap(size)` is the owner `RenderTarget`'s; the two entries in `builtins.c` are for editors), `check_cube_sample`, and `cube.faces` as an index's object alone, through `checker.colors_indexed` and `expr.target_colors`, as `target.colors` is. `ASSET_CUBEMAP` (`asset_files.h`): `asset_resolve` wants a glob, reads each match as a PNG, and `order_cube` puts the six in the faces' order by how their names end (`cube_face_of`), or says what's wrong.
  - **In the shaders** (`ex_cube_sample` in `compiler/src/shader.c`): `READ_CUBE` is a `shader_sample`'s and a `shader_texture`'s `read`, a binding for each filter a cube is read with; `Filter.Trilinear` in a frag lifts the direction as a uv is lifted (`tide_uv<n>`, `tide_dx<n>`, `tide_dy<n>`, of three components), and what's read goes through `tide_straight`, as a texture's color does. `compiler/tests/shaders/cubemaps` pins both languages.
  - **Generated C is what it was for a game with no cubemap, byte for byte** (dev's tidec against this one over every game's source list, 248 of them, with `--layout` and without), and no shader's text moved (`compiler/tests/shaders/`). The two games that differ are the ones that use it.
  - **Not built** (all in `docs/spec.md`): a level or a roughness a game picks; a cube of depth alone; a face's color or depth as a texture; several colors or samples on a cube; a cube as a draw's own texture or a frag's own parameter; files other than six PNGs; lists and maps of cubes; all six faces in one pass; a helper for the six cameras.
  - **Tests:** `check_cubes` in `platform/tests/targets.c` at `gpu.h` (six faces uploaded and six drawn into, each read towards both of its quarters, blended at a face's middle, with no smaller images and with them, a face read back, a pass over what's there, a float cube, everything made twice); pass 17 of `platform/tests/shaders.tide` with `run_cube_checks` in `shaders.c` (from Tide: the asset by name and through a field, a painted cube, a face read between its smaller images made of what was just drawn, a float cube, no cube, a vert's read, and each face against a camera at its middle), the same pixels on OpenGL 3.3, WebGL 2 and WebGPU, where the device is then lost and found; `compiler/tests/e2e/cubemaps` (values, ids, faces as destinations, each binding's filter, a reload in the same memory and in other memory, Resize and Dispose, the pack gone), natively and under Node; `pack_cubemaps_are_six_images_named_as_one` (`tests/test_pack.c`); `compiler/tests/shaders/cubemaps`; `compiler/tests/errors/cubemap_*.tide`, a file a diagnostic (48, with wrong face files in `compiler/tests/e2e/cubes/`, which is outside `e2e/assets/` so the asset tests' globs don't match them); `lsp_cubemaps`. Not run: Android, whose apps of these tests are started by hand, and OpenGL on a Mac, which nothing runs.
- A game's C draws through the view's list: an extern function takes a `DrawList` (`tide_draw_list *`), which only `Draw.list` is, in views and the functions they call. C calls `tide/draw.h` itself (`tide_draw_vertices` and `tide_draw_triangles` for vertices several batches share), so libraries that hand over triangles (Dear ImGui, Nuklear, Spine) need no copy through Tide lists.
- The GUI (`GUI` and `GUILayout` in Tide, `tide/gui.h` in C) is immediate mode, like drawing, and local: widgets change local state, never the match. It records into a list of its own, which `tide_gui_end` adds after the world's, in pixels (from the top left, y down), so widgets keep their size when the window changes. It measures text with the platform's font (`tide_platform_measure_text`). Views read the devices through a `Devices devices` parameter: `tide_gui_begin` works out what changed since last frame and hides what the GUI uses.
- A host samples the game's input before the frame's GUI runs, and views read the devices before its widgets do, so what the GUI hides can't wait for the GUI to see a press. Whether the pointer is the GUI's is where it is now against where the last frame drew its widgets and areas (`on_gui` in `engine/src/gui.c`, which `tide_gui_hide` and `tide_gui_begin` both ask), never where the GUI last saw it: a finger that touches a button is hidden from the sample of the frame it lands in. A widget that first appears under the pointer takes it a frame later. Tests of hiding go in the host's order, sample then frame (`finger_sample`, `mouse_sample` in `tests/test_gui.c`).
- A key or a gamepad's button is the GUI's by when its press began, as the pointer on the stats' drawer is by where (the owner, 2026-10-06, of the bug it was: "yeah sounds like a bug"): one that goes down while the keyboard and the gamepad are the GUI's (a widget has the focus, a field is typed into, a modal is up) stays the GUI's until it's let go, whatever became of the widget. Before, the keyboard was the game's again the frame after nothing had the focus, with the key that clicked still down: Space on a lobby's Play, which closes the lobby, fired the game's cannon too.
  - A frame notes each press as it sees the key go down (`keep_presses` in `engine/src/gui.c`, from the platform's devices against last frame's: `tide_gui.kept_keys`, `kept_pad`). A new press of a key is the GUI's or the game's afresh, and a key that was down before the GUI took the keyboard is never kept: it's the game's again as soon as the GUI lets go.
  - It's hidden from views until a frame has seen it up, that frame included (`hide_kept_from_views`), and from samples until one has (`hide_kept_from_sample`, in `tide_gui_hide`), apart, as a key's `pressed` and `up` stay in the platform's devices until a sample consumes them, which can be frames after it was let go. Where nothing samples and the host clears the devices itself, a frame that finds the key up with nothing left to read lets it go.
  - Both go by which keys are still down, so they run before the rest is hidden (`hide`).
  - Sticks and triggers aren't kept, nor what a view claimed (`GUI.ClaimKeyboard`): a claim that ends with a key down gives the key back at once.
  - `gui_a_key_that_clicked_a_widget_is_the_guis_until_its_let_go` and the two tests after it (`tests/test_gui.c`) check it in the host's order, with samples every frame, rarer than frames, and never. Siege's lobby (which is Towers' since), and the one Sumo's came from, waited a frame of their own for this, and don't any more.
- A field says when the player entered what they typed, through an `out` parameter (agreed 2026-10-06: "yeah im ok with that", of `GUILayout.TextField("Code", lobby.code, out var entered)`, with what it returns staying whether the value changed, `entered` true in the frame Enter ends the typing, and the number fields and both of `GUI` and `GUILayout` having it). A view couldn't tell before: Enter ends the typing and the next one starts it again, and the keyboard is the GUI's while a widget has the focus, so `devices.keyboard.enter.down` is never true there. The rest is picks (Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, GUI). What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **Two things in the owner's sketch aren't Tide,** and the docs show what is: an argument for a `mut` parameter is written as it is, with no `mut` (the parser says so now, where it said "expected an expression": `parse_args`), and a widget can't be on the right of `||`, so it's `if (GUILayout.Button("Join") || entered)`, the button first.
  - **Runtime** (`engine/src/gui.c`, `tide/gui.h`): `tide_gui_text_field_entered`, `tide_gui_int_field_entered`, `tide_gui_float_field_entered` and their `tide_gui_layout_` twins take a `bool *entered` last, which every call assigns, and the functions there always were call them. It's true when the field is typed into, has the focus and Enter went down this frame (`KEY_ENTER`, the numpad's too; for a number field `PAD_ACCEPT` as well, which ends its typing as it always did, and not a text field's), and false for anything else that ends the typing: Escape, the focus moving on, a click elsewhere, a Disabled block, a modal over it.
  - **What's typed in the frame Enter comes in is entered with it** (`type_text` and `type_into` run before the typing ends), in every field, with `entered` or not: it was dropped, which a script's `text` and `key enter` on one frame, or a slow frame, would have joined a room with.
  - **tidec:** a built-in signature has `out` parameters (`signature.out` in `compiler/src/builtins.c`, a bit each, with its bit in `mut` set, so generated code passes the address as for a `mut` argument and the call counts as one that writes: nothing in `codegen.c` changed). `add_gui` reads `out bool entered` in a widget's parameters, and the three fields are registered a second time, as `tide_gui_<field>_entered`, after every first version, so completion still leads with the one there always was.
  - **An `out` argument of one of the engine's functions goes through the code a function's does,** not a copy of it: where a version of the name has an `out` parameter (`builtin_takes_out`), the argument is begun as any (`begin_out_arg`, in `check_method`'s built-in branch), `resolve_builtin_call` picks the version by the arguments' count and lets an `out` argument go to the parameter in its place whatever that is (`fits`), and `check_gui_call` then finishes it and checks it against its parameter (`finish_out_arg`, `check_out_param`, with the parameter's own name from `builtin_param_name`). So the word missing, the word before another argument, a type that isn't `bool`, a read-only variable, a list's element and the match's state in a view are the errors, notes and quick fixes they are for a function. An argument with no `out` that isn't a `bool` variable gets "no version of ... takes", which lists both. `out` on a widget with no such version (`GUILayout.Float2Field(..., out var entered)`) says so, and which widgets have one, from the table (`refuse_entered_arg`, `builtin_out_versions`): the note `out` gets where nothing takes one, to declare the variable first, wouldn't help there.
  - A program that calls no such version generates the C it did, byte for byte: checked by running old and new tidec over every game's source list, with `--layout` and without.
  - **Editors:** signature help and hovers have each version from the same table. Completion offers `out var entered` where the argument goes (`out_argument_at` in `compiler/lsp/analysis.c` reads the engine's versions' signatures, `visit_builtin_out`), and a function of the engine's with several versions says so in its item's detail, ` (+1 version)`, with the others under its doc (`builtin_list_members`): that's every such function's, not only the fields'.
  - **A phone's keyboard:** its Enter is the GUI's Enter. On the web the page takes a key event that names Enter with no position, and a line break typed into its hidden field (`platform/web/tide.js`: `onKey`, the field's `input`), which `tide_platform_web_keys` checks with made-up events; on Android the system's keyboard sends the key itself to the input queue (`AKEYCODE_ENTER` in `platform/android/activity.c`). No phone was tried.
  - Tests: `gui_a_field_says_when_its_typing_is_entered` and the five after it (`tests/test_gui.c`), in the host's order, for the three fields; `compiler/tests/e2e/gui_entered` (each way to write the argument, both forms, by name, through a function that passes its own `out` parameter on, and a statement that reads the variable before and after the call); `compiler/tests/errors/gui_entered_*.tide` and `call_argument_mut.tide`; `lsp_gui_entered`. Duel's menu, Sand's panel and the docs' `rooms` example join with Enter.
  - **A number is kept whichever field the player goes to** (it was dropped when they clicked a field that a frame calls before the number's). The GUI has one buffer for what's typed (`tide_gui.edit`), which a field that starts its typing takes: by a click, or with the focus and something typed, as after Shift+Tab in a slow frame. Text is its field's as it's typed, so a text field never lost any. A number isn't until its field keeps it, and the field it was typed into has yet to run in that frame (having run, it would have ended its typing itself, without the focus), so before the buffer goes, a valid number in it is left for that field (`leave_typing` in `engine/src/gui.c`: `tide_gui.edit_left` and `edit_left_value`, read as the kind of number the typing started as, `edit_kind`). The field keeps it as it runs (`number_box`) and says it changed, with `entered` false. What code relies on:
    - The first one left in a frame stands: a field that took the buffer from it and is left in turn has run already, and was only typed into in that frame.
    - What no field took by the frame's end is dropped (`tide_gui_end`): a field that isn't called any more has nowhere to keep a number, and none is kept for a later frame.
    - Escape drops the number, as ever, and so does a field in a Disabled block, left for or not.
    - A modal that comes up doesn't end the typing where it takes the focus any more (`focusable`): the field under it ends its own as it runs, which keeps a number wherever the frame calls the modal. It was dropped when the modal was called first.
    - `gui_a_number_is_kept_when_the_player_clicks_another_field` and the eight tests after it (`tests/test_gui.c`) have fields at rects of their own, so a frame calls them in either order: an int and a float field left for a text field, two number fields, a number and a text field and back, a vector's and a color's boxes, Escape, Tab and Shift+Tab (with something typed in that frame too), a field that goes, one that's disabled, and a modal.
- A view's own widgets are controls (agreed: the GUI stays bare, and games bring their own controls). `GUI.Control(rect)` (`tide_gui_control`) is a widget the view draws itself: the GUI handles its input as a button's and draws nothing, so showing hover and focus is the view's.
  - It's a button's code without the drawing (`clicked` in `engine/src/gui.c`): its rect is one of the places a frame remembers, so a press on it is the GUI's from the sample it lands in (`on_gui`), it's in the focus's order with the other widgets, and a Disabled block or a modal over it stops it.
  - It gives a `tide_control` (`hovered`, `pressed`, `focused`, `clicked`), which generated C makes Tide's `GUIControl` of in a helper (`tide_gui_control_value`), as the two are different C types. `pressed` is the pointer holding it, wherever it goes meanwhile, as a `Devices` button's `pressed` is held.
  - While a control has the focus, the view that called it goes on reading the keyboard and the gamepad, less what moves the focus and clicks (`focus_view`, `hide_navigation`, `tide_gui_view`): the game and the other views read neither, as with any focus. So a menu's own shortcuts work with one of its controls focused.
  - Controls a loop makes come from one call, and are told apart as the GUI's own widgets in a loop are, by their turn (`tide_gui_id` counts the calls from a site in a frame): one left out gives its turn to the next.
  - `GUILayout` has no control: what a view draws is under the GUI's own widgets, a panel included, and under a modal's dim, since the GUI draws last. That's documented, and stays.
- The focus moves in the order widgets are called: down and right go on, up and left go back (`navigate`). A widget that steps its value with left and right keeps those (`focusable`'s `steps`: sliders and number fields), and the press that brought the focus to one doesn't step it too. The arrows, the d-pad and the left stick only start the focus while the game isn't reading the devices, or while a view says a menu is up, frame by frame (`GUI.Navigate()`, `tide_gui_navigate`), as a menu over a running match needs: a HUD's button never takes the d-pad.
- The GUI's own widgets have one look, and a simple theme for it (agreed: no styles for single widgets, no text sizes, no fonts). `tide_gui_theme`, Tide's `GUITheme`, is the colors the widgets use and their corners' radius, and nothing else.
  - Its defaults are the look the widgets always had, in two places: `tide_gui_theme_default` in `gui.c`, and the built-in struct's fields in `add_builtins` (`compiler/src/check.c`). A change to one goes to the other, and `gui_set_themes_hold_for_the_views_after` (`compiler/tests/e2e/gui_test.c`) checks they agree.
  - It follows the rule for a frame's settings: the plain name wraps a block and puts back what it found (`GUI.Theme(theme) { }`, `tide_gui_begin_theme`: a block that lays out nothing, like a Disabled one, `open_scope`), the `Set` name stays for the views that follow until it's set again or the frame ends (`GUI.SetTheme`), and every frame starts from the defaults (`tide_gui_begin`). Each form's misuse names the other (`check_gui_call` and `check_stmt` in `compiler/src/check.c`).
  - With a radius of 0 a part is the plain rect it always was (`paint`), so a game with no theme draws the commands it did. With one it's `Draw`'s round rect, and an area's panel, a shape then, is sized when its area closes like the plain one (`panel`, `close_area`).
  - The stats' drawer and the profiler's panel draw into lists of their own, never through the GUI, so they keep their look (`profiler_keeps_its_look_whatever_the_guis_theme` in `tests/test_profile.c`).
- A view with widgets that read the devices themselves, like a C library's, claims what they use, with the effect the GUI's own use has: `GUI.ClaimPointer()` and `GUI.ClaimKeyboard()` (`tide_gui_claim_pointer`, `tide_gui_claim_keyboard`). A claim lasts a frame and is made again each frame: it stands from the end of its frame to the end of the next, hiding the device from the samples taken meanwhile (`tide_gui_hide`) and from the other views, while the view that claimed it goes on reading it. So generated code says which view runs (`tide_gui_view`, before each), and `tide_gui.devices` is that view's. `GUI.ClaimPointer(rect)` (`tide_gui_claim_pointer_at`) says where a view's widget is each frame, and the pointer on it is the view's by where it is now, as on the GUI's own widgets: a claim with no rect can only hide a frame late, which a finger's first touch gets past. The GUI's own use comes first: no view reads what the GUI is using. `GUI.ShowKeyboard()` is apart from the keyboard's claim, as a widget's shortcuts don't want a phone's keyboard up. What's typed (`devices.keyboard.text`, `tide_keyboard.text`) is a frame's: views read it, and it's never in a sample or the input.

## Tech stack

- Local files are `File`, `Directory`, `Path`, and the read-only runtime properties `Application.persistentDataPath` and `Application.temporaryCachePath` (`docs/game/files.md`). File contents are UTF-8 text or `List<byte>`; `byte` is an unsigned 8-bit scalar. File/directory operations, application paths and `await Storage.Flush()` are local only, including functions that reach them; their results reach the match through input. `Path` is pure. Whole-file operations fail with `IOError`, except `Exists`, which is a bool. Directory results are sorted full paths, with recursion off by default; directory creation creates parents, writes do not, and recursive deletion is explicit. Relative paths use the persistent root. Save identity is the stable `appId`, never the window title. Native files use the OS; browser files are an in-memory filesystem loaded from IndexedDB before startup, with automatic background commits and an awaitable flush that confirms preceding changes committed. The page keeps its files and commits across hot reload. Storage failures are reported, and failed commits retain dirty data for retry; temporary storage stays usable when persistence is unavailable. Open file handles and streams are outside this first API.
  - A browser save is one IndexedDB transaction, and the saves of a page go one after another (`saveFiles` and `tree.chain` in `platform/web/tide.js`). A change queues a save for the end of its turn, and a flush is a save too, so a game that changes files and flushes before it returns to the browser asks for two. Each takes what's dirty when its turn comes, never when it's asked for: the second comes once the first committed and finds nothing left, so it opens no transaction. Copied when asked, both wrote every changed file, and the flush was done after the first with the second still on its way, which is what a page that then went elsewhere left behind. What follows from taking it then: a save commits the files as they are when its transaction opens, so a flush may also commit what changed after it was asked for and before its turn, and reports that transaction's failure; what a failed save left dirty, and a file changed again while its save was on its way (its entry in `tree.dirty` is then another object than the one the save wrote, so the save's end leaves it), go with the next save. `tide_platform_web_io` counts the transactions that write in each of its phases, and its `busy` phase changes files while the page holds a save's transaction open.

- Assets are built (agreed 2026-10-06; `docs/spec.md`, Assets, where the picks beyond what was agreed are Provisional; `docs/game/assets.md` for users): `asset Grid2<Color> hero = "art/hero.png";` is a declaration (`DECL_ASSET`) that resolves to a plain type, read-only, read by name wherever a value of its type is, in match code too, as every machine has the same pack. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **The language.** `asset` is a declaration word (`parse_asset` in `compiler/src/parser.c`): a type, a name, `=`, an expression and `;`. The type is `string` (UTF-8), `List<byte>` (any file) or `Grid2<Color>` (a PNG), or for a glob a `List<T>` or `Map<string, T>` of one, which is the one place a list or a map holds a grid (`asset_type` in `check.c` resolves the type at `PLACE_ASSET`, where `element_ok` takes one, and reads its shape off the tree; see A type is read as a tree, and Containers nest, under Language). The path is a constant text (`is_constant`, `text_literal`), relative to the declaring file whatever its namespace. A name is looked up as `NAME_ASSET` after `NAME_CONST` (`use_asset`: `BIND_ASSET`, with the declaration in `expr.constant`); `check_writable` refuses it, `local` and `virtual` are refused, and a frag samples one by its bare name only (`check_grid_sample`, `ex_sample` in `shader.c`: `shader_sample.asset`, `shader_texture.asset`), never an element of a list of them.
  - **Resolving** is `compiler/src/asset_files.c` (tidec_core, so tidels and tide share it; `folders.c` moved there from `compiler/lsp/`): a glob (`*` within a name, `**` across folders, `?`; `asset_glob_match`, which tidels uses too) walks from its fixed prefix and sorts matches by their written paths' bytes, which are a map's keys. The walk only goes into folders the pattern could match in (`glob_reaches`, through `folder_find_files_in`): each folder's name has to match the pattern's part in its place, up to a part with a `**`, so `*.png` reads one folder's entries and none of the folders under it. Walking everything under the prefix, as it first did, walked the whole disk for a file at its root, which is where the language server's tests have theirs (`file:///test.tide`): `tidels_tests` took 70 seconds on Windows and never ended on Linux. A file is read once per process until its size or time changes (`read_at`), hashed with SHA-256 and, for a grid, decoded with `tide_png_decode`; a URL is fetched with curl through `popen` once per process (`fetch_url`) into `<game>/.tide/assets/urls/<sha256 of the URL>` beside its `.etag`, asked for again with `If-None-Match` (a 304 keeps the cache), and taken from the cache when the server can't be reached; with no game folder (tidec on its own) a temporary folder stands in. The diagnostics say what tidels says, so its fixes attach to them: `there's no file at '<path>' (<abs>)` with a note naming a file of the same bytes (by the hashes `.tide/assets.sha256` keeps, which tidels writes) or files of that name, in order of their paths (`candidate_order`, as the server sorts its own: a system lists a folder in whatever order it keeps it in, which is by name on Windows and not on Linux), `'<glob>' matches no file`, `'<path>' isn't a PNG that decodes`, `'<path>' isn't UTF-8 text`, and one each for a glob where the type holds one file, one file where it holds many, a URL for many, a type an asset can't be, a path that isn't constant. tidec takes `--game <folder>` (tide passes the game's) for the hashes and the cache.
  - **The pack** (`tide/pack.h`: the builder in `engine/src/pack.c`, which tidec compiles too with `png_read.c`, `sha256.c`, `page.c` and `net.c`; the loader in `pack_load.c`) is one region laid out as a heap lays blocks out, so the code that reads text, lists, maps and grids reads it unchanged: a text or `List<byte>` is a block with a NUL after its bytes; a `Grid2<Color>` its directory and the chunks that have a color in them, with the file's hash in the directory's header as the version the draw list tells it by (`tide_pack_grid_version`, which `tide_draw_grid_texture` reads for a pack grid); a glob's list a block of handles; a map its entries and an index hashed as the game's lookups hash. A file is in the region once (by its content's SHA-256), and a declaration is an entry by the SHA-256 of its qualified name and its kind.
