  - `physics/` (Physics, after Presentation): the physics packages that come with tide, a page each, `box2d.md` and `box3d.md` (see Box2D and Box3D, under Packaging and releases), written from the packages' `README.md`s and their code, and from what a game that uses them does: a change to a package's components, events or rules updates its page. The owner asked for docs for the two packages (2026-10-05); a folder and a group of their own, where they are in the sidebar and the pages' titles ("2D physics (Box2D)", "3D physics (Box3D)") are picks they haven't confirmed.
  - `multiplayer/`: matches, and how machines stay in step (networking, determinism).
  - `language/`: the language those are written in (types, functions, errors, math, collections, namespaces).
  - `tools/`: `tide`, the editors and packages.
  - `c/`: calling C, and C that runs a game.
  - A page about how something works is with the pages about using it (the schedule with systems, networking with matches), not in a section of its own.
- Pages are Markdown as GitHub shows it, plus what VitePress had and the docs use (`docs/src/lib/server/markdown.ts`): links between pages by file name (`./systems.md#order`), `::: tip`, `info`, `warning`, `danger` and `details`, `::: code-group` over fences labeled `[name]`, and, in `content.ts` beside it, `<<< @/../demo/demo.tide` for a file of the repo and the home page's hero and features in `docs/index.md`'s front matter. Anchors are made as VitePress made them, so old links still land. Search looks through an index the build makes (`/search.json`). The Windows install line is written `Invoke-RestMethod ... | Invoke-Expression`, never `irm ... | iex` (the owner, 2026-10-07): uBlock Origin blocks a page's copy button that puts the short form on the clipboard and warns of a "ClickFix" attack (its `prevent-clipboard-write` rule in `filters.txt`, since September 2026: text that starts `irm` and has `iex`; `github.com` is among the sites it exempts, so the README never showed it). The long form matches nothing of the rule as it was that day, which can change: the lasting way is an exemption, asked of uBlock's maintainers (`uBlockOrigin/uAssets`), as other tools' sites have.
- It runs on Fly.io as `tide-website`, one machine in `fra` (`docs/fly.toml`): `docs/server.js` serves the build with SvelteKit's handler, and sends www.tide-engine.dev to the name without it. In the domain's DNS on Cloudflare, not proxied, the name has A and AAAA records to the app's addresses and www a CNAME to `tide-website.fly.dev`; certificates come from Fly (`fly certs`). The image takes what the build made (`docs/Dockerfile`), as the pages need the rest of the repo.
- Every response has `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin` and `Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy: require-corp` (`docs/headers.js`, in `npm run dev` too), so pages are cross-origin isolated and the demos in them run on threads. A page in a frame is only isolated when the page around it is, and moving between pages loads no new one, so every page has them: everything a page loads comes from the site itself (fonts included), or has to say it may be embedded (CORP or CORS).
- `.github/workflows/docs.yml` builds it on pull requests, and on pushes to `dev` deploys it (the `FLY_API_TOKEN` secret, a deploy token for the app).
- The workflow builds sand, Duel, Towers and Sumo for the web (`web-release`, targets `sand`, `duel`, `towers` and `sumo`) and puts them at `sand/`, `duel/`, `towers/` and `sumo/`, linked by the Samples gallery. The dedicated Duel, Towers and Sumo docs explain their implementation. Sand also serves as a benchmark (see Benchmarks); Duel is a standalone game in `examples/duel/`, and Towers and Sumo are the physics games in `examples/towers/` and `examples/sumo/` (see The physics games). To see them locally, copy each `build/web-release/bin/<game>.html` to `docs/static/<game>/index.html`; a build without them still works: the prerender lets a link to one of those four pages pass (`docs/vite.config.ts`), as nothing serves `static/` while it runs.
- A whole game is one frame on its page (the owner, 2026-10-06, of Siege, which Towers has since taken the place of, and of Alley, the game Sumo took the place of, which were two frames in one match: "make it only one game instance with a lobby setup"): an iframe in the page's Markdown, `<iframe class="demo-frame" src="/<name>/?embedded" ...>`, as Duel's is, and the game's own lobby is how a reader gets other machines in. `roomy` beside `demo-frame` (`docs/src/app.css`) gives a game that opens on a lobby the height it needs at narrow widths, as `duel` gives Duel. Nothing shows two frames of a whole game any more: `::: tide-game` is gone, with its CSS and its part of the test. A frame that was in the page as it came, and clicked before the page's script ran, shows its focus all the same (`showsFocus` in `docs/src/lib/examples.ts` looks at `document.activeElement`).
- Pages have live examples beside their code, wherever a small game says it better (`docs/guide/examples.md` lists them all, and a page that explains something a game can show gets one). Each is a standalone game in `docs/examples/<id>.tide`, included as code with `<<< @/examples/<id>.tide` inside `::: tide-example <id> <title>`. Code and the running game come from the same source, and a page shows the source of every example it runs.
  - An example starts by itself once it's in view, and stops once it's more than a screen away, so a page runs what its reader is looking at and nothing else (agreed: the owner asked why they'd have to start each one). In view is half of where it draws on screen, and still there a moment later, so scrolling past starts nothing. Stop holds until the reader presses Run, Restart starts it over, and leaving the page disposes its frames. A reader who asked their system for less motion or less data starts each one themselves. `backend=` in a docs page's address goes on to its games.
  - One comes up in one step, with nothing to watch load (the owner, 2026-10-05: the placeholder, then an empty frame, then the game was slow and distracting; about 800 ms on the live site, of which 450 an empty frame). It loads within a screen of the view, its program waiting (`wait`, see Web builds), so that once it has been in view a moment, starting it is all that's left: about 30 ms. Its frame is there unseen until its game has drawn (`Tide.start()`'s promise, then the `shown` class), over the background the examples draw on, and what it says while it loads only shows after most of a second (`.example-status.loading` in `docs/src/app.css`). A match loads its first player so, and the second once the first has its room's code. What loads in a docs build without compiled examples isn't a game (it has no `Tide.start`), which is how the page tells: no request asks first.
  - A page downloads all its examples once it has loaded and has nothing else to do (the owner, 2026-10-05: "why dont we pre-download it"), nearest the view first and one at a time, each after the examples near the view have loaded theirs (a frame's download takes no `fetchpriority`: Chrome gives every one its highest), each in a frame nobody sees with `#download` after its address (see Web builds), which is removed once it loaded (`download` in `docs/src/lib/examples.ts`). So an example the reader jumps to, or scrolls to faster than a screen ahead, has no download left: its frame asks whether its page changed (a few hundred bytes) and has it from the cache. The site sends no freshness for these pages, only an ETag, so nothing is ever stale after a deploy. Not for a reader who asked for less data or motion, nor a match's second player, whose address has the room's code; and the example in view as a page opens has no head start.
  - The page scrolls over them (`embedded`, see Web builds): the wheel always, and a finger until it taps the game, from when a swipe on the game is the game's until a tap elsewhere. A frame whose game has the focus, and so the keyboard and those swipes, shows it with an outline (`focused`, which `showsFocus` in `docs/src/lib/examples.ts` sets from the frame's own focus and blur: CSS's `:focus` isn't an iframe's whose page has the focus), and a press on the page elsewhere takes the focus back from the frame, as not every browser does that for a press on text. Sand's and Duel's frames, in their pages' Markdown, say `?embedded` in their address and show the focus the same way.
  - They're drawn for a frame about 375 pixels wide and 400 tall (360 with two players), and for a pointer first: a mouse or a finger. The keyboard is the frame's only after a click on it, which the page's text says.
  - They wear the site's colors (agreed: the owner asked for them to match), which are the dark theme's in `docs/src/app.css`, written out in each example as it has no file to share: the code background `Color(0.086, 0.086, 0.094)` behind everything, `Color(0.24, 0.25, 0.27)` for lines and `Color(0.64, 0.64, 0.68)` for quiet text, and for what stands out, the brand's amber `Color(0.98, 0.75, 0.14)` first, then the callouts' teal `Color(0.18, 0.83, 0.75)` and rose `Color(0.98, 0.44, 0.52)`. The GUI's own look is already that gray and amber, so an example with widgets only rounds them, `GUI.SetTheme(GUITheme { radius = 6 });`, but for the three whose lesson is the GUI's look. The first tutorial's two keep the tutorial's own colors.
  - Two players are two frames of one example, each a machine of its own (`docs/src/lib/examples.ts`, `docs/src/lib/server/markdown.ts`). `::: tide-players <id> <title>` starts both as they are, for the reader to bring together, like `rooms`, whose lobby is the lesson. `::: tide-match <id> [lag=<ms>] <title>` puts them in one match itself: the first frame's address says `?host`, the page waits for its room's code (the frame's `Tide.room`), and the second's says `?join=<code>` (see Web builds), so those games have a match `Main` and no lobby. A match has a slider, Ping, which sets both frames' `Tide.lag` (`lag=<ms>` is where it starts): the round trip the player who joined is to have, which that frame's game shows as its ping; the host's page adds nothing (see Web builds). Beside the slider the page writes the least it can be, "min 1 ms" (the frames' `Tide.minLag`, looked at twice a second while the match runs, and written again once two looks in a row say the same other number, as it can move by a millisecond), in the brand's color while the slider is under it. That's measured with nothing added, so a match starts with no lag, and the slider's ping starts once the least is known (`floor` in `docs/src/lib/examples.ts`), within a second of the second player joining. A container with nothing inside shows no source, for a page that has it beside another frame of the same example. They meet through the relay we host, as players anywhere do.
  - A position that a two-player example draws is `[Position]` (agreed 2026-10-05: the owner saw the other player's body jump in the first game's match), so a wrong guess about the other player glides in. Untagged, the first game's body moved 50 units in one frame at 75 ms each way, each time the other player started or stopped; tagged, 11 at most. An example one machine plays alone has nothing to correct, and stays plain; the first tutorial adds the tag where it adds the second machine.
  - Two examples are built with a package that comes with tide: `physics-2d` with Box2D and `physics-3d` with Box3D, on the pages of `docs/physics/`. `tide_add_game` reads no `tide.packages`, so `docs/examples/CMakeLists.txt` names each with its package's folder (`TIDE_DOC_PACKAGE_<id>`) and builds it as tide builds a game that lists the package: the package's `.tide` files as `PACKAGES`, so they compile first and its systems run before the example's, its `bridge.c` with the example's C, and the library's own sources as `tide_box2d` or `tide_box3d`, which `packages/CMakeLists.txt` builds for its tests. So the top `CMakeLists.txt` adds `docs/examples` after `packages`. It's the same natively and on the web, and `docs_examples` builds them with the rest, so the docs workflow builds what it did.
  - The 2D one draws no ball's turn and kicks a crate with no `turn`, on purpose: Box2D's `angle` wraps at π and isn't tagged `[Rotation]`, so a view's blend of it goes the long way round for the frame a body passes half a turn in. The physics pages say that, and the other things that trip a game up in the packages as they are (what's lost, asleep or not sent, each checked with a game), where they apply or under Not there yet: a line goes from its page when its package no longer does it.
- To build these locally: `cmake --preset web-release -DTIDE_DOC_EXAMPLES=ON`, then `cmake --build --preset web-release --target docs_examples`. The target stages their self-contained pages at `docs/static/examples/<id>/index.html`; these generated files are ignored. The docs workflow builds them and runs `npm run test:examples` after the site build (`docs/tests/examples.mjs`): headless Chrome or Edge finds every example in the Markdown and checks that each shows its source and draws once it's scrolled into view (of two players, the first frame; the second joins through a relay in the test's process, which nothing checks), that the frame of each whole game that opens on a lobby (Towers, Sumo: the frames the Markdown marks `demo-frame roomy`) draws on its page, and that each game the Samples gallery links to draws on its own page. That's all, on purpose (the owner, 2026-10-07: "its not that deep, just get the damn docs up"): the test used to drive the pages' scrolling, two players meeting through the relay, the lobbies' buttons and the phone layout in the browser, six minutes of the flakiest kind of test there is, between a docs change and the site; what a page does around a game is the platform tests' (`web_embedded`, `web_lag`, `web_rooms`) and the games' own. It lists a page's examples, and the gallery's cards, once the page's document is parsed (`document.readyState`), never as soon as the first one is there: the views page is over a megabyte of HTML, and listed at first sight it came up short of its last example, which failed a run on CI (2026-10-07; made to happen here with the page's own check in a loop, and on a slow connection nearly every time). `TIDE_BROWSER` selects a browser; `TIDE_EXAMPLE_BACKEND=webgpu` checks WebGPU too. `TIDE_EXAMPLES=<id>,<id>` runs only those (a game's name like an example's), and `TIDE_DOCS_SCREENSHOTS=<folder>` saves a picture of each as it runs in its page, to look at. A game whose page isn't in `docs/static/` fails there, so the workflow builds them first.
  - The quick way to look at one while writing it is the native build, with no browser: `cmake --preset debug -DTIDE_DOC_EXAMPLES=ON`, `cmake --build --preset debug --target docs_example_<id>` (dashes as underscores), then `build/debug/bin/docs_example_<id> --frames 40 --capture x.png --size 376 400`, or a `--script` for its clicks.
- The docs' Markdown fences Tide as `csharp`, which GitHub highlights; the site highlights it with the editors' grammar (`tools/tide-syntax`). The workflow tests the grammar first, through the site's Shiki: `npm run test:grammar` in `docs/` checks the scopes of `tools/tide-syntax/test/probe.tide`'s cases and of the compiler's test programs.

- Whole games are collected on `docs/guide/samples.md` (the owner, 2026-10-06: "a big samples page where they have a thumbnail and it opens the game in a full window", with the same room flow so people can play together). Its thumbnails open the game's own page in a new tab; the gallery starts no games. The smaller teaching examples stay in their docs pages.
  - A sample has a dedicated docs page only when it includes a tutorial, guide or code walkthrough (the owner's follow-up, 2026-10-06). Play-only examples belong in Samples. Duel has a code tour; Towers and Sumo have walkthroughs. Sand's former dedicated page was mostly play instructions and source listings, so it was removed.
  - Nine games from the docs-driven trial live in `examples/samples/<name>/`, each a `game.tide` and a `tide.packages` that lists `rooms` (the menu that comes with tide: see Rooms, under Packaging and releases), so its folder works with `tide run`, and a game opens on Play, Host a room and Join a room with no code of its own for it. Until 2026-10-07 each carried a copy of one lobby file. Their original source and the earlier public draft remain in git's history; the owner's room-flow request authorizes adapting those samples.
  - `sample_games` builds them natively, and on the web stages their pages at `docs/static/samples/<name>/index.html`, beside the gallery's checked-in screenshots in `docs/static/samples/thumbnails/`. The Docs workflow builds them, and the docs' test opens each game the gallery links to and checks it draws (see Docs site).

### The physics games

Two small games the owner asked for (2026-10-06), to show the physics packages in a match and to play with other people on other machines: Towers (`examples/towers/`, on Box2D, four players) and Sumo (`examples/sumo/`, on Box3D, eight). Towers took the place of Siege, the first Box2D game (two towers of crates and four cannons), the same day: the owner found the two games' play "a bit weird" and "a bit messy", wanted them to "make more sense" and to be "more massive in terms of physics", and picked Tricky Towers for 2D. They were toys of two files each; the owner then asked for one instance with a lobby, and polish, and for their pages to stay where they are in the sidebar (in Get started, after Duel). Their names, their rules, bots in Towers' empty seats, the lobby's pages and that players drop in with no ready gate are Claude's picks, which the owner hasn't confirmed.

Sumo took the place of Alley, a bowling game, the same day. The owner found the two games' play "a bit weird" and "similar", wanted them to "make more sense" and to be "more massive in terms of physics, like push physics more, better, higher", and picked "Smash arena, a sumo type game" for the 3D one. That a player is a heavy ball they drive, in a ring packed with physics, and shoves the others out of it is agreed. Its name, its rules (the charge, the bump and the daze, who a ring-out scores for, the rims, two minutes a round), its pickups and kegs, its bots, its camera, and that rubble breaks up are Claude's picks, which the owner hasn't confirmed.

- **Each is a folder as `tide run` builds it:** `game.tide` is the match; `play.tide` the camera and the pieces; `effects.tide` the sparks and the numbers; `hud.tide` the scores, the words and the buttons; Towers' `bots.tide` how a tower nobody steers is built; Sumo's `light.tide` its meshes and frags, and its `arena.tide` what a round's ring is built of, which is the match's too; and `tide.packages` says `box2d` or `box3d`, and `rooms`. Views say their layer (`[Stage(Layers.<x>)]`), as tide compiles a folder's files in the order of their names, which isn't the order they draw in. `tide_add_game` reads no `tide.packages`, so each one's `CMakeLists.txt` lists the files (in that order) and names its packages as `docs/examples/CMakeLists.txt` does (each package's Tide file after its name in `PACKAGES`, Box2D's or Box3D's `bridge.c` with the game's C, and `tide_box2d` or `tide_box3d`), which is why the top `CMakeLists.txt` adds them after `packages`. Their tests are in `test/`, a folder with a `tide.packages` of its own: a game's folder compiles every `.c` in it, so a test beside the game's files would be part of the game under `tide run`, and a folder with that file is no part of the game around it (`folder_find_own`).
- **The lobby and the menu are the rooms package's** (since 2026-10-07; see Rooms, under Packaging and releases, for what it does and what a game reads of it). Each had a `lobby.tide` of its own before, the same in both, whose design the package keeps: local state over a live match, never a scene (`Main` stays the match's, so the standard host starts a match of this machine's own at once, closed, and Towers' four towers build by themselves behind the title; chosen over a local `Main` like Duel's because the host refuses `--host` there, and because the title has a game running behind it for free), a machine told which match to be in with no page in its way, `Disconnected` putting a machine back on the title with a line that says why, and the player doing nothing while a page is up. What's each game's own, in `hud.tide`:
  - **`Hand.playing` is the package's `Lobby.playing`,** copied by the view that writes `Hand` (Towers' `Hands` in `play.tide`, Sumo's `Controls`), which the input's `Sample` reads (Towers' `Controls.hands`, whose match then builds the player's tower for them; Sumo's `Controls.away`, an `[Absolute]` field: a player whose page is up isn't left standing, their ball plays by itself as a bot's does, since a ball that stood still would be thrown out again and again). The HUD's views draw nothing while `lobby.page != Page.Playing`.
  - **`Roster`** tells the room's page who has each seat (`lobby.seats`, `manned`, `mine`, `bots`) and that the game has a new game and a bots' switch (`offersNewGame`, `offersBots`), and sends `Rebuild` and `Bots` in the frame the page's buttons say so (`lobby.newGame`, `toggleBots`). Play on the title sets `newGame` too, so a game of one's own starts from its start, as it did.
  - **Sumo's stick and its charge button aren't the GUI's:** a view reads them from the devices (`Controls`: every finger of the touchscreen, and the mouse), as a second finger has to hold one while the first holds the other, and the GUI follows one pointer. Towers keeps `Pill` (`GUI.Control`, drawn by the game) for the power's button. The `Menu` layer is gone from both `Layers` enums: the package's pages are modals of the GUI's, drawn over everything.
- **Seats.** Towers' four plinths are there from the start (`Towers.plinths`, by seat), and a player gets `Owner` on the first nobody has (`Towers.seats`): the join's handler goes by the singleton's map, never by a component of the plinth, as the plinths' spawn may be in the same tick's queue behind it. A seat with no player builds by itself (`bots.tide`, from `Deal`), which a command turns off, and so does the seat of a player whose lobby or menu is up (`Controls.hands`): the title has four towers building behind it, and a player who opens the menu comes back to a tower that went on. A falling brick has `Owner` too, which is whose input steers it. Sumo's eight `Seat`s are there from the start (`Ring.chairs`), and a player has the first that no player has (`Ring.manned`, a bit a seat, with `Ring.players` and `Ring.owners`). A seat that's in the match (`Live`: a player's, or one of the first that bots have until four balls are in the ring) has a ball, an entity of its own with the body, which comes and goes: `Seats` spawns it, `Roll` destroys it when it's under `OUT` and tells the seat (`Fell`), and it's back `RESPAWN` ticks later. `Hands` keeps the ball's `Owner` its seat's player, so a player who joins takes a bot's ball as it is. Every ball has a `Brain` (`Think`, with a query over the other balls), which `Drive` uses for a seat no player has and for a player whose input says `away`.
- **Towers' bricks.** Its rules are its page's (`docs/guide/towers.md`): steer the falling brick, be first to stand over the line for three seconds. What code relies on:
  - **A brick is one entity and one body from when it's dealt to the round's end** (since 2026-10-06, on what the package gained that day; before, it was a falling entity of sensors, then a dynamic one spawned in its place, then a kinematic one if it set). `towers_a_brick_is_steered_then_becomes_a_body` checks the entity and Box2D's handle stay the same.
  - **A falling brick is a kinematic body with no shapes** (`Falling`), which falls by its velocity and is put across and turned by having its position and angle written, from its owner's input. The input says where the player wants the brick and how turned, not which keys are held (`Controls.column` and `quarter`, both `[Absolute]`, worked out a frame at a time by the view `Hands` into the local `Hand`), and which brick that's about (`serial`: `Steer` only goes by an input that names the brick, so the next brick isn't sent where the last was).
  - **What's in its way is asked of the world** (the package's `Cast` and `Overlap`, through the game's `Free` and `Room`): `Steer`, before the step, turns the brick only where there's room for it turned, steps it half a cell only where it's free to, and lets it fall while it's free to go this tick's fall. A brick is never inside anything, so nothing is put back or undone. `Slim` makes each box `SLACK` smaller where it's asked about, as a cast shape touches what it's flush with: narrower only, for the way down, so that it lands flush.
  - **It lands where the cast says it touches**: its position is written there, at rest, its `type` becomes `Dynamic`, and its shapes go on, the first as its own `Box` (`this.Add`, with `Brick`) and the second, for the five bricks of two boxes, as a `Part` (`Spawn(Box, Part { of = this })`). `Fit` and its call into the package's `AttachBox` are gone, with the docs' warning. A brick lands at rest however fast it fell, which is what the two-touch landing was for: how fast a brick is when it becomes a body is what cost towers most (over 24 seeds, bots lost 25 bricks a tower a round landing at up to 5 m/s, 10 at 3, 4 at 1.5).
  - **Its view draws where it will land** (`Fallings` in `play.tide`): the same `Free`, 60 m down (`SIGHT`), asked each frame of the world the view draws, for this machine's player's brick: lanes down to an outline.
  - **Every brick is loose, from the plinth up**: nothing sets by itself (`SET_DEPTH` is gone). `Build` tunes the world for it: `substeps` 8, `contactStiffness` 60, `contactDamping` 40 (`SUBSTEPS`, `STIFFNESS`, `DAMPING`). How that was arrived at, bricks lost a tower a round by bots with no powers used: 11.0 with every brick loose at 60 and 8 with Box2D's damping of 10 (24 seeds), 7.4 at 120 and 16 substeps, then by damping at 60 and 8 over 48 seeds, 5.8 at 20, 4.5 at 40, 4.5 at 80, 6.7 at 160. The game before it, with the lower bricks solid, lost 4.8 (48 seeds), and 3.0 with the new tuning and bricks still set 10 m under the top: the towers are loose because the owner asked how much could be, not because it's steadier. `towers_a_tall_tower_is_loose_all_the_way_down` builds one to 24 m, checks no brick is set and that it's at rest a minute on with every brick where it was.
  - **Putting a tower's lower bricks to sleep isn't a way to steady it** (tried 2026-10-06, with a body's `asleep`, on the game as it was while its lower bricks were set solid, in the place of that): a tower is one island, its plinth with it, so the bricks low in it can't sleep while the ones over them are awake. Written `asleep` as they came to rest, they took the whole tower with them each time, the brick that had just landed among it, and every landing woke it all again: over 12 seeds of four bots, no tower lost a brick in 75 seconds, where they lost 8 with the bricks set, stood 35 m high where they stood 28, and no gust or quake did anything. That's no tower to bring down, and the game uses none of it.
  - **`Cement` is all that sets a brick** (`Brick.set`, `Towers.sets`): `Tend` writes the `type` of every brick at rest in the tower to `Kinematic` (through `world.Set<Body>(this, ...)`, as it reads every brick and writes a few), and `Push` gives each its plinth's velocity (`Towers.sways` and `rises`, which `Move` leaves), so a quake and a late joiner's rise carry it. When a round is over they're `Dynamic` again, from the bottom of each tower up (`CRUMBLE`), the winner's last, and everything comes down.
  - **A brick is off its tower** under `FALLEN`, or `SPILL` past its plinth's side, whatever it rests on: before the second rule, what fell between two plinths piled up to 20 m and counted as tower.
  - **A round ends.** From `LIMIT` ticks in, the line (`Towers.line`) comes down to the towers, to `LOWEST` at the least: without it, sabotage kept four bots under the line for five minutes.
  - **`bots.tide` plans from six column heights**: how far from level the top is left (squared, so the lowest columns are filled first), the room under the brick, the steps along the top, a brick whose middle would be over nothing, and one that would rest on a brick askew. A cell counts as resting on its column within 0.35 m: at 0.08, a top that wasn't level to the centimeter made every wide brick look unsupported, and the bots built spires one cell wide. Each seat is a little more or less careful, and quick, for a round (`temper`), so no two towers are alike.
  - **What the towers do to each other:** a gust is an impulse a tick, for `GUST` ticks, on the loose bricks within `WIND_DEPTH` of the top of the tallest other tower (by each brick's highest corner, not its body's place, which is under that for a brick that stands upright); a quake is every other plinth going there and back as a sine of its velocity, with what stands on it. A quake reaches all of a loose tower, where it used to carry the solid part: with every earned power made a quake, towers lost 28.9 bricks a round where the old game's lost 10.8, so `SHAKE` went from 1.2 to 0.9; `WIND` stayed 1.3. Over 48 seeds with every power in play, the game loses 16.9 bricks a tower a round, in rounds of 89 seconds (18.4 with `SHAKE` at 1.2); before, 16.1 in rounds of 98. With none used, 4.5 against 4.8.
- **Scores are by who touched last,** in Sumo: a ball remembers the seat whose ball touched it (`Ball.last`, for `BLAME` ticks; a blast counts as its owner's touch, `Rocked`), and when it goes over the rim that seat is sent `Scored`. A round is two minutes, and it's whoever threw out the most, the first to get there if two did (`Seat.round`, `Seat.scored`). Views read scores `verified`. Towers has no score but how high each tower stands (`Seat.top`, the highest corner of the bricks at rest over its plinth, which `Measure` reads with a query) and the rounds a seat has taken (`Seat.wins`).
- **What a machine shows comes from events the match sends for it,** which nothing in the match handles, so they cost it nothing: Towers' `Landed` (sent to the brick that landed, which is how its handler lights it), `Lost`, `Earned`, `Struck`, `RoundOver` and `RoundBuilt`, and the Box2D package's `Hit` (pieces fly where bricks meet faster than `CRASH`, the world's `hitSpeed`), Sumo's `RingOut`, `Clash`, `Smash`, `Burst`, `Boom`, `Taken`, `Dropped`, `Shrinking`, `Go`, `RoundOver` and `RoundSet`. Local handlers (`effects.tide`) make sparks, debris, a camera shake and the numbers that float up of them; a score's number and a round's winner are `verified` handlers'. The events carry what their handlers show (where, whose, how much), so no handler takes `Towers` or `Ring`: it could, but not name the lists and maps they hold, which aren't heard. What fades or eases (a landed brick's light, a falling brick's slide across and its swing round) is local fields.
- **Sumo's look** (`light.tide`, `play.tide`): every mesh's corners have a normal, and one function (`Lit`) lights a pixel for every frag. Nothing is drawn an entity at a time but the balls: a view for each crate adds a `Solid` to a list (`Fx.solids`), and one `Draw.Instanced` draws the list, so a frame of 2,400 crates is under thirty draw calls. Shadows are a shadow map (the next point). The floor is a `Grid2<Color>` of 15 by 15 in a local singleton (`Ground.floor`: there, or about to go), which the tiles' view writes each frame and the frags sample as a texture, for the line of light along an edge with no tile past it and for where what's flat on the floor stops. It's a singleton of its own because one with a grid can't be passed to a function a frag calls (`Room`, which `Lit` takes, has only floats), and the frags sample it themselves because a function can't (see `FloorPaint`'s parameters). Marks on the floor (the rings under the balls, a blast's edge) are one list of `Decal`s at one height (`ON_FLOOR`), drawn inside `Draw.Depth(Depth.Read)`, with no light of the ring's on them: they're the same in a shadow. The sparks, which glow and add both of a cube's sides, are inside `Draw.Cull(Cull.None)`; everything else is drawn from its front, so every mesh `light.tide` makes is wound clockwise as it's seen from outside, the floor's plate from above. The camera always looks from the same side, so the stick's up is the same way in the ring for everyone: it shows the whole ring when a ball is still 20 pixels across at that size, and as much as that takes otherwise, around this machine's ball and towards the middle, or around the middle while that ball is out (`Look`). A ball's own mark (`Tags` in `hud.tide`) is drawn in the screen's pixels, over everything, as towers hide balls.
- **Sumo's shadows are a shadow map** (2026-10-07, from the raw pieces the owner asked for where a shadows feature might have been: a target of depth alone, and a frag that compares with it; see Rendering and platform, A target says what it holds). Until then its casters were drawn a second time inside `Draw.Transform(Flatten())`, a matrix that slid every point along the lamp's light onto the floor, through a frag that painted the floor again there, darker: shadows fell on the floor and on nothing else. That it's a shadow map is the owner's; every choice here is Claude's, which the owner hasn't confirmed. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **The lamp's image** is `Shadow.map`, `RenderTarget(LAMP_PIXELS, LAMP_PIXELS, 1, Format.Depth)`, which the view `Cast` makes the first time it runs (`Shadow.made`: nothing says whether a target was made), with `Shadow.depth`, its `DepthTexture`, and `Shadow.toLamp`, the world to the lamp's clip space, which `Cast` reads each frame from the target's own camera (`shadow.map.WorldToClip()`, since the cameras are read as matrices; it spelled the turn of z out itself before). `Shadow` is a singleton of its own that each frag takes itself. Neither of the ways round that compiles: a singleton with a target in it can't be passed to a function a frag calls ("Shadow can't go to the GPU"), and `Compare` in a function is an error ("in a frag or a vert"), through the singleton or a `DepthTexture` parameter. So `FromLamp` is handed the matrix as a `float4x4`, and the four frags that light something (`TileLook`, `CrateLook`, `BallLook`, `Plain`) each have the same four `Compare`s, `LAMP_SOFT` apart, whose mean they hand `Lit` (`sun`).
  - **`Cast` is at `Layers.Lamp`, before anything draws to the window,** whose `Draw.Clear` moved from `Look` to `Floor` to be with the window's other draws: a frame's list starts with the lamp's target, which `sumo_effects_come_from_what_the_match_sends` checks. That's still three passes as the renderer is, one of them empty, and not two: `draw_list` (`platform/src/platform.c`) begins the window's pass as a frame starts, whatever the list's first command is, ends it at the lamp's target with nothing drawn, and begins it again after, with what was there. A renderer that began the window's pass at the first command for it would make such a frame two; nothing here measured what the empty one costs (on a desktop GPU the whole frame is 0.1 ms), and it's reported to the owner, not changed.
  - **What casts is the lists the window is drawn from,** with no frag and no vert, so an instance sends its transform and nothing else: `fx.tiles`, `fx.solids` and `fx.gems` with the box, which are one run as they're drawn in a row (2,458 instances with a ring standing), then `fx.rounds` and `fx.orbs` with the ball. With the clear that's 3 of a frame's draw calls, where the flattened shadows were 2. Pickups cast too, so they're gathered into lists (`Beacons`) that `Pickups` draws, where each was a draw of its own. The tiles cast, so what falls past the far rim goes into the floor's shadow. `Solid.low`, `Flatten`, `ShadowDrop` and `ShadowLook` are gone. The lamp's pass has a target's first cull: what's stored is each caster's side towards the lamp.
  - **The image is fixed to the ring as a round starts:** a box 40 m across (`LAMP_SPAN`: the floor is 39.1 m across and 31.2 up the image, where a stack 13 m high leans 7.8 more), its middle 2.9 m up the image from the ring's (`LAMP_RAISED`), seen from 60 m off along the light with no perspective. It doesn't follow the ring as the ring shrinks, on purpose: a box that changed size would slide its pixels over the ring, and every shadow's edge would crawl. As it is, a standing crate's shadow is the same pixels every frame.
  - **1,024 pixels a side** is 3.9 cm of the ring a pixel: half a pixel of the docs' frame (375 wide, where the camera shows 30 m, 8 cm a pixel), one at a display scale of 2, one and a half at 3, and about one in a window of 1080 lines that shows the whole ring (4.6 cm). Looked at beside 512, which blurs a crate's shadow at a scale of 3, and 2,048, which is sharper there and takes 0.03 ms more of the GPU here and up to 0.05 (0.08 to 0.11 at the docs' frame, 0.12 to between 0.15 and 0.17 at a scale of 3), and 16 MB where this takes 4.
  - **The bias is off the surface, not nearer the lamp** (`FromLamp`): a pixel is looked up from `normal * step * (0.4 + 1.8 * slant)` off its place, `step` a pixel of the image and `slant` the sine of the angle between its normal and the lamp, which is as far as four looks 0.75 of a pixel apart reach, each blended over the pixels around it. Tried, at the docs' frame with a display scale of 3: with none of it everything lit is striped; with half of it tumbling crates have a moiré (44,653 pixels of 1.35 million more than 8 of 255 off); a depth bias of a centimeter on top changed 206 of them, at shadows' edges, and is left out. A pixel of the floor beside a crate is lifted, and still behind the crate's side, so a shadow starts at its crate.
  - **A pixel outside the image is lit** (`FromLamp` gives it a depth of 0): past a depth's edge both reads give the pixels at the edge, so a caster across the edge would smear over everything beyond it, which an image of 22 m showed.
  - **In shadow** the lamp's light and its shine go, and `Lit` adds a little blue there (`dusk`), picked so that the floor in shadow is the paint the flattened shadows were: a pixel of it is (39, 43, 60) of 255 in a picture from before and in one from after, beside (77, 84, 103) in the light. A face that looks away from the lamp gets none of the lamp's light, so it's the same in a shadow and out of one, and whatever the lamp's image says of a caster's far side shows nowhere. The line of light along the floor's edge is its own light, where a flattened shadow dimmed it.
  - **Nothing of the match changed:** the pins are what they were, in debug, release, web-debug and web-release, and a run with no window is the same crates in the same places, frame for frame, before and after.
  - **Measured** (2026-10-07, release, an RTX 4080 SUPER, three runs each taken in turn with the build before, which agreed to the hundredth but where a range is given):
    - A frame's views with 2,345 crates standing (`sumo_costs`): 0.16 ms before and 0.15 to 0.17 after, for 33 commands in the draw list where there were 29; under Node 0.18, and 0.17 to 0.18.
    - Draw calls, by the stats' drawer in a run with no window: 21 to 22 with a ring standing and four balls, 25 to 26 in a fight.
    - The drawer's `gpu` line on OpenGL 3.3, a ring standing and then a fight: 0.12 ms to 0.11 and 0.10 to 0.10 at 1920 by 1080; 0.07 to 0.08 and 0.05 to 0.08 at the docs' 376 by 400; 0.15 to 0.12 for both at that size with a display scale of 3. The lamp's pass costs what it costs whatever the window's size; the flattened shadows ran a frag that samples the floor five times over every crate's shadow, each over the others', which grew with the window.
    - On WebGPU, in headless Chrome on the same GPU with timestamp queries, at 1920 by 1080: 0.20 to 0.20 with a ring standing, 0.19 to 0.15 in a fight (two runs each, of matches that differ, as a page's seed does).
    - With no GPU (Chrome's software rasterizers, 1000 by 640, a run each): WebGL 2 at 18 frames a second before and after, and WebGPU from 50 ms a frame to 55.
  - Not done: an image that follows the ring or the camera, for finer pixels late in a round. Android wasn't run, nor OpenGL on a Mac, which nothing runs.
- **Sumo uses none of what the Box3D package gained on 2026-10-06 yet** (parts, a type that changes, questions, `Inside`, `Hit`, tuning: see Box3D): it landed while they were being built, and adopting them is still to do. What they'd take the place of: a ball that turns heavy is destroyed and spawned again with a bigger `Sphere` (`Gain`, `Roll`), where its sphere could be a part that's swapped under a ball that stays; a tile that falls is a static body destroyed and a loose one spawned in its place (`Crumble`: "a body's type is read once"), where its type could change; how hard things met is worked out from the speed each ball had before the step (`Ball.before`, in `Bump`, `Fuse`, `Knock` and what `Smash` shows), which `Hit` says; and a blast looks at every loose body for the ones in reach (`Blasts`), which `Overlap` answers.
  - **Its two pins were taken again with the package, and its physics are as they were.** What moved them is the engine's, not the package's: each bot's `Brain` has a `Random`, a `Random` field is seeded with its component's number among all the program's components (the last argument of `tide_random_seed_place` in generated C), and a package's components come before a game's, so the package gaining one (`Part`) gave every bot other dice (2560 to 2816 for `Brain.dice`). The old package with one empty component added plays `sumo_bots_stay_on_and_fight_and_the_next_round_is_raised` to the same numbers as the new one, crate for crate. So a package that gains a component changes the match of every game with a `Random` in a component, which a game's author can't see coming: reported to the owner, and not changed here.
- **What they use of the packages,** which their pages say with the code: static, kinematic and dynamic bodies; every shape, several on a body; an impulse and an angular one where a body is spawned, and later from a system that's `[Before(PhysicsStages.Step)]` (Towers' gust, Sumo's drive, its charge and its blasts); `bullet`, damping and `gravityScale`; `Touched` between solid bodies, and a sensor's `Touched` and `Parted`; `Physics.gravity` written during play; a body's position written to put it elsewhere, with `Snap`; entities destroyed. Towers also, of what the Box2D package gained on 2026-10-06: a shape as a `Part`, shapes added to a body that's there, a body's `type` written (kinematic to dynamic as a brick lands, dynamic to kinematic and back for Cement), `Cast` and `Overlap` from a system before the step and from a view, `Hit`, and the world's tuning. It uses no sensor and hears no `Touched` any more. Sumo's are boxes and spheres, static and dynamic: thousands of bodies that sleep until they're hit; an impulse every tick as a force (`Drive`); a body replaced where its type or its shape would have to change, which are read once (a tile that falls is destroyed and a loose one spawned in its place, and so is a ball that grows heavy); impulses written into other entities' bodies through the `World`, from a query, only in a tick that has a blast (`Blasts`); `Touched` between balls for the bump; a sensor under everything that takes what falls (`Pit`) and small ones that are picked up; `Physics.gravity` set once, to 18.
- **Tests:** `towers_tests` and `sumo_tests` (`test/towers_test.c`, `test/sumo_test.c`), with no window. Each checks what its game promises on one world; runs its views and local handlers a frame at a time, as Duel's test does, for where the lobby leaves a machine by how it started (nothing said, a match that's open, joining, a match that ended, a code nobody has) and for the effects an event makes; and plays a match on the loopback network, 40 ms each way with jitter and 5% of packets lost: a server and three clients, of whom one joins in the middle of a round, with commands sent meanwhile. No client may be sent the world again (`resyncs == 0`, the late ones' too), and the server's world is pinned twice, mid-match and after a Reset: the same hashes in debug, release, web-debug and web-release. A change to a game's match moves its pins, and so does one to its package or to the netcode: update them from one platform, then confirm the others agree. Towers' tests have a tower over the line by putting its plinth where the tower's top would be (a kinematic body goes where its position is written), and get towers to test with by having every seat build by itself for a while, then stopping the bots and letting everything come to rest (`grow`). A gust is tested on one brick put on each plinth by hand: the tops bots build are too interlocked for a gust to move a brick far enough to check. `towers_a_falling_brick_keeps_out_of_what_is_in_its_way` steers one into a brick beside it and asks another to turn where it has no room. Its match's late joiner also has to be no more than 15 ticks ahead of what's confirmed a second after it's in. The matches there start with no lobby: a C host drives the world, and the lobby is a machine's own. Sumo's late joiner has the same check, and its test says what the joiner was sent and how long that took.
- **Sumo's tests start most of their matches with less on the ring than the game does** (2026-10-06: the whole of `sumo_tests` took three to five minutes in a debug build, and 1.15 GB, with a match of four players on whole rings, which is too much for a suite every session runs). A whole ring is 2,400 bodies, and every machine of a match keeps one. That the scene says how much is stacked is Claude's pick, for the tests, which the owner hasn't confirmed. What code relies on:
  - **How far out a match's rings are stacked is its scene's value,** `Main.stacked`, in tiles from the middle: every tile (`TILES`), as the game starts every match, the middle's pile alone with 1, and a bare floor with 0. `Build` leaves it in `Ring.stacked`, which `Plan` goes by, so a new game's ring is stacked as far, and a player who joins gets it with the world. A host starts a match with another value as `Session.Start(Main { stacked = 2 })` would: `tide_world_start(w, dt, &start)` and `tide_server_desc.start`, with a `tide_start` that names the scene and its values (`stacked` in the test). It's no compile-time switch: the game that's tested is the game. A whole ring's plan is what it was, die for die.
  - What a ball does (driving, the charge, a ring-out, the bump, a heavy ball, seats, the lobby's pages) is tested on a bare floor, and what's about the stacks on a whole ring: how each arrangement stands, the rims, kegs, rubble, a shock, bots, the effects, the costs. A test puts the round's clock on rather than wait (`played`, which sets `Ring.timer`): half a minute of a ring at rest is two thousand ticks of nothing. The bots' test is twenty seconds at a whole ring (they stay on, and meet) and, on a ring with only its middle's pile, the last half minute of a round, on what the third rim leaves, which is where they throw each other out.
  - **The match over the network** (`sumo_three_players_stay_in_one_match_over_a_bad_network`) is stacked two tiles out: the middle's pile and four cabins, 284 crates, 0.6 MB of Box3D's state that grows to 1 MB. Two players from the start, with two bots; a third joins at tick 420, with crates knocked about, and is sent 0.5 MB in under a second; the second turns the bots off; the first pin is at tick 640; the third asks for a new game; the second pin is at tick 880, a second into the new round. The players' inputs go by where the server has their balls (`play`: for the middle and a charge on the way, then one of eight ways), so they keep hitting the pile and each other, and each runs hundreds of ticks again.
  - **A whole ring over the same network** is the test after it, with one player: a server whose four bots have been at a whole ring for three seconds, and a player who joins then, is sent 2.4 MB in two seconds, with the match going on meanwhile, and a second later has to be no more than 15 ticks ahead of what's confirmed, with the world never sent again. Nobody else's input is guessed there (bots have none), so it runs few ticks again (13). What a wrong guess costs at a whole ring is `rerun`'s to measure, on one world, in `sumo_costs`.
  - `sumo_costs` prints what the game's page and What Sumo costs, below, say, and checks nothing. In a build that isn't optimized it measures over a tenth of the ticks (`MEASURED`, by `__OPTIMIZE__`), as its numbers mean nothing there, and says so.
  - All of `sumo_tests` takes 9 seconds in debug and in release, 16 in web-debug and 20 in web-release (half of that `sumo_costs`), with 260 MB in use at the most; the match is 2.5 seconds natively and 4 under Node. The debug figures are with Box2D's and Box3D's own sources built optimized in debug builds too (`packages/CMakeLists.txt`), which took the old tests from 190 seconds to 75 on this machine by itself. Never run on Android's emulator or under the sanitizers, which CI does.
- **Rooms through a relay aren't in the tests.** Checked by hand (2026-10-06), for both games, with pages of the web build in headless Chrome, each a machine of its own: one hosts by the lobby's Host a room, a second joins by typing the code into the lobby, shots are fired, a third joins by a `?join=<code>` link, and a fourth types a code nobody has and is told so; each one's menu shows who has which seat; then the host's page closes, another machine takes the match over, and it goes on with the list updated. Once with a relay on this machine, and once through `wss://relay.tide-engine.dev`, the relay the web build names itself, with every player's page built from the same checkout: machines of different builds can't share a match. Towers was checked the same way the day it took Siege's place (2026-10-06), with bricks steered by the keys and dropped by a press that's held in each page where Siege's fired shots, the third joining in the middle of a round onto the plinth a tower had built itself on; and once more with the host leaving by its menu's Leave the room, which puts it back on its title, in a match of its own, while the others go on. An earlier round also ran three desktop programs with no window, the game on a host of its own in real time as `platform/tests/rooms_native.c` runs its game (the standard host's runs with no window make time up, so they can't meet another machine), which said their confirmed world's hash every 60 ticks of `Time.tick`, and agreed at each. A session's own tick count starts over when a match changes hands, so such a check goes by the world's. Sumo was checked the same way when it took Alley's place (2026-10-06), with a relay on this machine and through the deployed one: the second joins by Enter in the code's field, the third by a link in the middle of a round, with crates in the air, and a bot has the host's seat once it's gone.
- **What they cost,** release, with everything moving: Towers about 0.07 ms a tick late in a round and 0.03 to keep and hash its world (150 bodies, 120 of them bricks on the towers, loose but for the towers that used Cement, 245 KB of Box2D's state), 0.01 with everything at rest, and 0.1 with every tower coming down at once (260 KB); Sumo is another size: see What Sumo costs. In a browser, under Node, on WebAssembly's SIMD: 0.11 (0.14 coming down). Ten ticks of Towers run again from a world kept ten ticks back, Box2D's world made again from its image first and each tick kept and hashed, take 0.9 ms (1.2 coming down), and 1.2 (1.6) under Node, which are its page's numbers, from a quiet sitting: `towers_costs` says all of these, in how many of its ticks the world stepped (none, at rest: it waits until nothing has stepped for a second with no fallen brick left to clear, as clearing one wakes what lay on it), and what a player who joins 75 seconds into a round is sent, 150 KB. Against the game before it (lower bricks solid, 4 substeps), old and new in turn on a machine other sessions were building on, so half as slow again as the numbers above, and before the web had SIMD, the least of nine runs natively and of five under Node: a tick building 0.109 then and 0.108 now (0.142 and 0.163 under Node), at rest 0.012 and 0.010, coming down 0.153 and 0.166 (0.222 and 0.251), ten ticks again 0.74 and 0.89 (1.23 and 1.36), and 1.59 and 1.76 coming down (1.97 and 2.30): twice the substeps and every brick awake cost about a fifth more where it's most, with fewer bodies on the towers at the moment that's measured. Box2D's image written as what changed (see Box2D) does little here, as nearly everything in a round is awake and an image is whole past half: 84 of 300 images are in parts late in a round and 2 of 150 while the towers come down, which `towers_costs` says. Old and new in turn, the least of twelve runs natively and of eight under Node: a tick building 0.075 and 0.073 (0.110 and 0.106), coming down 0.108 and 0.101 (0.133 and 0.131), ten ticks again 0.85 and 0.84 (1.17 and 1.12), and 1.24 and 1.21 coming down (1.55 and 1.48): nothing slower, and the pins as they were. So Towers is nowhere near what a frame has room for: what sets its size is how many bricks a player places in a round (about one every two seconds, so 60 a tower in two minutes) and how much of four towers shows in the docs' frame, 375 pixels wide, which is also why it has four seats and not eight. A frame's views take 0.2 ms in Sumo with a full ring standing, by its test.
- **What Sumo costs, and what its ring is shaped by.** It's as much physics as a match takes with the package as it is: about 2,400 crates on 113 tiles, 3.8 MB of Box3D's state as a round starts. Release, one thread, the world kept and hashed after each tick, and in a browser's WebAssembly under Node (`sumo_costs`; the machine was building other things, so these are the quieter runs). The browser's numbers were taken on Box3D's scalar path. On WebAssembly's SIMD (2026-10-06, see Box3D) the same scenes, each the least of seven runs in turn with the scalar build, took a tenth to a quarter less (four bots 1.24 ms to 1.12, the shock 2.95 to 2.27, coming to rest 3.5 to 3.0, five ticks again 10.4 to 9.7, ten 14.0 to 13.5), on a day whose scalar runs were that much over the numbers here: so the numbers here stand as they are, to within what a day does to them.
  - A fresh ring at rest: 0.03 ms a tick (0.04 in a browser). Four bots at a full ring: 0.4 to 0.8 (1.1), of which Box3D's step is 0.2 to 0.35, with 0.2 to 0.45 more to keep and hash the world. The worst, a shock beside the middle's pyramid: 1.2 to 1.6 for two seconds (2.3). Raising a ring: 1.5 to 2 to plan and spawn it and 9 for the tick that makes its bodies (7 and 25); it was then 1.5 a tick (3.1) for the second it took to come to rest, and since the ring is put to sleep after its one step (2026-10-07, `Settle`: see below) it's one tick of about 3 (6) and then 0.09 (0.5) a tick while the balls come to rest.
  - A wrong guess: Box3D's world made again from the image and five ticks run again take 5.5 ms (10 in a browser), ten take 6.6 (14); the first of them takes 1.8 to 2.1 (3.2 to 3.7) where a tick takes 0.45 to 0.75, for the image read and the next one written whole. So the engine's input delay (past 4 ms of ticks to run again, by a moving mean of what its predicted ticks took: `tick_cost`) starts at about six ticks ahead on a desktop and three in a browser while a full ring is coming down: in a browser, at nearly any ping.
  - **Measured again 2026-10-06, and what was done about it.** The numbers above still held at HEAD that day (release, a quiet machine, three runs within 5% of each other: 0.66 ms a tick with four bots at a full ring and 0.28 to keep and hash it, 5.5 ms for five ticks again, 0.75 for a tick that restores). `sumo_costs` counted its own bookkeeping in them, a copy of each image to count the blocks that changed: about 0.09 ms a tick, which it takes off since (`counted`), and it says how many of an image's 16 KiB blocks a tick changes. Where a client's tick goes at a full ring, natively (a profile of the tick's systems, with the image and the list's write-back timed apart): Box3D's step 0.30 ms; its image 0.11 (0.19 each written whole, 0.09 in parts); the match's list taking it 0.08; hashing the world after 0.28; `Enter`, `Leave` and all of the game's own systems together 0.03. So the game's Tide costs next to nothing, and keeping the physics state costs more than the physics. Why: 119 of the image's 253 blocks change in a tick, for about 210 KB of bytes that differ, as what's awake is scattered through arrays that go by id (the contacts' section alone is 49 KB that differ, in 54 blocks; the pairs' 171 bytes, in 8), so about 1.9 MB a tick is copied and hashed. And 186 of 600 images are written whole, 166 of them because the contacts' count grew, which moves every manifold after the contacts.
    - Two changes came of it, neither of which moved a pin: the web compares memory 64 bytes at a time (`tide_same_bytes`: see Web builds), and a world made from an image goes on from it in parts (see Box3D). Old and new in turn, the least of four runs natively and of three under Node, by the `sumo_costs` that takes its bookkeeping off. Natively, where only the second applies: a tick as it was (0.58 with four bots at a full ring, 0.9 to 1.1 after a shock), five ticks again 5.1 ms to 5.0, ten 7.1 to 6.9. Under Node: four bots at a full ring 1.13 ms a tick to 0.96, the 25 seconds after 0.71 to 0.53, a shock 1.56 to 1.18 and the two seconds after it 1.89 to 1.45, a ring coming to rest 2.4 to 2.0, five ticks again 7.9 to 7.1 and ten 11.0 to 8.5, the first of them 2.95 to 1.99. So input delay starts at about seven ticks ahead on a desktop and four in a browser, where it was six and three.
    - A third came the same day: what changed of a world is hashed on a few of the pool's threads (see ECS and simulation state), which takes keeping and hashing a full ring's world from 0.28 ms to 0.105 natively and to 0.117 under Node, where a page has threads.
    - A fourth, 2026-10-07: a page's hash is four chains side by side (`tide_hash_wide`, see ECS and simulation state), which takes the same keeping and hashing to 0.12 ms on one thread natively and 0.135 under Node, and with threads called in to 0.06 and 0.077 (after a shock: 0.19 and 0.23, and 0.095 and 0.14). So what a full ring's tick pays to be hashed is about a fifth of what it was two days before, and a tenth of the tick.
    - What's left, by what it would buy, none of it done. **The image's layout**: room kept after the contacts so that a count that grows moves no manifold (the 166 whole images, about 0.05 ms a tick), and the pairs kept so that a change doesn't touch 8 blocks; each moves Box3D's and Sumo's pins. **The restore**: 0.5 ms natively for 4 MB, most of it freeing and allocating a manifold for each contact, five arrays for each sleeping set and three for each island (0.12 to free what's there, 0.11 for the sets, 0.10 for the contacts, 0.07 for the shapes' hulls, 0.05 for the islands). **Box3D's step** is the rest, and upstream's.
  - A player who joins three seconds into a round is sent 2.4 MB, in two seconds on the test's network. Later in a round it's up to 2.6, as the state grows.
  - **A column stands 16 cm from the next** (`PITCH`), and everything is square to the floor. Box3D makes a contact for every two bodies whose bounds, each grown by 7 cm, overlap, touching or not, and each contact is about 430 bytes of the image. Crates 12 cm apart had seven contacts each, and the same ring's state was 13 MB where it's 4.5 with them 16 cm apart (4.4 KB a body, where a column's crate takes 1.5). A wall of bricks with such gaps doesn't stand (an end brick's middle is past the brick under it), so walls are rows one shorter than the row below.
  - **Rubble breaks up** (`Knock`, `Break`: a crate that's touched while it goes faster than 2.4 m/s has four to six seconds left). Without it, what four balls bring down lies in the middle of a floor that's getting smaller, most of it awake, and a tick took 2 ms by the end of a round.
  - **The image doesn't get smaller.** Box3D's arrays keep their room, so the state is 4.8 MB by the end of a first round, with a few dozen bodies left, and the next ring's is that or more.
  - **A ring is put to sleep once it has had one step** (`Settle` and the sparse `Fresh` in `arena.tide`; built 2026-10-06 with a body's `asleep`, and the way of it is Claude's pick). It's stacked where it rests, and Box3D stepped all 2,400 of its bodies for a second before it let them sleep, every image written whole. What was tried, release, the least of four or five runs in turn with the game before:
    - **Spawned asleep** (`Body { asleep = true }`): the second after a raise takes 0.16 ms a tick where it took 3.4 (the machine was busy for all of these), and a player who joins a whole ring is sent 1.7 MB where it was 2.4. But each crate is then a sleeping set and an island of its own until it's first hit, and a stack that's hit wakes a crate a tick: a shock beside the middle's pyramid took 4.8 ms a tick where it took 2.8, with Box3D's image 1.6 of it where it was 0.7, and four bots at a full ring had the image at 0.55 where it was 0.38. The worst ticks are the ones the game has least room for, so it isn't what's built.
    - **Awake for one step, then put to sleep**, which is what's built: in that step Box3D finds what rests on what, so a stack sleeps as one and wakes as one, as it did once it had come to rest by itself. The image is the size it was (3.78 MB), the ticks of a round cost what they did (a shock 1.5 where it took 1.6, four bots 1.13 where it took 1.14), and the second after a raise takes 0.11 ms a tick where it took 2.2, after 3.2 for the tick that puts it all to sleep. Woken all at once, a ring comes to rest in 61 to 67 ticks, the furthest crate 0.11 to 0.13 m from where it was stacked, which is what it did by itself before (`sumo_raises_a_ring_that_stands` wakes each arrangement so, with `asleep`, and checks it stands).
    - **`Settle` takes `Body` to read and puts the ones it changed back through the `World`.** It runs for the crates that have `Fresh`, which is none in nearly every tick, but a system that takes `Body` as `mut` has every crate's lanes copied and compared in every tick once a snapshot shares them, as a client's do: written so at first, a fresh ring at rest took 0.18 ms a tick in a browser where it takes 0.04, and 0.035 natively where it takes 0.03.
  - **The input sends nothing while nothing can be driven** (`Hand.live`: not while a ring is counted in, nor in the last 45 ticks before it's cleared). An input that stays as it was is guessed right, and a tick that was guessed right isn't run again: the ticks a ring is raised in, and put to sleep in, are the ones a client can least afford to run several times a frame. A new game asked for from the menu can still land in ticks that are run again.
  - **The stick is one of 32 ways, all the way over or let go** (`Sample`), so that a player who turns changes their input a few times a second, not every tick.
- **Looking at one with no window:** `build/debug/bin/towers --script play.txt --size 376 400` (see Rendering and platform). A script presses the title's Play first (`key enter`, from the third frame: the page's first button takes the focus a frame after it comes up), then `key leftArrow` and `key rightArrow` step the brick across, `key upArrow` turns it and `key downArrow down` drops it; a wider window (`--size 1000 640`) shows all four towers. With `--host` there's no lobby to click through. Sumo is driven by keys: `key w down`, `key space down` and `up` for a charge, or `touch` lines for the stick. A run that opens a room (`--host`, or a click on Host a room) reaches the relay for real, with no window too: set `TIDE_RELAY` to one on this machine (`node relay/main.mjs 8791`, `ws://127.0.0.1:8791`) for such a run.

## Packaging and releases

Users get Tide as the `tide` command, not this repo: see README.md.

- `tide run [--host [port] | --join code | --connect address]`, `tide build [--release] [--web | --android]`, `tide schedule`, `tide shaders`, `tide add`, `tide update`, `tide fetch`, `tide publish`, `tide editors`, `tide upgrade` and `tide version` (`compiler/cli/`, owned by Claude). It runs tidec's front end in-process, compiles the generated C and the engine's sources with the determinism flags (as separate files, like the CMake build), and links the prebuilt platform layer. `tide run` reloads the game as its files change (see Hot reloading), but for a run with no window (`--capture`, `--frames`, `--script`: see Rendering and platform), which runs once: tide makes the files' paths whole from where it was run, since the game runs in its own folder.
- Packages have clang and lld built into tide (`TIDE_EMBED_LLVM`, `compiler/cli/llvm/cc.cpp`), so users need no compiler: `tide cc` is clang, whose compiles run in tide's process and whose links go to lld in it. tide links the static libraries of LLVM's own release, downloaded once into `build/llvm-<version>` and pinned (`cmake/LLVM.cmake`, which also builds the zlib and zstd they were built with, and on Windows libxml2; unpacking needs the `zstd` program). macOS's release holds them as LLVM bitcode, so tide links there with that release's lld. Builds of this repo without the option use an installed clang (`--web` needs `wasm-ld`, next to it or on `PATH`).
- tide is nearly all LLVM, so its size is what of LLVM it links and how (the owner, 2026-10-06: "if we can make it smaller we should"). Only what tide builds with is in it:
  - **The targets tide doesn't build for are left out.** lld's drivers register every target LLVM's release was built with, for link-time optimization (`InitializeAllTargets` and the like, which are inline, so lld's own objects name each target's functions), and naming a target's `LLVMInitialize...` functions links the whole of it: 7 targets in LLVM's Windows release, 20 in its Linux one, which were a third of tide for Linux (AMDGPU alone 28 MB). `cc.cpp` defines those functions, empty, for every target but tide's three (x86-64, arm64 and WebAssembly), from LLVM's own lists (`llvm/Config/Targets.def`, `AsmPrinters.def`, `AsmParsers.def`), so the linker never takes theirs out of the libraries, which stay on the link line. A target a new LLVM adds is left out with no change here. A left-out target's object taken some other way is a duplicate symbol, an error; a kind of function LLVM starts naming for every target (a disassembler's, say) takes the targets' libraries again, which shows as tide growing by tens of megabytes, and gets a line in `cc.cpp`.
  - **The same for Polly,** which LLVM's Linux release builds into every pass pipeline (`llvm/Support/Extension.def`): each extension there is one that registers nothing. tide never asks for it, and `tide cc -mllvm -polly` is an unknown option.
  - **What nothing reaches is left out, functions that are the same code are one, and there's no table of names** (`cmake/LLVM.cmake`, on `tide_cc`, in Release builds). Windows' linker (lld-link) does the first two by itself where there's no debug info, and a program there has no symbol table: the explicit `/OPT:REF,ICF` gives the same bytes. Linux and macOS have to be told: on Linux `--gc-sections` and `-s`, and `--icf=safe` with lld, which the build links tide with where clang finds one (CI's does; GNU ld folds nothing, and tide is 8 MB more); on macOS `-dead_strip`, `-x`, `--icf=safe` and `-no_exported_symbols`, as nothing loads into tide, which makes every name one the table leaves out. So a crash of tide's own says addresses, not functions, on every platform. macOS's lld keeps the code it made of LLVM's bitcode in a cache that CI keeps between runs, under keys that leave out most of how the code is made (whether a function's address matters, which `--icf=safe` goes by): a change to that takes another number in the cache's name in `build.yml` (`lto-2-`).
  - lld's link-time optimization still works for tide's three targets, and `tide cc` for another target says there's none, as it did (cc.cpp never registered the others for clang).
  - Sizes, in millions of bytes, from 0.3.0-nightly.73 to nightly.75: tide went from 120.9 to 105.0 for Windows, from 210.1 to 99.5 for Linux and from 192.2 to 92.7 for macOS, and their packages from 63.2 to 59.1, from 71.9 to 45.2 and from 67.5 to 46.0. A link map says where the bytes are (`-Wl,-Map=<file>` with lld, `/lldmap:<file>` with lld-link), a line a function with its library.
  - **tide keeps LLVM's own release libraries, which are built for speed, with profiles** (decided 2026-10-06: "i prefer speed for sure"). An LLVM of our own built for size (MinSizeRel, the three targets, no static analyzer) made tide for Linux 78.3 MB where it's 99.4, and its clang took 1.3 to 1.5 times as long to compile a game, which is every build and every hot reload. The same LLVM built for speed was no smaller than the release's, and 1.1 to 1.2 times slower, having no profiles. So no size is bought with how fast tide compiles.
- Generated game and launcher objects are reused in `.tide` when their C contents, generated header, installed headers, compiler and flags are unchanged, in builds and within a hot-reload run. Tide still checks and generates the whole game and links each build; this is object reuse, not per-file incremental Tide analysis. Replacing installed headers requires restarting a running `tide run`.
- The package brings the C library for web games (wasi-libc) and for Windows games, which build for MinGW-w64 (see MinGW builds): Microsoft's C runtime can't ship with tide, and the UCRT is part of Windows, so players need nothing else. Linux games use the system's C development files, and macOS games the SDK of Apple's command-line tools (tide sets `SDKROOT`).
- A game is a folder of `.tide` files. tide keeps its work in `<folder>/.tide/` (hidden on Windows too, and it ignores itself in git) and puts `tide build` output in `<folder>/build/`.
- A game's packages are what its `tide.packages` lists (`docs/tools/packages.md`, `docs/spec.md`, Packages): git repositories, each pinned to a commit, folders, and the packages that come with tide (below). No lock file: the game's file lists every package it builds with, those its packages need included, and its line wins. `compiler/lsp/packages.c` reads the file and finds each package's folder (tide and tidels share it). `compiler/cli/fetch.c` downloads a commit as its host's archive, and reads branches and tags from git's `info/refs`, with curl and tar as `tide upgrade` does, so users need no git; commits are kept once for every game (`TIDE_PACKAGES`, or `%LOCALAPPDATA%/Tide/packages` and `~/.tide/packages`). A commit unpacks in a folder of that tide's own, and its files move into the commit's folder one by one, never as a folder (`fetch_place`): Windows renames no folder while a program has anything in it open, as antivirus has each new file for a moment, and a file such a program has open still moves. So a commit's folder is whole when it has `.tide-fetched` (`packages_fetched`), which goes in last, and one without it is downloaded again; a file that's there already is that commit's, so a download goes on from one that stopped and two tides can get a commit at once. `compiler/cli/packages_cmd.c` is `tide add` and `tide update`, which only change the file's commits and add what packages need. Packages' files compile first, each package after those it needs, then the game's (`compile_inputs`), and their C with the game's. A folder with a `tide.packages` is a game or a package of its own, never part of the one around it (`folder_find_own`). Tests stand in for git hosts with `TIDE_GIT_MIRROR` (`compiler/cli/tests/packages_test.cmake`).
- Packages that come with tide are in its installation, and a game opts into one with a line: one that doesn't list it compiles and links nothing of it. How the line is written and where the package is looked up are picks (Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Packages, awaiting the owner). What's built, and what code relies on:
  - The line is a name alone, lower-case letters and digits after a letter (`packages_is_builtin` in `compiler/lsp/packages.c`): `box2d`. Nothing else in the file is written so, as a git source has a `/`, a folder starts with `.` or is absolute, and `package` and `tide` take a second word. It's `package_line.builtin`, apart from `local`: no commit and no `@ref`, each an error that says it's the version this tide came with.
  - The package is the folder `<name>/` of the one folder that holds them, which tide and tidels say as they start (`packages_set_builtin_folder`); until then no package comes with tide, as in tests that say nothing. In an installation that's `<root>/src/packages/`, beside `src/engine/`, as both are sources that compile with the game. tide says its own: `<root>` is the folder its `bin` is in (`install_root` in `compiler/cli/main.c`), as for `include/` and `src/engine/` (`build_setup`), so a tide built from this repo has them once `cmake --install` put them beside it, as it has the engine's files. tidels says its installation's (`installed_root` in `compiler/lsp/main.c`, the folder above its own with `include/tide/math.h`) or, in a build of the repo, the repo's `packages/` (`TIDE_SOURCE_DIR`), as its engine headers are the repo's then.
  - This repo's are in `packages/`, a folder each, which every tree installs as `src/packages/` (the top `CMakeLists.txt`; `OPTIONAL`, as there may be none), without what tide makes in a game's folder (`.tide/`, `build/`). Not as `packages/`: where the installers put tide, an installation's `packages/` is where packages from git are kept (`packages_cache`), and `tide upgrade` replaces every folder its release has, whole. So an upgrade replaces these with `src/`, and never touches that one.
  - Beyond that it's a folder's package: `packages_folder` gives its folder, so resolving, the build's Tide files, C and libraries, `tide run`'s watching (`game_stamp`) and tidels go as for a folder. Its `tide.packages` says `package Name` and lists what it needs, which the game's file lists too: by name, or from git, never a folder.
  - `tide add <name>` writes the name, in lower case however it was typed, and what the package needs. `tide update` leaves such lines alone and says nothing about them. A name no package has is an error that lists those there are (`packages_builtin_names`: the folders named as one that have a `tide.packages`) and the closest (`packages_builtin_unknown`, with `suggest_close` of `compiler/src/common.c`).
  - `tide_cli_tests` checks the lines and a folder of packages made up for the test (`compiler/cli/tests/packages_test.c`); `tide_cli_packages` puts tide in an installation of its own, with packages, and runs `tide add`, `update` and `schedule` there; `lsp_open_folder_with_a_package_that_comes_with_tide` checks tidels.
- A game's C is in its folder too, outside hidden folders and `build/`: every `.c` file compiles with the game's flags, and every `.cpp`, `.cc` and `.cxx` one as C++ with no runtime (see Language), each again only when it, a header there (`.h`, `.hpp`, `.hh`, `.hxx`, `.inl`) or the flags change, and every library (`.a`, `.lib`, `.so`, `.dll`, `.dylib`) links when its contents say it was built for the target's platform and CPU (`compiler/cli/libraries.c`), since `.a` is every platform's. `.so`, `.dll` and `.dylib` files are copied next to the program, and `.so`/`.dylib` ones found there through its rpath; a `.dll` links through its import library. Linux's libraries and Android's are both ELF, for the same CPUs: a shared one is Android's when it has Android's note (`.note.android.ident`, which the NDK's `crtbegin_so.o` puts in everything linked for Android, Go's libraries too), never by what it needs (musl's C library is `libc.so`, as Android's is). A static one has nothing that tells (clang makes the same objects for both), so an ELF archive is Linux's, and Android games bring shared libraries: no folder or name marks one as Android's. An Android build links the game's library for each CPU to that CPU's shared libraries, and they go in the APK and the bundle beside it (`lib/<abi>/`), stored on 16 KiB pages, each under the name the game's library asks for it by: its soname, or its file's name when it has none, which has to be `lib<name>.so`. tide warns about one whose segments are aligned for less than 16 KiB, as phones with those pages may not load it; when an Android link fails, it names the ELF libraries for that CPU it left out as Linux's. A web program may only leave undefined what the prebuilt platform layer does, which are GL's functions, from the page: tide lists them from that library's objects (`page_imports_flag`, `wasm_archive_imports`) for the linker, so any other function nothing defines fails the link, where allowing them all would leave it to fail when it's called. After a failed web link, tide says which of the game's extern functions nothing defines.
- The package is everything installed as the `tide` component: `bin/` (tide, tidels), `include/`, `src/engine/`, `src/packages/` (the packages that come with tide, as sources), `lib/native/`, `lib/web/` and `lib/android/<abi>/` (the prebuilt platform layer, one library each, with GLFW's objects inside the native one), `wasi/` (wasi-libc and the compiler runtime for wasm), `mingw/` (on Windows: MinGW-w64's headers, C runtime and compiler runtime), `lib/clang/` (clang's own headers), `web/` (the page and `tide.js`), `editors/` and `VERSION`. It comes from the `package` preset (clang and lld built in, static C runtime on Windows), the `web-package` preset, the `android-package-arm64` and `android-package-x86_64` presets and, on Windows, the `mingw-release` preset, put together by `cmake -DNAME=tide-windows-x64 -P cmake/package.cmake` into `build/dist`. Anything tide needs at build time must be installed into the package; a game build can't see this repo. The same goes for `tidels`, which reads the engine headers from the package's `include/`. Nothing else goes in: games are C, built for this machine's CPU, Android's and the web, so the package leaves out C++ headers, Windows' interface definitions (`.idl`) and type libraries, and clang's headers for other CPUs, GPUs and the runtimes it doesn't ship (`cmake/LLVM.cmake`, `platform/CMakeLists.txt`). Every C header of the C libraries stays, since a game's C can include any of them.
- `editors/tide.vsix` is the VS Code extension (`tools/tide-vscode`), for VS Code, Cursor, VSCodium and Windsurf. Only the `package` preset builds it (`TIDE_EDITOR_EXTENSIONS`), since it needs Node's npm. The installers run `tide editors`, which installs it into every one of those editors it finds, and `tide upgrade` updates it in the editors that have it.
- The JetBrains plugin (`tools/tide-jetbrains`, Gradle and Java; on Marketplace as `io.github.blenminer.tide`) isn't in the package: CI builds it and checks it with the plugin verifier on every run, attaches it to each release, and publishes stable releases to JetBrains Marketplace with the `JETBRAINS_MARKETPLACE_TOKEN` secret. Marketplace installs LSP4IJ, which it needs, along with it.
