- Users install with `install.ps1` or `install.sh` into `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Tide` or `~/.tide`, with `bin` on `PATH`, and `tide upgrade` replaces the installation from the releases the site lists (below). It checks each download against the release's `SHA256SUMS`, and renames running programs aside instead of overwriting them. Once a day, tide says when a newer version is out. tide and the installers pick a channel's highest version by semantic versioning (`compiler/cli/release.c`), never the last one published, of those with this platform's package (some old nightlies went out without macOS's, back when its failures didn't hold releases back); the site's list has the latest stable release too, which nightly ones push out of GitHub's. `tide upgrade` only goes forward, except to an exact `--version` or when switching channels. Builds made from this repo are versioned `<VERSION>-dev` and never look for updates.
- **Anyone installs and upgrades from the site, with no account, and nothing they're sent names the repository or its owner** (the owner, 2026-10-07: releases stay on GitHub, free, in the private repository, "publicly downloadable while keeping my identity safe"; open to anyone, which replaced the whitelist of GitHub accounts and the tokens tide and the installers sent). What's built, and what code relies on:
  - `docs/releases.js`, in the site's server (`docs/server.js`), answers `/releases.json` (the versions out), `/releases/<tag>.json` (one, for a version the list no longer has) and `/releases/<tag>/<file>` (a redirect to the download). It asks GitHub with a token that only reads the repository (the Fly secret `TIDE_RELEASES_TOKEN`; `TIDE_RELEASES_REPO` in `docs/fly.toml`), and without one answers 503.
  - What it sends is its own JSON, in the shape tide always read (`tag_name`, `prerelease`, each asset's `name`, `size` and `browser_download_url`, which is the site's own address), never GitHub's, which has the owner's login, the repository's URLs and the release notes.
  - A download is GitHub's own link to the asset (the API's 302 for `Accept: application/octet-stream`), passed on: five minutes long, on `release-assets.githubusercontent.com`, with the repository's number and no name (looked at, 2026-10-07: the number resolves for nobody without access). So the bytes are GitHub's to send. The site keeps a link 3 minutes and the list 1, so a burst of upgrades costs GitHub a request or two, and it gives what it last had while GitHub is away.
  - `tide upgrade` reads `https://tide-engine.dev/releases.json` (`TIDE_RELEASES` in `compiler/cli/upgrade.c`), sends no credentials and names no repository. `TIDE_RELEASES_URL` still points it at a list in a folder, for tests and for the installers.
  - The installers are the site's too (`/install.sh`, `/install.ps1`: the repo's files, copied into `docs/static/` as the site builds, `installers` in `docs/vite.config.ts`). Over a tide that's there they download and check the package themselves and hand it to that tide's updater as local files, whichever version it is: a tide from before this looks for releases at GitHub, where it finds none without access.
  - A tide installed before this can't upgrade itself any more unless it has access to the repository: running the installer again moves it over.
  - The request-access page (`docs/src/routes/request-access`) is still there and gates nothing; nothing links to it.
  - Tests: `docs/tests/releases.test.mjs` (a made-up GitHub: what goes out, what's cached, what's refused, GitHub away), which the Docs workflow runs; `tide_cli_installers` (both installers against a made-up site, on each platform's shell); `tide_cli_upgrade`.
- An upgrade never loses what's installed, and one that stopped says so (`compiler/cli/install.c`):
  - The package's `VERSION` is the installation's version, and an installation is whole when that's its tide's own (`tide_installation_whole`). `cmake/package.cmake` takes it from the native tree, whose tide it is: the other trees install one too, and aren't all configured with the version.
  - `VERSION` goes before anything of the installation changes and comes back last, and tide and tidels go just before it, after everything they build with. So an upgrade that stopped leaves the old tide, which still sees a newer version, or an installation without its `VERSION`. tide builds nothing with one that isn't whole (`build_setup`), `tide version` says so, and `tide upgrade` installs the version again even when it's the newest already. The installers run `tide upgrade` on an installation that's there, so running one again finishes it too.
  - Nothing goes before what replaces it is in its place: a folder moves to `.upgrade/old`, and back when the new one can't take its place.
  - Windows moves no folder while a program has anything in it open (an editor that shows an engine header watches the header's folder, a terminal is in one), so what's in such a folder is replaced one by one, and what the package has none of there goes. Only a file a program has open without sharing stops an upgrade, which names it and stops there. No retries, and nothing timed.
  - `tide_cli_tests` holds folders and files open as editors and compilers do (`compiler/cli/tests/install_test.c`, those cases on Windows only), and `tide_cli_upgrade` (`compiler/cli/tests/upgrade_test.cmake`) runs `tide upgrade` against releases in a folder (`TIDE_RELEASES_URL`), where the tide that upgrades replaces its own program.
- An upgrade that went well says one line, the version it had and the one it has now, and nothing else (agreed): not which editors it updated, nor where the release's notes are. In a terminal, a bar each for the download and the unpacking is drawn over itself above that line (`run_step` in `compiler/cli/upgrade.c`), so nothing waits unseen:
  - curl and tar run in the background, and what they say waits in a log until the bar is done with its line. One curl gets the checksums and then the package, so the download's bar, which follows the package's file as it grows, ends when both are there.
  - The unpacking's bar follows the bytes of the files unpacked so far, against what the package unpacks to: a zip's directory says (`unzip_size`), and a gzip's last four bytes, the size of its tar.
  - `tide editors --update`, which the new tide runs, says nothing unless an editor refuses the update, and that waits for the upgrade's line. While editors take it, a terminal says so on the line the upgrade's own then takes.
  - `tide_cli_upgrade` checks the line is all there is, where there's no terminal.
- `tide build --android` (`compiler/cli/build.c`, `android.c`) builds the game for both of Android's CPUs, as `libgame.so` with `lib/android/<abi>/`'s platform layer, and makes the app with `apk.c`, signed with `~/.android/tide.pem`, or the key `TIDE_ANDROID_KEY` names (made once: a phone only updates an app with the key it was installed with, and it's the user's upload key on Play); `--release` makes the bundle too. The ID is the game's `appId` setting, or `dev.tide.<name>`, which `--release` refuses, and the version people see its `version` setting (1.0 without it). The version code is the minutes from 2020 to now (`SOURCE_DATE_EPOCH`, where it's set), so every build updates the last with no number to keep. `tide run --android` installs it with adb, starts it with the session's flags (the intent's `tide.args`, which the backend gives `main`), and shows what it prints (`adb logcat -s tide`) until it ends. Saving a game's file builds the app again, installs it and starts it over (the match with it: no code is swapped into a running app yet). The NDK and adb are the ones Android's tools installed (ANDROID_NDK_HOME, ANDROID_NDK_ROOT, ANDROID_HOME, ANDROID_SDK_ROOT, or where Android Studio puts them), or else tide fetches them once from Google, pinned and checked against Google's SHA-1s, after the user accepts Google's license (or `TIDE_ACCEPT_ANDROID_LICENSE` is set; editors' runs pass on what's typed, and set `TIDE_INTERACTIVE` so tide asks there though its input isn't a terminal), into `<root>/android/`: only the NDK's C headers, its libraries for Android 10 and its compiler runtime, unpacked by tide's own unzip (`unzip.c`: Linux's tar can't read zips). tide's built-in clang has X86, AArch64 and WebAssembly, and lld's ELF driver, on every host.
- `.github/workflows/build.yml` tests and packages Windows, Linux and macOS on pushes to `dev` and `release` and on pull requests, tests the MinGW target on Windows, runs the Android tests on the emulator on Linux (KVM), whose image comes through the runner's SDK manager, and runs the suite once more on Linux under AddressSanitizer and UndefinedBehaviorSanitizer (the `sanitize` preset: see Language, no Tide code can corrupt memory). That job leaves two tests to the others: the relay's, which is Node's, and rooms with the runner's browsers, which nobody has run under sanitizers yet (without browsers it passes). Windows and Linux build with the LLVM tide's built-in clang comes from (`TIDE_LLVM_VERSION`): LLVM's own release on Windows, and LLVM's apt packages of that major version on Linux. macOS builds native code with Apple's clang and web code with Homebrew's LLVM. Every platform's Node is 24 (`actions/setup-node`; see Building for why the runners' own won't do on Windows). Pushes to `dev` publish nightly pre-releases (`0.3.0-nightly.48`); pushes to `release` publish stable releases, and semantic-release commits the new `VERSION` there. A failure on any platform fails the run and publishes nothing, so every release has every platform's package; nothing in CI may swallow a failure (no `continue-on-error`). Native tests that need a GPU (label `gpu`) are skipped in CI: no runner can open their window.
- **For now CI runs on the owner's machines, and builds nothing for macOS** (the owner, 2026-10-07, with the account out of hosted minutes: "if we can live with that for now"). Both workflows' jobs go to two runners of theirs, by label: `tide-windows` on the Windows PC, and `tide-linux-home` on the home server (Debian, 4 cores; it took over from a runner in the PC's WSL the same day, to take load off the PC). `.github/runner/README.md` says how each is set up, and `setup-linux.sh` there installs what the Linux one needs. What follows from it:
  - Releases have Windows' and Linux's packages and no macOS one, which goes against "every release has every platform's package" on purpose: `tide upgrade` and the installers only pick a version that has their platform's package, so Macs stay on the last nightly that had one. Nothing runs the suite on ARM or on a Mac meanwhile.
  - A runner keeps its checkout and `build/` between jobs (`clean: false`), so there's no compiler cache and no cache step there, and the Build and Docs workflows share one checkout on Linux: a job passes what it needs explicitly (`-DTIDE_DOC_EXAMPLES=OFF` for the web tests), as a CMake cache variable another job set is still set.
  - Steps only a hosted runner needs are conditioned on `runner.environment == 'github-hosted'` and still work there; going back is the `runs-on` labels and the matrix's macOS rows.
  - A run is one job at a time for each OS, on a machine sessions also build on: a push's run takes longer than it did, and a newer push still replaces the one waiting (the workflow's `concurrency` group), never the one running.
- **Windows' package is built on Linux** (the owner, 2026-10-07, to take the PC out of what a push runs: the home server "is always on... sitting mostly idle"; published untried, their choice over running it under Wine). What's built, and what code relies on:
  - Its `tide.exe` and `tidels.exe` are for Windows' own target (`x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`), as on Windows, since tide links LLVM's Windows libraries and so Microsoft's C++ runtime: the `windows-package` preset, which is `package` with `cmake/windows-msvc-toolchain.cmake`. clang takes Microsoft's headers and libraries from a folder xwin made (`-Xmicrosoft-windows-sys-root`, `TIDE_XWIN` or `~/xwin`), laid out as a Windows machine has them, so they come after clang's own headers, as system headers: given with `-isystem`, Microsoft's `vadefs.h` came before clang's and `va_arg` was a warning. They're Microsoft's, under Visual Studio's license, which `.github/runner/setup-linux.sh` accepts when told to (`TIDE_ACCEPT_MICROSOFT_LICENSE`): never in the repo or the package, whose programs have the C runtime linked in, as they always had.
  - CMake's own setup for that target wants LLVM's tools named: `llvm-rc`, `llvm-mt`, `lld-link`, and `llvm-ar` (with clang's GNU-style driver CMake writes `ar`'s arguments, which `llvm-lib` doesn't take). Resources are preprocessed without the C flags, so the toolchain gives their tool Microsoft's folders itself (`CMAKE_RC_FLAGS_INIT`): libxml2's has one.
  - `cmake/LLVM.cmake` takes the release of the system tide is built for (`WIN32`, `APPLE`), where it took the build machine's, and a cross build runs a native tidec as web builds do (`CMAKE_CROSSCOMPILING` in the top `CMakeLists.txt`). `mingw-release` configures anywhere (off Windows `cmake/mingw-toolchain.cmake` says it's a cross build; llvm-mingw's files were the Linux archive's already), and `cmake/package.cmake` tells Windows' package by its name, with `-DNATIVE=build/windows-package`.
  - The Package job of `windows-x64` runs on the Linux runner: MinGW's tree, then `windows-package`, then the web's and Android's trees, which it shares with Linux's package job on that runner. The steps that try a package only run where the package is the runner's system's.
  - Checked once by hand (2026-10-07): the package the server made has the 2,417 files of that day's nightly, with programs within a twentieth of a percent of their sizes, and on Windows its tide built the demo natively and for the web. Nothing checks it again: a Windows nightly that doesn't start is found by whoever upgrades.
  - `tide-windows`, the PC's runner, is then only a full run's: the Windows test suites.
- **The test suites run when asked for, and for a stable release, never for a nightly** (the owner, 2026-10-07: "dev/nightly is meant to move fast; we can introduce them back later, right now its too early for always running it"). What the paragraph above says the workflow tests is what a full run does:
  - A push to `dev` (and a pull request) runs the two Package jobs: each builds its package and tries it as a user would (a game built natively, for the web and for Android, a game's C++, incremental builds), and the nightly is published from them. No test suite runs, nor the Android emulator, the checks for Google Play or the build service's page.
  - A run started by hand (`gh workflow run build.yml --ref dev`, or Run workflow on GitHub) is the packages and every suite (native, web, MinGW, the sanitizers, Android), and publishes nothing. A push to `release` is the same, and its stable release waits for all of it.
  - Which jobs a run has is the Version job's to say (its `Checks` step: the matrix as JSON, and `tests`), so the jobs' steps are written once.
  - A nightly can therefore go out with a suite failing somewhere this machine's sessions didn't run it. That's accepted for now.
- **A nightly is published by the workflow itself, not by semantic-release** (2026-10-07): semantic-release publishes nothing for a commit that isn't its branch's newest any more ("The local branch dev is behind the remote one"), and with several sessions pushing, `dev`'s rarely is by the time its packages are built: runs passed every job and released nothing. `.github/next-nightly.mjs` says the version in the Version job (the highest tag's, one further: `0.3.0-nightly.7` after `.6`, `0.4.0-nightly.1` after a stable `0.3.0`), and that nothing is published for a commit with no `feat`, `fix` or `perf` since the last tag behind it, or that's itself behind a tag. The Release job tags the commit, adds the note semantic-release keeps for each of its tags (`refs/notes/semantic-release-<tag>`, `{"channels":["dev"]}`), and makes the release with `gh`, which the runner has. A stable release (`release`) is still semantic-release's, start to end. The workflow's `concurrency` group is what keeps two runs from taking one number.
- Versions come from conventional commits (`.releaserc.json`): `fix:` is a patch, `feat:` a minor version. While the version starts with 0, breaking changes (`feat!:`) are minor versions too, and we avoid them until 1.0 anyway.

### Box2D

An experiment the owner asked for (2026-10-05: "we can always remove it"), written as a user's package would be: Tide and C in `packages/box2d/`. What it needed of the engine is general, and noted where it is: jobs, `out List<T>`, systems written in C and `world.Set<T>(this, ...)` beside a read parameter (see Language and Determinism), and how a big list goes to C (`tide_list_flatten`). Its names and its shape aren't agreed, and its `README.md` says what it has and hasn't, and what it costs. Its page for users is `docs/physics/box2d.md`, with a live example (see Docs site).

- `packages/box2d/box2d/src/` is Box2D as it came (`main` at `ac7c751`, MIT, so a game that lists the package ships Box2D's notice), its public headers in `src/box2d/` beside the sources so they're found with no include path, and the changes `box2d/TIDE.md` lists, each marked `Tide:`. `box2d.tide` (namespace `Box2D`) is the components, events and systems, and `bridge.c` their C, whose functions are `b2t_`: tidec keeps `tide_` for the engine, in a package too.
- **Box2D's state rides in the match.** Its world is in C's memory, where rollbacks, joins, host migration and reloads don't reach, so after each step its whole state goes into the match: Box2D's own image of its world into `Physics.image`, a `List<byte>`, with `Physics.stamp`, which names it. C's worlds (`worlds` in `bridge.c`, four) are only where a state is stepped: a tick's first system finds the one at its match's stamp (`b2t_open`) or has one made from the image (`b2t_restore`). Two matches at one stamp are at one state, so either's world serves both, as the server's and the client's in one program are. Nothing else is needed for any of it, and nothing in the engine knows.
- **A stamp is the stamp before it and everything done to the world since, hashed** (`note` in `bridge.c`): Box2D's next state is its last and what's done to it. So the image is never read to name it, and everything that changes C's world has to say so there, or two worlds that differ would go by one stamp.
- **Everything that changes Box2D's world happens in the package's systems, from what the match says.** Code moves a body by writing its component: `Enter` compares each with where the last step left it (the `step` fields, which `Leave` writes) and syncs the ones that differ, and a destroyed entity's body is listed (`Physics.gone`) and goes at the next step. A call into Box2D from anywhere else would change C's world after its image was taken, and a machine that then restored would have another world.
- **Its systems are one stage, in an order nothing a game writes can change** (`PhysicsStages.Step`, an enum of one member; each system says its offset in it: `Begin` 0, `Enter` 1, `Own` 2, the shapes' 3 and 4, the parts' shapes' 5 and 6, `DropParts` 7, `Simulate` 8, `Leave` and `Report` 9). Ordered only by where they were declared, a game's `[Before(Step)]` moved the step itself to after `Leave` and `Report`, and `[Before(Enter)]` moved `Enter` to after `Own`, which stopped the program. What a game uses, and the docs teach, is one thing: `[Before(PhysicsStages.Step)]` on a system that has to write into this tick's step, which then runs before all of them. The names `PhysicsStages` and `Simulate` are agreed (the owner, 2026-10-06). What code relies on:
  - The system that steps is `Simulate`, not `Step`, on purpose: a system of a game's that's `[Before(Simulate)]` runs after `Enter` gave Box2D what code wrote, and `Leave` then writes the step's own over what it wrote of a body's place and speeds, silently. With no system of that name, `[Before(Step)]`, which is what one writes first, is an error. Don't name a system of the package's `Step`.
  - A game's system with no attribute runs after the stage in a game where none is before it. Once one is, the ones declared before that one run before the stage too, by the default order (the package waits for a system that comes after them): `[After(PhysicsStages.Step)]` holds one after. The docs say so.
  - `tide_box2d_order` (`packages/CMakeLists.txt`, `tide_package_order_test`) runs `tidec --schedule` on `test/order.tide`, a game that orders a system each way against the package's, with the package as tide builds a game that lists it (`--package`), and compares the order of the systems' names with `test/order.txt`; `box2d_orders_a_games_systems` (`test/course_test.c`) checks that what's written before the stage is in that tick's step, and what's written after it in the next.
- **A body's impulses are given once, and a new body's by the step** (`push_new` in `bridge.c`): `Enter` makes a body before the systems after it put its shapes on, when it weighs nothing, so the impulses its component was spawned with are kept with the body in `made`, for the tick, and `b2t_step` gives them right before it steps. They're noted where they always were, with the body's component (`sync_body`), and bodies that are there already get theirs from `Enter` as before, in the order of its rows.
- **A body code moves wakes, and what it touched** (`b2Body_SetAwake`, `b2Body_WakeTouching`, after `b2Body_SetTransform` in `sync_body`): Box2D moves a sleeping body and leaves it asleep, hanging where it was put, and code has no other way to say. **Gravity that changes wakes every body**, and is stepped though nothing was awake: `b2t_step` compares the gravity it's given with the world's own (`b2World_GetGravity`), which is in the image, so it's the last step's wherever the world came from, and turns sleeping off and on again (`b2World_EnableSleeping`), which is the one call Box2D has that wakes everything; with `Physics.sleeping` off it turns on and off instead, leaving the setting as the match says, because manually sleeping bodies still need to wake. Neither is noted: the first follows from the move and the second from the gravity, which are.
- **A body says whether it's asleep, and code that writes it puts the body to sleep or wakes it** (the owner, 2026-10-06: "we might also want to allow to manually Sleep/Wake bodies"; that it's a field of `Body`, `asleep`, with no function for it, and every rule here are Claude's picks, which the owner hasn't confirmed: `docs/physics/box2d.md`, Bodies at rest). After each step `Body.asleep` is Box2D's, as a body's place is: true for a dynamic or a kinematic body the step has no work for, and never for a static one. Box3D's package has the same, rule for rule (see Box3D, A body's `asleep`; brought onto dev 2026-10-07 from the branch it was built on, with Sumo's ring put to sleep after its one step). What code relies on:
  - **A write is told by a second field, `stepAsleep`, the package's own**, as a body's place is by its `step` fields: `Enter` compares the two, a byte each of the four a body's bools are in (`FLAGS`, `flags_asleep`), with no call into Box2D. Asking Box2D whether the body is awake, and comparing, was what was to be preferred, and doesn't hold: by the time `Enter` comes to a body the tick may have woken it already (`Begin` destroyed a body beside it, `Enter` moved one before it in the rows), and Box2D's answer would read as code having written `asleep = true`. A body's type is told the other way (`kinds`, below), which holds because only code changes a type, where every step changes what's awake. The two bytes are two of the six a `Body` had to spare after `bullet`: it's 96 bytes as it was, with no lane more, and a world where nothing sleeps is, byte for byte, the world it was.
  - **Waking is `Enter`'s, and sleeping the step's, last** (`lulls`, `put_to_sleep` in `b2t_step`: after the world's tuning, the new bodies' impulses, the gravity and what a new one wakes). Newborn sleepers join the same deferred list, so gravity or automatic sleeping turned off in their creation tick cannot override their sleep. Box2D sleeps and wakes an island at a time, so a body put to sleep where `Enter` came to it would be woken again by whatever the tick wrote into a body of its island further down the rows: by which of them comes first, that is. So what code puts to sleep in a tick is asleep as the step starts, whatever else the tick wrote: a push or a speed written with it is lost, a place written with it is where it sleeps, and a new gravity in that tick doesn't wake it. A body another runs into in that step wakes all the same. Both are noted where `Enter` syncs the body, with its component as code wrote it and a bit of `did`, so nothing is noted at the step.
  - **Only a write does anything.** `asleep = true` in a body that's asleep already is nothing written, so such a body that code moves wakes, as any that's moved does, and so does one a new gravity wakes: `asleep` is false after that tick, and code that wants it asleep says so then. A static body's `asleep` is written back false wherever `Enter` syncs it (`sync_body`), with nothing noted for it: one that was written asleep, and a sleeping body that was just made static.
  - **It works where `Physics.sleeping` is off**: Box2D's switch only says what the step does, and a body that's put to sleep there stays so until something wakes it.
  - **An island is the bodies that touch each other, as the last step found them**: every touching contact between two bodies of which neither is static joins their islands (`b2LinkContact`), a kinematic body's too. So a stack goes to sleep from any of its crates, a crate that slides along a plank stops where it is when the plank is put to sleep, a lift goes with what stands on it, and two crates on one static floor are apart. What hasn't touched it yet stays awake, and wakes it when it does. A body that's put to sleep has no speed any more (its `b2BodyState` goes): `Leave` writes zeros.
  - **`Leave` writes the field for the bodies the step says something of**: awake for one it moved, asleep for one that fell asleep in it (`left.awake`). Of any other it reads the field only in a tick whose step put bodies to sleep for code (`slept`): Box2D says none of which bodies an island took along, so each that was awake by its component, isn't static, and that the step says nothing of is asked (`b2Body_IsAwake`). The flags are only asked for to write once a body's changed.
  - **Spawned asleep** is `b2BodyDef.isAwake`: alone in a set of its own, where it's spawned, with none of the speed or the impulses it was spawned with (`made` gets none for it). Bodies spawned asleep on each other are each an island, as no step saw them touch: one that's hit wakes, and the ones on it a contact a step. Where there are thousands, that costs more in the ticks many wake than a stack that sleeps as one (measured in 3D, with Sumo's ring, on the branch above): the docs say to spawn such bodies awake and put them to sleep a tick later.
  - **The one change to Box2D's sources**: `b2TrySleepIsland` looked a body's move event up among the last step's, which a world that was made from an image has none of, so a body put to sleep before such a world's first step wrote past them (`box2d/TIDE.md`).
  - **Putting a body to sleep and waking one are calls Box2D's image in parts follows** (`b2SnapNoteOp`, which named `b2Body_SetAwake` already, for the wake of a body that code moves): `tide_box2d_sleep` runs with every image in parts compared with the image written whole (`b2t_check`), 253 of its scripted match's 320, and `tide_box2d_parts` puts bodies to sleep at random right after a restore too, which it kept away from until `b2TrySleepIsland` was changed.
  - **Pins.** The bodies' pin (`BODIES_HASH`), Box2D's own images' (`BYTES_HASH`) and what was answered (`ANSWERS_HASH`) didn't move: every image is the bytes it was. The worlds' did (the stack's two, the sensors' two, the kit's three, and Towers' two), with the worlds stepping as they did: a sleeping body's `asleep` and `stepAsleep` are 1 where its component had two zeros, and a stamp notes a body's component as code wrote it, so a body that was asleep when code pushed it is noted otherwise, which every stamp after it carries (with the two bytes left out of the note, the pinned match's last world had the hash it had before: tried).
  - `tide_box2d_sleep` (`test/sleep.tide`, `sleep_test.c`: a game built with the package as tide builds one that lists it) is its tests, each a few bodies: a resting crate put to sleep, which then costs nothing, and woken in a world at rest, where the write is seen; one made static while it sleeps; a world whose bodies don't fall asleep; a falling crate put to sleep, which hangs where it was until a ball hits it, and one put somewhere else and to sleep in one tick; a plank and the crate sliding on it, a lift and the crate on it, and one of an island put to sleep while another is pushed, either way round; a stack from its bottom crate, with a crate on its way down that wakes it; bodies spawned asleep, three of them on each other; gravity; and a match that does all of it from a system, at its ticks, whose hashes are pinned, the same in debug, release and on the web, and which is run again from a snapshot three times, from its packed bytes, and with C's world made from the image before every tick.
  - **What it costs against `dev` at `6011c2b`**: `box2d_costs`, release, 2,400 crates, seven alternating runs behind the continuation benchmark gate. The least tick was 0.739 ms before and 0.910 after with everything moving, 0.084 and 0.078 with one crate pushed, and 0.019 and 0.019 at rest. The moving ticks ranged from 0.739 to 1.317 before and 0.910 to 1.284 after, with concurrent browser tests and builds taking about 43% CPU before the round: these do not establish the cost of the extra field under quiet conditions. No regression is visible with one crate moving or at rest. The image is still 2,549,152 bytes, the same bytes as before; the moving run writes all 120 images whole, the one-crate run 13 whole and 227 in parts, and rest writes none. The image's least time was 0.092 and 0.141 ms for the whole moving world, and 0.013 and 0.013 in the one-crate run. This round supersedes the comparison made before Box2D's image in parts was merged; its raw runs remain in the task's `build/sleep-wake-costs-contended.txt`.
- **A sensor sends `Touched` and `Parted`** (`b2t_touches`, `b2t_touch`: the step's contact events, then its sensor events, each a sensor's shape and the shape that came into it or left). What code relies on:
  - Box2D only reports a shape to a sensor when both were made with `enableSensorEvents`, which is off by default, for a sensor too: every shape is made with it (`shape_def`). It's a byte of each shape in the image, which is what moved the pinned hashes when sensors were built (below).
  - Sensors see each other in this Box2D, each reporting the other: the package's are made in one collision group below zero (`filter.groupIndex = -1`), which Box2D never sets against each other, so a sensor doesn't report another, and nothing is heard twice.
  - What a sensor has in it is in the image (its arrays of visitors), and a step's events aren't, so a world made from an image says the same after its next step. Upstream writes a visitor as its 8 bytes, of which 2 are no field's: `snapshot.c` writes zeros there (`b2SerVisitors`, `box2d/TIDE.md`). Without it the match of sensors had another hash of its images in a debug build than in a release one.
  - A shape that's gone has no owner (`owner_of`), so `Parted` from an entity that was destroyed while they touched comes with `default` for `other`, from a sensor as from a contact (decided to leave it: saying which entity would take keeping whose each body was for a tick after it's gone).
- **A `Body`'s `position` is `[Position]` and its `angle` `[Rotation]`** (see Networking), so views see it turn the short way where Box2D's angle goes from π to -π, and glide through a correction. They change nothing of the match, which the pins show.
- **The image is in the match's hash, so it's the same bytes on every machine for the same state**, which Box2D doesn't promise by itself: its own determinism check is of bodies. The changes for that: `userData` as wide on 32-bit platforms, a layout hash that doesn't name the pointer's width, a tree's capacity as a restore would leave it, and a contact point's `normalVelocity` decided per contact, where upstream decides per block of 4 or 8 by the CPU's SIMD width (the same simulation, other bytes on an AVX2 machine than on the web).
- **The image is in sections that keep their place** (`b2World_GetSnapshotAt`, `place` in `bridge.c`): a table after its header says where each of 17 starts, and each stays where it was while the one before it leaves it between none and a quarter of itself to spare. Upstream's image is one run of bytes that starts with its parts of changing size, so a contact that starts or ends moved every byte after it. Where the sections go is part of the state: a restore takes it from the image's table. It's what makes a tick's image differ from the last where the world does, which is what the match's snapshots can share and what's hashed again.
- **An image is written as what changed of the last one, where Box2D can say** (built 2026-10-06, a card the owner asked for; `b2World_GetSnapshotParts`, `snapshot.c`; `b2t_image`, `bridge.c`), by the design Box3D's has (see Box3D, An image is written as what changed of the last one, for what's the same: one writer that writes both, the match's list keeping the rest through `tide_list_out_keep` and `tide_list_out_part`, every call that changes a world going through one gate, and `b2t_check`). So what the package adds to a tick goes by what's awake, where the image written whole goes by how big the world is. **The image the parts make is, byte for byte, the image written whole**: no pin moved, in the package's tests or in Towers', and the pins of `tide_box2d_stack` are of images written both ways. What's Box2D's own, which code relies on:
  - **What a contact is to the solver isn't in the contact.** Box2D keeps a contact's manifold and impulses (`b2ContactSim`), and a body's place and mass (`b2BodySim`), in a solver set, or in the constraint graph for a contact that touches and is awake. There's a set for the static bodies, one for the disabled ones, which also holds every contact between two bodies that are asleep and don't touch, one for what's awake, and one for each island that sleeps. So beside the three sets of bits (`b2SnapTrack`, `physics_world.h`: bodies, shapes, whose bits are their fat bounds' too, and contacts) there are four flags, each for a section an image in parts leaves out when nothing was written in it (`b2SnapKeepSection`): the static and the disabled set (`fixedTouched`), the sleeping sets (`setsTouched`), the islands (`islandsTouched`) and the pairs (`pairsTouched`). The awake set, the graph, the trees, the sensors, the joints, the chains and the world's settings are written every time, the big ones as parts that point at Box2D's own arrays.
  - **Which may have changed** is as Box3D's: awake at the last image, at this one or between (`b2SnapCommit`, `b2SnapSeed`, `b2SnapTouchBodyDeep` where a set wakes or an island falls asleep), or written from outside the step's workers, which is said where it's done: 93 lines, each marked `Tide:`, which `box2d/TIDE.md` lists by what they're for. The places are Box2D's own, found in its source and not taken from Box3D's list. The ones that aren't obvious: a body taken out of a set moves the last one into its place, and every contact of that one is told where it is (`b2RemoveBodySim`, `b2RefreshBodyContactIndices`), which for a static body that goes is every sleeping contact of another static body; a body's mass worked out again clears a flag in each of its contacts' sims (`b2UpdateBodyMassData`); a contact taken out of the disabled set moves another into its place, which is any contact between two sleeping bodies anywhere (`b2DestroyContact`, `b2WakeSolverSet`). `b2SplitIsland` may run as a task of the step's, one at a time while nothing else says anything, and only says the islands were written: what it tells its new island is awake.
  - **More calls are followed than Box3D's**, as the package makes them for bodies that sleep: waking a body, waking what touches it and turning sleeping off all come to `b2WakeSolverSet`, which has to say what it writes anyway, as a step's contact that starts touching wakes the island it touches. So a body that code moves or wakes costs no whole image. Followed (`b2SnapNoteOp`): the step; a body made and destroyed, its transform, speeds and impulses, `b2Body_SetAwake`, `b2Body_WakeTouching`, its dampings, gravity scale, bullet and motion locks; a circle, capsule, segment or polygon made, and a shape destroyed; the world's gravity, tuning and thresholds, and `b2World_EnableSleeping`. **Any other call writes the next image whole and can't make a wrong one**: `B2_REC` and `B2_REC_CREATE` say which call it is, by an enum `recording.h` makes from `recording_ops.inl`, as Box2D had none. A body's type, enabling and disabling, forces and torques, mass, a shape's settings and geometry, chains, joints, a blast. Every call that changes what an image holds is recorded (looked for: the ones that aren't set callbacks and user data, which no image has). **A call the bridge starts making is either in that list, with its writes read for marks, or costs an image.** What the package gained the same day (below) was read for it as the two were merged: a part that's shed is `b2DestroyShape`, followed; tuning is the world's setters, followed, and each makes its tick step; a body's type that changes is `b2Body_SetType`, which isn't followed, on purpose, as Box3D's isn't (it lets go of every contact the body had and moves the body between sets, `b2TransferBody`), so a tick that changes one writes its image whole; and questions (`b2World_CastRay`, `b2World_CastShape`, `b2World_OverlapShape`, a sensor's visitors) write nothing and don't come to the gate at all: Box2D records them apart (`b2RecQueryCommit`), only while a recording is on. A world made from an image to answer a question is a restored one, whose next image is whole (`before_good` in `bridge.c`).
  - **A world with more bodies, shapes or contacts than its last image had goes on in parts**, where Box3D's is written whole: each of those sections is a count and then its things, so new ones go after the ones that were there, and are all written.
  - **Whole images remain** after a restore; with more than half of the world awake (`B2_SNAP_AWAKE_SHARE`: measured, parts pay until about two thirds are awake, and from three quarters cost what a whole image does, so one half is safe); and when a section has to move (`place`), as when a stack wakes or falls asleep: 13 images of 240 with one crate pushed among 2,400. A body that's destroyed wakes what it touched (`b2DestroyContact`), which often does that.
  - **The trees aren't followed.** A step's workers write a tree's nodes for each shape that leaves its fat bounds (`b2DynamicTree_MarkProxyMoved`), the next step builds what moved again, and nothing says which nodes: the three trees go as parts that point at them, which the list compares, 250 KB of 2,400 crates' image. Flags for them were weighed and left: a body that moves leaves its fat bounds every few ticks.
  - **Every section is come to, in order** (`b2SnapSection` for the awake set's and the sleeping sets' in a world with no such set): an image in parts writes zeros where a section had more in the last image as it comes to the next one. And an image written whole has its bodies, shapes and joints as their bytes in one run, with zeros over each one's `userData` after (`b2SerSimRun`), and its islands written into room asked for once (`b2SerIslands`, `b2RecBufReserve`): the same bytes as upstream's copies one at a time, in under half the time.
  - `b2t_check(on, whenever)` has every image in parts compared with the image written whole (`b2World_CheckSnapshotAt`), the program ending at the first byte apart, saying which section and which body, shape or contact (`b2GetSnapshotRecordSize`). It's on in the package's tests but the timed ones.
  - `tide_box2d_parts` (`packages/box2d/test/parts_test.c`) is the tests of it. A match of its own, `yard.tide`, so that the others keep their pins: a sleeping crate moved onto another stack, destroyed from under one, given a shape, a sensor, a speed and a turn; a static body that comes, is moved under a sleeping crate and goes; a ball that hops over sleeping crates without touching, and sleeps over them; a sensor destroyed around a sleeping stack; one island waking another; gravity changed. With images in parts whenever and as a match has them, against a world made from its image before every tick, through rollbacks, and with the step shared out over threads. And Box2D by itself: whatever a game might call at random (joints, sensors, chains, restores and rollbacks among them), six seeds of 2,400 steps, about 7,600 images in parts, each compared; a world asleep where each thing done fits the image's room, so that one line says it (`box2d_parts_where_only_one_line_says_so`: each of fourteen steps has to be in parts); and a recording taken meanwhile, which replays.
  - Taking each of the 93 lines out in turn and running `tide_box2d_parts`: 57 are noticed by themselves, 14 more with another line that says the same taken out too (the two times everything awake goes into the sets, waking with the second and falling asleep with the first, and the like), 9 say again what being awake says and weren't noticed together, and 13 weren't tried together or sit under calls that write a whole image anyway. Two gaps the first run showed were the test's, and are cases in it now: a contact of the disabled set destroyed among sleepers, and a sensor destroyed ahead of another.
  - Not done: a section that moves has all of the image written again, as Box3D's.
- **A world at rest isn't stepped**: nothing awake, nothing noted since the last stamp and the gravity its last step had, and `b2t_step` returns that stamp, which tells the systems after it there's nothing to read (`Physics.moved`).
- **`Enter` and `Leave` are systems written in C** (`extern system`, see Language), the first to be: every body is looked at every tick, most ticks most have nothing written, and a system written in Tide copies each one out to look at it (and, taking it `mut`, compares and copies it for every entity once a snapshot shares the world's pages, which a client's do every tick). They go through the bodies' fields as arrays, and ask for the fields to write only once a body has something to write. What code relies on:
  - `Enter` (`b2t_enter`, `mut Body`, `mut Physics`) makes new bodies and syncs the ones code wrote, in the order of its rows: `mut Physics` keeps it on one thread, as everything done to C's world is noted into the stamp in that order (C doesn't touch the singleton). The bytes it notes of a body are the component's as a row has them (`tide_rows_get`), padding and all, as they were when Tide passed its copy.
  - **Whose each body is stays Tide's to write**, as it's a map: `Own` (per entity, `with Body`, no component) runs for the bodies `Enter` ran for, in its order, and asks C of each whether it was made (`b2t_made`, which only looks at the next one made; a tick that left one untaken stops the program at the next). It's per entity, and not a loop over what was made, on purpose: the match's heap frees what a map let go of after each entity a system runs for, so the map's blocks are where they were when `Enter` was written in Tide, and the match's hash with them. A loop gave another heap for the same map, and another hash.
  - `Leave` (`b2t_leave`, `mut Body`, `Physics`) writes where the step left the bodies it moved, 12 of a body's 22 fields. `b2t_step` notes those once, right after the step, from Box2D's move events (`note_left`: the bodies that were awake for it, each found by its index, with its speeds read for the ones still awake), so `Leave` asks Box2D nothing for them. A body with no such note wasn't awake: where its component says it's where the last step left it, and still, it's skipped unasked, and otherwise (code moved a body the step then left alone, or it just fell asleep) its component gets what Box2D has, asked for that body, so the next `Enter` finds nothing written and the world comes to rest. It splits across threads: each run reads the notes and writes its own rows.
  - Shapes that are on their body have a sparse tag and aren't visited.
- Its step runs on the tick's threads through jobs, once 2,048 bodies are awake, 4,096 on the web (`SHARED_BODIES`, measured: a tick whose C starts jobs runs its systems on threads too, so with fewer the whole tick was slower shared though the step was quicker; `b2t_share_from` lowers it for the test of threads); Box2D gives the same world on any number of workers. On the web its wide code is WebAssembly's own SIMD (below). Its workers there sleep when a wait for the next stage has gone on long, as upstream's pause and yield do nothing on the web (`b2t_stage_sleep`, `b2t_stage_wake`: see Box3D, which has the same change, and Web builds).
- **On the web it solves 4 contacts at a time on WebAssembly's own SIMD** (`B2_SIMD_WASM`, built 2026-10-06, for the owner's ask that the physics games be as fast as they can be, a browser being where they're slowest). Upstream's one path for WebAssembly is SSE2's headers as Emscripten emulates them, which clang's own target has none of, so Tide's web builds took Box2D's scalar path. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - `core.h` has one branch for every WebAssembly (`__wasm__`, Emscripten's too, where it stands in for the emulation; nobody here built that): `B2_SIMD_WASM` where it's built with `-msimd128` (`__wasm_simd128__`), as CMake's web builds and `tide build --web` build everything, and else the scalar path. tide's package has clang's `wasm_simd128.h` (`cmake/LLVM.cmake` leaves it in).
  - Each place upstream has code for SSE2 has the same for WebAssembly after it, marked `Tide:`: the contact solver's wide floats and its gather and scatter of bodies (`contact_solver_wide.inl`), the trees' bounds (`simd.h`), and the face search of two polygons (`b2FindMaxSeparation` in `manifold.c`).
  - **Each function gives, bit for bit, what its SSE2 twin gives, for any numbers,** and whatever is added there has to: a browser's players are in x64's matches. The least and the greatest are `wasm_f32x4_pmin` and `wasm_f32x4_pmax` with their operands the other way around, which are `minps` and `maxps` exactly (the first where it's less, or greater, and else the second, whichever zero that is and whatever is NaN); never `wasm_f32x4_min` and `wasm_f32x4_max`, which take -0 for less than 0. Comparisons are the ordered ones, a mask is read by each lane's top bit (`wasm_i32x4_bitmask`, as `movmskps`), a blend takes bits by its mask's bits, and nothing is relaxed SIMD (see Determinism).
  - It's nearer x64 than the scalar path was: that one gives the first of two equal numbers where SSE2 gives the second, so it could leave the other zero, which the pins never showed. Box2D's Neon code still has ARM's own least and greatest (`vminq_f32`, `vmaxq_f32`, -0 less than 0), which Box3D's was changed from; CI's Macs give Box2D's pins all the same.
  - The pins are what they were, in web-debug and web-release. Beside them, a scratch program (not in the repo) ran every one of those functions on awkward numbers (every three of 24 values with both zeros, denormals, infinities and NaNs among them, 40,000 of any bits, 40,000 small ones with many equal) and about 5,000 pairs of polygons through `b2CollidePolygons`, built for x64 and for the web: the same bits for all 23 kinds of result, where the scalar path differs in 9. Which NaN arithmetic gives of two wasn't compared.
  - **It gained a quarter of the step, not the half that four at a time sounds like**, because clang had already made SIMD of most of the scalar path by itself: its wide float is a struct of four floats, which clang's vectorizer turns into `f32x4` instructions where it can (of the solve's float operations before, 201 were `f32x4` and 59 scalar; warm starting was all scalar). What the path adds is what clang missed: warm starting, bodies gathered and scattered as transposes where they went a lane at a time, masks as bits, and the polygons' face search. Measured 2026-10-06, 2,400 crates moving, one thread, the least of seven runs taken in turn with the build before, under Node: the step from 0.60 ms to 0.46 and a tick from 1.27 to 1.18; in headless Chrome 154, the step from 0.61 to 0.47. Towers as it was that morning (its lower bricks set solid, 4 substeps) was as before while its towers built (0.12 ms a tick) and went from 0.16 to 0.135 while they all came down; the Towers of that afternoon (every brick loose, 8 substeps), the least of nine runs in turn with a build of it on the scalar path, went from 0.129 ms a tick to 0.105 while its towers build and from 0.166 to 0.137 while they come down, and ten ticks run again from 1.32 ms to 1.18 and from 1.87 to 1.56. Natively the same hour, the least of nine: 0.073, 0.104, 0.85 and 1.24, which with the SIMD numbers are what `docs/guide/towers.md` says; the numbers under What they cost are of a busier hour, on the scalar path.
  - Shared out over a page's 24 threads the step is what it was (0.62 ms and 0.57 under Node; 0.66 and 0.65 in a scratch page in Chrome that ticks frame by frame: the tests' own host never hands a page's thread back to the browser, so in a page its workers never start and its "24 threads" are one), and a tick of 2,400 crates is still quicker on one thread (1.32 against 1.60). With less of a step left to share out, sharing pays later than it did: a tick of 4,800 crates is 2.6 ms on one thread against 2.75 shared (3.6 against 3.1 on the scalar path) and one of 9,600 is 5.9 against 5.7 (6.4 against 5.2). `SHARED_BODIES` is still 4,096 there, which is about even now; nobody moved it.
- `tide_box2d_stack` (`packages/box2d/test/`, a game of its own that lists `..`; in this repo's build its `stack.tide` is the game and the package's file is `PACKAGES Box2D`, as tide builds it: as plain sources tidec warns that the parts' systems never run, which a package's own are exempt from) pins the world's hash and a hash of every tick's world, the same in debug, release and on the web; goes on from a snapshot three times over; makes C's world from its image before every tick against one that never was; packs and unpacks a world with C's forgotten; ticks on threads against one; and says what a tick costs with everything moving, with one crate pushed and at rest (`box2d_costs`, which only checks that rest steps nothing). The library its tests share is built with `tide_flags`: without them clang fuses multiplies and adds on ARM, and Box2D steps another world there. A new Box2D steps differently, so its pins change with it, and so do games' matches.
- **Its pinned match runs twice**, with its images in parts whenever they can be (269 of 300; `b2t_check(true, true)`, which also has each compared with the image written whole) and as a match has them (all 300 whole: its crates never come to rest, so more than half of them are awake at every tick). The same four pins either way, which is what says an image in parts is the image written whole.
- **A third pin is of the bodies alone** (`BODIES_HASH`: the crates' places and speeds at each of the 300 ticks), **and a fourth of Box2D's images alone** (`BYTES_HASH`: the bytes of `Physics.image` at each tick, with nothing else of the match), to tell apart what a change moved. The world's two pins move with anything in the match; the fourth only with what Box2D writes into its image; the third only with how the world steps. Sensor events on every shape moved the first two and left the third (2026-10-06, before there was a fourth): one byte a shape, 23 of the last image's 30,146. What was added the same day moved them twice more, each shown apart: `Physics` and `Body` gained fields and two maps, which moved the world's two pins with Box2D's image byte for byte what it was (`d4fedd75d681acd7` before and after, taken with hit events off) and the bodies' pin as it was; then hit events on every shape, a byte a shape again, moved the image's (to `b43aa576ef744d1e`) and the world's again, with the bodies' pin still as it was; then `Physics.sensors`, one more map in the match, moved the world's two a last time, with the image's and the bodies' as they were. All four are the same in debug, release and web-debug.
- `tide_box2d_course` (`test/course.tide`, `course_test.c`: a game built with the package as tide builds one that lists it, `PACKAGES`) is what a game does with it, each a few bodies of its own: systems before and after the stage; a view that sees a body's angle the short way round and glide through a correction (`tide_local_correct`); an impulse a body is spawned with, against the same one written a tick later; a sleeping crate that's moved, one that's turned, a static ledge moved from under one, and gravity turned over, each seen to act, with the world stepping nothing once it's at rest again; and a match of sensors (one a marble falls through, one that falls through a static body and the floor, two that cross, a body that's in one from the start, a body destroyed inside one and one destroyed around a body), whose hashes are pinned, the same in debug, release and on the web, and which is played again with C's world made from the image before every tick.
- What it costs, on a release build, 2,400 crates (an image of 2.5 MB), with the world kept and hashed each tick: 0.51 ms a tick with everything moving (Box2D's step is 0.30 of it), 0.06 with one crate pushed, 0.018 at rest. It was 0.59, 0.21 and 0.018 until the image was written as what changed (2026-10-06, old and new in turn, the least of fifteen runs each, against the package as it was with parts, types, queries, hits and tuning): the image takes 0.007 ms to write where it took 0.12 with one crate pushed, and putting it into the match with the bodies in and out 0.04 where it took 0.08; with everything moving it's written whole as before, in 0.06 where it took 0.14 (bodies and shapes as runs of bytes, the islands into room asked for once). Under Node, a tick went from 1.25 to 1.02, from 0.90 to 0.17 and stayed 0.028. A whole image goes into the match through `out List<byte>` (`b2t_image`): Box2D writes it once into room for a little more than the last one took, and the list is then exactly it; one in parts through `tide_list_out_keep` and `tide_list_out_part`, each part at its place. It was 1.4 with everything moving until pages a snapshot let go of were handed out again (see ECS and simulation state): most of what the package added to the step was the system's memory, a page fault for each 4 KiB of each chunk that changed. And 0.75 (0.26, and 0.031 at rest) until `Enter` and `Leave` were written in C: each body read back took 0.17 of it through a system written in Tide, a call, a copy and a `world.Set` for each, and takes 0.015 now; looking at each for what code wrote took 0.034, and 0.024 at rest, and takes 0.015 and 0.008; `Own` takes 0.009 to 0.012. What was left then was the image written (0.14), compared and copied into the match's chunks, and the changed pages hashed. Parts, types, queries, hits and tuning (below) took it from 0.601 to 0.611, 0.213 to 0.214 and 0.017 to 0.018, old and new in turn, the least of seven runs each (`costs.py`-style: other sessions build on this machine).
- **What the tests say a tick costs leaves the measuring out** (`b2t_measure_blocks`' `took`): counting how much of the image changed keeps a copy of each and compares the next with it, inside the tick, which no match does.
- What the language still makes awkward: a package's extern functions are as public as its components, and so are the functions it only wrote for itself (`Ask`, `Told`, `Owners`, `PartOf`). And its names are a game's to trip over: a game with a `Hit` or a `Part` of its own means its own by the plain name (see Language, a plain name means what's nearest), and writes `Box2D.Hit`.
- **What it gained 2026-10-06** (the owner decided each: shapes as entities, "lets go with A"; a body's type that changes, "lets do it"; queries, "yep"; with what's in a sensor, hits and tuning). The names are picks, listed in the package's `README.md` (Picks) for the owner to confirm. `tide_box2d_kit` (`test/kit.tide`, `kit_test.c`, built as a game with the package as `PACKAGES`) has a small scene for each, so the pinned match of `tide_box2d_stack` is what it was; every image it writes in parts is compared with the image written whole (`b2t_check`), as in the package's other tests. What code relies on:
  - **A shape on an entity of its own is a `Part`** (`Part { of = brick }` beside a `Box` or a `Circle`, and no `Body`): `AttachPartBoxes` and `AttachPartCircles` (`without Body`, offsets 5 and 6) put it on the body of the entity it names, through the `World`, and keep its shape's handle in the part (`part.box`, `part.circle`) and in `Physics.parts` (shape to part entity). The one-of-each rule of the ECS isn't lifted: an entity still has one `Box` and one `Circle`.
    - A part whose `of` has no `Body`, or whose body isn't made yet (`id` 0: the body went before the part's first step, or never was), is destroyed, with nothing said (a pick, over an error that would stop a match).
    - A part that's destroyed lists its shapes (`Physics.shed`, from the `Destroyed` handler `Shed`), and `Begin` takes them off at the next step (`b2t_shed`: `b2DestroyShape` with the body's mass updated, noted `'h'`). C remembers which body each shed shape was on for that tick (`sheds`), as the end-touch events of that step name a shape that's gone: `Parted` then comes to both bodies with the right `other` and `default` for the part.
    - A body's parts go with it: `Begin` leaves how many bodies went before this step in `Physics.dropped`, and `DropParts` (offset 7) only looks at each part's `of` in a tick where that's above 0. Box2D destroyed their shapes with the body, so nothing is listed for them.
    - `Touched`, `Parted` and `Hit` say which shapes met: `part` and `otherPart`, the part's entity for a part's shape and the body's own for a shape on it (`PartOf`, which only looks in the map when a game has parts). `b2t_touch` gives the shapes' handles with the bodies'.
    - `b2t_box` and `b2t_circle` return the shape's handle now, where they returned a count.
  - **A body's `type` can be written, any to any**, with no new field in `Body`: `sync_body` asks Box2D (`b2Body_GetType`), which is in the image, so it's right wherever C's world came from; where it differs it wakes what touches the body (`b2Body_WakeTouching`: what rests on a body that turns static would stay asleep in the air otherwise) and calls `b2Body_SetType`, noted with the body's bytes as every sync is (`did |= 64`). Asking each body each tick tripled `Enter` at rest (0.006 to 0.018 ms for 2,400), so each of C's worlds remembers the answer (`world.kinds`, a byte a body's index, `retyped` in the scan; forgotten by `make` and `restore`; a debug build checks it against Box2D each time). `Enter` at rest takes 0.008.
    - `b2Body_SetType` lets go of the body's contacts, so the next step sends `Parted` and `Touched` again for what touched it. That tick's image is written whole (see An image is written as what changed of the last one, above).
    - A body that turns kinematic keeps its `velocity` (a pick): code writes the speed it should have with the type.
  - **Queries are functions that take `Physics`** and give their result through `out`: `Raycast`, `Cast` (a `Box` or a `Circle`), `Overlap`, `Inside` (the entity a sensor is a component of), with `RayHit`. `Raycast` and `Cast` have a second version with a trailing `Body ignore`. They change nothing of C's world, so they aren't noted, and they're ordinary functions: a system that asks takes `Physics` to read, and runs on the tick's threads beside others that do.
    - **Which world:** `Ask` finds C's world at the caller's `Physics.stamp` (`b2t_seek`) or has it made from the caller's image (`b2t_recall`), in a slot the stepping world isn't using. `Physics.size == 0`, a world nothing stepped, answers nothing. So a system before the step sees the last tick's world, one after it this tick's, and a view the world it draws (what a question goes by of `Physics`, the stamp, the image and the lists and maps, is no float, so the copy a view is handed, blended between two ticks, has them as that world's). Measured in release: 23 questions by a frame's views of a small world take 0.011 ms, and 0.019 when the world (17 KB) is made first; a world of 2.5 MB takes about half a millisecond to make, once.
    - **What they don't see:** what code wrote into a body since the step, and bodies and shapes made since (they're `Enter`'s to give Box2D, at the next step). An entity destroyed since is hidden: `Ask` hands C the match's `gone` and `shed` lists (`b2t_hide`) whenever they aren't empty, and the callbacks pass those bodies and shapes by.
    - **The same answer on every machine**, a restored world included: everything a query reads is in the image; and nothing is taken in the order Box2D reports it, which is its trees' walk, which a restore doesn't keep. The nearest is the least by (fraction, the shape's index), with the walk clipped to `just_past(best)`, the next float up, not to the best itself (a shape met exactly at the clip is a miss, so one as near would never be compared); lists are sorted by the bodies' indices and each body is there once (`give`). Sensors are passed by (`b2Shape_IsSensor`). `box2d_answers_what_code_asks` pins a hash of every answer of 150 ticks (`ANSWERS_HASH`), the same in debug, release and web-debug, asked before the step, after it and by a view, and of a world made from the image.
    - **Threads:** what a query keeps (which world it asks, what's hidden, the best so far, its scratch arrays) is thread-local (`TIDE_THREAD_LOCAL`), and finding or making a world is behind a spin lock (`finding`, an `atomic_flag`), looked up again once it's held. What changes a world takes `Physics` to write, so the schedule never runs it beside a query of that world. `box2d_answers_on_every_thread` has 1,200 crates each ask what's under it on the platform layer's threads, with every tick starting from none of C's worlds, so that the first thread to ask has one made (once a tick, whichever thread it is), against one thread. It goes on for as many ticks as it takes another thread to have answered, as `platform/tests/jobs.c` does, and its crowd never sleeps (`Physics.sleeping` off): a world at rest isn't stepped, a tick with no step to share runs on one thread, and no other thread would ever answer. Asserting it of the first 60 ticks failed on CI's two-core runners and, under load, on the web, where a pool's workers start late.
    - **A cast shape that's flush with something touches it** (Box2D counts within about 6 mm), at fraction 0 with a zero normal: code that slides a shape along what it rests on casts a slightly smaller one. The docs say so, and Towers' `Slim` does it.
    - Left out, and listed in the docs: every hit along a ray, layers and masks, more than one body left out, sensors as things to meet, other shapes to cast.
  - **`Inside(physics, sensor, out found)` takes an entity, as Box3D's does**, and reads its sensors' visitors (`b2Shape_GetSensorData`), which are in the image. A function has no `World`, so the match keeps each entity's sensor shapes: `Physics.sensors` (`Map<Entity, Sensors>`, a box's and a circle's handle), written where a shape that's a sensor is put on (the four `Attach` systems) and taken out by the `Destroyed` handlers, with an entry only for entities that have a sensor. An entity's own sensors are what's asked: a body's parts are entities of their own.
  - **The names are Box3D's wherever both packages have the thing** (reconciled the day both landed, 2026-10-06, Box3D's first): `Part { of }`, `part` and `otherPart`, `Hit`, `RayHit`, `Raycast`, `Cast`, `Overlap`, `Inside` by entity, `contactStiffness`, `contactDamping`, `contactSpeed`, `bounceSpeed`, `hitSpeed`, `sleeping`. A name that changes in one changes in the other. What differs is listed in the package's `README.md` (Picks): this one's `Hit` says the parts, its rays and casts leave a `Body` out, and a `Body` has `sleepSpeed`; Box3D's body settings other than the type follow later writes, and this one's don't.
  - **`Hit`** is sent to both bodies that meet faster than `Physics.hitSpeed`, from Box2D's hit events (`b2t_hits`, `b2t_hit`), with the point, the normal (from the receiver into the other: the second entity gets it turned round) and the speed. Box2D only reports a contact between shapes made with `enableHitEvents`, off by default: every shape is made with it (`shape_def`), a byte a shape in the image, which also has the solver keep each contact's `normalVelocity` (see `box2d/TIDE.md`). That's what moved the image's pin. Events of a step aren't in the image, like the contact events.
  - **Tuning is fields of `Physics`**: `contactStiffness`, `contactDamping`, `contactSpeed` (Box2D's contact hertz, damping ratio and push-out speed), `bounceSpeed` (its restitution threshold), `hitSpeed` and `sleeping`, each Box2D's own default, so a game that sets none has the stamps it had. `tune` in `b2t_step` compares each with what C's world has, which is in the image (`b2World_GetContactTuning` is ours, the one vendored change of this work: upstream has the setter alone), sets what differs and notes it (`'k'`, `'r'`, `'i'`, `'z'`), which makes that tick step. `Body.sleepSpeed` (Box2D's sleep threshold) is read when the body is made. Box2D counts contact hertz up to an eighth of the substep rate, 30 at 4 substeps and 60 ticks, the default: stiffer needs more substeps, which the docs say and `box2d_a_tall_stack_stands_longer_with_stiffer_contacts` shows (30 crates and a heavy one dropped on them: 22 off at 15, 15 at 30, none at 60 with 8 substeps; 60 at 4 substeps is 30).
  - **Nothing in `compiler/`, `engine/` or `platform/` changed for any of it.** What got in the way, each worked around in the game or the package: a `local event(Spawned)` handler that takes a component nothing ever spawns (only `Add`s) generates a `tide_hear_Spawned` nobody calls, which `-Werror` refuses (Towers lights a landed brick from its own `Landed`, sent to the brick); and the public helpers and the names above.
- Box3D is the same package for 3D (below).

### Box3D

The Box2D package again, for Erin Catto's Box3D: `packages/box3d/`, namespace `Box3D`, its C functions `b3t_`, the same experiment on the same terms. Everything the Box2D section says of how a match holds the physics is so here too, as it's written there: the state in the match as an image and a stamp, the stamp as a hash of what was done, every change made by the package's systems from components, a world at rest not stepped, the step on the tick's threads through jobs, and the same six tests. So is what it says of what a game does with the package: its systems as one stage with `[Before(PhysicsStages.Step)]` the way in ahead of it (its shapes' systems at offsets 3 to 5, its parts' at 6 to 8, `Simulate` at 9, `Leave` and `Report` at 10), a new body's impulses given by the step, a body that code moves waking with what it touched, gravity that changes waking the world, sensors, `[Position]` and `[Rotation]` on a `Body`'s `position` and `rotation`, the third pin, and `tide_box3d_order` and `tide_box3d_course`. Its page for users is `docs/physics/box3d.md`. What differs:

- **What those call, and what an image in parts makes of each** (2026-10-06; see An image is written as what changed, below):
  - A new body's impulses, at the step: `b3Body_ApplyLinearImpulseToCenter` and `b3Body_ApplyAngularImpulse`, which are followed, as when `Enter` makes them.
  - Waking a body that code moved: `b3Body_SetAwake`, which is followed since a body's `asleep` was built (below). `wake` only makes the call for a body that's asleep and not static, so a position written every tick costs nothing of it. Box3D has no `b3Body_WakeTouching`: `wake_moved` asks what touches the body (`b3Body_GetContactData`) and wakes each.
  - Gravity that changed: `b3World_EnableSleeping` off and on, not followed either, and everything is awake after it, so the next image is whole anyway. (`b3World_SetGravity` is a setting every image writes.)
  - Sensors: no call that changes the world. Every shape is made with `enableSensorEvents`, a bit of its `flags` in the image, and a sensor's is a made shape like any other (`b3CreateShapeInternal`). What a step does for a sensor it writes into the sensor's own arrays, which are a section an image in parts writes whole every time, and into no body, shape or contact. The step's sensor events are asked (`b3World_GetSensorEvents`), as its contact events are. Box3D's writer zeroed a visitor's two spare bytes already.
  - `tide_box3d_course` has every image in parts compared with the image written whole (`b3t_check`) through all of it: 181 of the match of sensors' 330 images are in parts.
- **A body's `asleep`** is as the Box2D section says of its own, rule for rule (the same picks, awaiting the owner; `docs/physics/box3d.md`, Bodies at rest), with `tide_box3d_sleep` for `tide_box2d_sleep`. What differs:
  - The two bytes are the two a `Body` had to spare after `stepBullet` (`FLAGS`: `bullet`, `stepBullet`, `asleep`, `stepAsleep`), so it's 176 bytes as it was. `flags_apart` says both pairs at once, for `written`.
  - **A body can be put to sleep where `Physics.sleeping` is off**, and stays so until something wakes it: Box3D's switch only says what the step does. `sync_body` asks Box3D what type the body is, so one that was made static in the same tick is neither asleep nor awake, and a sleeping one that's made static reads awake after the step (`Leave` asks).
  - **`b3Body_SetAwake` is followed by an image in parts, both ways** (`b3SnapNoteOp`, `box3d/TIDE.md`): `b3WakeSolverSet` and `b3TrySleepIsland` already said what they write, for the step, and the island that's split before it's put to sleep is of awake bodies, which are written anyway. So code that moves a sleeping body, changes how it is, wakes it or puts it to sleep no longer costs that tick's image whole by itself. It still does where a section of the image has to move, as when the step wakes a stack or lets one fall asleep: in a world that's all asleep, the first body to wake has no room where the awake ones go (`box3d_costs`: a crate put to sleep and woken in turn, in a world otherwise asleep, has all 60 of its images whole, 0.26 ms a tick). `tide_box3d_parts` has the cases: `yard.tide` wakes a stack from its bottom crate and puts it to sleep from its top one, another in ticks that follow each other, the ball in a tick that also pushes it, and a crate that's spawned asleep; Box3D by itself wakes a body and puts one to sleep at steps whose images have to be in parts (`where_one_line_says_so`: a body that never falls asleep by itself among them), and at random, right after a restore too.
  - **`Leave` reads no more of a body the step says nothing of than which it is**, as before, but in a tick whose step put bodies to sleep for code (`slept`): then, of each such body, the four bytes of its flags and what Box3D has its type as. A body the step says something of and didn't leave awake is asked whether it's asleep (`b3Body_IsAwake`), as it's asked where it is: one that fell asleep is, and one that code wrote to and the step left alone, a static body that was moved, isn't.
  - The same change to Box3D's sources as to Box2D's (`b3TrySleepIsland`), which `box3d/TIDE.md` had as a thing not fixed and `tide_box3d_parts` kept away from.
  - **Bodies spawned asleep are each a sleeping set and an island of their own**, which are sections an image writes whole whenever one changes: with thousands of them, the ticks in which many wake cost more than where they sleep a stack to a set. Sumo measured it (The physics games), and the docs say what to do instead.
  - **Pins.** The bodies' pin, Box3D's own images' (`OWN_HASH`) and what was answered (`ANSWERS_HASH`) didn't move: every image is the bytes it was. The worlds' did, each for the two bytes of the bodies that sleep in it and for what its stamps note of them.
  - What it costs: nothing that shows (six runs of each, old and new in turn): 1.86 ms a tick with everything moving where it took 1.97, 0.10 with one crate pushed where it took 0.12, 0.010 at rest where it took 0.011.
  - The pins moved with sensor events on every shape as Box2D's did: 24 bytes of the last image's 40,812, each a shape's `flags`, 66 to 67, with the bodies' pin as it was.

- **Five things the owner asked for of both packages (2026-10-06) are built here**: shapes on entities of their own, a body's type that changes, questions asked of the world, what's in a sensor and what meets hard, and tuning as fields of `Physics`. That the five are wanted, that shapes go on entities of their own (and not several of a component on one entity), and that tuning is fields of the singleton are the owner's. The names and the details are Claude's picks, listed under Provisional in the package's `README.md`, made from the same proposals the Box2D package's are to be made from: when that lands, the two are reconciled, name for name. Sumo uses none of them yet (see The physics games). What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **`Part { Entity of; }`** puts an entity's `Box`, `Sphere` and `Capsule` on `of`'s body. The systems that make a body's own shapes say `without Part`, and three more make a part's (`AttachBoxParts` and the like, after the bodies' own, so a game with no part has its shapes made in the order it did): each reads the body through its `World` (`world.TryGet<Body>(part.of, ...)`), keeps the shape's handle in the `Part` (`box`, `sphere`, `capsule`) and whose it is in `Physics.parts` (a map from a part's shape to its entity, with nothing in it for a body's own shapes), and destroys a part that has no body to be on.
    - **A part that goes takes its shapes** (`RemovePart`, a `Destroyed` handler: `Physics.goneShapes`, which `Begin` gives C before the bodies that went: `b3t_gone_shapes`, `b3DestroyShape`, noted `'h'`). **A body that goes takes its parts**: before C removes it, `Begin` asks C for its shapes (`b3t_body_shapes`, `b3t_body_shape`) and destroys the entity of each that's a part's. The part's own handler then finds its shape out of the map already, and lists nothing.
    - **`Touched` and `Parted` say which shapes**: `part` and `otherPart`, each the entity itself for a shape of its own (`PartOf`). They still go to the bodies' entities. C gives each event's two shapes with its two bodies (`touch`, `b3t_touch`). A shape that went by itself this tick is still named by the step's events, and Box3D no longer knows whose it was: `b3t_gone_shapes` keeps that for the tick (`parteds`), so such a parting says the body, which is still there, and no part.
  - **A body's `type`, `gravityScale`, `linearDamping`, `angularDamping` and `bullet` follow what code writes**, where they were read when the body was made. What Box3D has of each is in the component (`stepType` and the like, the package's own, after `bullet`), which is how `Enter` tells: nine more lanes looked at for every body every tick (`written`: the type against what Box3D has, three floats against three, and `bullet` against the byte beside it, which are one lane). `sync_body` sets the type first, so that a speed written with it is the new type's to have, and wakes a sleeping body whose other settings changed.
    - **`b3Body_SetType` isn't followed by an image in parts, on purpose** (measured: a tick that changes one costs 0.25 ms with 2,400 bodies asleep, against 0.010 at rest and 0.09 with one crate pushed): it goes through `b3TransferBody`, which nothing follows, lets go of every contact, and makes each shape's proxy again in another tree.
    - **It sends nothing for what still touches.** Box3D ends every contact of a body whose type changes and begins the ones that still touch in the step, so in a tick that changed a type (`retyped`), `b3t_touches` leaves out a pair of shapes that has both an end and a begin. A pair that only ends is said: two bodies that never move don't touch.
  - **Tuning is fields of `Physics`**: `contactStiffness`, `contactDamping`, `contactSpeed`, `bounceSpeed`, `hitSpeed` and `sleeping`, with Box3D's defaults, so a game that writes none steps as it did. `b3t_step` is given them with the gravity, notes them into the stamp every step, and sets the world's before it steps (`b3World_SetContactTuning`, which has no call to read it back, `b3World_SetRestitutionThreshold`, `b3World_SetHitEventThreshold`: numbers of the world's own that every image writes, so calls an image in parts follows). `sleeping` is `b3World_EnableSleeping`, only called when it differs from the world's: not followed, so that tick's image is whole. Turned off, it wakes a world at rest (`roused`), as a gravity that changed does; the other fields step nothing by themselves.
  - **`Hit { other, point, normal, speed }`** is Box3D's hit events, which need `enableHitEvents` on a shape: every shape is made with it. `normal` is from the other body towards the one that hears it (Box3D's is from its shape A to its shape B: `Report` turns it for A's entity). **What it put in the image** is exact, and the only thing here that changed Box3D's own bytes: a bit of each shape's flags, a bit of each contact's, and in each point of a touching contact the speed the two closed at (`normalVelocity`, 0 before): 280 bytes of the pinned match's last 40,812. With that one line taken out, every one of its 300 images is the bytes it was before any of this (`OWN_HASH`, below). It's the same bytes on every SIMD path (the solver writes it a lane at a time, for the lanes that ask), and nothing reads it but the events: the bodies' pin didn't move.
  - **Questions** (`Raycast`, `Cast`, `Overlap`, `Inside`) are functions that take the `Physics` singleton, so they're asked from systems, handlers and views alike. `Ready` makes sure C has a world at the match's stamp (`b3t_find`), or has one made from the image (`b3t_rebuild`); then one C call asks Box3D and gives back handles, which Tide turns into entities (`Met`, `Owners`). What code relies on:
    - **They change nothing**, of Box3D's world or of the bridge's: Box3D's queries only read, and `b3SnapNoteOp` names them as calls that write nothing. `tide_box3d_course` asks thirteen things every tick, before the step, after it and from a view, with every image in parts checked against the image written whole, and has Box3D's image at every tick the bytes it is with nothing asked.
    - **They're asked of the state the match's stamp names**, found by the stamp alone (`at`: the running tick's world first). After the stage that's this tick's step; before it, the last tick's; a system written among the package's own would be asked of the world with what `Enter` gave it.
