Handlers and spawns, which change one row, make the page their own as they write, and a component put back for one entity (a handler's `mut`, `world.Set`, `tide_set_<Component>`) only where a lane isn't what's there already (`tide_table_scatter`): code that changes one field of a component of forty lanes leaves the other pages shared and their hashes good.
  - Sparse filters count matching rows before the chunk's lanes are taken (`gen_rows_count`). A whole chunk with none is left alone, its pages and hashes too. When writing apart, the loop writes the rows it skips as they were, so filtered systems need no first copy of a page.
  - A system that takes its entities' input reads their Owner from the lanes the loop reads (`gen_row_owner`), including when it changes Owner and writes it apart. The schedule counts that as a read of Owner (see Determinism).
  - A chunk in pieces begins its changed lanes once before the pieces and ends them once after (`tide_table_lanes_begin_pieces`, `tide_table_lanes_piece`, `tide_table_lanes_end_pieces`; see Determinism for who calls them). Apart, the page to write is in the table from the start and remembers the old page in `tide_page.was`; each piece writes all its rows, including the skipped ones unchanged. The end keeps the old shared page when no row changed. A whole chunk with no matching rows takes no pages for its pieces.
  - Systems written in C still copy shared pages before their chunks run in pieces; handlers, spawns and other one-row updates keep their existing path. These paths must read what they write in place.
  - This rests on the schedule keeping two systems off the same chunk's lanes at once when either writes them, including systems whose sparse filters admit opposite rows (see Determinism). `compiler/tests/e2e/rows` compares whole chunks and pieces, private and shared pages, and one and several threads, and checks unchanged pages stay shared; `tests/test_table.c` checks the pieces functions.
- A component is kept in lanes: columns of 4 bytes of it each (2 or 1 when its size isn't a multiple of 4: `tide_lane_width` in `tide/table.h`), so each of its fields is an array of its own, as C written for speed keeps them. Generated code gathers an entity's components from their lanes into copies (`tide_gather_<Component>`), and puts the `mut` ones back once it's done with the entity (`tide_scatter_<Component>`), so a system's ifs become selects and its loop over entities runs with SIMD. That only holds while the C compiler can tell the lanes' places don't change under the loop:
  - A system's loop over a chunk's rows is a function of its own (`gen_rows` in `compiler/src/codegen.c`): `tide_rows_<System>_<n>`, called for each chunk or piece. Its parameters include each component lane separately, with `restrict` where the loop has exclusive access to its lanes (`rows_alone`). `TIDE_ROWS` keeps the function from being inlined, so the C compiler can use that promise across the whole loop without checking every read/write pair for overlap. Wide loops can then vectorize without those overlap checks; whether a loop vectorizes still depends on its body and filters.
  - The function makes the arrays the loop reads from its own parameters (`tide_in_<Component>` to read what it changes, `tide_col_<Component>` to write and to read the rest). Their places stay fixed through the loop; arrays handed to other functions could otherwise be taken to change under a write through a byte pointer.
  - A system that can write apart has separate in-place and apart functions: the former reads and writes a changed lane through one pointer, the latter through two. A system that can write apart and has sparse filters also has filter-free versions (`every`). After counting the matching rows in the chunk or task's piece, if all pass it calls `every`; if none pass a whole chunk is skipped; otherwise the filter stays in the loop. Apart, the mixed loop gathers and puts back each changed component for every row, running the body only for the matching rows (`through`).
  - Archetypes share functions whose generated text from the parameters on is the same (`rows_list`): the loop depends on the rows' columns, including scenes, Owner and sparse presence, rather than on which table supplied them. More archetypes with the same columns add calls, not copies of the loop.
  - `restrict` is omitted when code can reach the loop's lanes another way: writes through World, reads through World of a component it also changes, and starting tasks, which get and put back their entity's components themselves (`rows_alone`). Those systems keep the same aliasing behavior.
  - At most 240 scalar lane pointers are parameters of a rows function (`ROWS_LANES_MOST`), counting read and write pointers separately when apart. The whole signature also stays within [V8's 1,000-parameter limit](https://chromium.googlesource.com/v8/v8.git/+/main/src/wasm/wasm-limits.h) for Wasm functions (`ROWS_PARAMS_MOST`): sparse presence columns, fallback arrays, and the leading parameters count too. A component that would cross either budget is passed as arrays of pointers instead, copied into arrays of the loop's own; the fallback has no `restrict` promise for those lanes. `tidec_rows_parameters` checks a legal game at the component limit and, in the web suite, validates its compiled Wasm module.
  - Views keep their loops in their run functions (`gen_chunk`), with only the in-place version: the local world they change has no snapshots. A system whose one Send can be batched also keeps its existing path.
  - Two measured gaps remain, and neither optimization is implemented: a chunk with mixed passing and skipped rows still runs its filtered loop, rather than sending runs of passing rows through `every`; and all lanes of a `mut` component are still written apart and compared at the end, even when the body never changes some of them. Knowing which individual lanes can change could avoid those writes and comparisons. These are implementation gaps, with no change to the language's `mut` rule.
  - The wide loops without a filter vectorize at width 4 on SSE2 and Wasm, and 8 on AVX2; mixed filters remain scalar. Five alternating native runs on Windows, 12 cores, using the least saved median and the same corrected C snapshots in both builds (`row-functions-final-before` and `row-functions-final`, measured before later dev integration), put a million entities of twelve float lanes, six changed, at 6.22 -> 3.62 ms a tick on one thread while the prior state is kept. With a sparse tag and every row passing, without a kept state, it is 3.77 -> 1.32; with none passing and a kept state, 2.99 -> 0.105. Unfiltered private rows on twelve offered threads measured 0.615 -> 0.671, while identical C measured 0.534 -> 0.583; that result does not isolate dispatch costs from variation. The settings, ranges, hashes and compile measurements are in `bench/results/row-functions.md` and its metadata.
  - Five alternating end-to-end build rounds of 100 helper functions and 100 entity systems, using one or four archetypes and the least saved median, show the fixture tradeoff: with four archetypes, a Tide-body edit in native release is 33.07 -> 3.16 s, and in web release 16.22 -> 2.69 s; native debug is 0.62 -> 0.73 s and web debug 0.90 -> 0.95 s. Cold builds and unchanged/C/header edits are in the report. These Windows measurements include build completion and were taken with recorded CPU contention; their ranges and the separate direct Clang phases qualify the comparison.
  - A task's components are copies too, loaded each time it goes on and put back when it waits or ends, and around what it awaits or starts, which may change them (`gen_entity_copies` in `compiler/src/codegen.c`).
- Each page keeps the hash of what's in use in it until it changes, so a world's hash (`tide_world_hash`) only reads what changed since the last one. It covers contents, never addresses or capacity, so it's the same on every machine for the same state. The command queue, empty between ticks, isn't shared.
- **A page's hash is `tide_hash_wide`** (2026-10-07; the owner, of a faster hash that would move every pinned world hash: "if its faster at no cost other than out tests then its not even a question, update it"). What's built, and what code relies on:
  - It's four of `tide_hash`'s chains side by side (`engine/src/net.c`): chain k takes the words at 8k, 32 + 8k and on, with `tide_hash`'s own step (`add_word`); what's left after the last round of 32 bytes goes a word a chain, the last short and zeros past its bytes; then the size and the four chains are one chain's words, and the end is `tide_hash`'s. One chain has the CPU wait for each word's multiplies before the next word; four don't. Three more were measured and not taken: eight chains were no quicker here, and one multiply a word, with four chains or eight, was a tenth quicker, but a word that differs in one bit near its middle then leaves its chain differing in the top bit alone, which the chain's next word undoes if it differs in its own top bit.
  - **Only pages use it** (`tide_page_hash`). `tide_hash`, `tide_hash_more` and `tide_hash_end` are what they were, for everything that isn't a page: how a world's hash puts its pages' hashes together, the physics packages' stamps, what entities are born as, shaders' ids. `Math.Hash` and the seeding of `Random` are functions of their own. So no simulation changed, which the pins say: the physics packages' pins of their bodies and of their own images' bytes, and what was answered, stayed, and every pinned hash of a world moved (40 values in 17 files, two of them decimal in `compiler/tests/CMakeLists.txt`, four the web's own in `local_fields_same.h` and `local_events_same.h`, whose deltas carry the hash).
  - `PROTOCOL` went to 13: two builds of one game that hash pages differently would connect, and then disagree about every tick. `tests/test_commands.c` writes that number by hand.
  - Measured with Sumo's full ring, by `sumo_costs` (three runs each, a quiet machine): keeping and hashing a world on one thread from 0.28 ms to 0.12 natively and to 0.135 under Node; with threads called in (below) from 0.105 to 0.06, and from 0.117 to 0.077.
  - Tests: `tests/test_hash.c`, natively and under Node: it's what its definition says, written the plain way, at every length around its rounds and words; a page that differs in one bit anywhere is another number, the top and middle bits of a word in each chain among them, and so is a run of zeros of another length; and four pinned numbers, the same everywhere.
- **What changed is hashed on a few of the pool's threads, where it's megabytes** (2026-10-06, the owner of cores that sat idle while one hashed: "sounds like we should"; how is Claude's). A page's hash is its own piece of work, and a session hashes between ticks, when the pool has nothing to do. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - `tide_game.hash_world_on(world, jobs)` is the hash sessions ask for when they have threads (`hash_of` in `engine/src/session.c`: the server after each tick, a client as each tick is confirmed). The generated one (`tide_game_hash_on`, in the `.c` alone, so no game's header or hash moved) gathers every page `tide_world_hash` is about to ask of whose kept hash isn't of what it holds (`tide_entities_stale`, `tide_table_stale`, `tide_heap_stale`, into a `tide_stale_pages`, `tide/page.h`), has `tide_pages_hash` hash them, and then calls `tide_world_hash`, which finds each kept. So the hash is the one it was, by the code it was: the threads only fill what it reads. A container that joins a world's hash joins that gathering too, or its pages are hashed on one thread as before, which is slower and never wrong.
  - `tide_pages_hash` asks for a thread more for each half megabyte to hash (`SHARED_HASH_BYTES`), three at the most (`SHARED_HASH_MOST`), and with less than half a megabyte does nothing: the hash that follows does it. Each thread takes the next page nobody has (a counter), so a page is hashed by one thread, and the list has no page twice, as a world has none twice among what it hashes (a heap's page of several places is one).
  - **It asks through `tide_jobs.run_some(self, others, work, context)`, never `run`** (new, and the platform layer's pools have it: desktop's wakes that many workers where `run` wakes all, the web's notifies that many). Waking all of them is what was tried first, and it lost what it gained: on this machine's 12 cores the hash took 0.10 ms where it took 0.28, and the ticks around it 0.69 ms where they took 0.585, the ones after a hash on one thread as much as the ones after a shared one. Waking one, two, three and five took the hash to 0.17, 0.13, 0.105 and 0.09, a run each, with the ticks within a few hundredths of a millisecond of where they were. Why every thread awake slows the ticks wasn't found out. A pool with no `run_some` (a host's own) isn't asked.
  - On the web a round that asks for a few makes no workers: they're made by the first round that wants them all, as a tick with work for threads does, and until then such a round is the caller's alone (`run_some` in `platform/src/jobs.c`). A page pays far more for its workers than hashing would save it; Sumo's are made as its first ring is raised, whose step is shared out.
  - Measured with Sumo (`sumo_costs`, which hashes every other tick's world this way where its program has the platform's pool, so the two are of one scene; old and new in turn, four runs natively and three under Node): keeping and hashing a world with four bots at a full ring 0.28 ms to 0.105 natively and 0.28 to 0.117 under Node, after a shock 0.44 to 0.16 and 0.43 to 0.18, with the ticks between as they were (0.57 to 0.58 natively).
  - Tests: `page_stale_pages_are_hashed_on_threads_before_the_hash` (`tests/test_page.c`, a pool that takes turns: what's gathered, how many threads are asked for, what's left to the hash that follows, pools of other kinds); `jobs_hash_what_changed_on_threads` and `jobs_some_workers_join` (`platform/tests/jobs.c`, the platform's pool: 60 rounds of changes to a table of 4.8 MB against a twin hashed on one thread, and a game's world through `hash_world_on`), natively and under Node.
- A chunk has 2048 rows (`arch_shift`): as many as keep its widest column, the entity's 8 bytes, to a 16 KiB page, so a lane's pages are 8 KiB at most. The first chunk starts with room for 4 rows and grows, so archetypes with a few entities take little memory.
- Structural changes and events wait in the world's queue until the end of the tick (`tide_queue` in `tide/table.h`, `tide_command` in the generated header). Each is queued with the bytes it has, what's before `data` and the member of `data` it carries (`TIDE_COMMAND_SIZE`), so an event costs its own size whatever the game's biggest spawn is: nothing copies or reads a whole `tide_command`. An item starts with its size, which is how the queue goes from one to the next (`tide_queue_next`, from a `tide_queue_place`), and is whole in one page. A task's queue hands its pages to the world's, for the empty ones the world kept (`tide_queue_append`), so merging the tasks' queues copies next to nothing and, after the first ticks, allocates nothing. A world unpacked from bytes checks that each change is as big as its kind needs (`tide_command_fits`), since applying one reads that much.

## Performance

- Performance is a top priority. Weigh design choices by their runtime cost.
- Runtime, compilation and rendering speed are requirements. Compare equivalent work with the best practical handwritten implementation; an unexplained order-of-magnitude gap is a performance bug to investigate and fix. Runtime comparisons keep the same determinism guarantees. Fast iteration matters alongside the speed of the shipped game.
- Internal performance changes are authorized when they preserve observable language behavior, including deterministic results, ordering, snapshots and hot reload. Storage, generated code, scheduling, batching and caching are implementation choices; changes to the language's expectations remain the owner's decision.
- Flexible features are fine even if they cost more, as long as the common, fixed path stays fast.
- Friction between engine code and user code should be minimal or non-existent.
- Snapshotting and restoring the full simulation state must be cheap. Rollback depends on it.

## Networking

- Devices use explicit read-only parameters everywhere they can be read: `Sample(Devices devices)`, views, systems, match handlers and helper functions. Bare `Devices.keyboard` is an error. Sample and views get this machine's devices; match systems and handlers get the entity owner's tick input, or the server's for unowned entities and code that runs once. Only fields match code reads are sent. Frame text and window positions keep their existing restrictions.
- UI actions enter the match as typed `command Name { ... }` payloads, sent by local code with `Send(...)` or `entity.Send(...)`. A command has one void handler, `command(Name value, PlayerID sender) Handler(...)`. The engine supplies the sender from the connection; ordinary guard clauses decide which actions to take. There is no acceptance method, return status or transaction. Match events remain simulation notifications.
- Commands are bounded copied wire payloads, queued independently of tick input and resent until a verified world confirms their per-player sequence. The server chooses the tick and order; handlers join the existing deferred event queue at tick end. Sequence state is part of snapshots, hashes and host migration. Initial limits are 1024 packed bytes per command, 32 pending per player and 16 scheduled per tick. Game-specific validation belongs to the handler.
- The command C contract is agreed: `tide_game.check_command(data, size, &channel)`, `command_sequence(world, player, channel)` and `command(world, sender, channel, sequence, data, size)` validate bytes (saying which channel the command's type is in), read the world's sequence in a channel and record a received command. `tide_session_send_command` and `tide_client_send_command` copy bytes into the pending queue, and derive the channel from the bytes. Generated `tide_local_command(local, channel, &size)` peeks the local queue's oldest command in a channel and `tide_local_command_sent(local, channel)` removes it after a successful send; the host table exposes both, and the standard host goes through the channels in turn, so a channel that's full at the session holds back only its own. Payloads include a type and target plus copied fields; string bytes and a `List<byte>`'s bytes, never heap handles, go over the network.
- **A command's ordering is its type's, per player** (agreed 2026-10-06; built the same day, the picks Provisional in `docs/spec.md`, Commands): `[Channel(Channels.Uploads)]` on a `command`, with an enum member of the game's, puts it in a channel of its own, in order among that channel's commands and against nothing else; `[Unordered]` gives it no order at all. The server still picks the tick, and every handler runs in the tick's end queue in the server's order. What's built, and what code relies on:
  - **Channels are numbered in the world's state, not named there:** 0 is the default, and the members `[Channel]` names are 1 to N in the order of their enums' declarations and then their members (`number_channels` in `compiler/src/check.c`, `program.channels`; `decl.channel`, `decl.unordered`), 63 of the game's own at most (`TIDE_COMMAND_CHANNELS_MOST`). `tide_game.command_channels` says how many (0 for 1), and `TIDE_COMMAND_UNORDERED` (`UINT32_MAX`) is the unordered ones' channel on every contract that takes one. The channel is never on the wire: the command's type is in its first 16 bits, and `check_command` says the type's channel, so a client can't put a command in another's.
  - **The sequence state is the hidden `tide_command_state` singleton** (`compiler/src/codegen.c`): `seq<p>` for the default channel, as it was, then `seq<p>_<k>` for channel k, then, only in a game with an unordered command, `top<p>` (the highest id applied) and `seen<p>` (a `long`: a bit for each of the `TIDE_COMMAND_WINDOW` ids down from the top). So a game with no channel attributes has the world layout it had. `tide_game.command_applied(world, player, id)` reads the window (NULL in a game with no unordered command): an id older than the window counts as applied, which is why a client never has more than 32 pending unordered ids, allocated in sequence from the top. An unordered command's id goes in `sequence`'s place everywhere.
  - **A `List<byte>` field is a file's bytes** (`type_has_bytes`): on the wire as a varint count and then the bytes on a whole byte (`tide_command_align`), packed into room the list's count sizes, through `tide_list_flatten`, and read into a scratch list (`tide_list_from`) that `tide_own_` copies into the match's heap with the rest of the event. A command with one is `TIDE_COMMAND_LIMIT` (4 MiB) packed bytes at most, any other `TIDE_COMMAND_BYTES` (1024) as before; the error for any other list stays.
  - **Over `TIDE_COMMAND_BYTES` a command goes in chunks** (`engine/src/session.c`: `MSG_UPLOAD` up, `MSG_DOWNLOAD` down, `PROTOCOL` 12): `CHUNK` bytes each, `COMMAND_CHUNKS_PER_UPDATE` (16) an update each way, under the inputs and frames, each sent again after `resend_after` without an acknowledgement. A client uploads one at a time (`send_upload`), the server acknowledges in its SERVER datagrams (`UPLOAD_PART`, the chunks it has, as a CLIENT says a world's; `UPLOAD_WHOLE`, with the download's number once it's published) and takes the whole command into the player's queue as any other (`take_command`). **No tick waits for a transfer:** when a big command's turn comes, the server publishes it (`publish_command`, `published`: `PUBLISHED_MOST` kept, until their tick's frame is gone), sends it down to each player (`send_downloads`, acknowledged in CLIENTs after what the world lacks), and puts it in a tick only once every player in the match has it (`everyone_has`; a player being sent a world, or catching up, is sent it with the ticks it lacks instead, as `needs_published` says by the tick of the world it was sent). The frame then names the download (`size` over `TIDE_COMMAND_BYTES`, then its number) instead of carrying the bytes; a client's `pending_frame` reads the names once its pieces are there (`read_frame_names`) and is complete once those downloads are whole (`frame_complete`). Its sender keeps the bytes and, told the number, says it has the download (`note_download`), so nothing goes down to it. The wire adds nothing for a game without commands (`g->command` guards both sides), so `net_a_match_costs_little_to_keep_going` stands as pinned.
  - **The local outbox** is `tide_outgoing_item`s (a malloc'd buffer, its size and channel) in the local state, `TIDE_COMMAND_OUTBOX` of them (32 a channel, the unordered ones counted as one), packed after the struct in `tide_local_pack` (each checked again as it's unpacked) and freed by `tide_local_free` and `tide_local_disconnected`.
  - Generated C: a game with no commands generates the C it did, byte for byte (old and new tidec over every game's source list); one with commands differs only in the contract's signatures, the outbox and `TIDE_COMMAND_CHANNELS` in its header, and so in its hash, which is one hash, not a layout change.
  - Tests: `compiler/tests/e2e/commands_channels` (channels numbered and their sequences apart, the window, a 3 MB command whole on one world and through the outbox's bytes, a chat line handled within half a second of a 3 MB upload on a lossy network while no player's verified tick falls behind, the same upload on a lossless one with no input late, 20 unordered commands once each under 30% loss, a late joiner's world with the sequences, a handover keeping every channel's and the window), natively and on the web; `compiler/tests/errors/command_channel_*.tide` and `command_unordered_*.tide`, one per diagnostic; `tests/test_commands.c` as it was, on the default channel.

- Rollback netcode with full server authority. The server is the source of truth. Clients predict ahead and roll back when the server disagrees.
- Single-player is a match too: the machine runs the server itself and connects to it through a loopback transport, so there's no separate offline path. The engine assumes nothing about what games do with matches (no built-in pause): it gives them the tools.
- There's one kind of match, so no special case for playing alone: a match starts closed, and whether other machines can join it is a switch on it (`Session.Open` and `Session.Close`), not another way to start one. Single-player is a match nobody else was let into.
- A match takes as many players as the game says: its `maxPlayers` setting (`tide_game.max_players`, `TIDE_GAME_PLAYERS` in the generated header), 16 without one (`TIDE_PLAYERS_DEFAULT`) and 256 at most (`TIDE_PLAYERS_MOST`, `tide/player.h`). The world keeps an input for each and the server's in the slot after them, so the count is part of the layout: builds that differ in it are different games, and a reload that changes it starts the match over (`prepare_server` refuses). The netcode has no smaller limit of its own: a server's arrays by player are allocated for its game's count, sets of players are `tide_player_set` (320 bits: the players and the server's slot) and go on the wire as a varint of as many bits as they have (`write_players` in `engine/src/session.c`), so a match of a few players pays what a 32-bit set did; a tick's count of joins and leaves is a varint; and a handover record goes in parts of a datagram (`HANDOVER_PART`), as 256 digests don't fit one. A scene's `tide_players` is as many 32-bit words as the game's players take (`add_scene_player_words` in `compiler/src/check.c`). `compiler/tests/e2e/players` runs a match of 150 on the loopback network, through a handover.
- Clients have no input delay: they run ahead of the server by about half the round trip plus a small margin, so their inputs arrive in time, and only other players' inputs are guessed. The server says how early their inputs arrive, and clients keep them as early as they have to be by it (below). A client that sends none (a game without an input) ignores it and keeps the lead it joined with.
- How late other players see you is the priority (the owner, 2026-10-05: "the entire point"; a player's own input was never the issue, and a change that made views draw the tick in progress was built by mistake and reverted the same day, as it also made each machine guess the others a tick further). What's between a key read on one machine and another's view showing it, beside the network: the wait for a tick to sample it, how early the client sends its inputs (the server holds each until its tick), the server's frame, and the watcher's.
  - A client sends its inputs as early as they have to be, and no earlier (`keep_early` in `engine/src/session.c`). The server says with everything it sends how long the player's inputs waited before their ticks ran (`note_wait`: from when each arrived, by its transport's clock, to the update that ran its tick, the least of the ticks an update ran; under 0 by a tick for each input missing when a tick ran, counted from the newest input the player sent, one that came too late among them: `connection.newest_sent`). It's said in 960ths of a second (`WAITS`), a signed varint after the newest input, which is a byte while the wait is under 65 ms; the tick before them went from 32 bits to a varint, so a SERVER is no bigger than it was for a match's first 9 hours at 60 ticks a second (a byte more after), and `PROTOCOL` went to 10. The least of those waits over a while is what counts, as being early is for the input that's nearly late.
  - What the client keeps that least at (`early_wanted`): its lead in ticks (`tide_client_desc.lead`: 0 on the server's machine, whose inputs go straight in), or, with `lead_frame`, which a session's player over a network has, one of its own frames where that's less (the owner, 2026-10-06, of what it saves: "1 tick would be huge"). A frame because inputs are read and sent a frame at a time, and every unacknowledged input goes again in each: so a frame that came late, or a datagram that was lost, still leaves the input in time. A machine's frame is the time between its updates, on the whole, the last while it watched (`frame`), and counts as a 60th of a second where it's shorter (`EARLY_FRAME`): that's what a match of 60 ticks a second has with a tick, so a fast display gets no less. Hosts that say `lead` alone keep whole ticks, as tests and benchmarks do.
  - It watches a while at a time. Under what's wanted, its clock goes ahead at once by what was missing (`clock_start`, to a 960th of a second, where it used to be whole ticks). Over it for the whole while by as much again (a tick at the most), its clock loses the difference, a tenth of the time that passes (`CLOCK_SLOW`, in `update_client`), so no tick stands still. The while is 2 seconds (`EARLY_LOOK`), and 10 after an input was nearly late (`EARLY_KEEP`): what made it so may come again, and has to stay away that long. One that was nearly late in the client's first second in the match (`EARLY_START`) doesn't count for that, as a machine starting up stutters, and that doesn't come again. After a change it watches again once what the server says is about the clock as it is now (below).
  - **What the server says only counts when it's about inputs the client sent by its clock as it is** (2026-10-06, found with Alley, the game Sumo took the place of: a player who joined a world of 160 KB mid-match ran the 60 ticks ahead a client may, its inputs waiting 0.9 seconds at the server, for ten seconds and more, and ran 2,736 predicted ticks again where the others ran 80). Two things made it so, and both are fixed:
    - The server ran ticks for the player while its world was on its way, 53 of them there, and said each was a tick the player's input was late for, counted from the tick it joined. They're ticks the player had no world to sample an input for. A server says nothing of a player's waits before the player's first input (`connection.sent_input`; `WAIT_UNKNOWN` on the wire, 3 bytes while it lasts, and `PROTOCOL` went to 11), and how late a player is goes by the newest input it sent, late ones too (`newest_sent`), where it went by the newest that came in time: a player whose inputs all come two ticks late is two ticks late, however long that has gone on.
    - The client took what it last heard as being about its clock once a round trip and three ticks had passed since it set it (`shown`). On a network that's late or loses datagrams, what it last heard was still from before, so it read that it was a second late and put its clock a second ahead. It goes by the tick now, never by a time: after a change, and as it joins, `early_settled` notes the first tick whose input is sampled when its time comes by the clock as it is (`early_tick`: the next one, once it has sampled up to where the clock is and isn't losing time any more), and nothing the server says counts until it has run that tick (`waited_tick`, the server's tick as it said so). Datagrams come in any order, so the newest is what it said last, by its tick.
    - A client behind the server catches up with its confirmed ticks before predicting (`fall_behind`, `play`): after a world arrives, after it arrives again on a resync, and after this machine stopped while the server went on. It discards predictions made from the old timeline, and samples nothing for ticks that can't get there in time (`skip_old_inputs`, `sampled_from`); that boundary is checked each catch-up update, so a second pause doesn't make it sample old ticks. Even outside catch-up, it samples no tick the server has already said it ran. A shorter pause with input delay enters catch-up too when new inputs are behind the server and a complete, due confirmed tick is left because the frame allowance is exhausted. Future inputs still go out while it catches up. Ticks it sampled nothing for aren't counted as late, nor do their waits change its clock; a fresh input that comes late still is. It predicts once every tick the server said it had run is confirmed, or the next confirmed tick's time hasn't come.
    - Catch-up work belongs to a frame's moment, shared by both client updates a session makes (`catch_up_room`), not to each call: 8 authoritative ticks at the most normally (`CATCH_UP_TICKS`), or twice the ticks of a consistently longer frame interval where that's more. The interval is the shorter of the last two, so one stalled frame doesn't ask for more. With a tick clock (`now_ns`), authoritative work has a budget of half that interval, checked between ticks, after running the ticks that came due and one more, capped at 8 mandatory ticks; a machine too slow for the match falls behind without each frame asking for more work than the last. Prediction and re-simulation keep their existing budgets.
    - A clock proven far ahead comes back at once (`clock_back`), as when the server's machine stopped and dropped time. In two updates that hear from the server, with every tick it says it ran confirmed here, the client's clock must be past the input window, and a clock set as a join sets it, from the server's tick and that answer's round trip, must fit within it. A delayed answer includes its delay, so a slow network alone doesn't reset the clock. Predictions past the new clock go, while inputs already sampled stay: it samples nothing more until their time comes.
    - `net_join_big_world_runs_no_further_ahead_than_its_inputs_need` (`compiler/tests/e2e/net_join_big_test.c`) joins a world that takes over a second to come on 8 lossy networks, and checks from its arrival that prediction starts only after catching up, stays within 20 ticks and 150 ms of input wait, and confirms at most 8 newly run ticks a frame. The same file checks a resync, two updates per frame, expensive ticks with made-up clocks, and bounded frames on a machine too slow for the match. `compiler/tests/e2e/seen` checks a client returning after a pause, a server returning after one with the client's clock restored, and delayed/reordered answers that leave a running clock alone.
  - Measured in made-up time, frames of a 60th of a second from a key read to another player's view showing it, on a network with no delay and one 30 ms each way. With inputs 2 to 6 ticks early, wherever in that the clock started: 3 and 6 frames at 60 ticks a second, 6.5 and 9.5 at 30, 8 and 12 at 20. A tick early at the least: 2 and 5, 4.5 and 7.5, 5 and 9. A frame: 2 and 5, 2.5 and 5.5, 3 and 6, with no input late. What's left at fewer ticks a second is the wait for a tick to read the key, half a tick on the whole.
  - All the time an input waits less is also time less that a machine runs ahead of the server, guessing the others: what it puts right when a guess was wrong is that much smaller.
  - `tide_client_status.early_ms` is the wait the server last said, and `late_ticks` how many ticks it ran without an input this machine had sampled for them. `server_tick` is the last tick the server said it had reached; `run_ticks` counts every tick this client ran, and `ahead_ticks` the predicted ones among them, including `again_ticks`. `compiler/tests/e2e/seen` pins the frames above as the most they may be, with none late, and checks that on a network that delays some datagrams 40 ms more than others the inputs go as early as that needs, that a machine of 30 frames a second keeps a 30th of a second, and that a frame that never came makes no input late.
- Running predicted ticks again is prediction's cost, and a client keeps it within what a frame can afford by delaying its input (`tide_prediction`, the `prediction` setting): it samples inputs as far ahead as ever (`sampled`), so they arrive in time, but runs ticks `delay` short of them. Predicting (`Prediction.Rollback`, the default), it starts with none and keeps the least at which running ticks again fits, by what that costs in practice, measured with the host's clock (`now_ns`, which `tide/host.h` sets to `tide_time_now_ns`); without a clock, as in tests and benchmarks, there's no delay and a run is the same on every machine, and the server's own machine (`tide_client_desc.lead` 0, whose inputs go straight in) takes none. What a rollback costs is measured as it happens (`note_tick_cost`): its first tick apart, which makes Box3D's world again in Sumo, as the median of the last five rollbacks' (`rerun_firsts`; none in the client's first second with a world, when everything is cold), and each after it at what a predicted tick costs (`tick_cost`, a moving mean, a sample of which counts for four ticks at the most: a stall isn't a cost); and what rollbacks have cost an update on the whole (`resim_load`, a moving mean over `LOAD_SECONDS`, an update counting for two budgets at the most, rescaled when the delay changes, as each rollback then runs a tick less or more). A rollback would run again about the client's lead of late and a tick (`lead_mean`, sampled only while it's level with the server: a server that fell silent, or a burst of its ticks still being confirmed, isn't a lead), and may take up to a frame, the time between its updates (`ROLLBACK_FRAMES`), and never less than `tide_client_desc.resim_budget` (4 ms unless the host says) nor than a 60th of a second (`ROLLBACK_SECONDS`), so a 144 Hz display gets no more delay than a 60 Hz one; rollbacks together may take up to the budget an update. It takes a tick more when either is over and its lead is settled (within `LEAD_SETTLED` ticks of its mean, and level: a client whose clock jumped while its machine stalled, or whose server's ticks are arriving in a burst, runs far ahead for a moment, and that moment is no reason for a delay that takes seconds to come back), and one back once both would still hold with a tick less, with a tenth to spare, for `ROOM_SECONDS` (half a second), or at once where half of what it may take would be to spare, with a round trip and three ticks between changes; and with no rollback for `QUIET_SECONDS`, nothing says the delay is still needed, so it takes none more and takes them back a tick at a time, which is what keeps a stale estimate from holding a client at a delay forever. `net_a_stall_leaves_no_lasting_delay` (`compiler/tests/e2e/net_test.c`) stalls a client's machine for a second and a half, and then its network, after which it runs 60 ticks ahead on its clock, and checks neither leaves it any delay. `tide_client_status` says what it goes by (`rollback_ms`, `rollback_most_ms`, `resim_ms`), which the lag harness records (the last three of `tide_run_netcode`'s values). Until 2026-10-07 it charged a full rollback of every predicted tick against the budget every frame, whether any happened, and took the delay back only once that was under a third of the budget for 5 seconds: so Sumo's players sat at whatever delay a join or a round's ring raise had pushed them to, with two ticks of lead on a desktop and one in a browser, whatever the ping, and no cut to the tick (the hash was never in `tick_cost`) could reach it (`bench/README.md`, Input delay in Sumo, which has the before and after). Two cuts before this one are in that section too: one that ratcheted to 41 ticks after a 600 ms stall of the machine, and one that gave the browser's host a delay from a cold first tick nothing refreshed. With `Prediction.Delay`, clients never predict: the delay starts at the lead and round trip plus half a round trip and two ticks, grows when a frame hadn't come by its tick, and shrinks once frames have come three ticks early for five seconds (`SLACK_SECONDS`, that mode's alone). Either way a frame whose tick the clock hasn't reached waits (`play`), so ticks run at the match's pace however early frames come, and views blend up to the clock less the delay. One change per round trip (`adjust_delay`).
- A client guesses the other players' inputs, and the server's, by their last ones for only so long (the owner, 2026-10-05: "we should limit how far we extrapolate inputs; if we have 500ms ping the corrections are WILD"): 0.15 seconds past the last tick the server confirmed (`GUESS_SECONDS`, or `tide_client_desc.guess_seconds`; to the nearest tick), and in the ticks it predicts past that, as if they'd let go. A guess that was wrong then moves things by what a player does in that long at most, where it was for as far as the client runs ahead, about its round trip. Agreed 2026-10-06: the 0.15 seconds, as the engine's with no setting for now; that fields are let go unless they're marked; and the mark, `[Absolute]`, on the input's own fields (`docs/spec.md`, Input, Decided; the details under it are Provisional). How:
  - Let go is the input's defaults, through the steps any input takes, but for the fields marked `[Absolute]` (the owner's name for it, over `[Hold]`: a field whose default isn't rest, as it says where something is, like an aim or a pointer's cell), which a guess keeps. The devices match code reads are let go with the rest. tidec generates it as `tide_game.let_go_inputs(world, keep)` (`gen_game_api` in `compiler/src/codegen.c`), in the generated `.c`, and not at all for an input that's all `[Absolute]`. It gives the tick before the same input (`previous_inputs`), so no `.down` or `.up` is true for a guess that lets go. `[Absolute]` is an attribute on the field and nothing else (`check_absolute_attribute`, `field_absolute`): nothing of it is in the header, so a game's hash is what it was.
  - It's only ever done to a predicted world: `run_predicted` in `engine/src/session.c`, at the first tick `guess` ticks past `verified`, and the ticks after start from that world. What the match is stays as it was: a slot whose input didn't arrive keeps its last one, on the server and in every tick it confirms.
  - A tick run that way is another world than the same tick with the last inputs kept (`tide_client.let_go` is the first such tick), so two things follow. It never counts as having gone as predicted (`tick < c->let_go` in `play`): its hash isn't the server's, and the client would ask for the world again. And once ticks before it are confirmed, so that it's within the guess, it runs again with the last inputs, and the ticks after it (`keep_guessing`, `predict_from`): while another player goes on doing something, a client further ahead than its guess runs the ticks past it again each time a tick is confirmed.
  - It costs nothing where nothing is let go: a client that runs no further ahead than its guess (a round trip under about a tenth of a second), and a guess that changes no input, as nobody is doing anything or all that differs is `[Absolute]`. The budget for running ticks again (`adjust_delay`) already counts every predicted tick as run again each frame, so it's as it was.
  - `compiler/tests/e2e/guess` checks it in made-up time, with both players a quarter of a second from the server: what a client shows of a player who runs and stops is never more than the guess past what it knows, which is the most their body moves in an update (30 ticks and more with no limit, which the same test shows), an `[Absolute]` field and this machine's own input go all the way, no button goes up for a guess, nothing runs again while nobody does anything, and no client's world ever differs from the server's, on a network that loses and reorders too. `compiler/tests/errors/absolute_*` has a file for each diagnostic.
- Matches go over WebRTC data channels, unordered and unreliable, on the web and on desktop alike, so web and desktop players meet in the same rooms: they're the only way a browser can reach another player's machine directly, so browsers host matches as well as join them. Desktop hosts also take players on a UDP port, our own thin layer, which `Session.Connect` reaches by address. The protocol above the transport stays the same.
- Desktop WebRTC is our own (`platform/src/rtc/`): P-256, ChaCha20-Poly1305 and the hashes, ICE with STUN and TURN, DTLS 1.2 and SCTP, only as much of each as a data channel needs, written from the RFCs with no third-party code, so games carry no license terms for it. Browsers are its check: it has to work against Chrome's and Firefox's (`platform/tests/rooms.mjs`). Desktop games reach the relay at `wss://` with the system's TLS (`src/rtc/tls.c`): SChannel on Windows (`secur32`), Secure Transport on macOS (the Security framework), and OpenSSL's libssl on Linux, loaded at run time rather than linked, so nothing ships with games; without it, Linux games can't reach the relay. Android apps can load no OpenSSL, so there it's Java's `SSLEngine`, through JNI. Browsers hide their local address behind `.local` names, which desktop games don't look up (hearing mDNS answers means listening on port 5353, which Windows' firewall asks players about): browsers' checks still reach the desktop side, which learns their address from them.
  - A peer is connecting until its channel is open, and connecting has an end, one way or the other (`rtc_peer.state`): ICE has 30 seconds to find a pair, DTLS 30 for its handshake, and SCTP 20 from when it starts for its association and the channel on it, both (`CONNECT_TIMEOUT` in `sctp.c`, until `rtc_sctp.connected`). Until 2026-10-06 SCTP's time was the association's alone. Ours is up from their COOKIE ECHO on, whether our COOKIE ACK reaches them or not, and from there the side that opened the channel waited for its acknowledgement, and the other for the open, with no end, while ICE's consent and DTLS held: the peer was never open and never failed, so the room said nothing (`pump` in `platform/src/rooms.c` fails a joiner's room when its peer does), and a host kept such a joiner's peer for as long as its consent held. What ended a joining player's wait was only its session's own 15 seconds for a room's host to first answer (`ROOM_TIMEOUT` in `engine/src/session.c`), as a time-out, where there's a session: the rooms test's echo cases have none, and waited their whole 45 seconds. A browser whose association never came up is no help: Chrome goes on echoing its cookie after 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 seconds, and sends no ABORT within 45. It's the deadline there was, over all it was for: nothing is sent again for it, and nothing waits longer.
  - An answer to an ICE check counts for its pair only from the address the check went to, the same way (straight, or through TURN), as RFC 8445 has it (7.2.5.2.1; `on_response` in `ice.c`, which is handed where the answer came from). From anywhere else the pair has failed, and on the pair in use it's no consent. A socket on every address answers by whichever of them the way back leaves by: on Linux and macOS, a check from a loopback socket to this machine's network address arrives, and is answered from 127.0.0.1. Taken by its transaction alone, as it was, that made the pair to the network address the one in use: everything was sent there from a loopback socket, what came back arrived on another pair, and consent was kept by the checks' answers alone. What they send comes from where they answer from, and their own check from there is how that pair is found (peer-reflexive): the pair Windows always ended on, where such a check never arrives. Seen on Linux with two desktop players, the host's socket on every address and the joiner's on loopback (`TIDE_RTC_LOCAL`), before and after.
  - SCTP starts no later than DTLS's first data for it (`dtls_in` and `start_sctp` in `peer.c`). A DTLS server is open as it answers their last flight, in the middle of reading what came, and their INIT can be the next thing it reads, in that update or that datagram. SCTP used to start once the reading was done, so that INIT was dropped, and the association came up a second later from both sides' INITs crossing: it works, at the cost of that second, on a path nothing else takes.
  - DTLS is given their datagrams once it can answer them, and not before (`dtls_answers`, `hold` and `give_held` in `peer.c`): once it's started, which for a server is at their description, and ICE has a pair in use. It answers what it reads as it reads it, and what it sends with no pair in use goes nowhere (`rtc_ice_send`). So a ClientHello read too early had its answer lost, and the handshake waited for a flight to be sent again: half a second, with our stack on both sides. Their ClientHello does come that early. They send it as soon as their own ICE has a pair, ICE hands over what comes from any address it knows, in use or not (`on_datagram` in `ice.c`), and the answer to our own check can be behind it: late, lost, or from another address than the check went to (above). Chrome, hosting, sends it earlier still, once a check of its own was answered, which takes our description alone: before its answer reaches us, when that's slower through the relay than a check is between the two machines, and nothing here says yet which side is the server (seen with its signals held back 150 ms by hand; Firefox waits until we picked a pair). What comes before DTLS can answer is held, in the order it came (`rtc_peer.held`), and DTLS gets it the moment it can: in the update that reads the answer to our check, before anything that came after. What code relies on:
    - It holds 4 datagrams (`RTC_PEER_HELD`): a ClientHello can take more than one, and they send it again meanwhile (Chrome's is 1.4 KB in two datagrams, sent again 60 ms later). One that finds no room is lost as one on its way would be, and theirs comes again.
    - Only while this side is, or may still be, the server: a client is sent nothing before its ClientHello, and what was held is dropped when their description says we're the client.
    - Nothing is timed, nothing is sent again for it, and no deadline moved: a server's 30 seconds for its handshake still start at their description.
  - A DTLS client starts, which sends its ClientHello, in the update that finds ICE's pair (`rtc_peer_update` in `peer.c`): after that update read what came, as ICE finds its pair in what's read and nowhere else (`select_pair` in `ice.c`, from the answer to our check or from their check that picks the pair). Until 2026-10-06 the start was looked at before the reading, so the ClientHello went in the next update, a frame of the game later. With our stack a room's host is the client (a peer that didn't offer answers `active`). What that update read after the pair was found is no answer to a ClientHello that hadn't gone yet, and is dropped as it was (`hold`). On Linux with two desktop players whose frames are 2 ms apart (`rooms_native.c`), from the host's pair to the joiner reading its ClientHello: a median of 3 to 4 ms before and 1 after (20 runs each, twice).
  - A check of theirs that came before their description is checked back the moment their password is there (`owed`, `check_back` and `rtc_ice_set_remote` in `ice.c`, which takes the time for it), as RFC 8445 has a triggered check done once the credentials are known (7.3, 7.3.1.4). Such a check is answered at once, which only takes our own password, and makes a pair of the address it came from; the check back is signed with theirs, so it's owed until then. It used to wait its turn after that as well: the pair ranks below the addresses their description names (a peer-reflexive candidate's priority against a host's), each of which was checked first, 20 ms apart (`CHECK_EVERY`), while they could already send on the pair their check found.
  - Those two were found together (2026-10-06), on Linux with two desktop players, the host's socket on every address and the joiner's on loopback (`TIDE_RTC_LOCAL`): the host's check arrives before its answer does, the joiner's check to the address the answer names is answered from 127.0.0.1 and counts for nothing (above), the pair their check found waits its 20 ms, and the host's ClientHello arrives in them. From their answer to the channel open, over 10 runs each: a median of 549 ms before (9 of the 10 lost the half second), 63 with the hold alone (the ClientHello held for those 20 ms), and 61 with both, the pair in use 2 ms after their answer where it took 23.
- Direct connections come first, since they cost the relay nothing. Players find each other in rooms, by a code the host picks itself (`Session.room`), so there's nothing to wait for; a code already taken gets another before anyone could read it. The relay we host (`relay/`) introduces players in a room, passing along what WebRTC needs to connect them, and nothing else goes through it. Players whose networks can't connect directly go through Cloudflare's TURN: the relay makes short-lived credentials with the key in its Fly secrets, and hands them to players. Dedicated servers stay.
- A ping is the network's round trip, and nothing else (agreed 2026-10-05: two players at one desk read 14 ms, two frames of waiting, where the owner expected 0 to 2; "logic depend on latency has other ways to use it"). `Session.ping`, the stats' ping and `tide_client_status.ping_ms` are from when this machine sent to when the answer arrived, less what the server held it. What an answer takes to be read, the waits for an update on both machines in it, is `answer_ms` (the client's `rtt_ms`), which is what the netcode still goes by: when to send again, how far ahead to run, how long to wait for a change to show. How:
  - A transport says when a datagram arrived, not when it was read: `tide_transport.clock`, its own clock in seconds, and `arrived`, when the datagram `receive` last gave arrived by it. Both NULL where it can't say (a wrapper in a test): datagrams then arrive as they're read, by the session's time, and a ping counts the waits (`clock_of`, `arrived_of` in `engine/src/session.c`).
  - The server notes when the datagram that said the time it echoes arrived (`connection.their_arrived`), and says how long it held it with every echo: `held_ms`, a varint of milliseconds after the echo in a SERVER (a byte, while it's under 128 ms) and 16 bits in a WELCOME. `PROTOCOL` went to 9 with it.
  - A client remembers when it sent each of its last 256 times, by the transport's clock (`said_time`), and `measure` finds the one echoed: the ping is the answer's arrival less that, less the held time. No session time is in it, so neither is the work of an update between reading and sending.
  - The loopback network's clock is its time, and a datagram arrived when its time came (`engine/src/net.c`): a machine's ping to its own server is 0, and tests and benchmarks repeat.
  - Rooms on the web: the page notes `performance.now()` as a datagram comes, or as the page lets go of one it held back (`Tide.lag`), which the program's clock is too (`room_arrived` in `platform/web/tide.js`).
  - UDP and desktop WebRTC read their sockets through `platform/src/arrivals.c`, which says when each datagram arrived, by `tide_time_now_ns`. Linux, macOS and Android stamp datagrams themselves (`SO_TIMESTAMP`, read with `recvmsg`; by the wall's clock, so it's how long ago that was, never longer than since the socket last had nothing). Windows only does from Windows 11 on, so there the system calls when the socket has something (`WSAEventSelect` on an event the thread pool waits on, `RegisterWaitForSingleObject`), and the call reads it and notes the time into a buffer the frame takes from (up to 256 KiB for sessions' UDP, 64 KiB for a WebRTC peer: what a socket held by itself); no thread of ours. WebRTC carries it up: the ICE socket's (`rtc_ice.arrived`), to the message it came in (`rtc_peer.arrived`), to the room (`backend_arrived` in `platform/src/rooms.c`). A host on UDP and in a room at once says each datagram's by the transport it came on.
  - `net_a_ping_is_the_networks_round_trip` (`compiler/tests/e2e/net_test.c`) checks it in made-up time: 80 ms on a network 40 ms each way with updates 11 and 22 ms apart, 0 on one that takes no time, and through a transport with no clock, the waits counted. `udp_a_ping_counts_no_wait_for_a_frame` (`platform/tests/udp.c`) checks real sockets with frames 60 ms apart: the least of 20 pings, as a busy machine can note one late (measured on Windows: 0 ms, with answers taking 120). In the docs' match on one machine it went from 13.9 ms to 0.6.
- How it works now (`tide/session.h`): the server ticks the one true world and sends each player every tick: who joined or left, the inputs that changed, and a hash of the world after it. Inputs that didn't change or arrive keep the last one, on every machine. A client keeps a snapshot of every tick from the last one the server confirmed (the verified world) to the one it predicted. A tick that arrives as the client guessed only has its hash checked against its snapshot; one that went otherwise runs on the snapshot before it, and the ticks after it run again. Worlds go as deltas (`tide/delta.h`), each a numbered transfer in chunks. A region that goes is XOR'd byte by byte with the one a stride before it where that packs it shorter: the writer tries none, 1, 2, 4, 8 and the size of the region's rows or cells, counting the bytes each leaves that aren't zeros in a sample of it, 8 at a time, and the region says which. Joining players get the world, and a cookie that gets them their `PlayerID` back when they join again. In a game with a seed (`tide_game.seeded`), the server picks the match's as it starts it, and the world carries it. A hello alone gets a welcome: the join and the world wait for the player's first message back (`greet`), so a hello from a spoofed address costs a datagram, never a player in the match or megabytes sent there, and one that never writes back gives its slot up in two seconds with its cookie. A whole world is packed once a tick, however many players join in it (`pack`). A join or leave for the next tick is never dropped: a player who joins and leaves before the tick never was in the match, so the two cancel. The server keeps every tick since a world it sent until that player catches up, however long the world took to arrive (`keep_needed`), up to eight times its four seconds; a player that falls behind otherwise, or further than that, gets the world again. A player with a world to build it from (its own, when its hash differs; its last one, coming back to a match that changed hands; or, joining, the match as it started, which it starts itself when starting calls no C: `tide_game.pure_start`) gets the hashes of the server's pages first, after how the match started, says which its world lacks, and gets those; one that doesn't come out as the hash says is asked for again, whole. Worlds that go in one update anyway go whole. Inputs go as what changed (`write_input_delta`): in frames, from the slot's input as the world keeps it before the tick; from players, each from the tick before. In an input, ints, int enums, players and entities go as varints in bits (7 bits a group and a bit for whether more follow, `tide_bits_put_varint`), signed ones zig-zagged first, so small numbers take a byte; floats keep their 32 bits, and byte and ushort enums their 8 and 16. As a delta, an int or int enum that changed goes as by how much (`tide_bits_put_changed_difference`, wrapping), and a float as its bits' XOR with what it was, the run of bits that differ and where it starts (`tide_bits_put_changed_xor`, as Gorilla packs floats), or its own 32 bits where that's shorter. Packing never changes a value: every one comes back bit for bit, NaNs and -0 too, and `tests/test_bits.c` checks it. Datagrams keep times to 16 bits and small numbers as varints, and a tick where nothing happened is its hash and a byte. `net_a_match_costs_little_to_keep_going` pins what a match costs to keep going: a change that makes it cost more updates the test on purpose. A client sets its clock from the server's tick as it sent the world, not the world's own tick, which a transfer leaves behind. Everything is resent until acknowledged; nothing waits on a reliable stream. A machine that runs the server and stops for a while (a breakpoint, a browser that freezes the page) stops its server and its player with it: its session drops the time beyond what the server can run in one update, so the two never time each other out and nothing runs ahead to catch up. A client of another machine keeps to the real time.
- Host migration is a game's choice, off by default: the `hostMigration` setting (see `docs/spec.md`, Sessions). When a room's host leaves or times out, another player's machine goes on from its verified world, which is bit for bit the server's at that tick, and the others join it again with their cookies. The server sends its players a handover record (`MSG_HANDOVER` in `engine/src/session.c`): its room's code and key, its own player, whether it's open, and who's in the match with their cookies' SHA-256. The relay decides who hosts (`{migrate: code, key}`, in `relay/relay.mjs`): it pings the room's host, and gives the room to the first player there when the host is gone. `tide/host.h` drives it (`tide_run_migrate`), with `tide_platform_room_migrate` on both platforms. A goodbye can be lost, so a match that ends (`Session.End()`, or its last scene) also tells its transports (`tide_transport.end`), and a room tells the relay (`{end}`), which keeps it as ended for five minutes: a player who comes back to take it over hears it ended. The room keeps its relay connection (or makes one) until the relay has read it (on desktop, the relay's answer to the WebSocket close that follows says so: `rtc_ws_finish`, as a socket closed at once can be reset before the relay reads it), and asks for the room first on one that hadn't, since the relay may not know it yet: a room's code shows before the relay hears of it, and a host that lost the relay is reaching it again. Nothing about it is timed on the server. So in a match with host migration on, the server keeps nothing beside the world that the players don't also have: what it keeps of its own (each player's cookie, as a hash, and whether the match is open) goes to every player. A feature that keeps more on the server has to send it too, or not work in those matches. Once visibility hides them (below), private scenes the new host wasn't in are lost, which is the game's to know; a match on an address (no relay to meet at again) ends as it does today.
- Sync relies on determinism as much as possible. The main thing sent over the network is inputs.
- Inputs come from clients, so they're attack points. The engine is forgiving with them and makes bad values hard to turn into broken math or a broken game: NaN and infinite floats in an input become the field's default before the game's `Sanitize` runs, and math functions avoid spreading NaN where they can (`Math.Clamp` always returns a value in range; `Math.Min` and `Math.Max` with one NaN return the other argument; `Math.Normalize` gives zero for a vector too short to normalize, and the identity for a quaternion, so there's no `NormalizeSafe`: `tide_normalize_*` in `tide/math.h`).
- State corrections are supported. Divergence is detected through state hashing.
- A world is hashed and checked every tick, in every build (decided 2026-10-07, when the question was whether the check should be a debug build's alone, the language being deterministic): "i would rather check it every tick or not check it at all; so if we are to check, we should do it on tick. its more predictable, reliable and its the fastest to correct or notify of error." Never every Nth tick, which was proposed and turned down: it pays the same cost as a spike. The language keeps Tide code deterministic, not the C a game calls, which is where both divergences found so far were (Box3D's signed zeros and its lock on the web), and the check is what turns one from players silently seeing different matches into a correction within a tick. So what it costs is made small, not seldom (see ECS and simulation state: the wide page hash, and hashing on a few threads).
- **Provisional:** `[Position]` and `[Rotation]` describe match fields for view interpolation and smooth prediction corrections. `[Position]` on a float or floating-point vector keeps linear tick interpolation and accumulates additive presentation error. `[Rotation]` on a float in radians or a quaternion uses shortest-path angular or relative-quaternion interpolation and correction error. Error is local to presentation and decays exponentially with frame time, with a provisional 75 ms half-life; later corrections accumulate, while ordinary movement follows the corrected simulation without being low-passed. Untagged fields keep ordinary tick interpolation. `entity.Snap()` and `singleton.Snap()` remain immediate and clear accumulated error. These attributes never change simulation writes, collisions, world hashes or network state. Ordinary structs can contain annotated built-in fields; correction smoothing through a type's custom `Interpolate` is deferred, with no new custom-type contract or rate setting agreed. Tags take no arguments and cannot combine with each other or `[Snap]` on one field. They do not belong on local state, inputs or events. A new match, hot reload or replacement without comparable presentation history clears the error; migration retains it while drawing the frozen world, then clears it on adoption of the new network timeline. See `docs/spec.md`, Provisional correction smoothing.
- Clients can be denied specific state through a visibility system, for example other players' cards in a poker game. Not built yet: a scene says who's in it (`SceneVisibility.Private`, `Scene.AddPlayer`), but every player is still sent the whole world.
- Visibility is dynamic. The server decides at runtime what each player can see, through an API.
- Each client tracks a **verified tick**. It is the latest tick for which any state the client lacked but should have has arrived, and any divergence has been corrected by the server. Ticks after it are predicted and may be wrong for a while.
- Game code tells verified state from predicted state with one word, `verified`, a parameter's modifier like `mut` (agreed 2026-10-05; `docs/spec.md`, Views and drawing, and Events). For example, a player death visual can wait until the death is verified.
  - In a view, `verified Health health` and `with verified Dead` read the world the server has confirmed, the session's verified world, which `tide_frame` is handed. A view with components of both worlds runs for the drawn world's entities that are the same entity in the confirmed one (`decl.verified_join`: each row looks the entity up there, `gen_row_join` in `compiler/src/codegen.c`, by handle and by what each was born as); one whose components are all `verified` runs for the confirmed world's entities (`decl.verified_entities`, whose `need_mask` is theirs). A view that takes any is handed the confirmed world (`decl.takes_verified`: `tide_run_view_<View>` gets `tide_verified`, which is `w` where the host gave no verified world). Values aren't blended. Showing other players from confirmed state, blended between confirmed ticks, is open, and may belong at another level.
  - A `verified` value is whole (owner, 2026-10-05: `verified` is valid wherever it makes sense): what the confirmed world keeps in its heap is read there, beside the heap of the world the frame draws. A handle's two `where` bits have a fourth value for it, `TIDE_IN_VERIFIED` (`tide/text.h`), which is only ever in a handle, never a `where` a write is given: `tide_frame` says which heap it is (`tide_text_use_verified`), reads find a handle's heap with `tide_heap_read`, and everything that changes a block still finds its heap with `tide_heap_of`, which the confirmed world's never is, so nothing writes there. Where a view's wrapper copies a `verified` component or singleton out of the confirmed world, it retags the copy's text, lists, maps, sets and grids (`tide_confirmed`, `gen_confirmed_copy` in `compiler/src/codegen.c`), so nothing of them is copied and functions the value is passed to read it by what it says. A list's elements and a map's entries are in the heap with their own handles, which still say the drawn world's, so a list or map whose elements hold text is copied to the scratch area with that text retagged (text is all elements can hold); the wrapper frees those when it's done (`tide_confirmed_mark`). A read path that's added to the runtime resolves a handle's heap with `tide_heap_read`, or it reads a `verified` value as empty. `compiler/tests/e2e/verified_heap` reads every kind from two worlds that ticked differently, and from a snapshot that shares the drawn world's pages, and checks the confirmed world's bytes are as they were.
  - In match code it's an error: the match is the same on every machine and has no "confirmed".
  - `compiler/tests/e2e/verified_views` checks views on one world, on two worlds that disagree about a handle, and on a client that predicts wrong for a while.
- **Local handlers of match events** (agreed 2026-10-05; `docs/spec.md`, Events): `local event(Hit hit) ShowHit(Body body, mut Fighter fighter, mut Fx fx)` hears the match's `Hit` on each machine, at the start of a frame, before the views, for each tick simulated since the last frame, in order. It takes the event, the target entity's components (read-only, `mut` reaching only their local fields, as in views), match singletons (read-only) and local singletons (`mut`), all of the match's as the event's turn left them, even if the entity is gone by the frame. The engine's own events have them too. `local event(verified Died died)` runs once, when the server confirmed the tick that sent the event.
  - **Once, under rollback:** each tick remembers which events it delivered locally. Run again, the same event (tick, event, entity and fields) stays silent; one only the corrected tick has is heard then, late; one the correction removed isn't taken back.
  - **Events only local code handles** cost the match nothing: its bytes, hash, snapshots and traffic are the same without them, and a machine with nobody to show it to does no work for them. `compiler/tests/e2e/local_events_same.h` pins that against the same game without them (`local_events_same`, `local_events_plain`), as `local_fields_same.h` does for local fields: a change to how worlds are kept or sent updates its numbers too.
  - In the compiler, such a handler is a listener: `decl.listens` (`check_listener` in `compiler/src/check.c`), kept in its event's `listeners`, never in its `handlers`, which the tick runs. Nothing of it is in the world. A tick records what listeners take only when it's given a log: generated `tide_world_tick_events` sets the log for the thread that runs the tick (a thread-local of the generated `.c`, `tide_listening`), and as each event's turn comes, after the match's handlers of it, `tide_hear_<Event>` (`gen_hear`) pushes a `tide_heard` (`tide/events.h`) and then a `tide_heard_<Event>`: the event, and the components and singletons its listeners take, gathered then, with a bit for each listener that runs for the entity. `Spawned` is recorded as the spawn applies and `Destroyed` before the row goes. An event with listeners and no match handler isn't even queued while no tick records (`tide_listeners`), unless it holds text, whose heap blocks every machine has to take the same way.
  - The frame runs them from the records (`tide_local_hear`, `gen_heard`), before `tide_local_forget`, so a handler still finds the local fields of an entity that's gone since; a row kept for another entity that had the handle goes first (`tide_let_go_<Component>_local`, by what each was born as). Games with listeners of entity events keep what entities are born as (`entities_born`).
  - Text, lists, maps, sets and grids of the match aren't recorded yet: a listener that names one is an error, and the copies have them empty. `World`, `Devices`, the input, `with ownership` and `async` are errors there too.
- The contract for what local handlers hear (Claude's first version, proposed 2026-10-05, for the owner to confirm; `tide/events.h`):
  - `tide_event_log` is a growing run of records, each a `tide_heard` (its size, tick, event, `wants` and `as` (`TIDE_HEARD_PREDICTED`, `TIDE_HEARD_VERIFIED`), target and what it was born as, the bits of the listeners that run, and `same`, a hash of the event's fields) and then what the game's build lays out after it. A log says whose build its records are (`build`), and another build takes none of them. A record whose event is `TIDE_HEARD_ROWS` is no event: the rows a tick recorded for its events' handlers' queries, after the tick's events (see Queries in views and local handlers, under ECS and simulation state), which an event says it reads with `TIDE_HEARD_READS_ROWS` in its `wants`.
  - `tide_game.tick_events(world, jobs, events)` is `tide_game.tick` recording into `events`; NULL in a game with no listeners. Generated `tide_world_tick_events(w, jobs, events)` is always there, and `tide_world_tick` and `tide_world_tick_on` record nothing.
  - `tide_client_desc.events` is the log a client's host takes from, once it's `taken`; `tide_session_events(session)` gives a session's, and marks it taken, so a session records from the first call on. A client keeps a `tide_hearing`: for each tick it ran and hasn't had confirmed, what the tick delivered (`tide_heard_mark`) and what its last run sent (`run_tick` in `engine/src/session.c`: `tide_hearing_begin`, `tide_hearing_end`). A tick's new events go out as `TIDE_HEARD_PREDICTED`; once the server confirms the tick, its last run's go out as `TIDE_HEARD_VERIFIED` (`tide_hearing_confirm`). A world the server sends again drops the ticks before it unheard (`tide_hearing_skip_to`); a host that stopped taking for `TIDE_HEARD_TICKS` ticks is recorded nothing more until it asks again. The server's own world records nothing.
  - Generated `tide_local_events(local, events)` moves a log's records into the local state and leaves it empty; the next `tide_frame` runs them first. Records with no `as` come straight from a tick, with no session between, and run both kinds of handler: a C host that ticks a world itself passes a log to `tide_world_tick_events` and hands it over, and what it ticks is all there is. `tide_host_game.events` is `tide_local_events`, which the standard host calls before `frame` with `tide_session_events`, asked for before the session's update.
  - Hot reloading drops what's recorded (`tide_hearing_rebuilt` in `tide_session_set_game` and `tide_session_migrate`): what each tick delivered is kept, so ticks run again stay silent, and ticks confirmed as the old build ran them tell `verified` handlers nothing. A match that changes hands starts a new client, which hears the ticks after the new host's confirmed one again.
  - `compiler/tests/e2e/local_events` checks all of it: a C host with one world, a match on the loopback network where a client predicts wrong (heard once through replays, late for what only the correction has, never taken back, `verified` only what the server had), and one machine through a session.
- Secrets such as RNG seeds are ordinary state behind visibility, for example an entity the client can't see. They need no special mechanism.
- Clients don't have to simulate the whole world. LOD borders and culling are resolved through state sync. Not built yet: every client ticks the whole world.
- Hosting (not built yet): Tide will host games for people, and hosted matches will run as WebAssembly in a sandbox (a runtime like wasmtime), with a time budget per tick. Owning the language doesn't make hosted code safe by itself: games can call C, and even pure Tide can loop forever. In the sandbox, C only reaches the game's own memory and what the host gives it, so `extern` stays allowed. A hosted game's match code, and the C it calls, has to build for the web; a server build can leave out views and local code.

## Modding

What's agreed (2026-10-05); the design proposed for the rest, and its open questions, are in `docs/spec.md`, Mods. Two parts are built: `virtual` and `override`, in the language for any package (see Language), and the service's first slice, for web games (The build service, below), which is on hold. The rest isn't: tidec's rules for what a mod may be, games that load builds, the prompt before joining, the sandbox.

**Open again** (the owner, 2026-10-06: "its no longer decided"): who builds a game with its mods. That a service we run does was agreed, and its first slice was built, which is on hold (The build service, below). It isn't decided any more, and neither is what rested on it in the list below, each marked: builds fetched from the service, signed by it and revoked there, what the `mods` setting lets a game run, and a closed game's source being the service's alone. Nothing is agreed in its place. What's being weighed is in `docs/spec.md`, Mods: a game that loads a build of itself for its platform, as `tide run`'s host loads one, compiled on the player's machine from the mods' source where a compiler can run (the developer opting in to ship it), or made elsewhere and fetched where one can't. The rest of the list stands.

- (Who builds is open again, above.) Mods are a service we run, the third beside the relay and hosting: remote compilation. A mod is a package that someone other than the game's developer adds to it, and the service builds the game with its mods, whole, natively, for each set of them. So mod code is game code, with its storage, schedule and speed: nothing is interpreted (the owner: the modding community is big, and limiting it is rough), no game carries a runtime for mods, and the engine gains no dynamic path for them.
- (Where a build is fetched from, who signs it and how it's revoked are open again, above.) Every machine of a match runs the same build, local code included, fetched from the service, never from the host: mods never differ between a match's players, not even ones that only change views, which can show a player what gives them an advantage. Before a game joins a match of another build, local code is told its mods and has to say yes, so the game can ask its player, who then knows code from outside is about to be downloaded; nothing is fetched before that. The prompt is the game's own, and the engine draws none and enforces none (decided over a prompt of the host's that no game could skip: it would be our look and language in their game, and the sandbox is what protects players, not the prompt). A game whose code never says yes joins no such match; for mods a developer listed, asking is the game's choice; for mods from anyone, telling the player is a condition of the service, which turns them off for a game that doesn't. A build's mods are a set, whatever order they're listed in. Builds are signed with a key per game, and can be revoked.
- A mod is Tide alone: no `extern`, C, C++ or libraries. Its files are virtual, as a web page's are, and its code calls nothing that reaches outside the game (`Session.Connect` and `Clipboard.Copy` first; the list is part of the sandbox's work).
- A mod replaces a game's declaration with an explicit `override`, and only one the game marked `virtual`: what can be replaced is the developer's choice. An override matches by name and by parameters, both, filters among them: systems can have the same filters under different names. It's a substitution while compiling, and the override takes the replaced declaration's place in the schedule; nothing calls what was replaced for now (a `base()` may come later). Two mods replacing one declaration are an error that names both.
